# Matthew

# ANNOTATIONS ON THE GOSPEL

# ACCORDING TO S. MATTHEW

## THE ARGUMENT

The Greek word *Εὐαγγέλιον*, translated Gospel, 1. In general, even among profane authors also does usually signify good or glad tidings, and it is used also for a reward given to him that brings such tidings: as also, for sacrifices offered upon such occasions. But in Scripture it is taken sometimes more largely for the doctrine of salvation procured, and purchased by Christ; either foretold and promised by God and his prophets, before Christ’s coming; or by him and his apostles published after his coming. So is both the noun and the verb deduced from it, used Gal. 3.8. Heb. 4.2. Matt. 11.5. Luke 4.28. Acts 13.32. And in this sense Paul calls it his Gospel, Rom. 2.16. and our Gospel, 2 Cor. 4.5. as preached by him, and his fellow-ministers, and to the Ephesians, Chap. 1.13. their Gospel, as preached and delivered to them. But taken more strictly in common speech it is used to design the history of Christ’s birth, life, and death. The word Gospel, whereby we express the Greek word, is an old Saxon word, importing not Gods-spel or word in general, as divers mistake it, but according to the manner of that language a Gods-spel, that is, a good or gladsome word, and so does precisely answer to the Greek. The writers of this history are hence commonly called Evangelists: which term, howsoever it may in general signify any preacher of the Gospel, yet in the Scripture of the New Testament it is more peculiarly applied unto some that were assistant unto the apostles in their ministry, and employed by them to settle things in some churches by them founded, when upon their necessary occasions of departure, some extraordinary things were to be further carried on. Such were Philip and Timothy, Acts 21.8. 2 Tim. 4.5. And so the word seems to be taken, 2 Cor. 12.28. Eph. 4.11. but in common use it is more peculiarly applied to those that writ the history of Christ’s life and death, as was before said. Of this kind we have four only, whose writings have been from time to time received by the Church, and approved of by the people of God, as immediately inspired and directed by the Spirit of God in this their employment. Of these, two were apostles, to wit, Matthew and John: the other disciples of the apostles, Mark and Luke. As for other Gospels, as that termed of Nicodemus, that fathered on Thomas by the Manichees, and that among the Nazarens, mentioned by Hierom, and others the like; they were never acknowledged, or admitted as authentical in the orthodox churches. The first writer in order is called Matthew, for so he terms himself, Chap. 9.9. 10.3. As also Mark calls him, Chap. 3.18. and Luke, Chap. 6.15. Howbeit, he is also termed Levi by the same Luke, Chap. 5.27. and said to be the son of Alpheus, Mark 2.14. But whether of that Alpheus, who was the father of James and John, is not so certain. Some have affirmed that he writ his Gospel, first in Hebrew, and that it was afterward turned into Greek, and of the same there are two Hebrew copies abroad. But neither is there any certainty, nor probability that he so did, and those Hebrew copies are from the style of them deemed by the learned to be of a far later date. He was before his conversion and calling, by profession and employment a publican. Matt. 9. Mark 2. Luke 5. one that gathered the emperor’s tolls and tributes, and was from the customhouse called to be an apostle to gather souls unto God, Matt. 10.3. as some other of them also were called from being fishermen to be fishers of men, Matt. 4.18, 19. He begins with Christ’s genealogy, birth, and flight into Egypt, proceeds to his baptism, temptations, and calling of disciples, relates at large his sermons, disputes, and parables, with the interpretations of them, records many of his famous works and miracles, and concludes with a large discourse of his prodition, passion, death and resurrection, shutting all up with a solemn commission given to his apostles, before his departure from them. God give us grace so to read, that we may believe in the Son of God, and by trusting in him alone be eternally saved. Amen.

## CHAPTER 1

*{The book}* The word does not always signify a large book, but oftentimes a brief roll or catalogue, or enumeration, Jer. 3.8. *ספר* *sepher*, a book or bill of divorce, 70. *βιβλίον*; so the Jews did begin their genealogies, Gen. 5.1.

*{of the generation}* Luke 3.23. of the ancestors of our Savior Christ successively; yet Gen. 5.1. it signifies the posterity of the first Adam; this is not the title of the whole book, but only of the beginning of it; it may include not his ancestors only, but also the manner of his conception and birth, which is likewise set down in this chapter, v. 18. and so it may be the title of the whole chapter. Matthew purposing to write of the life of our Savior, begins with his pedigree, because he knew the Jews stood much upon their genealogies.

*{of Jesus}* The name given him by the angel with the reason thereof v. 21. it signifies a Savior; it was sometimes given to others that were types of Christ, as to Joshua, who brought the people into Canaan; to Jehoshua the high priest after the captivity, and to divers others as, Acts 13.6. Col. 4.11. but in a more peculiar manner it is given to Christ, who saves us from our sins.

*{Christ}* It is all one with Messiah, and signifies, Anointed, it sets out unto us the King and Priest of the Church, for such were wont to be anointed.

*{the Son}* Herein Matthew differs from the other Evangelists. Mark begins with John Baptist’s ministry, who was the forerunner of our Savior. Luke with the birth of John Baptist, John with Christ’s divinity; but this Evangelist with his humanity, ancestors and birth.

*{of David}* He names only David and Abraham at first, because to these was the solemn promise made, that the Messiah should come of their seed, Gen. 12.3. and 22.18. 2 Sam. 7.12. Ps. 132.11. Acts 12.30. And to the one was promised a populous progeny, Gen. 15.5. and 22.17. to the other a stable kingdom, 2 Sam. 7.12, 13. both which are eminently fulfilled in Christ. He names David first, because he was a king; and the Jews, who expected a temporal 4kingdom with the Messiah, looked upon him principally, as the Son of David, chap. 9.27. and 12.23. and 15.22. and 20.30, 31. and 21.9, 15. and 22.42. Mark 11.10. Luke 1.32. John 7.42. Also that ambiguity might be taken away; for if he had named Abraham first, and then David, men might have thought Abraham misnamed for a son of David. Lastly, that the genealogy might go on the more smoothly, and having once named Abraham, he might go on with him, not naming David between.

*{the son of Abraham}* Which David was son of Abraham, or which Jesus Christ was the son of Abraham, as well as of David. Thus Matthew reckons upon his genealogy, to show that he was the promised Messiah, and to take away the scandals which the Jews had, because of the poverty of Mary his mother, and Joseph his supposed father.

**V. 2. ***{Abraham begat Isaac}* Gen. 21.3. Matthew brings our Savior down from Abraham, for the comfort of the Jews, who drew their genealogies from him, because God had taken him and his seed into a peculiar covenant. Luke carries him upward unto Adam, for the comfort of the Gentiles, who were not lineally of the seed of Abraham.

*{Isaac begat Jacob}* Gen. 25.26.

*{Jacob begat Judas}* Gen. 29.35.

*{and his brethren}* These are added, not that Christ came of them, but to show that he esteemed himself their kindred, and that all the tribes were included in the covenant, as well as the tribe of Judah; for this reason is Zara also named, v. 3. yet Ishmael is not added to Isaac, though he were the firstborn; nor Esau to Jacob, although at the same time with Jacob in the womb, because these belonged not unto the covenant, which said, *Unto thy seed have I given this land*, Gen. 15.18. Gen. 17.8. and *all the nations of the earth shall be blessed in him*, Gen. 18.18. & Gen. 17.21. he says, *But my covenant will I establish with Isaac*, who was a type of Christ; therefore the apostle says, Gal. 3.16. *He saith not unto seeds, as of many, but as of one, and to thy seed which is Christ*, and Heb. 11.18. *In Isaac shall thy seed be called*.

*{Judas begat Phares}* Gen. 38.27.

*{of Thamar}* Christ vouchsafing to come of Thamar, Rahab and Ruth the Moabitess, shows that he came to save great sinners, as well as they who were not so notorious, Gentiles as well as Jews, chap. 9.13. Eph. 2.1. 1 Tim. 1.15. it may be also a part of his humiliation; as Christ took all our infirmities upon him, so for our sake was he not ashamed of such parents. True virtue, though but humane, cannot be discovered by parents’ shame.

*{Phares begat Esrom}* Ruth 4.18. 1 Chron. 2.5.

**V. 4. ***{Aram begat Aminadab}* Of these to Jesse little is said in Scripture, for either they lived in slavery in Egypt, or in trouble in the wilderness, or in obscurity in Canaan before the kingdom was settled.

**V. 5. ***{Of Rachab}* This is not mentioned in the Old Testament, but seems to come as a true tradition from father to son, as that of Jannes and Jambres resisting Moses, 2 Tim. 3.8. and Enoch’s prophesying, Jude verse 14. Some think Matthew had this out of the civil tables of genealogy, which the Jews kept more exactly than other nations.

*{of Ruth}* This shows that the Gentiles that were not to be destroyed, as the Moabites of whom Ruth came, might be saved by Christ. (See Deut. 2.9.) as the mentioning of Rahab of the seed of Canaan may import, that such as remained of those that were to be cut off, Deut. 7.2. might also be saved by him.

*{Jesse}* It seems that he was of no great account, because David is called in contempt the son of Jesse, 1 Sam. 20.27, 30, 31. 1 Kings 12.16. as Christ was called the son of Joseph, Luke 4.22.

**V. 6. ***{Jesse begat David}* He was the youngest of Jesse’s sons, 1 Sam. 17.12, 14. yet Christ comes of him, to show that this line was not guided by natural succession all along, but sometimes as God’s favor preferred the younger before the elder; so in another line, Gen. 48.14, 18, 19.

*{the King}* The first king in the line of our Savior Christ. David’s kingdom lasted long over Judah, but Christ’s spiritual kingdom, over the Church, is everlasting, Dan. 2.44. Luke 1.32, 33.

*{begat Solomon}* 2 Sam. 12.24. Christ succeeding great David, and wise Solomon, shows that some great and noble shall be saved by him, though not many in comparison of others, 1 Cor. 1.20. most think that the legal line of our Savior is henceforth prosecuted by Matthew, as the natural by Luke, who derives him from Nathan, Luke 3.31. and that here he is reckoned as heir to Solomon in the kingdom, whose race some think failed, at, before, or in the captivity though some think otherwise. See on Jer. 22.28, 30. Others, think the line to be natural in either, but the one draws down to Joseph, the other to Mary.

**V. 7. ***{Solomon begat Roboam}* 1 Chron. 3.10.

**V. 8. ***{Joram begat Ozias}* Not immediately; for three kings came between, Ahaziah, Joash, and Amaziah, 1 Chron. 3.11, 12. but mediately, as verse 1. Luke 1.5. and 13.16. John 8.33, 39. it is likely those three kings are omitted, because Matthew intended to keep within the compass of fourteen generations here, as well as afterwards: some think these three kings omitted rather than others, because God’s curse lay on Joram (for marrying of Ahab’s daughter) to the fourth generation, as the blessing was on Jehu, for destroying Ahab’s posterity, 2 Kings 10.30.

**V. 10. ***{Ezekias begat Manasses}* 2 Kings 20.21. 1 Chron. 3.13.

**V. 11. ***{Josias begat Jechonias}* Some read, *Josias begat Jakim, and Jakim begat Jeconias*.

*{and his brethren}* Some conceive therefore, that by Jechonias here must needs be meant Jehoiakim, (because Jechoniah had no brethren) and that his brethren are here mentioned, because two of them were kings, Jehoahaz and Zedekiah: yet were they all noted for calamity, to show, that it was no strange thing to see the royal stock so low afterwards: yet Jehoiachin is said to have a brother called Zedekiah, 1 Chron. 3.16. which cannot be Zedekiah his uncle, that was king after him, for he is reckoned there verse 15, as the son of Josiah.

*{about the time}* Or, *who lived in the time*; for Josias was dead before, and so could not then beget children.

**V. 12. ***{Jechonias begat Salathiel}* 1 Chron. 3.16, 17. Salathiel is called the son of Neri, Luke 3.27. but is said to be the son of Jechonias, because he succeeded him in authority, and it is hard to give a reason why he should be said to be a meaner man’s son, if he were a king’s: others conceive him to be the natural son of Jeconias. See 1 Chron. 3.17. and notes on Jer. 22.28, 30.

*{Salathiel begat Zorobabel}* He is conceived to be that Zerubbabel that reedified the temple, Ezra 5.2. he is said to be the son of Pedaiah, 1 Chron. 3.19. either this was another Zerubbabel, or else Salathiel died without children, and Pedaiah took his wife, and raised up seed to his brother according to the law, and then he was the legal son of Salathiel, and the natural son of Pedaiah.

**V. 13. ***{Zorobabel begat Abiud}* Here the Old Testament is silent, and neither Abiud, nor any of his posterity is mentioned, 1 Chron. 3.19, &c. it seems the Evangelist had the rest ensuing, either by tradition, or out of the civil records, see on verse 5, or by revelation.

**V. 16. ***{Jacob begat Joseph}* It seems that Joseph was the natural son of Jacob, and the son of Eli by marrying Mary, Luke 3.23. and this line is carried to Joseph, because women’s genealogies were not reckoned, but included in their husbands: yet Luke reckons Mary’s, that it might appear, that Christ came of David’s seed, though sometimes they married out of their tribes.

*{of whom}* Of which Mary; for Christ was not the son of Joseph, verse 18.

*{Jesus who is called Christ}* See on verse 1. he was the Messiah expected.

**V. 17. ***{All the generations}* Or *descents* marshaled here by the Evangelists in three ranks, before the kingdom, under it, and after it: some of Jesse’s race are omitted to make the number in each equal, 1 Chron. 3.11, 12.

*{from Abraham to David}* Reckoning themselves for two.

*{from David}* Accounting him and Josias in the number.

*{from the carrying away}* Beginning with Josias, and ending with Joseph; or beginning with Jechonias, and ending with Christ.

**V. 18. ***{the birth of}* See on verse 1.

*{on this wise}* Or, *came thus to pass*.

*{espoused to Joseph}* God would have her to be espoused for the safety and honor of Christ in his infancy, and the credit and comfort of his mother. *Jerom. advers. Helvid.* gives three reasons: 1. That by the genealogy of Joseph, of whose kindred Mary was, Mary’s pedigree might also be showed. 2. Lest according to Moses’ law she should have been stoned by the people as an adulteress. 3. That fleeing into Egypt, she might have the comfort *Custodis potius quam mariti*, of a keeper rather than an husband: the two first may seem probable.

*{before they came together}* Had knowledge one of another, as verse 25. or rather before they came to dwell together: for he had not yet taken her home, verse 20.

*{she was found with child}* It was perceived by Joseph, verse 19. that she was with child, Gr. Having in the womb.

*{of the Holy Ghost}* This was not known to Joseph, till afterward v. 20. the angel informed him, that this was by the extraordinary power and work of the Holy Ghost: Luke 1.35. So this particle *ἐκ*, of, is often used, John 8.41, 44, 47. 1 John 3.8, 9.

**V. 19. ***{Joseph her husband}* So called, because he was betrothed to her, Gen. 19.8, 12. & 29.21. Deut. 22.24.

*{being a just man}* Of holy conversation, as Luke 1.6. and tender disposition.

*{and not willing}* He was not willing to take her to wife, for fear of dishonor, nor yet to shame her, or destroy her, by putting her away publicly.

*{to put her away privily}* To leave her to herself, or friends’ disposal, without publishing any reason of such desertion.

**V. 20. ***{to take unto thee Mary thy wife}* To take her home to you as your wife, who is already betrothed to you.

*{that which is conceived in her}* Or *bred*, to wit, of her substance; he says, that, not he; because Christ took on him the nature, not the person of man.

*{is of the Holy Ghost}* See on verse 18. as man he had no father, as God he had no mother, Heb. 7.3.

**V. 21. ***{she shall bring forth a Son}* Luke 1.31.

*{for he shall save his people}* He is called Jesus, because he 5delivers us from our sins, which would else condemn us. See on verse 1. those are his people, who being redeemed, believe in him, and obey him, John 3.16. Heb. 5.9.

**V. 22. ***{All this was done}* Christ was thus miraculously conceived.

*{that it might be fulfilled}* It came not by accident, but the counsel of God foretold long ago by the prophet Isaiah, 7.14. The Holy Scriptures, though written by men, are the undoubted word of God, 2 Pet. 1.21.

**V. 23. ***{Behold a Virgin}* In Isaiah 7. *העלמה*, *ahalma*, and in the Greek text here, *ἡ παρθένος* in either as an article to point out the woman, and to set her out plainly, as if you should say, that virgin, or a certain virgin.

*{they shall call his Name}* Or, *his name shall be called*: we read not anywhere that he was so called; but the meaning is, that he should do that which the name imports well deserving so to be called, as being one by whom God would dwell with us, united to our nature by incarnation, to our persons by reconciliation. See the like phrase Isaiah, 9.6. and 62.4. Jer. 23.6. Ezek. 48.35. So the Scripture many times gives the name from the event; the meaning is, thus it shall be, mortal men shall see Christ, God and man, conversant with men. See 1 Tim. 3.16. 1 John 1.1. So after Chrysostom Theophylact interprets, *αὐτὰ τὰ πράγματα δείξουσιν αὐτὸν Θεὸν ὄντα*, his works shall declare him to be God, conversant with us.

*{which being interpreted}* Some think, this shows that Matthew writ not this Gospel in Hebrew, for then he needed not to have interpreted Emmanuel, which is an Hebrew word. The Old Testament was written in Hebrew, because it was the Jews’ language, who were then the only visible Church: but the New Testament in Greek, which was the language then most in use in all countries since the decay of the Grecian monarchy.

**V. 24. ***{did as the Angel said}* *Προσέταξεν αὐτῷ*, had commanded him. So the interpreter of the Syriac, *sicut mandaverat ei*. This sudden change of his resolution to put her away, verse 19. sets forth Joseph’s great faith, and ready obedience to God: the Spirit of God imprinted in him by that dream, such a confidence of the truth, as left him no more fluctuant and doubtful therein: when God speaks to our hearts, we cheerfully and speedily address ourselves to that, which before not only scrupled us, but seemed most inconvenient, unpleasing to our sense, and unconsentient with our own principles of humane reason. So Moses took up the serpent, Exodus 4.4. So Abraham departed out of his native soil, Genesis 12.4. and rose up early to go to offer his only son Isaac, Genesis, 22.4. So when God had spoken to Laban, Genesis, 31.24. he could speak no evil to Jacob.

**V. 25. ***{And he knew her not}* Or, *but he, &c.* so this particle *καὶ* is used, Chapter 21.19. knew her not: See Gen. 4.1. Luke 1.34.

*{till she had brought forth}* She was therefore a pure virgin when Christ was born, as well as when he was conceived: neither does it necessarily follow that he knew her after; for these words *ἕως οὗ*, till, import the exclusion of all former, but not the admission of a later, as Chapter 5.26. Suidas interprets the raven’s not returning, Gen. 8.7. *עד* *bad*. 70. *ἕως* (until the floods were dried) by *εἰς τὸ παντελὲς οὐκ ἀνέστρεφε*, never, or not at all returned. So on Psalm 110.1. *ἕως ἂν*, &c. *Sit thou on my right hand until I make, &c.* *οὐ χρονικὸν ἐστὶ τὸ ἕως, ἀλλ᾽ ἰδίωμα τῆς γραφῆς*, &c. this (till) imports not any definite space of time, but is a certain propriety of Scripture speaking. See *Jerom. advers. Helvid.* and *Gr. Nazianzen orat. 36.* (a little after the beginning) See Genesis 28.15. Deuteronomy, 34.6. 1 Samuel 15.33. 2 Samuel 6.23. Matthew 28.20. 1 Tim. 4.13. Matt. 5.26.

*{her firstborn son}* Neither does this import that she had any son after, but that she had none before: he was *πρωτότοκος* and *μονογενής*, her firstborn and only Son: so the Scripture uses to call him the firstborn, *non tantum post quem & alii, sed ante quem nullus. Jerom. advers. Helvid.* not only after whom others are born, but before whom there was none.

*{he called his Name Jesus}* Joseph did as he was commanded by the angel, verse 21. and as a legal father. Mary is said also to do it, Luke 1.32. as a natural mother; for mothers sometimes imposed names, Gen. 4.25. and 29.32. and 30.11.

## CHAPTER 2

**Verse 1. ***{When Jesus was born}* Luke 2.6, 7. was newly born: not long after his birth: as Heb. 11.23. Before their leaving Bethlehem: that is, within forty days after the birth of Christ. Compare Luke 2.22. with Leviticus 12.2, 4. their short stay at Bethlehem was the cause, that little notice was taken of Christ’s birth there in after times: See John 1.43, 46.

*{in Bethlehem of Judaea}* To distinguish it from that other Bethlehem in the tribe of Zebulun, Josh. 19.15. See Mic. 5.2. here David was born, 1 Sam. 16.1. *בית* *לחם* *יהודה* *beth lechem Jehuda*, Judg. 17.7, 8. 70. *Βηθλεὲμ Ἰούδα*, that Bethlehem which was in the tribe of Judah: *Jerom. de loc. Hebr.* the name Judea signifies either all the province of the Jews (so distinguished from other nations and provinces) of which, because the tribe of Judah was the principal, they received the name of Jews; and their country (which fell to the lot of all the tribes) Judea: *amiserunt vetus nomen Hebraei, et quoniam princeps examinis eorum Judas erat Judaei sunt appellati, & terra quam incoluere, Judaea. Lactant. l. 4. c. 10.* the Hebrews lost their own name, and because Judas was the prince in their republic, they were called Jews, and the land in which they dwelt, Judea; or Judea did particularly signify that part in which Jerusalem was, containing the two tribes Judah and Benjamin as one, (1 Kings 11.13. and 12.21.) who clave to the family of David: this was called Judea in contradistinction to Galilee as Matthew, 19.1. Mark 3.7. John 4.4, 54. and to Samaria, (Acts 8.1.) Decapolis and other parts of the same province and land, which God promised, and gave to the Israelites.

*{Herod the king}* Herod the great, called Ascalonita, he was an Idumean, and son of Antipater, as Theophylact says; but (in the right of a proselyte) a Jew. See *Isa. Casaubon Exercit. 1. N. 5.* he is here called the king; there was another Herod, who slew John Baptist, but he had only the honor of a tetrarch, this of a king. *Chrysost. Hom. 6. in Matth.* concerning Herod’s rising to the kingdom; see *Joseph. Antiq. l. 14. c. 26. & 15.10.*

*{wise men}* Learned men; it seems they were astronomers, because God taught them by a star.

*{from the East}* In respect of Jerusalem, as Deut. 4.41. Zech. 14.4. Some think the word *magi* to be a Persian word, and that these came out of Persia, *Chrysost. hom. 6.* Others think they came out of Arabia, anciently famous for wise men, 1 Kings 4.30. and bare eastward from Jerusalem, Gen. 10.30. & 25.6. Judg. 6.3. Job 1.3. Isaiah 12.14. Jer. 49.28. their gifts also (verse 11.) show it, which were plentiful in Arabia, to wit, myrrh, and frankincense, and gold, 2 Chron. 19.14. Psalm 72.15. See the learned *Isa. Casaubon. exercit. 1. N. 19.* Their coming so far to find Christ condemns the obstinacy of the Jews, who would not receive him who came to them.

*{to Jerusalem}* Where it was most likely the king of the Jews should be born, or at least to be heard of, because it was their chief city.

**V. 2. ***{he that is born}* Or, *the born king, &c*.

*{King of the Jews}* Christ a poor child, laid in a crib, and nothing set by of his own people, notwithstanding, receives an honorable testimony of his divine, and kingly power from strangers, to whom God revealed him.

*{his Star}* As God made known the birth of Christ to the Jews by an angel, Luke 2.9, 10. so to the Gentiles by a new created star: they knew by it that Christ was born, either because it stood over, or went toward Judea; or by some old tradition; or new revelation.

*{in the East}* In our own country: see on verse 1.

**V. 3. ***{he was troubled}* For fear of the loss of his kingdom; because he was a stranger, and might think the Jews would be glad to have a king of their own, whom they would readily assist: *ipsos terret potestas sua, quos facit esse terribiles, Cyprian, l. 2. ep. 2.* their own power terrifies themselves, which makes them terrible to others.

*{and all Jerusalem}* As fearing trouble from Herod, or the Romans: as John 11.48. or by change of government.

**V. 4. ***{the chief priests}* The heads of the four and twenty priestly families, or courses, 1 Chron. 24.5, 6, 10. Luke 1.5. or such as by corruption had most authority among the priests. See 2 Chronicles, 36.14.

*{Scribes}* Men of special skill in expounding the law, 2 Sam. 8.17. Ezra 7.6. These were most likely to satisfy Herod’s question. See on Luke 20.1.

*{where Christ should be born}* He would not acknowledge that Christ was born, lest by some means he should be conveyed away from his fury, who would gladly destroy him.

**V. 5. ***{They said unto him in Bethlehem, &c.}* See on verse 1. they read the prophecies, and thereby could direct others to Christ, but not themselves, they could (like windows of the Church) let in lights to others, but not see themselves.

**V. 6. ***{And thou Bethlehem}* Micah 5.2. John 7.42. The Evangelist sets down the words, in likelihood as the scribes spake them, which was sufficient to answer Herod’s question, and did not differ from the scope of the prophet.

*{not the least}* Though you are a small town, yet shall you be famous, and honored, by the birth of the Messiah in you.

*{a governor that shall rule}* Or *feed*, for kings are fitly called *feed*ers, or shepherds of the people: and the Messiah has both these titles, Isaiah 40.11. Ezek. 33.23, 24.

**V. 7. ***{when he had privately called}* He would not have it noised abroad, lest his purpose of killing Christ should have been prevented; but there can be no wisdom against God: therefore vain are all humane policies against his determinate counsel. See Psalm 2.1, &c. Acts 4.25, 26, &c.

*{enquired of them diligently}* And learned exactly the time, as appears, verse 16. possibly intending now the murder of the infants, in case he would not find out Christ. Compare this with verse 16.

*{appeared}* First of all.

**V. 8. ***{for the young child}* He does not say for the king of the Jews, as they styled him, verse 2. he would not so much as in word acknowledge him to be so.

*{bring me word again}* He does not say, Tell it publicly, lest all the people should flock to him, and so disappoint his purpose of destroying him: it was a great providence of God, that Herod had no suspicion of the wise men, and that he sent no spy along with them, which had he done, he might probably have found out Christ.

*{and worship him}* See Herod’s hypocrisy, pretending to worship Christ, but intending to kill. Rarely is there any eminent wickedness, without some pretence of religion.

**V. 9. ***{which they saw in the East}* It did not come before them to Jerusalem, neither appeared to them there; for then they would possibly have showed it to Herod, and the Jews, and not have said only, We have seen his star in the East, verse 2.

*{and stood over where, &c.}* It left them not till it brought them to Christ: it appeared not for a time, that they might by enquiry, acquaint the Jews at Jerusalem with the birth of Christ, which in likelihood they would not have done, if the star had led them first to Bethlehem. All this might be a means to keep them from being offended at the low condition wherein they found Christ.

**V. 10. ***{when they saw the Star}* This was a great confirmation, that the star did lead them to the place designed in the Scripture: revelations are not to be credited, that agree not with Scripture.

*{they rejoiced with exceeding, &c.}* First, because they were confirmed in the truth of the birth of the child. Secondly, because they were directed from heaven to the place, that they might not mistake the person.

**V. 11. ***{into the house}* The place where Christ was born, Luke 2.7. or it may be, the guests being gone, she was now removed into the house itself.

*{fell down}* A kind of humble and lowly reverence, Exodus, 34.4.

*{opened their treasures}* Their vessels wherein they brought their treasures.

*{they presented gifts}* Or *offered gifts*; for with such the men of the eastern parts, used to present themselves before kings, Genesis, 43.11, 25, 26. these were the choicest things the country could afford, 1 Kings 10.10. Ps. 45.12. & 72.10, 15.

*{gold, frankincense and myrrh}* Gold was plentiful in Arabia; frankincense so peculiar to the Sabeans a people of Arabia; myrrh is said to be found in Arabia only, Ps. 72.15. 2 Chron. 9.14. God so provided to furnish and supply Joseph, for his flight with Mary and Jesus into Egypt.

**V. 12. ***{being warned of God in a dream}* Not by such visions as needed interpretations, as Pharaoh’s officers were, Gen. 40. and Pharaoh, Gen. 41. but they were plainly forbidden to return to Herod: thus God kept them from betraying Christ as he did Abimelech from adultery, Gen. 20.3.

**V. 13. ***{when they were departed}* Not presently, (some think) but some time after, for (say they) Joseph and Mary were at Jerusalem after this, before they went into Egypt: but seeing we read not of any certainty, how many days after the birth of Christ the wise men came to Bethlehem, we leave it to conjecture: it being uncertain, whether that Luke 2.39. conclude their immediate going to Nazareth again, or whether they first went back to Bethlehem, and afterward home to Nazareth, it being but two days’ journey to Jerusalem.

*{appeareth to Joseph}* Whether at Jerusalem, or at Bethlehem, or at Nazareth is not certain; for they came from Nazareth (where they dwelt before) to Bethlehem to be taxed, Luke 2.4. and when the days of Mary’s purification were accomplished, they presented him at Jerusalem to the Lord, and offered according to the law, Luke 2.24. and when they had performed all these things, they returned into Galilee, to their own city Nazareth, this was their first dwelling at Nazareth: the second is mentioned here verse 23. after the death of Herod, where they dwelt long, until Christ was grown up.

*{flee}* Because the time of his death was not yet come; and because Christ must live to preach and do many miracles. This flight was part of Christ’s humiliation.

*{into Egypt}* Far out of Herod’s reach. See 1 Kings 11.40.

*{till I bring thee word}* This shows that God would provide for them there, and bring them back again.

**V. 14. ***{by night}* Lest his flight should be perceived, and by some means prevented.

**V. 15. ***{that it might be fulfilled}* That performed, which was in the former shadowed: the deliverance of Israel out of Egypt, being a type of God’s bringing Christ out of the same place.

*{out of Egypt have I called}* Hos. 11.1. In the type he calls Israel his firstborn son, Exodus 4.12. Jer. 31.20. here he calls Christ his Son: his Son by nature, and so his only begotten Son. See Luke 1.35. John 1.14. & 3.16.

**V. 16. ***{Then Herod}* Not presently after the departure of the wise men; but after Joseph’s going into Egypt with the child.

*{that he was mocked}* As he apprehended it; for he knew not, that God had sent them another way, verse 12.

*{sent forth}* Soldiers to kill the children: among whom, some say, he slew his own: whereof when Augustus Caesar heard, he is reported to have said, It is better to be Herod’s hog, than his son, *Macrob. sat. l. 2. c. 4.*

*{all the children}* The male children; for the article is masculine in the Greek; and there was no need to kill the females: it being a male which he sought for. So it was in Egypt, Exod. 1.16.

*{in all the coasts thereof}* Lest Christ should be out of the town.

*{from two years old}* Lest Christ should have been born before the star appeared and so escape. It was just with God to take away their children, who so lightly regarded his Son, manifested to them so strangely, by angels, shepherds, wise men, and the star.

*{and under, according to the time}* Some think Herod committed not this murder, till near two years after the wise men’s coming to him; and that he went first to Rome, to clear himself of things objected to him by his sons: others, that he would be sure to take time enough before, and after, that Christ might not escape him: others, that those words, *according to the time, &c.* must not be referred to, *from two years old*, but only to the last words, *and under*: that is, Herod killed only such as were born, before the wise men came to Bethlehem, and not those that were born after: others think, that the star appeared at Christ’s conception, and God directed the wise men, to come after his birth; for such tokens use to go before the things they portend. And Herod might put off the business, till he was further provoked; for many crosses fell on him in his last year, as Josephus reports.

**V. 17. ***{Then was fulfilled}* See on verse 15.

*{by Jeremiah the Prophet}* Jer. 31.15.

**V. 18. ***{In Rama}* Between which and Bethlehem, was Rachel’s sepulcher, Gen. 35.16, 19, 20. in the confines of Judah and Benjamin, 1 Sam. 10.1. it was a place in the tribe of Benjamin, near Bethlehem, Jerom on Jer. 31.15. (whence this place is transcribed) translates, *vox in excelso audita est*: there were sundry Ramas, in mount Ephraim 1 Sam. 25.1. 2 Sam. 28.3. Ramath Lehi Judg. 15. Ramath Mispe Josh. 13.26.

*{a voice heard}* The cry was so loud, as if it had been heard from Rachel’s sepulcher to Ramah.

*{Rachel weeping}* The Benjamitish women who came of Rachel, did weep so bitterly for their children led into captivity, as if she herself had bemoaned them out of her grave: and so did the women of Bethlehem now mourn for their slain infants.

*{because they are not}* Because they of whom the prophet spake, were carried into captivity, and these were killed: See the like phrase, Gen. 42.13, 36. they were not in being, or dead.

**V. 19. ***{appeared in a dream}* So God performed his promise, verse 13.

**V. 20. ***{go into the land of Israel}* Lest Christ should be esteemed a stranger in Israel, if he had been bred in Egypt and that they might enjoy the public service of God, their country and friends.

*{they are dead}* The plural number for the singular: Herod is dead. So Exod. 4.19. others conceive that some of Herod’s instruments, or of the scribes, who hated Christ, died also with Herod.

*{which sought the &c.}* Not to preserve it, as Prov. 29.10. but to destroy it, 1 Kings 19.10. Psalm 40.14.

**V. 22. ***{that Archelaus}* One of the cruellest of Herod’s issue.

*{he was afraid to go}* It seems he had some thoughts of going thither, peradventure because Christ was born there, or that he might be nearer the temple.

*{being warned of God in a dream}* This is the fourth time that Joseph was thus warned, Chap. 1.20. chap. 2.13, 19. and here. It is fit time for God to acquaint man with his will, when rest takes him off from all external distractions, and worldly cares: hereby God also intimates, that he takes care for his, when they can take none for themselves.

**V. 23. ***{spoken by the Prophets}* He does not say by the prophet, as verse 15. because it is not found in any one prophet, but in divers: and that not in the words, but in the sense.

*{he shall be called}* See on chap. 1.23.

*{a Nazarene}* *Ναζωραῖος*, the word Nazareth was not known in the Old Testament, it must therefore be an allusion to *נצר*, *netzer* a branch, Isaiah 11.1. a name given to Christ, from thence it is likely that Nazareth had that name; for so it is written in the Syriac *נצרת*, *notsroth*, the application of it to the Nazarites agrees not with the letters, (this is written *נזיר* *nezeir*, the other *נצר* *netser*. Judg. 13.5, &c.) though some expound it so, and apply it to Christ, as typified in Samson, who was a Nazarite from his birth. See *Is. Casaubon, exercit. 7. N. 1. Fran. Jun. Parallel. Beza in loc. Jo. Drus. ad voc. N. T. com. 1. Nazarenus*.

## CHAPTER 3

**Verse 1. ***{In those days}* The Evangelist omits the course of our Savior’s life in private, and begins at his preparation for his public ministry; intending not to satisfy men’s curiosity, but to write those things, which may further our salvation. The days here mentioned, are not meant of Christ’s first settling in Nazareth, but while he abode there; for this was above twenty years after his coming thither: about the fifteenth year of Tiberius Caesar, when Christ began to be about thirty years old. See Luke 3.1, 23.

*{came}* Gr. *cometh*, he went not far, but showed himself then, Luke 1.18.

*{John the Baptist}* Mark 1.4. Luke 3.2. So called, because he was the first that was sent of God to baptize, John 1.33. John, who through his singular holiness, and rare austereness of life, caused all men to cast their eyes upon him, now prepares the way for Christ, presently to follow him; delivering the sum of the Gospel, which in a short space after should be more fully delivered.

*{preaching}* Having received a special command so to do, Luke 3.2.

*{in the wilderness of Judaea}* A desert place, more fit for pasture, than for corn: see Luke 15.4. and full of mountains, Luke 1.39. yet inhabited, though not so full of towns and houses, as other parts of Judea, 1 Kings 2.34. Josh. 15.61. 1 Sam. 25.1, 2. it seems not to have been far from his father’s house, Luke 1.39, 40. and the place where he lived, till he showed himself to Israel, Luke 1.18.

**V. 2. ***{Repent ye}* So our Savior begins his preaching, Chap. 4.17. confirming John’s doctrine. John taught other things also, Luke 3. but this he began withal, and this was the main scope of his doctrine. The word in the original signifies, the change of our minds from false ways to the right, and of our hearts, from evil to good: *Non ex delicti confessione, sed ex animi demutatione compositum est. Tertul. advers. Marc. l. 2. c. 24.* it is compounded not of confession of sin, but change of the mind. So *Greg. ho. 4. in Evang.* says, to repent, is *perpetrata plangere, & plangenda non perpetrare*, to bewail evils committed, & not to commit things to be bewailed; for he, that so deplores some sins, as that he commits others, either counterfeits, or is ignorant of repentance. He shows them here what they should do, not what they could do of themselves: repentance is, as it were, *secunda post naufragium tabula*, as Jerom speaks *l. 2. ep. 20. Demetr.*, a second plank after shipwreck. *Primus felicitatis gradus est, non delinquere: secundus delicta cognoscere, Cyprian, l. 1. ep. 3.* the first degree of happiness, is not to sin: the second to know our sins, and repent of them. There runs that entire and unstained innocence which may save; here succeeds the medicine to heal.

*{the kingdom of heaven}* The kingdom of the Messiah, whose government shall be heavenly, and lead to heaven, Daniel 2.24.

**V. 3. ***{For this is he}* The words of the Evangelists, giving a reason, why John preached thus.

*{the voice of one crying}* Isaiah 40.3. Mark 1.3. He spake thus loud and fervently concerning Christ, because it was his office.

*{prepare ye the way of the Lord}* As men make the ways plain and smooth taking all rubs away, when a harbinger gives them warning of the king’s coming: so do you spiritually prepare your hearts to entertain Christ, who is now at hand.

**V. 4. ***{his raiment of camel’s hair}* A coarse rough garment made of that hair, such as the prophets used to wear, Zech. 13.4. As he came in the spirit of Elias, so in habit he resembled him, 2 Kings 1.8.

*{a leathern girdle}* Herein also he was like Elias, 2 Kings 1.8. not as was usual with them of the better sort in those parts, of finer stuff, which they used also as purses to carry their money in, see on Chap. 10.9.

*{about his loins}* Which parts used to be girt, Luke 12.35. Eph. 6.14. 1 Pet. 3.13.

*{meat was locusts}* The living creatures called locusts, were not unclean by the law, but allowed Israel for food. Levit. 11.22. and were eaten of many people in those parts: understand it of a fruit called locusts, growing on the carob tree. See on Luke 15.16. and *Job. Drus. praeterit. l. Matth. 3.4.*

*{wild honey}* Either natural honey, such as is found sometimes on the leaves of some tree: or such as bees make ordinarily in wood and clefts of rocks, of which Deut. 32.13. Psalm 81.16. 1 Sam. 14.26. Theophylact.

**V. 5. ***{Then went out unto him}* The scarcity and want of prophets, his extraordinary manner of preaching, and austerity of life, drew multitudes unto him.

*{Jerusalem}* The people of Jerusalem.

*{round about Jordan}* On both sides of the river.

**V. 6. ***{were baptized}* Washed by dipping in Jordan, as Mark 7.4. Hebrews 9.10. So the Jews used formerly to do with their proselytes, but this was his special commission, John 1.33.

*{confessing their sins}* In words professing their detestation of them, and their repentance for them: or by that rite acknowledging they were sinners, and that their souls needed cleansing.

**V. 7. ***{Pharisees}* A sect that divided themselves from others, as more righteous than they; as the name imports; or it may signify expounders of the law, though they gave many false glosses. See Chap. 5.20, 21. & 23.16, 17. See on Luke 14.1.

*{and Sadducees}* Another sect, so named from Sadoc their first founder, some say: or as others, because they also professed themselves righteous, though indeed they were mere libertines, and held opinion destructive to all piety. Acts 23.8. See on Luke 20.27.

*{come to his baptism}* Which some of them, it seems, embraced, though their generality refused it, Luke 7.30.

*{generation of vipers}* Or, *O viperous brood*. Chapter 12.34. and 23.23. a pernicious creature, Acts 28.3, 4. He means not that they were corrupted from their holy parents, but degenerated, or as *Gregor. hom. 20. in Evang.* says. *Malae soboles, malorum parentum actionem imitantes*, wicked offsprings imitating the action of wicked parents.

*{who hath warned you}* It may seem strange that you could by any man be induced to deem yourselves in danger of wrath and destruction, conceiving yourselves to be so holy and perfect, as you outwardly pretend to be: and it may be well doubted, whether this your profession of repentance be indeed sound and sincere; you Pharisees thinking you are in no danger of hell, Luke 18.9, 11. and you Sadducees believing that there is no hell, Acts 23.8.

*{to flee from the wrath to come}* By repentance, without which you cannot prevent it; Luke 13.5, 7. the wrath to come imports God’s judgment, either in this life, or that which is to come, 1 Thess. 1.10.

**V. 8. ***{fruits}* A metaphor from trees, verse 10. they must not only have leaves of legal ceremonies, but also fruits of good life. So men are called trees of righteousness, Isaiah 61.3. and their works fruits of righteousness, Phil. 1.11.

*{meet for repentance}* Or *answerable to amendment of life*: actions testifying repentance and agreeable thereunto, Acts 26.20, let the sincerity of your repentance appear by the integrity of your lives, Daniel 4.27. Salvian says well *de gub. D.* good works are *Christianae fidei quasi testes*, as witnesses of Christian faith.

**V. 9. ***{think not to say}* Think it not enough to say, or say not: as what he thinks he has, or seems to have, Luke 8.18. for that which he has, Matt. 25.29.

*{Abraham to our father}* John 8.39. Do not think yourselves safe and well, as those that need no repentance, nor have cause to fear judgment, because you are of the holy Abraham’s posterity, Rom. 11.16. or do not think that God either will not, or cannot cut you off, because he has promised to continue a seed to Abraham forever, Jer. 31.35, 36. He forbids them not to profess themselves children of holy Abraham; but this he says, lest vainly trusting in that prerogative (seeing they degenerated from the faith and sanctity of Abraham) they should continue in their sins, and neglect the only means of salvation, now offered them in Christ.

*{God is able}* He can perform his promise to Abraham, though you all perish.

*{of these stones}* An emphatical expression, like that, Luke 19.40, 50. Numbers 14.12. It may be an allusion to Isaiah 51.1, 2. Some interpret it as relating it to the Gentiles, whom God would make children of Abraham, that is, true believers, and heirs of the blessing; as he did Zaccheus, Luke 19.9. as if he said, God is able to gather him a church of those who seem most unlikely to be converted to the faith of Abraham. See *Cyprian l. 2. ep. 3. Gregor. hom. 20. Theophylact*, &c.

*{to raise up}* As to the deceased, seed or issue, Gen. 38.8. Chap. 22.24.

*{children to Abraham}* Spiritual ones, Rom. 9.8. by taking away their stony hearts, and giving them hearts of flesh, Ezek. 11.19. children of promise, Gal. 3.29.

**V. 10. ***{Now also the ax is laid}* The Romans are coming quickly to take your city: or the ax of God’s judgment, as Theophylact interprets, to root you out of the earth, and cast you into hell, if you repent not speedily. See Acts 17.30, 31.

*{unto the root of the trees}* The Babylonians lopped you before, but now you shall be utterly scattered, and as it were stocked up branch and root.

*{therefore every tree}* Chap. 7.19.

*{which bringeth not forth good fruit}* See Isaiah 5.5, 6. it seems an allusion to Deut. 20.20.

**V. 11. ***{indeed}* Mark 1.8. Luke 3.16. John 1.26.

*{baptize you with water}* I can only give you the outward element, and the seal: thus John depresses himself, and extols Christ, because the people thought too highly of John, Luke 3.15, 16.

*{unto repentance}* That thereby you may make profession of your repentance, Mark 1.4. which by that rite, or seal of God’s covenant, is on man’s part obliged or sealed.

*{he that cometh after me}* Christ, who begins shortly after to execute his ministry, as I have begun mine,

*{is mightier than I}* He is therefore able to give you the thing signified in baptism, which neither men nor angels can do.

*{whose shoes I am not worthy to bear}* As he is stronger than I, so, is he so much more excellent than I, that am not worthy to do the office of the meanest of his servants. See 1 Sam. 25.41.

*{with the Holy Ghost and with fire}* With the Holy Ghost, purging your souls as fire does metals, Isaiah 1.25. and 4.4. he shows here the effect of baptism in the regenerate, mortification and cleansing away sin: wherein appears the difference between the law, and the new covenant of grace in Christ; the law indeed *peccatum relevat, non revelat*, reveals, but not relieves, or cures sin: but the seals of the new covenant, confirm unto us remission of sin by Christ, being made effectual by the inward working of his Holy Spirit; without which, none can be saved by baptism. As in the deluge, all perished save only those who were in the ark; so in this water of baptism, they perish who are not in the true Church by faith in Christ. *August. To. 7. l. 4. c. 1. de bapt. contr. Donat.* excellently expresses this by comparing baptism to the waters of Paradise: the waters of Paradise when they are out thereof, in Mesopotamia, &c. there is no happiness there: so says he, the baptism of the Church may be out of the Church, *munus autem beatae vitae non nisi intra Ecclesiam reperitur*, but the gift of a blessed life is not found anywhere, but within the Church.

**V. 12. ***{Whose fan is in his hand}* Who is now ready to make a separation between the good and the bad: the fan signifies cleansing at hand, that is, division, separating the fruitful from the barren, as *Nicetas com. in Gr. Naz. orat. 11.* interprets, and that partly by general or special judgments, Mal. 3.18. & 4.1, 2. & Chap. 13.30, 49. or by the preaching of the Gospel. See on Luke 3.17.

*{gather his wheat into the garner}* That is, his own people into his Church here, and bring them into heaven, the place of their eternal abode hereafter, as men lay up their corn in granaries.

*{burn up the chaff}* That is, those who have only a show of religion, without fruit of good works, and rest secure, only in the outward communion of the Church: those (which as Theophylact says, *ἄχυροι εἰσι οἱ τοῖς πνεύμασι τῆς πονηρίας κινούμενοι*, who are light and driven by the spirit of maliciousness: according to which, *Cyprian de simplic. prelat. tract. 3.* says, *inanes paleae tempestate jactantur*, it is the light chaff which is driven away with the wind. *They went out from us, they were not of us*; says the apostle, 1 John 2.19. All things against the truth, *de ipsa veritate corrupta sunt, Tertul. de praescript. c. 47.* are corrupted from, and out of the truth, the spirits of error working that emulation. See on Luke 3.17. by burning up the chaff, he means destruction of the wicked here, and casting them into hellfire hereafter.

*{fire unquenchable}* Isaiah 66.24. Chap. 13.30, 42. Mark 9.45.

**V. 13. ***{Then cometh Jesus}* Mark 1.9. Luke 3.21. to wit, when John by preaching and baptizing had prepared the people to receive him.

*{from Galilee to Jordan}* He might have sent for John to come into Galilee to baptize him; but he comes to him, that he might more publicly enter into his ministry.

*{unto John}* He shows his humility in coming to him.

*{to be baptized of him}* He needed not baptism, having no sin in him; but as he took on him the similitude of sinful flesh, so would he take also the sacrament of purgation, and remission of sins. As being circumcised, he declared that he was the Savior of them that believed in the Old Testament; so by being baptized, that he is the Savior of us in the New. Hereby also he confirmed John’s baptism, and sanctified ours.

**V. 14. ***{John forbade him}* Out of modesty; for though they lived not together before, lest they should have been thought to conspire together, yet now doubtless he knew him by revelation, John 1.33. See *Theophylact. in loc.*

*{I have need to be baptized of thee}* Inwardly and spiritually, for you only can do it; or it is fitter that you should baptize me, being so far the more worthy person.

**V. 15. ***{Suffer it to be so now}* Though I am greater than you, yet suffer me now to be baptized of you. For three reasons Christ came to be baptized of John. 1. That as he became man, so he might fulfill all the righteousness of the law, whereof the first Adam (as Euthymius notes) fulfilled no part. 2. That by his baptism, he might approve of John’s baptism. 3. That he might sanctify the flood Jordan, and all other waters, to the mystical washing away of sin: therefore says he, *Suffer it to be so now*, that I who have now taken on me the form of a servant, *expleam & humilitatem eius*, says Jerom, may also fulfill the humility of a servant: thus *ἐβαπτίσθη μὲν, ὡς ἄνθρωπος, ἀλλ᾽ ἁμαρτίας ἔλυσεν ὡς Θεὸς, Nazianzen. orat. 35.* he was baptized as man, but remitted sins, as God.

*{to fulfil all righteousness}* As of the law so of the Gospel: it becomes me to submit to God’s ordinance, and you to do your office: it is just we should so do.

*{he suffered him}* Being willing to obey Christ, and convinced by the reason given him, he was content to do that, which otherwise he thought in his own reason inconvenient,

**V. 16. ***{went up straightway, &c.}* Or, *as soon as he came out, &c.* an usual introduction to solemn visions: See Ezek. 1.1. John 1.51. Acts 7.56. this was done now that Christ and John might see the dove descend, and hear the voice coming from thence.

*{unto him}* To John, John 1.32.

*{like a dove}* To show the innocency and meekness of our Savior.

*{the spirit of God descending}* To assure John that this was the true Messiah, John 1.33. and to make Christ known to be so to others, who believe the Gospel: this was because he was presently to enter into his public ministry.

*{upon him}* Upon Christ. See Isaiah 11.2. lest any should have thought, *vocem patris ad Iohannem factam; non ad Dominum*, Jerom; that the father’s voice came to John, and not to the Lord Jesus.

**V. 17. ***{a voice from heaven}* The voice of God the Father, as appears by the words following, consecrating and authorizing Christ to be our Mediator, and to go about the work of our redemption: here is also a demonstration of the Holy Trinity; the Father speaks from heaven, the Son comes out of the water, and the Holy Ghost appears in the likeness of a dove.

*{this is}* He speaks of Christ, but to the people; he on whom the dove sits, is

*{my beloved Son}* My natural Son, by eternal, and incomprehensible generation, and therefore dearest to me.

*{in whom I am well pleased}* My judgment is satisfied, and my love settled upon him, and in him upon all believers, who were before the children of wrath, Eph. 2.3.

## CHAPTER 4

**Verse 1. ***{Then was}* Presently after he was baptized, Mark 1.12. and solemnly proclaimed the Son of God, by whom mankind was to be reconciled to God.

*{Jesus led up}* Mark 1.12. Luke 4.1. relating to the wilderness, as higher than the confines of Jordan, where he baptized: or it may signify, led aside, or withdrawn, as Luke 4.1.

*{of the Spirit}* Or, *by the Spirit*: either by inspiration, as Rom. 8.14. which was so prevalent, that it is called driving, Mark 1.12. or guided by that dove, that rested on him, Chap. 3.16. as the Israelites were led by the cloud and pillar of fire, Psalm 78.14. and the wise men by the star, Chap. 2.9. or lifted up from the earth (as the word *ἀνήχθη*, here used, may signify according to Budaeus) and his body carried into the wilderness by the power of God’s Spirit, as 1 Kings 18.12. 2 Kings 2.16. Ezek. 3.12, 14, 15. & 11.1.

*{into the wilderness}* Not a place little inhabited (like that wherein John Baptist lived, Chap. 3.1. but a place not inhabited by men, but by wild beasts, Mark 1.13. The devil had the more advantage to tempt our Savior, in a place where no man could hinder him, nor assist Christ.

*{to be tempted of the devil}* Not to be tried by God with afflictions, as Abraham was, Gen. 22.1, 2. but to be solicited by Satan to evil, as 1 Chron. 21.1. that he might overcome him, who by temptations overcame our first parents.

**V. 2. ***{fasted forty days}* As Moses and Elias had done before, Exod. 34.28. 1 Kings 19.8. the first before the law. The second under the law; so now here in the beginning of the Gospel.

*{and forty nights}* He did not eat at night, as the Jews did, or at some other times, when they fasted or abstained from pleasant bread many days, as Dan. 10.3. but fasted day and night, during this mentioned time.

*{afterward}* It seems he felt no hunger till the time expired, but was kept by the power of the Deity; as the three Jews from feeling the heat of the fire, Dan. 3.27.

*{an hungred}* The devil takes him at the weakest, and his first occasion of temptation from hence verse 3. which had been to no purpose before Christ was hungry.

**V. 3. ***{the tempter}* The devil, whose constant practice it is, to endeavor to draw men to sin, 1 Thess. 3.5. as *Minucius Fel. Octav.* says, The impure spirits, to the comfort of their own calamity, *non desinunt perditi jam perdere*, being castaways, they cease not now to cast away others.

*{if thou be the Son of God}* The natural Son of God, as was affirmed from heaven chap. 3.17. and taught by John Baptist, John 1.34. or a son of God, for the article is not in Greek, and the temptations, answers and Scriptures alleged, are such as may become us in the like temptations. See on Luke 4.3.

*{command}* Show yourself to be God, by commanding this to be done, Luke 4.3. Command this stone, that it be made bread; as Gen. 1.3. or say, that is, speak to God to turn these stones into bread.

*{these stones}* Luke 4.3. It is this stone, as if Satan had appeared like a man, and pointed to some stone lying there before them.

*{bread}* Not flesh, or some dainty thing, Num. 11.4. Ps. 38.18, 20. bread alone would satisfy hunger, though by bread the Hebrews expressed, all food, and necessaries to sustain this life. chap. 6.11. See on Luke 11.3.

**V. 4. ***{it is written}* Our Savior does not say, I am God, and so drive away Satan by his divine power: but answers him by Scripture, that he might overcome Satan, as man, and teach us how to do it; to wit, by the sword of the Spirit. Eph. 6.17. Satan was here deceived, he came to overthrow, and was overthrown, *ἠγνόει γὰρ ὅτι περιπεσεῖται θεότητι προσδραμὼν ἀνθρωπότητι*, he knew not, that when he encountered man, he should light upon the Deity, *Nazianzen Orat. 18.*

*{man shall not live}* Deut. 8.3.

*{by bread alone}* Bread does not nourish by its own power, but by God’s appointment: as if he said, though here be no bread to nourish me, God can put the same virtue into other things, or nourish me without any food, and on him will I wait.

**V. 5. ***{Then the devil taketh him}* Soon after the former temptation, he carries him up, as God’s Spirit did Philip, Acts 8.39. else how could he set him on a pinnacle of the temple? No wonder if Christ suffered Satan to carry him, who suffered his instruments to crucify him.

*{into the holy city}* Jerusalem, chapter 27.53. Dan. 9.24. So called, notwithstanding the wickedness of it, because of God’s solemn worship there; or in respect of those good men left there, who were the better, though not the greater part of Jerusalem.

*{on a pinnacle}* An out-wing on the top of the temple; the devil might have set him on some rock in the wilderness: but he thought Jesus would be more willing to show himself at Jerusalem: and from the temple (as Christ’s kindred did, John 7.3, 4.) and that if he fell thence, all the Jews would think him an impostor, if he died of that fall.

**V. 6. ***{if thou be the Son of God}* As chap. 3.17.

*{cast thyself down}* Satan does not offer to cast him down, for that God would not suffer him, or because he sought to bring Christ to sin, rather than unto death (though he desired both) whereas if Satan had cast him down, Christ had not sinned. Though God permitted the sacred body of Christ to Satan, yet was his commission limited, as Job 2.6. therefore Satan said, Cast thyself down; he may tempt, and persuade to evil, he cannot compel. The devil (says *Irenaeus l. 5. c. 9.*) being an apostate angel, *hoc tantum potest, quod egit in principio, seducere, &c.* can do that only which he did in the beginning, seduce and draw away the mind of man to transgress the commandments of God &c.

*{For it is written}* Ps. 91.11. The devil now seeks to foil Christ with his own weapons, and cites Scripture, as he had done before verse 4. *mendacium abscondens per Scripturam, quod faciunt omnes haeretici*, says Irenaeus before cited, veiling his lie by Holy Scripture, as all heretics use to do. There is no charge or temptation of Satan more pernicious and dangerous, than that which is colored with misapplied Scripture, and show of sanctity: apparent falsehood and impiety we more easily avoid: but beware the devil and his ministers, when they are transformed into angels of light: Here appears the venom of the old dragon, when he pretends the sacred authority of God’s Holy Word, to lead men to sin, and disobedience to God.

*{he shall give his angels charge}* As if he said, you depend on God (verse 4) what need you fear hurt by falling, seeing the Scripture testifies, that God has appointed his angels to keep you from the least hurt. See on Luke 4.10.

*{they shall bear thee up}* A metaphor taken from them that carry children in their arms.

**V. 7. ***{it is written again}* Deut. 6.16. Our Savior does not allege Scripture against Scripture, as if one Scripture did, or could cross another; but shows that Satan did misallege the former Scripture, by producing a text, which forbids that which Satan would have him to do.

*{thou shalt not tempt the Lord thy God}* As if he said; as I must wait upon God for food (verse 4.) so likewise for means of coming down from the temple, else I tempt God by casting myself into a needless danger, so might justly fall without breach of his promise.

**V. 8. ***{Again, &c.}* Satan, though foiled, yet gives not over tempting Christ: when one temptation is past, we must look for another.

*{taketh him up}* See on verse 5. and on Luke 4.5.

*{shewed him all the kingdoms, &c.}* Presents them to his senses, which way the Scripture has not revealed. See on Luke 4.5.

**V. 9. ***{all these will I give thee}* He comes not now like a friend to advise, as verse 3. or like a divine to direct, as verse 6. but like a monarch to seduce our Savior with great gifts. See on Luke 4.6.

*{if thou wilt fall down}* As if he said, I ask but a small requital of so great liberality, only one act of submission, in acknowledgement that you have all these things from me: hence it appears, why Satan begins not as before, *If thou be the Son of God*: for he knew that Christ if he took himself to be the Son of God, would not worship him: and if he were not so, he might peradventure be willing to accept of Satan’s large offer. This was says *Irenaeus l. 5. c. 3.* as some apostate surprising a region, should trouble the inhabitants, *ut regis gloriam sibi vindicet, apud ignorantes*; that among the ignorant he might challenge to himself the glory of a king. See on Luke 4.7.

**V. 10. ***{get thee hence}* Luke 4.8. Tempt me no more at this time: Christ could endure him no longer, when he sought to usurp that which belongs to God alone.

*{thou shalt worship the Lord thy God}* Deut. 6.13. & 10.20. 1 Sam. 12.3. you are not God, and therefore I may not worship you, to get all the world.

**V. 11. ***{Then the devil leaveth him}* When Christ had exercised his divine power, in commanding him away, verse 10. it is our comfort in temptations, he limits Satan, how long, and how far he will permit us to him; and will never suffer us to be tempted above that he will make us able.

*{angels came and ministered unto him}* To render their service and homage unto him, Heb. 1.6. or to bring him food, as to Elias, 1 Kings 19.5. or to convey him back from this mountain, where Satan had set him. See Luke 4.14. *Homo est quem diabolus tentat, & idem ipse Deus est, cui ab angelis ministratur; Gregor. hom. 16. in evang.* he is man, whom the devil tempted, but the same is also God, to whom the angels minister.

**V. 12. ***{Now when Jesus had heard}* Mark 1.14. Luke 4.14. John 4.43. It appears that divers things came between, which are set down, John 1.2. and 3. chapters.

*{cast into prison}* Or *delivered up*, See Mark 1.14. Mark 6.18, &c.

**V. 13. ***{leaving Nazareth}* Wherein he had been brought up, and had now been a while, Luke 4. he retired into a safer place out of Herod’s jurisdiction, governed by his brother Philip, a man of a milder temper.

*{dwelt in Capernaum}* Conversed there for the most part; for he had not an house of his own, chapter 8.20. this was a town more famous than Nazareth: so was there more hope of doing good there.

**V. 14. ***{That it might be fulfilled}* It was first literally fulfilled when that people had some freedom after their afflictions by the Assyrians; but now especially by Christ’s preaching there.

**V. 15. ***{the land of Zabulun}* Isaiah 9.1. The first calamity began there, 1 Kings 15.29.

*{by the way of the sea}* Of Tiberias, because it was not far from the sea of Tyrus.

*{beyond Jordan}* Beside, at, or near Jordan; for it was part of the holy land. See Num. 32.19. John 1.28.

*{Galilee of the Gentiles}* So called, because the cities which Solomon gave to the king of Tyrus (1 Kings 9.11.) were there: or Galilee of the nations: because by reason of traffic, people of divers nations had concourse thither: or populous Galilee: that part of it which was best peopled, as being a richer soil than other parts thereof. There were two Galilees: hereof see *Drus. ad Voc. N. T. com. 1. Hierom de loc. Hebr. Beza in loc. C. Adricom. Theatr. sanct. Neptalim. N. 50.* The best authors agree that this was the upper Galilee, the lower was near Tiberias, and the lake, or sea of Genezareth.

**V. 16. ***{The people which sate in darkness}* They whose predecessors were first afflicted by the Assyrians, and delivered, being now the captives of Satan, do first hear of their deliverance by Christ himself.

*{shadow of death}* Deathful ignorance: for this is antithetically answered to the light of knowledge sprung up: and troubles as grievous as death, Psalm 23.4.

*{light is sprung up}* Christ bringing the joyful tidings of salvation to them, Psalm 97.11.

**V. 17. ***{From that time}* Mark 1.14.

*{Jesus began to preach}* To take upon him the public office of a prophet, or preacher; for he had doubtless taught before: but now John is in prison, he undertakes the care of preaching to the people.

*{Repent, &c.}* Chap. 3.2. He begins as John did, and so confirms his doctrine.

**V. 18. ***{And Jesus}* Mark 1.16.

*{by the sea of Galilee}* Called also the lake of Genezaret; the sea of Tiberias. Luke 5.1. John 16.1, 23. See also on John 21.1.

*{two brethren}* They were the more likely to assist one another in the apostleship, because of their near relation.

*{Simon called Peter and Andrew}* They were with Christ before, John 1.40, 41. but went home again to follow their own business, as Nicodemus also did. John 3.1.

*{casting a net}* Busy in their calling, as the shepherds were when the angels came to them, Luke 2.8.

*{they were fishers}* Christ could have sent some of the glorious 11angels, or the learned doctors of the law; but he takes poor fishermen, and fits them for apostles, that he might have the glory of that great work entire to himself. See Acts 4.13. 1 Cor. 1.26, 29.

**V. 19. ***{follow me}* That is, says *Gregor. hom. 2. in Evang. Imitare*, imitate or follow my example: as, 1 Cor. 4.16. See Eph. 5.1. Heb. 6.12. Here is more enjoined: to wit, that they should also give their names unto him as disciples, and stay with him, that he might teach them in private and public, to fit them to that ministry, to which he would set them apart.

*{fishers of men}* The world is the sea, unbelievers fishes, the apostles and succeeding ministers the fishermen; and the Gospel preached the net. See Chap. 13.47. Luke 5.10. Thus Christ teaches them the ministers’ duty by their own trade. So David was taught his kingly office. Psalm 78.71, 72.

**V. 20. ***{straightway left their nets}* As soon as they had brought their ship to an anchor, or to the haven, and were come ashore. See Luke 5.11. Mark their ready obedience and willingness to leave all for Christ, yet left they not their propriety in their goods, but left them to the care of others. See Chap. 8.14. John 19.27. & 21.3.

**V. 21. ***{two brethren}* See on verse 18.

*{mending their nets}* Working in their calling. See on ver. 18.

**V. 22. ***{immediately}* See on verse 20.

*{and their father}* Not neglecting him, but leaving him to the care of his servants. Mark 1.20. Here was Christ’s special command: who says also, *honor thy father and thy mother, &c.* Where God’s service and honor requires it, we must forsake all, or we cannot be true disciples of Christ, we must love and honor our dear parents, but God’s honor and service must have the first place: we must love our parents in God; but God above all.

**V. 23. ***{went about all Galilee}* *περιῆγεν*. He led about his new called disciples, but *ἄγειν* is often to go. So here, he went about all Galilee, where they were more ignorant than in Judea, verse 15, 16. He did not send for men out of all places, but went into the towns and villages to them.

*{in their synagogues}* A word used for meeting-places civil and sacred: as also the word *ἐκκλησία* Acts 19.39. 1 Cor. 11.18. Here it is used for the place where people met to worship God in public: here Christ taught, to take away all suspicion of his doctrine; truth loves the light, and rejoices to be seen. John 3.20, 21. & 18.20. Acts 26.26. 2 Tim. 3.6. Falsehood sits like Tamar in the way to Timnath, Gen. 38.14, covered with a veil; wrapped up in such disguises that she may be embraced unknown. Christ preached in public, to encourage the Jews to the public service of God, and to gain the more souls, where many were constantly assembled.

*{preaching}* Publishing by authority, as heralds do the will of kings: and privilege of subjects.

*{the gospel of the kingdom}* So called, because it brings us into the kingdom of grace, when it is received, and shows us the way into the kingdom of glory, Chap. 3.2. 1 Cor. 15.1, 2. James 1.21.

*{healing}* By this miraculous healing, he confirmed the truth of his doctrine; and showed his divine power.

*{all manner}* Or *all*, so Acts 10.12, not *all* the persons that were sick (for he cured not many there, Mark 6.5), but as *all* sorts of men, so *all* sorts of diseases presented unto him to be cured, as verse 24.

*{of sickness}* Such as bring more pain, and are more hardly cured: he could have wrought signs from heaven, but he did such miracles as did good to their bodies, to show them by his doctrine and healing them, that he assumed a human body, and reasonable soul, to save the bodies and souls.

*{diseases}* Infirmities less painful or mortal, and more easily cured.

**V. 24. ***{throughout all Syria}* Even to the Gentiles to prepare them to their future conversion; and to make the condemnation of those Jews the greater, who followed him not.

*{they brought unto him, &c.}* They were more careful of the bodies of themselves and theirs, than of their souls; as John 6.26. Hence it is that men seek more to physicians than to divines.

*{torments}* Most painful diseases, the word properly signifying a touchstone, was spoken concerning answers given *ἀπὸ* *τῶν* *βασανιζομένων*, by those who were examined by tortures; or tormented (as upon the rack) to make them confess. *Suid.* See *ibid.* *βάσανος*.

*{possessed with devils}* So that Satan tyrannized over their reason and senses, that they could make no good use of them: but to the hurt of themselves and others.

*{lunatic}* Such as were more or less afflicted, according to the state of the moon; as those that are frantic, or have falling sickness.

*{those that had the palsy}* Weak and feeble men, who had their limbs and joints loosed, and so weakened, that they cannot pull them up, nor put them forth when they would.

**V. 25. ***{there followed him a great multitude}* Besides those that brought their sick to be healed: some come out of affection to novelty, others to carp, others to learn.

*{from Decapolis}* A part of Syria: so named from ten famous cities that were therein.

*{beyond Jordan}* See on verse 15.

## CHAPTER 5

**Verse 1. ***{Seeing the multitude}* The people mentioned, Chapter 4.25. This was the occasion of this large sermon following.

*{he went up into a mountain}* See on Luke 6.17. As the law was given on a mount, Exod. 19.3, 23. Exod. 20.1. So Christ expounds it here on a mountain: it was also a fit place for so many to hear.

*{when he was set}* As their doctors used to do, to show their authority, some think, Chap. 23.2. See on Luke 4.20.

*{his disciples came unto him}* Such as usually repaired to him, or constantly attended upon him, as being more familiarly affected toward him, and desirous to learn something of him, *Euthym.* Luke says chap. 6.20, he lifted up his eyes on his disciples: not limiting his speech in them, but indefinitely proposing these blessings to all, as Chrysostom here notes.

**V. 2. ***{He opened his mouth}* An Hebraism, signifying his beginning to speak, as Psalm 78.2. Job 3.1.

*{taught them}* His disciples and the multitude.

**V. 3. ***{blessed are}* Luke 6.20. So David begins divers of his Psalms, Psal. 1.1. & 32. & 41. & 112. & 119. & 128, blessedness being the main end that all aim at.

*{poor in spirit}* That are afflicted in mind, in the apprehension of their spiritual wants and sins, Isaiah 57.15. Isaiah 66.2, or poor in estate, if their minds be holy and lowly. See on Luke 6.20. 1 Cor. 1.26. James 2.5. They are not blessed because they are poor, but though they be poor, so verse 4. The poor in spirit are they, who are not spiritually proud: but know, and are truly sensible of their spiritual wants and poverty; as knowing themselves (by nature, and of themselves) destitute of true righteousness; and in extreme need of the riches of God’s free grace in Christ, which they can never merit, but must beg of him, Rom. 7.18, 24. This was wanting in the church of Laodicea, Revel. 3.17, and is wanting in all that pretend to their own merits, or self-excellency.

*{for theirs is the kingdom of heaven}* To them is the kingdom of heaven appointed, see Matth. 25.34. Poverty is not in itself a blessed condition: but they are therefore blessed, because they shall be rich in heaven. A kingdom is the richest temporal estate: but the kingdom of heaven, in respect of the unspeakable and eternal blessedness thereof, surpasses all earthly and temporal kingdoms. See Psal. 103.11. Dan. 7.27.

**V. 4. ***{they that mourn}* That is, for their sins, as *Chrysostome, Euthymius, Theophylact. Hilar. Can. 4. &c.* interpret. You see, says *Gr. Nazianzen orat. 17. τῷ στεναγμῷ τὴν σωτηρίαν συνεζευγμένην*, that salvation is joined with sighing, or sorrow. *Tertul.* hereto cites Psal. 126.5. Isaiah 61.3. *adv. Marcian. l. 4. c. 14.* Understand here, those who mourn with godly sorrow to repentance, never to be repented of, 2 Cor. 7.10, or that are in sad condition, by reason of persecution for the profession of the Gospel, verse 11, or any other heavy affliction, they being holy: such are happy, though externally, or otherwise afflicted: not because they mourn, but albeit they mourn. *Minucius Felix* gives a good reason; for says he, *Calamitas saepius disciplina virtutis fuit*, calamity has often been the discipline of virtue. A Christian may seem unhappy, he cannot be found to be so.

*{they shall be comforted}* In part here, by taking away, or mitigating their sorrows; or inward consolation by God’s Spirit: completely in heaven, 2 Cor. 1.4. John 16.20. James 1.12. Rev. 7.17. & 21.4.

**V. 5. ***{Blessed are}* Psal. 37.11.

*{the meek}* Such as patiently and quietly endure injuries: such as (*nec felicitas aut inflat propria, aut urit aliena, Ambros. ep. 84. l. 10*) neither their own prosperity makes proud, nor others’, envious. Admirable is patience which in the storms of afflictions places the soul, *ὥσπερ* *ἐν* *εὐδίῳ* *λιμένι*, as it were in a calm haven, *Chrysostom in Job. 18. hom. 83.* The meek are they, who *cedunt improbitatibus, & non resistunt malo, Augustin. de serm. Domini in mont. l. 1. c. 3*, who give way to injuries, and resist not evil; those who can be angry, (not those who are *ἀόργητοι*, senseless, or insensible of wrongs, as Theophylact interprets) but who contain themselves until they ought to be angry, as Ephes. 4.26. Such as meekly bear, neither provoking others, nor easily provoked by them.

*{they shall inherit the earth}* They shall have a comfortable right to it, enjoy it with inward peace, and be free from many vexations; in which hasty and froward men embarque themselves: this is here promised, because men sometimes take occasion to abuse those most, who are of the meek and quiet disposition, Isai. 59.15.

**V. 6. ***{hunger and thirst for righteousness}* Who earnestly long for it, as hungry men do for meat, or thirsty ones for drink; it may 12be understood of such as knowing their own emptiness, long to be filled with Christ’s righteousness; or of such as being persecuted under the title of impious and ungodly, long to be cleared and righted. See on chap. 11.30.

*{they shall be filled}* Isaiah 65.13. Psal. 17.15. Luke 11.53. Hunger pinches, but food satisfies.

**V. 7. ***{the merciful}* Who being inwardly affected with the miseries of others, do outwardly relieve them according to their abilities, Luke 6.36. &c. See the contrary, James 2.13.

*{for they shall obtain mercy}* They may be subject to misery in this world, but they shall find mercy sometimes with men; but always with God.

**V. 8. ***{Blessed are}* Psal. 24.4.

*{pure in heart}* The single-hearted and sincere; who are not doubly minded, 1 Tim. 1.5. Jam. 1.8. 1 Pet. 1.22.

*{they shall see God}* They shall converse with him familiarly in heaven; or see some evident and glorious signs of his presence there, 1 John 3.2, for his essence is invisible and incomprehensible, Heb. 12.14. He cannot be seen (as he is in his essence) *visu clarior — nec aestimari, sensibus major — magnitudinem Dei qui se putat nosse, minuit, &c. Minuc. Fel. Octavio*, being more clear than any sight; nor can he be valued, who is greater than all sense (of the creature) he that thinks he knows the greatness of God, therein lessens it. Augustine (*Paulinae ep. 112*, after Ambrose on Luke) says well, *non in loco Deus videtur, &c.* God is not seen in a place, as in the oak in Mambre, or mount Sina, but in a pure heart, when we think he is absent, we see him: and when he is present, we see him not: he is invisible, and yet is seen, *cum vult, sicut vult*, many have seen him; not as he is, but as he pleased to appear, and manifest himself unto them.

**V. 9. ***{peacemakers}* Who endeavor to reconcile such as are at variance: either God to man, as the preachers of the Gospel do, or one man to another, as all should do.

*{shall be called the sons of God}* They shall be so indeed, as imitators of Christ, Coloss. 1.20, and so accounted to their honor, verse 19. Luke 1.32. John 1.12. 1 John 3.1. They are the likest to God, and most beloved of him, verse 45. They are the children of the God of peace, Rom. 16.20. 1 Cor. 14.33. 2 Cor. 13.11. Philip. 4.9. 1 Thes. 5.23. 2 Thes. 3.16. Heb. 13.20.

**V. 10. ***{which are persecuted}* 1 Pet. 3.14.

*{for righteousness sake}* Not for sin, or for the world, 1 Pet. 4.15. 1 John 3.12. Satan, like Pharaoh, pursues those who go out of his dominion, he possesses his own vessels in quietness for the present: pirates will not chase the empty ship (says Chrysostom on Isaiah 6), nor will Satan assail those, in whom he knows, there is no riches of grace.

*{theirs is the kingdom of heaven}* See on verse 3. There are two things most considerable in a kingdom, wealth and prosperity: a kingdom therefore is opposed to poverty there, and to adversity here.

**V. 11. ***{Blessed are ye}* He turns his speech now to his present hearers, to comfort them against persecutions which were coming on them.

*{revile you}* Reproach you, and miscall you, 1 Cor. 4.12. 2 Cor. 6.8. So as reproach is a kind of persecution, compare, Gen. 21.9, with Gal. 4.29. Not only the martyrs, but many others, who suffer injury for the truth, and cause of Christ are persecuted: humility is a sure fortress against calumny: he that is not ambitious of the praise of men, will not value their reproach; therefore as Augustine said, in Psal. 57 *non timebis convitiatorem; si non amas adulatorem*; you will not fear a reviler, if you love not a flatterer.

*{persecute you}* Scourge, imprison, or kill you, chap. 10.17. Luke 21.16. John 16.2. Acts 5.40. & 16.22, 23. 2 Cor. 11.23, &c.
