*{all manner of evil, &c.}* 1 Pet. 4.14, for as Jerome says, *ep. 55*, with Christians, *non qui patitur sed qui facit contumeliam, miser est*. He is not unhappy who suffers contumely, but he that does it.

*{falsely}* Gr. *lying*, they have no reward, who are punished justly in word or deed: thieves and murderers suffer, but (as the penitent thief said on the cross) they are justly here. It is the cause, not the passion which makes the martyr.

*{for my sake}* For religion, not for other causes: if reviling be for our own faults, that is no mark of happiness to us.

**V. 12. ***{Rejoice and be exceeding glad}* Joy inwardly, and express it outwardly, Luke 15.32. Psal. 32.11, and that in tribulation, Rom. 5.3.

*{Great is your reward}* Gen. 15.1. Psal. 19.11. It is impossible that he who has been wicked in this life should escape that which he has deserved; or that he who for truth and righteousness’ sake, has here been most afflicted; *suâ mercede fraudari*; should be defrauded of his reward: as Lactantius speaks, *l. 4. c. 19*, because God is just.

*{So they persecuted the Prophets}* Who were eminent men; and therefore you may be content to endure what they did, Jam. 5.4.

*{which were before you}* Both in time and office; and therefore you may expect the like usage with them.

**V. 13. ***{Ye are the salt of the earth}* I have chosen you to season, and preserve those who are corruptible by sin: as salt suffers not flesh to corrupt (*Synes. de regno. Clem. Alexandr. Paed. l. 1. c. 9*). So wholesome doctrine works with the mind: reprove them therefore that they may be saved; though you displease some thereby, and suffer persecution for the same: yet be you savory in yourselves, and by good exhortations help to season others with the salt of grace, that they may be pleasing to God, Colos. 4.6. Ephes. 4.29.

*{But if the salt}* Mark 9.50. Luke 14.34. It may be understood of ministers, or of private Christians, who are spoken of before, and in the following verses.

*{have lost his savour}* *Μωρανθῇ* become foolish: the unsavoriness of salt is (as Euthymius says) *debilitas mordacitatis ipsius*. The weakness of its acrimony: the meaning is, if you have not grace in you, and zeal to reprove sinners.

*{wherewith shall it be salted?}* As nothing else can recover the taste of salt once lost; so, bad ministers cannot easily be mended by others.

*{good for nothing}* Other things may be good for somewhat else, when they decay, salt cannot. So is it with unsavory ministers, and relapsing Christians.

*{to be cast out}* Of the church as unprofitable.

*{to be trodden under foot}* As not good for any use. See Luke 14.35. & 21.24.

**V. 14. ***{Ye are the light of the world}* Not originally, like the sun, as Christ is, John 1.8, 9, but by participation, as the moon and the stars: so are ministers, Rev. 1.20. John 5.35, as also are other Christians, 1 Thessalo. 5.5. Phil. 2.15.

*{A city that is set on a hill}* You must show forth good example to others, far and near, whereby you may be as exemplary to others, as an high hill or city thereon is conspicuous.

*{cannot be hid}* Your lives will be looked on, and scanned by all men therefore see they be good.

**V. 15. ***{Neither do men light a candle}* Mark 4.21. Luke 8.16. & 11.33. Neither can you reasonably think that I communicate to you divine light, to the end that you should hide it in silence.

*{and put it under a bushel}* God intends that his graces given to his ministers or people, should be used for the good of others and not kept for their own good only; the master of a family lights a candle for the use of the whole family, Luke 19.20, 23.

*{a bushel}* *Μόδιος*. Is a measure, some say, containing about a pint less than a peck; others reckon, that it contains sixteen *Sextarios*, who (accounting the *Sextarios* at about a pint and an half) make the great *Modius* two gallons and a pottle; which is the *Modius Ponticus*: the *Modius Cyprius*, is reckoned a peck and a pint.

**V. 16. ***{Let your light so shine}* Manifest the graces that are in you, by your actions, as beams of light.

*{before men}* Who see not your hearts, but your deeds.

*{that they may see}* Not hear of them by others; but see, and be witnesses thereof. See Phil. 2.15. 1 Pet. 2.12.

*{your good works}* Not words only, but works does God require: good works, not other men’s, but yours: not what your friends, kindred, or predecessors have done, but what you do. See Deut. 5.28, 29. Rom. 2.13.

*{and glorify}* Your end must be God’s honor, not your own, Matth. 6.1. &c. Though you can add nothing to the infinite glory of God; yet must you confess it, and give men cause to praise him for you, Gal. 1.24.

*{your father}* All good children seek their fathers’ honor, and maintain it, as knowing their interest therein; hypocrites seek their own glory with men, but live so, that the holy name of God is blasphemed because of them, who profess good, but practice impiously, Rom. 2.23, 24. We may indeed vainly flatter ourselves in that great prerogative of the name of Christian, who yet so do, and live, *ut hoc ipsum quod Christianus populus esse dicimur, opprobrium Christi esse videatur*, says, *Salvian. de gub. Dei. l. 4*, that it may seem a dishonor to Christ, that we are called Christians.

*{which is in heaven}* Not your earthly fathers only, but principally your heavenly Father, chap. 6.9. & 23.9. Mal. 1.6.

**V. 17. ***{Think not that I am come}* This is a preface to the following exposition of the law, wherein our Savior prevents their misconstruction thereof: they hearing the law otherwise expounded than their teachers used to do, verse 21, 22, might think that Christ did abrogate the moral law, and bring in a new one: he warns them beforehand, not to think so; for as *Ambrose lib. 9. ep. 77* says of circumcision, it is true of the whole ceremonial law; *non — gentibus mandata est, sed semini Abraham*. It was commanded the seed of Abraham, but not to the Gentiles: and that but for a time, till all was fulfilled in the Messiah exhibited, with all the ceremonies thereof prefigured: and the fullness of the Gentiles was to come in, the moral law stands still in force though the curse due to the transgressors of the least precept thereof be taken away from those that are in Christ by faith, Rom. 8.1. Galat. 3.13.

*{to destroy the law}* Gr. *To dissolve*; he neither by doctrine, or precept destroyed it, nor by practice, nor by absolving those from obedience thereto, which he delivered from the curse thereof.

*{or the prophets}* The interpreters of it. I give none other moral precepts than Moses did, nor any other interpretations of that law, than the prophets gave.

*{but to fulfil}* Both by instruction, observation, and full satisfaction, Rom. 3.31. & 8.3. Others take it of the fulfilling the ceremonies, types and predictions of the Messiah in his own person, which he likewise did.

**V. 18. ***{till heaven and earth pass}* Luke 16.17. Till this world come to an end, the moral law shall stand in full force: heaven and earth shall be changed, rather than reduced to nothing.

*{one jot, or one tittle}* Not the least letter, or particle of a letter. A proverbial speech, signifying that exact obedience is due to the moral law, to the world’s end; but not concluding that we have power to fulfill it. This was a needful doctrine because the Pharisees endeavored to make void some part of it then, by their traditions, chap. 15.3, 4, and chap. 23.4. The meaning is, that the least letter, (such as is *jod* in the Hebrew, or *iota* in the Greek: a tittle *κεραία*, such as are the Hebrew points, or accents) should not pass, till all were fulfilled: how much more needful is this doctrine now, when men would make all the law void, and not obligatory to believers?

*{shall in no wise pass from the law}* No man has power to abrogate, or dispense with any part thereof; not that which he thinks least needful.

*{till all be fulfilled}* Gr. *Till all be done*: that is, nothing contained in the law, shall ever fail, but attain his full effect.

**V. 19. ***{Whosoever}* Of what rank or degree soever he be.

*{shall break}* James 2.10. Disobey in his practice.

*{one of these least commandments}* One of those precepts of the moral law, which the Pharisees and others make least account of, though indeed nothing is little in the law of God because the lawmaker is great: therefore Salvian said well, *nihil leve aestimetur quo Deus laeditur*. Let nothing be esteemed little, with which God is offended; *de gubern. Dei. l. 6.*

*{and shall teach}* Or *shall teach*: as Exod. 1.15.

*{he shall be called}* He shall be so, and so accounted.

*{the least in the kingdom of heaven}* He shall be of no account in God’s church here, nor have any part in heaven, as verse 20.

*{do and teach}* Confirm the law both by teaching and example, public or private.

*{great in the kingdom of heaven}* Of great account in the kingdom of grace and glory. Let not therefore your works confound your words: let the minister of Christ’s mind, life, and mouth accord. See *Hieron. Nepotian ep. 14. l. 2.*

**V. 20. ***{For I say unto you}* Whatsoever the scribes say unto you to the contrary, if you will believe me, it is otherwise.

*{except your righteousness}* Your obedience to God’s law.

*{shall exceed the righteousness of, &c.}* The one sort were accounted men of the greatest learning, the other of the strictest life, see chap. 15.1. You must go beyond them both; for they made conscience of outward obedience only, verse 21, and of some commandments only, chap. 15.3, but you must make conscience of inward obedience also, verse 22, and of keeping all the commandments, as Luke 1.6.

*{in no case enter into, &c.}* As chap. 7.21. & 18.3. & 19.23, 24. Mark 10.23, 24. Luke 18.24, 25. John 3.5. Act. 14.22, alluding to Numb. 14.30. Psalm 95.11. It is true, their righteousness was generally, rather to say, than to do good, &c. to be seen of men, rather than in sincerity unto God; and many other blemishes it had in the sight of an all-seeing God: but the comparison is not intended here between degrees of righteousness by the law, but between it (to which only the Pharisees pretended, and cleaved) and the righteousness of the faith in Christ, without whom no flesh ever was, never can be saved (Act. 4.12). So *Irenaeus l. 4. c. 27* interprets this *exceeding*; the Pharisee in his way could never come to heaven, because he sought righteousness only by the works of the law, which made none perfect, *in that it was weak through the flesh*, Rom. 8.3. None were able to fulfill it: therefore Israel attained not to the law of righteousness, because they sought it not by faith but as it were by the works of the law, Rom. 9.31. Your righteousness must exceed this: as does that righteousness which is of faith in Jesus (who perfectly fulfilled the law for us) or you shall not enter into the kingdom of heaven; for, by the deeds of the law there shall no flesh be justified, Rom. 3.20, and we are justified by faith only, without the works of the law.

**V. 21. ***{by them of}* Or, *to them*.

*{of old time}* It is a received opinion, delivered by tradition from father to son. Of what value prescription, and antiquity of error is, may hence appear: it is a good observation of *Tertul. de praescript. c. 31*, from the parable of the envious man’s tares, Matth. 13, it is (says he) manifest by the very order, *id esse Dominicum & verum, quod sit prius traditum: id autem extraneum & falsum, quod sit posterius immissum*: that is, the Lord’s and true, which was first delivered; but that foreign and false, which was afterwards cast in: add hereto that of *Cyprian ad Caecil. l. 2. ep. 8*, if Christ only is to be heard, *non debemus attendere quid aliquis ante nos faciendum putaverit; sed quid, qui ante omnes est, Christus prior fecerit, neque enim hominis consuetudinem sequi oportet sed Dei veritatem*, we must not attend what some before us thought was to be done: but what Christ did, who is before all: neither may we follow the customs of men, but the truth of God: antiquity of truth is venerable; but of error the more detestable, by how much more it has misled. The holy Scripture sufficiently determines what is truth, and what is error: and no question ought to be moved among Christians, where God’s *ipse dixit* has so determined; lest yet we break the bond of charity (which is the sinews of religion) Augustine’s rule to *Casulanum ep. 86* is to be held: *in his — rebus, de quibus nihil certi statuit Scriptura divina, mos populi Dei, vel instituta majorum pro lege tenenda sunt*; in those things concerning which the divine Scripture determines nothing certain; (that is, neither commands nor forbids) the custom of God’s people, and institutions of ancestors (or elders) are to be held for law.

*{thou shalt not kill}* Exod. 20.13. Deut. 5.17. They repeated the words of the law, but built false glosses thereon.

*{whosoever shall kill}* That is, for private revenge; or if it be acted by a private person. The magistrate or judge, taking away the life of a malefactor, according to the law of God and man, sins not therein; he sins, if he executes not justice; when the murderer is executed, *suo scelere punitur: Salvian. l. 7*, it was not so much the judge’s, as his own sin that killed him.

*{shall be in danger of the judgment}* Shall be liable to give account, to the less court of twenty-three judges, who judged ordinary causes, and persons.

**V. 22. ***{But I say unto you}* I give you not a new law, but rightly expound the law of God unto you.

*{whosoever is angry, &c.}* They counted nothing sin, but the external act: but unadvised anger is murder of the heart; therefore *Minucius Felix Octav.* said well, *apud nos, & cogitare peccare est*, with us evil thoughts are sins: and *Salvian. de Gub. Dei l. 8. non sunt longè ab occidentibus, qui animo occisionis oderunt*, they differ not much from murderers, who hate with a mind to kill.

*{without a cause}* Rashly; *aliud ira, aliud odium*, says Augustine *hom. 40*, the father is angry with his child, but hates him not, but to amend him; so is his anger an effect of love; so the apostle says, *be angry, but sin not*, Ephes. 4.26. Truly as long as we here live, affections will oftentimes over-bias our reason; yet must we watch over their exorbitancy, *moderari & regere possumus, amputare non possumus. Hieron. l. 2. ep. 20*, we may (and must) moderate and rule them, we cannot cut them off.

*{in danger of the judgment}* That is, he shall be liable to the punishments to be inflicted by the judges. Christ speaks not here of civil judgments executed by magistrates, but of God’s tribunal before which men are guilty of murder, not only for taking away the life of man, but also for affecting to hurt their neighbor by reproach; though, as there are degrees of this sin, so shall there be of punishments thereof: but Christ accommodates this whole speech to the form of the usual judicatures of the Jews: as their judgments by their *triumviri*, or three men deciding small matters, their twenty-three judges determining greater matters than the former; and their high court of seventy and one judges, who judged of the greatest matters within their jurisdiction: Christ expresses God’s judgments, by these things which were common, and better known to the Jews.

*{to his brother}* To another man, verse 23, 24, an Hebrew phrase, Gen. 26.31, one to another, *איש* [*כאחיו*?], a man to his brother.

*{Raca}* O vain man. See Judg. 9.4. 2 Sam. 6.20. Syr. *רקא* coming of the Hebrew word *רק* *rek, vacuus, inanis*, Jerome against the Pelagians, l. 2, interprets *Raca, vanus, & absque cerebro*; a vain, or brainless fellow: so *Hilary on Matth. can. 4. vacantis opprobrium est*: it signifies, says the learned *Jun. Tremellius*, one void of judgment, reason, and brains: understand this of him that says so in rash anger: otherwise we find the phrase used, James 2.20, as our Savior would not have us rashly angry, lest we should be malicious: for *ira mater est odii, Labo de Gub. Dei. l. 3*, anger is the mother of hatred: so would he here prevent the effects thereof, *primo enim semper inertum tela, maledicta sunt, & quicquid non possumus imbecilli, optamus irati. Salu. ibid.* The first weapons of anger are evil speeches, and that which we cannot do, being weak, we wish we could, being angry.

*{in danger of the council}* The meaning is he shall be liable to a greater punishment in hell, as a greater offender was liable to the judgment of the Sanhedrim, or great council.

*{shall say}* Out of rash anger, as before, otherwise our blessed Savior, who never sinned, used that word, Matth. 23.17, 19.

*{thou fool}* A word of greater disgrace than the former, *μωρέ*, it signifies *unsavory*, or without relish: *Raca* answers the Greek word *κενός*, empty that is *μάταιος*, foolish; and a fool is here, by a metaphor, called insipid, Heb. *שוטה* *sote*, which we call, sot.

*{in danger of hell fire}* To be cast into Gehenna, to have the greatest torments of hell inflicted on him; as the greatest offender among the Jews, was to be judged, either to be burnt alive in the valley of Hinnom, or to have his carcass denied burial, and burnt there, among the carrion cast out of the city, which was the greatest disgrace. See on Luke 12.5. See *Augustine de Civ. Dei, l. 21. c. 4, 10, 23.*

**V. 23. ***{therefore}* Seeing God forbids anger, and evil speaking as well as murder.

*{if thou bring thy gift}* The Jews thought God like some worldly princes, more regarding gifts, than the unity of their subjects; therefore offered many sacrifices, but lived still in malice: Christ shows them here, that such sacrifices did not please God. Charity is the salt of the covenant, with which every sacrifice must be seasoned.

*{to the altar}* He speaks according to that present state of the church, wherein altars and sacrifices were in use: they are deceived who gather hence, that we must have altars and sacrifices now: yet it may teach us by proportion to seek reconciliation before we come to the Lord’s table.

*{and there remembrest}* It should have been done before: but if it were forgotten or neglected then, it must of necessity be remembered and done now. This supposition of remembrance relates to another supposition of a right preparation of a man’s self to that duty, which cannot be without a due examination of his own state towards God and man.

*{hath ought against thee}* *Si nos eum in aliquo laesimus. Augustin. de Serm. Domini in mont. l. 1. c. 20.* If any man can accuse you, or charge you with any wrong done to him. See Rev. 2.4.

**V. 24. ***{leave there thy gift}* Do not carry it away; our sin against man, does not discharge us of God’s service; but God will not accept our service, till we have sought peace with all men, Heb. 12.14.

*{be reconciled}* Do your part to be reconciled; confess your fault, desire peace, make satisfaction, Levit. 6.1. &c. A purpose of reconciliation is not enough, it must actually be sought, before we can be fitted for God’s service.

*{then come}* Then persuade yourself God will accept your service.

**V. 25. ***{Agree with thine adversary quickly}* See on Luke 12.58, a similitude taken from a debtor.

*{in the way}* He alludes to the customs of adversaries in law, going before the judge: the meaning is, prepare presently (while the accepted time, and day of salvation lasts) to stand before God’s tribunal; agree while you are both alive.

*{be cast into prison}* Lest for your abusing God’s patience and long-suffering, you be condemned and cast into hell fire; the whole is allegorical: by adversaries delivering you, is meant, lest he whom you have wronged, complain to God of you: and by the judges delivering to the officer, understand, God’s execution of temporal and eternal punishments on the impenitent sinners.

**V. 26. ***{till thou hast paid}* The meaning is, that God will not forgive injuries done to men, except the injurious make what satisfaction they can. Some understand these two verses literally, as counsel given us by our Savior to avoid, and compose suits before trial.

*{the uttermost farthing}* That is, never, because that by suffering, you can, in no finite time, satisfy the infinite and eternal justice of God. See on chap. 1.25 and on Luke 12.58.

**V. 27. ***{by them}* Or *to them*.

*{Thou shalt not commit adultery}* Exod. 20.14. The Pharisees taught that the act of this sin, was only sinful before God, as it was punishable before men.

**V. 28. ***{Whosoever looketh}* With intent to lust after her; or till he lust after her, Mark 13.22. Job 31.7. So that both wanton thoughts and looks are here condemned. See Rom. 7.7. 2 Pet. 2.14. Therefore Isidore’s rule is, *ἢ* *ὁρῶν* *μὴ* *ἔρα*, *ἢ* *ἐρῶν* *μὴ* *ὅρα*, either seeing, lust not, or lusting look not. *ep. 173.* In this, as Euthymius notes, *radicem abscidit*; he cuts up sin by the root in the heart and affections, lest it should bring forth death. See James 1.14, 15. For the most part the actions of the inward man at last, show themselves in the outward man; as, chastity of the inward man passes to the bodies’ undefiled chastity; for he that is not acquainted with the heart’s adultery, is certainly and invincibly modest and chaste; therefore, as all seeds, and incentives of sin must carefully be avoided; so this, *oculus meretricius, laqueus amatoris est. Ambros. de poen. l. 1. c. 14*, the harlot’s eye, is the lover’s snare. *Lust not after her beauty in thine heart, neither let her take thee with her eye-lids*, Prov. 6.25. Flee fornication, 1 Cor. 6.18. Fly also youthful lusts, 2 Tim. 2.22. *Fugiamus ergo tanquam sequentem, quia non post nos, sed in nobis sequitur. ib. Ambros.* Let us therefore fly it, as that which follows, not after, but in us; how? by dashing the serpent’s eggs — *parvulos ad petram*, that is, (as the same *l. 2. c. 11. de poenit.* interprets) — *infirmas & lubricas cogitationes elidat ad Christum*: destroying sin in the thoughts, by recalling them to the dreadful tribunal of an all-seeing judge.

*{on a woman}* Understand all this to be a common rule to women as well as men, (which Chrysostom, Theophylact, and Euthymius note) so that when women look with such affections, they sin, and if they adorn themselves to be looked upon, and lusted after, though possibly they do not affect any man, yet *ἐκεῖναι* *φάρμακον* *ἐκέρασαν* *κἂν* *μηδεὶς* *πίῃ*, Theophylact; they have tempered the poison, though no man drink it. See *Tertul. de cult. foem. c. 12.*

*{hath committed, &c.}* For as Jerome says well, *ep. 22*, in respect of the sin, *nocuit & qui nocere disposuit*, he has hurt, or done evil, who disposes so to do, so here, *jam affectu adulter est*, is already an adulterer in affection. *Ambros. l. 9 epist. 77.*

**V. 29. ***{and if}* Chap. 18.8. Mark 9.47.

*{thy right eye}* Anastasius understands this *περὶ* *τῶν* *φίλων*, &c. of friends and kindred, the meaning is general: whatsoever is most useful or necessary to this life, as the right hand, or right eye.

*{offend thee}* Or *do cause you to offend*.

*{pluck it out}* The meaning is, there is a body of sin in us, which must be mortified, (Colos. 3.5) and all occasions and incentives thereto abandoned, if we will be saved; therefore Jerome’s counsel is very good *l. 2. ep. 15 nulli parcas, ut soli parcas animae*; spare nothing, that you may spare your soul.

*{for it is better}* It is better for a Christian, *ministerio carere quam vita. Salvian. de gub. l. 3*, to want the service (of some members) than life eternal; as it is better for a man that has a gangrene in one member, to cut it off, than to suffer it to infect the rest, and endanger his life; so it is better for you to leave your dearest sins, or the occasions of them, than that the whole man should perish by them.

**V. 30. ***{If thy right hand}* See on verse 29.

**V. 31. ***{Whosoever shall put away his wife}* Deut. 24.1, or whosoever will put away his wife; it seems the scribes misinterpreting the law, taught, that any man that disliked his wife, might put her away, so that he gave her a bill of divorce, whereby it might appear to any other man that desired to have her, that she was set free from her former husband.

**V. 32. ***{Whosoever shall put away, &c.}* Luke 16.18. 1 Cor. 7.10. Matth. 19.9.

*{saving for the cause of fornication}* For that cause he might put her away, but for none other. Adultery dissolves the bond of matrimony, which consists in their being one flesh.

*{causeth her to commit adultery}* Understand this, if the divorced woman marry to another, her former husband, being yet alive, which the party divorcing occasions, by putting her away.

**V. 33. ***{by them}* Or *to them*.

*{Thou shalt not forswear thyself}* Exod. 20.7. Levit. 19.12. Deut. 5.11. It seems by Christ’s answer, that the Pharisees taught, that if men did not forswear themselves, they did fulfill the third commandment; and so allowed or connived at swearing in ordinary communication, and of swearing by creatures: both which our Savior condemns.

**V. 34. ***{Swear not at all}* James 5.12. That is, in your ordinary communication; not at all, except where you have a lawful calling thereto: as in case of witness-bearing before the judge, or lawful magistrate, for the ending or deciding of controversies, or further confirmation of just duty. See Jer. 4.2. Heb. 6.16.

*{neither by heaven}* See Isaiah 66.1. Swear not by them, because they are creatures, not God; nor are they yours; they know not the truth of what you say, nor can of themselves punish you for your perjury; some interpret this prohibition with this condition, except you mean to perform vows, or swear truth; for God will punish perjury, though you swear not by him, but by some creature, heaven, earth, Jerusalem, your head; God is injured when his creatures are called to bear witness to a falsehood, or engaged for it. See chap. 23.16. &c.

**V. 35. ***{nor by the earth}* See on verse 34.

*{it is his footstool}* Isaiah 66.1.

*{neither by Jerusalem}* See on verse 34, the city where he is publicly worshipped.

*{city of the great King}* Of God, Psal. 48.2, 3, with 87.3.

**V. 36. ***{by thy head}* See on verse 34. You had it and every hair thereof of him.

*{one hair white or black}* One hair thereof, by giving it that native color: art only imitates nature, it cannot make any natural thing.

**V. 37. ***{let your communication be}* James 5.12. Your ordinary communication. See on verse 34.

*{yea, yea, nay, nay}* Affirmative or negative, without oaths for confirmation of that which you say.

*{whatsoever is more than these}* Customary swearing, by whatsoever they swear.

*{cometh of evil}* From Satan, corruption of heart, evil habit, other men’s incredulity; or your vain persuasion, that men will 7not believe you except you swear, whereas that makes your assertions more suspected, to good and wise men.

**V. 38. ***{an eye for an eye}* Exod. 21.24. Levit. 24.20. Deut. 19.21. This intimates that the Jewish doctors taught, that a private man might revenge himself, so that his revenge did not exceed the wrong; this Christ condemns, for the judges were to determine this.

**V. 39. ***{that ye resist not evil}* Luke 6.29. Rom. 12.17. 1 Cor. 6.7. He does not forbid private men to defend themselves against injuries by lawful means, or to seek for repair and satisfaction by the use of the lawful magistrate: but forbids private revenge or public, out of malice; or by rigorous prosecution of the laws.

*{turn to him the other also}* Though he smite maliciously, or in way of disgrace, as Job 16.10. Micha 5.1. Be so far from rendering evil for evil, as that you be prepared, rather to endure more injuries, than unlawfully to revenge the former.

**V. 40. ***{sue thee at the law, &c.}* Unjustly: if by force, fraud, unjust cavils, vexing suits, or force he get away your goods.

*{let him have thy cloak also}* See on vers. 39.

**V. 41. ***{compel thee to go a mile}* Under pretence of authority, compel you or your cattle, to carry or serve him; (as pursuivants, and such messengers, use to take up post-horses) see chap. 27.32.

*{go with him, &c.}* See on verse 39.

**V. 42. ***{give to him that asketh thee}* If he have need, and you can spare it, Luke 6.30. Galat. 6.10. 1 John 3.17.

*{from him that would borrow}* Deut. 15.8.

*{turn not thou away}* As those are wont, that refuse to hear or regard those that entreat them. See Isaiah 58.7. Lend him for his need, Luke 6.35.

**V. 43. ***{Love thy neighbour}* Levit. 19.18.

*{and hate thine enemy}* This the scribes collected (though falsely) out of the text, either from the misapplied rule of contraries, or else because they were commanded to destroy the inhabitants of the land of Canaan, Deut. 7.2. & 25.17, 19, which rule they would hold with their particular enemies also.

**V. 44. ***{love your enemies}* Luke 6.27. A man may happily do good to some whom he loves not; but here our Savior requires love of heart to them, who heartily hate us: love your friends in God, and your enemies for God’s sake.

*{bless them that curse you}* Express your hearty love in good words and wishes to them who wish you ill.

*{do good to them that hate you}* Show your love in good deeds to them, as 1 Sam. 24.17. Psal. 7.4. Gal. 6.10. Rom. 12.20. Render not evil for evil, but overcome your enemy with good, which is the most glorious victory of the saints.

*{pray for them}* As Luke 23.34. Acts 7.60. 1 Cor. 4.12. This shows, that Christ would have us so far from desiring revenge, that he will not have us pray for it: but in case of injury to commend our cause to God, and to pray him to give them, repentance, and mercy: therefore he would have us bless them: he says not, speak well of them who are wicked and injurious (God’s Spirit teaches no man to lie) but pray for them.

*{and persecute you}* See verse 11, 12. This is to imitate Christ, and his saints, Luke 23.34. Acts 7.60.

**V. 45. ***{that ye may be the children}* That you may hereby appear God’s children, who being the God of love, does good to his enemies. See 1 John 3.10.

*{of your father}* Of God, whom you must imitate, Ephes. 5.1.

*{which is in heaven}* Chap. 6.9. Whom you must imitate, so may you not earthly fathers, in all things, because they may err, and do, in some: God is absolutely holy, so is no mere man.

*{maketh his sun to rise}* Causes his sun to give light, and warmth, and the rain to cool and refresh wicked men in their persons, and their fields, that they may be fruitful.

**V. 46. ***{for if}* Luke 6.32.

*{ye love them which love you}* Them only, verse 47, for we must love them that love us, else are we worse than publicans or heathens, verse 47.

*{what reward have ye?}* You shall have no reward from God, for that which you do for your own ends and affections; for you do but render love for love, and love yourselves in loving them.

*{do not even Publicans the same?}* You do no more than the light of nature teaches men to do. The publicans, taking up tribute and custom from the Romans, were counted most vile, and impious by the Jews: (see Luke 19.8. & chap. 9.10) according to their sense Christ here speaks; do not those, whom yourselves condemn as wicked, as much? Will you not excel them, whom you esteem the worst of men?

**V. 47. ***{if ye salute}* Or *lovingly embrace, or ask of their welfare*, as 2 Kings 9.17, 18, 19, 22. The word *ἀσπάσησθε* signifies lovingly to embrace: but the Syriac interpreter gives it, if you salute: as in this one office of humanity, he understands all the offices of civility and friendship.

*{what do ye more}* See on verse 46.

**V. 48. ***{Be ye therefore perfect}* That is, sincere and complete in all parts of piety, especially in love towards all men, James 1.4. & 3.2. Colos. 4.12.

*{as your heavenly father}* Be like him in this, though you cannot be equal to him, see on verse 45. The Lord commends charity not only through all the Scriptures, but also by his own holy example. *Salvian. l. 4.*

## CHAPTER 6

**Verse 1. ***{That ye do not your alms}* Or *deal not your alms*, *ἐλεημοσύνην*, mercy and alms: some read *δικαιοσύνην* righteousness, so the Syria: words which signify alms or righteousness, come both of the Hebrew root *צדק*, to be just. 2 Cor. 5.21. James 1.21. 1 Pet. 2.24. Luke 11.41. Acts 3.2, 10. &c. See *Joh. Drus. praeterit. l. 1.*

*Eleemosyna, justitia, Synecdochicè, praecipua justitiae species: Jo. Buxtorf Jun. lex. Chald. Syr. Mart. Trost. &c.*

*{before men to be seen of them}* We may and ought to do good before men, that God may be glorified thereby. See chap. 5.16, but our end must not be to be praised of them, but to please God, verse 2. *Non est ingrata eleemosyna quae fit & videtur, sed quae fit ut videatur*, that alms is not unacceptable, which is given, and seen of men; but that which is given to be seen of men. True righteousness, which is self-glorious despises the applause of the world, *à Deo genita Coelum spectat, P. Chrysologus serm. 9*, being born of God, it looks up to heaven for its reward and approbation. That righteousness, *quae se humanis oculis locat*, which hires itself out to the eyes of men, cannot expect the reward of the heavenly Father. It would be seen, it was seen; it would, and it has pleased men; it has the reward it looked for, it shall never have the reward which is undervalued. Let us take great heed to ourselves: a wrong end proposed, deprives of all the fruits of those things we do, though with never so much labor, cost, or vigilancy.

*{no reward}* The word is usually taken in Scripture, for a free recompense, Rom. 4.4. See chap. 20.8.

*{of}* Or *with*.

*{your father which is in heaven}* See on chap. 5.45.

**V. 2. ***{when thou doest thine alms}* When you give alms. See verse 1, and Rom. 12.8.

*{do not sound a trumpet, &c.}* Cause not a trumpet to be sounded before you, alluding to the manner of calling the people together, Numb. 10.3, 4. It may be they did use to sound a trumpet when alms were to be given, that the poor might not go up and down begging, or lose their times in coming, when nothing was yet ready for them: the Pharisees gave their private alms so, that they might have many witnesses of their liberality to praise them, as well as receivers to thank them.

*{as the hypocrites do}* The Pharisees who are counterfeits, as those that act other men’s persons in a comedy. See on Luke 13.15.

*{in the synagogues, and in the streets}* This they did, that all the city might take notice of their charity.

*{that they may have glory with men}* That their neighbors might praise, and admire them for good and holy men: the vainglorious hypocrite is, as Jerome *l. 2. ep. 23* said of a philosopher, *popularis aurae vile mancipium*, a base slave to popular applause: but however men ignorant of the heart, praise and canonize the wicked for saints, yet God cannot be deceived; the most rotten wood shines most in the dark; the light discovers it: and so that which is highly esteemed amongst men, is abomination in the sight of God. See on Luke 16.15.

*{they have their reward}* *τὸν* *μισθὸν* *αὐτῶν*, their own reward, or have all their reward: they may make their acquittance: as the word *ἀποχὴ* (derived from the word here used, and Phil. 4.18) signifies they have that which they desired, and aimed at the praise of men. See on verse 1. Having therefore received the reward they proposed to themselves here of men, they cannot reasonably look for it again of God in the life to come. Such was that word which the rich man heard in hell, Luke 16.25. *Remember that thou in thy life time receivedst thy good things, &c. now thou art tormented*: but *O quanta apud Deum merces, si unusquisque in praesenti, pretium non speraret? Jerome l. 2. ep. 14.* O how great should be our reward with God, if no man would hope for the reward in this present life.

**V. 3. ***{let not thy right hand know}* A proverbial speech signifying, that we should be willing to give alms when none sees us, or not regarding other men’s taking notice thereof: give with a mind so free from ostentation, and divulging your alms to others that you may (if it were possible that you could) conceal it from yourself; lest your frail mind should incline to any desire of human applause.

**V. 4. ***{thy father which seeth in secret}* Whereas men look for honor and reward for the good they do, how vain and foolish is it to expect these from men; seeing the all-seeing God, who is a most just and plentiful rewarder of all good, sees the good we do, and will before men and angels publicly recompense even that which men knew not? See Matth. 25.34, 37. Honor is a shadow of merit; which, if you follow, you can never overtake: if you flee, it will follow, and not be divided 15from you, while the light gives it appearance.

**V. 5. ***{as the hypocrites are}* See on verse 2.

*{love to pray standing in the synagogues}* They affect it, Chap. 23.6, 7, and use it to be heard of men.

*{in the corners of streets}* They loved to pray in public places, as at the corners; where divers ways meeting, many, passing to and again, might take notice of their devotion.

*{have their reward}* See on verse 2.

**V. 6. ***{when thou prayest}* Privately, for public prayers are to be made in God’s public worship, in places appointed, and convenient for conventions, and assemblies of people.

*{enter into thy closet}* *Ταμεῖον* properly signifies any private place, where grain, any other goods, store or treasures were laid up. See on John 5.2. What then, says Theophylact, shall I not pray in the church? Yes, but with a well composed mind, and without ostentation: *ὁ* *τόπος* *οὐ* *βλαβερός*, *ἀλλ᾽* *ὁ* *τρόπος* *καὶ* *ὁ* *σκοπός*, the place hurts not, but the manner, and intention: many pray in private to be heard of men.

*{pray to thy father which is in secret}* Remember your business is with God in private, not with men in public, and seek to please him alone, and not men, as they do, who pray so loud in their chambers, that they may be heard into the streets.

*{which seeth in secret}* This is that sovereign remedy against vain ambition, to recall and set us ever in the sight and presence of God, who beholds all things in heaven and earth; yea, the very thoughts and intentions of every heart so that being contented with his knowledge of us, and all our actions, we may faithfully endeavor to approve ourselves to him. See Heb. 4.13. 1 Chron. 28.9. Rom. 8.27. 1 Cor. 2.10. Revel. 2.23. Psalm 44.21. Jerem. 17.10.

*{shall reward thee openly}* Luke 14.14. It appears when God will reward the merciful, *in the resurrection of the just*. See 1 Cor. 4.5. He is your Father, who as he is delighted and well pleased with such your works, Heb. 13, so will he not conceal your virtues: how willingly do we speak of any excellencies in our child, or those we love? God will assuredly reward you, not before a few men of this soon-perishing generation, but before all the world; men and angels. See on verse 4.

**V. 7. ***{use not vain repetitions}* Eccles. 7.14. Eccles. 5.2. All long prayers are not here condemned: nor such repetitions as are useful to stir up affections, but vain and needless ones (see Chapter 26.44, and Luke 22.44) such as Christ upbraided the Pharisees with, Chapter 23.14, or Elias Baal’s priests 1 King. 18.27.

*{as the heathen do}* It was a shame for the Jews to imitate the heathens in prayer, whom they abhorred, as impious. See Chap. 18.17.

*{they think they shall be heard for their much speaking}* This is his first reason to dehort them, what more vain, than to think to please the all-wise God, with such folly, as is irksome to any prudent man? The brethren in Egypt, (says Augustine *ep. 121. c. 10*) are reported to have frequent, but very brief prayers; and those in a manner suddenly ejaculated: lest that intention of mind, which being vigilantly erect, which is very necessary to him that prays, should vanish, and be dulled with long continuance: and by this they sufficiently show, that this intention, as it is not to be dulled, if it cannot continue: so neither is it quickly to be broken off, if it could last. *Absit enim ab oratione multa locutio, sed non desit multa precatio; si fervens, perseverat intentio*; let much speaking be far from prayer, but let not much prayer be wanting, if the intention continue fervent: to speak much in prayer, is (says he) *rem necessariam superfluis agere verbis*, &c. to do a thing necessary with superfluous words.

**V. 8. ***{your father knoweth}* This is another reason, drawn from the omniscience and goodness of God: you know that God knows your wants before you ask, and has a fatherly care towards you; and therefore need not many repetitions of the same thing, to acquaint him with your wants, as if he were ignorant of them: prayer is (as Augustine says, *ep. 120*) necessary to move us, not to inform God: because he is a gracious Father, his care and readiness to give, prevents your duty in asking: we do not beg to inform God; but to perform our duty, to recount his promises, to show our filial dependence on him, and to acknowledge him the author and giver of all good.

**V. 9. ***{after this manner}* This is a true sum, and form of all Christian prayers, set down both in precept and pattern. See Luke 11.2.

*{Our Father}* Who are willing to hear us your children, see on Luke 11.2.

*{which art in heaven}* Whence you see our wants, Psal. 33.13, 14; and are able to relieve us, Psal. 115.3.

*{hallowed be thy Name}* By us, and all other persons: let all men acknowledge you to be holy, and glorious.

**V. 10. ***{Thy kingdom come}* Of grace here, and glory in heaven: *manifestetur hominibus, Augustine*; let it be manifested to men. See on Luke 11.2.

*{thy will be done, &c.}* By us here, as by angels there. Psal. 103.20, 21. See on Luke 11.2.

**V. 11. ***{our daily bread}* That which is meet for us, or may suffice our nature every day. Prov. 30.8. Luke 11.3.

**V. 12. ***{our debts}* Our sins, which make us liable to your judgments, see on Luke 11.4.

*{our debtors}* Such as do us wrong: he understands debts *de omnibus quae in nos quisque peccat, Augustine*, of all those things wherein any sins by hurting us: which may teach us the necessity of charity: for how shall we daily say, *Forgive us, &c. as we forgive*, &c. *animo discordante cum verbis, oratione dissidente cum factis? Jerem. l. 1. ep. 39*, our mind disagreeing with our words, and our prayer dissenting from our actions? See on Luke 11.4.

**V. 13. ***{lead us not into temptation}* Let not Satan tempt us who are so weak, let your power keep us from falling. See Chap. 26.41. John 17.15. God leads none into temptation, Jam. 1.13. *sed induci patitur eum, quem suo auxilio deseruerit, ordine occultissimo & meritis, August. lib. 2. cap. 14. de serm. Domini in mont.* But suffers him to be led, whom he shall have left destitute of his aid, in order and deserts most secret. See on Luke 11.4.

*{deliver us from evil}* Or *from that evil one*, that is, Satan 2 Cor. 13.7. See on Luke 11.4.

*{for thine is the kingdom}* We are your subjects, and you alone are able to deliver us, and to grant all our requests: and your name shall be glorified in doing those things for us, which you see best.

*{Amen}* We desire it may be, and believe it shall be so, see on Chap. 28.20, and on John 3.11.

**V. 14. ***{for if ye forgive}* Mark 11.25. This of all the petitions is insisted on, because of our unwillingness to forgive wrongs; and for that all religion and gifts without charity are vain, and liveless: they that forgive shall be forgiven, and none else, verse 15. *nec frustra sperat parcendum peccatis suis, qui ignoscit alienis, Prosper sent.* Neither does he hope in vain, that God will spare him for his sins, who pardons others’ offenses.

*{men their trespasses}* We must forgive others, because they are men like us, and we may need forgiveness of them.

**V. 15. ***{if ye forgive not}* See on verse 14.

**V. 16. ***{as the hypocrites}* See on verse 2.

*{of a sad countenance}* Sour and sorrowful, Luke 24.17. *σκυθρωποί*, a word compounded of *σκυθρόν*, wreathed, grim, sour, and *ὢψ*, a countenance, a lowering look. See Luke 24.17. Isaiah 58.5. The godly were wont to fast in sackcloth and ashes, 2 King. 19. 1 Chron. 21.16. Nehemiah 9.1. Esther 4.1. Job 16.15, &c. This Christ reproves not; but the counterfeit semblance of humiliation, which is to ostent sanctimony, and to be noted of men.

*{they disfigure their faces}* To compose their countenance to such a rueful aspect, as quite alters the native fashion thereof.

*{that they may appear unto men to fast}* Not that they might seem to fast when they did not; but that they might be known abroad to fast, when they fasted in private.

*{they have their reward}* See on verse 2.

**V. 17. ***{when thou fastest}* In private, for in public fasts they might not do so.

*{anoint thy head, &c.}* If at the time of your private fast, you be necessitated to go into the public, leave the signs of your humiliation at home: and use your ordinary garb, as at other times; they used so, ordinarily to anoint their heads unless occasion of mourning hindered; and washing and anointing was usual in feasts, Ruth 3.3. 2 Sam. 12.20. 2 Sam. 14.2. Amos 6.6. Eccles. 9.8. Luke 7.46.

**V. 18. ***{to thy father which is in secret, &c.}* See on verse 6.

**V. 19. ***{lay not up for yourselves}* Let it not be your chief care to be rich: or labor not so much to be rich here, as in heaven: all treasuring here is not simply condemned. See 2 Cor. 12.14. John 6.27, and here, Chap. 9.13.

*{treasures upon earth}* Where can be neither stability of any possession, nor continuance of possessors.

*{where moth, &c.}* Jam. 5.2. Such treasure, either silly creatures can destroy, or matter of corruption out of themselves: or they may be taken away.

**V. 20. ***{but lay up}* Luke 12.33. 1 Tim. 8.19.

*{treasures in heaven}* In a sure place, free from all the former inconveniences: get the spiritual riches of grace, which will go with you into heaven: do good to the poor, that you may be rewarded in heaven, as Luke 12.33. These are true riches: so are not the secular; for they many times hurt the possessors, and commonly make them worse: *if you love them, peribis cum illis, Augustine, de verb. Domini, s. Luc. serm. 35*, you shall perish with them: they are always in danger of losing: those are true riches *quas, cum habuerimus, perdere non possumus*, which, when we once have, we can never lose again, says the same. The poor man’s hand is a bill of exchange for heaven, whereby you may safely remit your estate, because the just God undertakes repayment, Proverbs 19.17.

**V. 21. ***{your treasure}* That which you make most account of.

*{there will your heart be}* That is, your mind and affection. See Colos. 3.2. The mind will follow its treasure; which, if earthly, are commonly to the heirs *breve gaudium*, a short joy: but to those that got them unjustly, a cause of eternal sorrow, *Timoth. ad ecclesiam* 16*lib. 1.* It is a shame then, that an heavenly soul should be fastened to the earth. Phil. 3.19.

**V. 22. ***{the light of the body is the eye, &c.}* Luke 11.34. As in the body, the eye guides all parts and actions which may be conveniently performed, if the sight be clear, which otherwise must be troublesome, and confused: so, in the soul, if the reason be rightly illuminated, all the acts of the soul, will be more easily and orderly performed, which otherwise must needs be exorbitant, and evil.

**V. 23. ***{but if thine eye be evil}* See on verse 22. An evil eye imports sometimes a covetous mind; Prov. 23.6, sometimes an envious disposition, Chap. 20.15, but here, more generally a mind not well informed, or affected: to the good spiritual eye belong, charity in the intention, and truth in election. *Bernard. de praecep. & dispensat. cap. 17.*

**V. 24. ***{no man can, &c.}* For God will be served with the whole man. See Luke 16.13.

*{serve two masters}* That is, who have contrary employments: one man may, otherwise, serve two.

*{either he will hate the one, &c.}* Either his affection will be more to one than the other: or if it be possible to love both alike, yet in doing the commands of the one, he must needs neglect the business of the other.

*{Mammon}* That is, riches. See on Luke 16.9.

**V. 25. ***{Therefore I say unto you}* That you may not with too much care make yourselves more unhappy.

*{take no thought}* Take not too much care, no, not for things necessary, much less for things superfluous: we may not understand this simply, as if we might take no care, nor use any providence for ourselves and ours: (see 1 Tim. 5.8) but be not immoderately careful or distrustful of God’s care and providence over us. The word *μεριμνᾶτε*, is derived *παρὰ* *τὸ* *μερίζειν* *τὸν* *νοῦν*, from dividing the mind, and distracting it from God’s service, and confident trust in him: they mistake this place, who would hence conclude that Christians may not labor, or take care, but live idly: the Nicene council, well determined (*l. 3. Bin. To. 1. p. 401. C.*) against such inordinate living. See 2 Thes. 3.10, 11.

*{what ye shall eat}* *Cogitatio victus, spinae sunt fidei*, says Jerome *l. 2. ep. 19*, thought of viands is the thorns, which choke up faith: understand it of care, with distrust of God’s providence.

*{is not the life more than meat?}* That is, more excellent, of more value: God that has given you the greater, has given you the means to maintain it, and will not deny you the lesser.

**V. 26. ***{behold the}* Consider well how they are fed.

*{fowles of the air}* Not those about the house, which we feed: but those which fly in the air, which no man provides for, neither can they, without God’s universal providence, provide for themselves.

*{they sow not}* Neither is this added to forbid us to labor, but, from diffident care would he hereby dissuade us, because God can, and does provide for the most helpless: see 2 Thes. 3.10. Prov. 24.31. He would teach us, in an holy, and prudent endeavor, to rely upon God’s providence, who feeds those creatures made for man’s use, without their sowing and care for times to come, and therefore will not fail to provide for men, without their vain perplexing themselves: we must labor, but not be oversolicitous, believing that God gives the increase and fruit of our labors, as the forecited council determined.

*{yet your heavenly father}* Not theirs, as yours: their Creator, but your Creator, and Father by adoption; much more will he provide for you, children: if he does it for those who are only his creatures. See Rom. 8.31.

**V. 27. ***{which of you, &c.}* It is a vain thing for you to take care for the one, who cannot do the other.

*{to his stature}* He that would be tall, cannot: or the word may signify, to his age, as John 9.21. Heb. 11.11, then it should signify, that none could add to his appointed time. See Luke 12.21.

**V. 28. ***{why take ye care for raiment?}* Especially for superfluous garments, which the Spartans were wont to call, *δολερὰ* *τὰ* *ἱμάτια*, *Clem. Alexandr. strom. lib. 1*, deceitful garments.

*{consider}* Learn, observe their admirable beauty. See on Luke 12.27.

*{of the field}* Not those of which any gardener takes care, but grow natively in the fields.

*{how they grow}* And shoot out beautiful leaves instead of raiment.

*{they toil not}* As men and women do, to get apparel, or to adorn themselves or others.

**V. 29. ***{Solomon in all his glory}* Sitting in his royal robes, on his stately throne, 2 Chron. 9.7.

*{like one of these}* God puts more glory upon one flower, than art and cost can put on the greatest monarchs; their conjecture, that think Christ here speaks of that flower, which is now called among us, the *Tulipa*, is very probable: their reasons are, that the shape so agrees, that we may think them, both for their leaves and bulbous root, to be a kind of lily: these are but strangers with us but grow commonly in the fields in those eastern parts, where Christ conversed: and though, in regard of the strangeness of the flower, the proper name was also strange, and is now to us of these parts; yet might it be a species of that which the Greeks called *κρίνον*, which some interpret by *λείριον*, sometimes put for any flower. Secondly, that admirable and glorious variety of colors to be found in the tulip, more than in any flower, may persuade us so to think. So lastly, may be wondrous beauty and mixtures of those colors in these flowers conclude, Solomon’s princely magnificence in raiment, for color, variety, and beauty overcome by these flowers.

**V. 30. ***{If God so clothe}* This is another argument from the lesser to the greater, to dissuade us from distrusting care: if God so gloriously clothe the flowers of so short continuance, what will he not do for man, created in his own image to eternity?

*{the grasse of the field}* Not only the flowers of the garden, and the high and fruitful trees, but even the grass which we tread upon. Genesis 1.11. James 1.10, 11. 1 Peter 1.24. See on verse 28.

*{to day is, and, &c.}* Of so short continuance, Psalm 90.5, 6. Basil on Psalm 44 notes, that this lily, is a flower, of all soon perishing and fading away, and so aptly resembling the frailty of man.

*{cast into the oven}* Of so little account as to be made fuel.

*{shall he not much more}* Much rather will he clothe you who are far better.

*{O ye of little faith}* You that are unbelieving and diffident of his providence.

**V. 31. ***{What shall we eat}* He expresses the care he forbids in their own words: a distrustful care for food and necessaries.

**V. 32. ***{do the Gentiles seek}* Because they know not the eternal blessedness of the life to come, but only that which concerns this present, therefore they care for nothing else: you should not be like them. See on verse 7.

*{your heavenly Father knoweth}* And therefore out of his love will provide for you. See on verse 8.

**V. 33. ***{seek ye first}* Principally, and before the things of this life: he takes their minds off from worldly cares, by setting before them a more excellent object.

*{the kingdom of God}* Prayed for, verse 10. See Rom. 14.17. & Chap. 25.34.

*{his righteousness}* God’s, which he has promised to you, and prescribed you, Tit. 2.11, 12, as the way to his kingdom.

*{all these things}* Whatsoever you have need of, for this life, and that which he knows best for you, though possibly, not all that you desire, or think you need. See 1 Tim. 4.8. Psalm 34.10.

*{shall be added unto you}* Not as the main gift, but over and above, Mark 10.30, as riches to Solomon, 1 Kings 3.2. *προστεθήσεται* imports, a casting in, as some overweight, measure, or number.

**V. 34. ***{no thought for to morrow}* No distracting care, as before, God forbids us all faithless, diffident, and impious care for the future; *incuria autem solicitudinis relaxatae, non negligentiae, sed fidei est. Hilarie in Matth. 6. Canon. 5*, respite and freedom from care, which is, a moderate unbending and resting thereof, is no effect of lazy negligence, but faith.

*{the morrow shall take thought}* The time to come, as Exodus 13.14. Proverbs 27.1. It will bring care enough with it, you need not vex yourselves for it beforehand; or it will bring provision with it.

*{sufficient unto the day}* You will find trouble enough today, you need not add to it.

*{the evil thereof}* The grief and vexation it brings.

## CHAPTER 7

**Verse 1. ***{Judge not}* See Rom. 2.1. Judge not rashly of men’s persons and actions, as they who misconstrue doubtful things; nor rigidly and uncharitably, as those who exasperate small faults in others, and are indulgent to great crimes in themselves: such judges were the Pharisees. See Rom. 14.4. *Periculosum quippe est, de servo alterius judicare, & non facilis venia prava dixisse de rectis, Jerom l. 1. ep. 45*, for indeed it is a dangerous thing to judge another man’s servant; and to speak evil of the good, is not easily pardoned. *οὐκ* *οἶδας* *ὅτι* *καὶ* *χαλεπώτερον* *σαυτῷ* *ποιεῖς* *τὸ* *δικαστήριον* *οὕτω* *δικάζων*, says Chrysostom *hom. 85. in John.*, know you not, that you prescribe, and fore-pronounce to yourself a more grievous judgment, while you thus judge another man.

**V. 2. ***{with what judgement ye judge, &c.}* Oftentimes you shall find men ready to judge as hardly of you, as you of them, or, God will judge as severely of you, as you do of others: some retaliation shall be. See Luke 6.38. James 2.13. Jerem. 30.16. Ezek. 35.15. Obad. verse 15. Levi. 24.19. He speaks here against unjust, and rash judgment: unjust, as when an incompetent judge condemns (of which Rom. 14.4), or when a competent judge, unjustly; 17rash, of which 1 Cor. 4.2. See Luke 6.38, such also is partial judgment; of which *Salvian l. 4. de gub. Dei* says, in the very same crimes, *punimus alios, nos absolvimus*, we punish others, and absolve ourselves.

*{and with what measure you mete}* Mark 4.24. Luke 6.38. Judg. 1.7. A proverbial sentence: it is very equal that a man should buy by the same measure by which he sells: you would be loath to be hardly censured by others: do not that to others, which you would not suffer at their hands.

**V. 3. ***{and why beholdest thou}* That is, you do it evilly: indeed, as *Lactantius* says, *l. 2. c. 3, Facilius est de alienis judicare, quam de suis*, it is easier to judge of other men’s faults, than one’s own: the eye sees not itself, nor anything too near it: so we hardly see our own sins, but are too quick-sighted, towards others: and as the most ravenous and mischievous birds are most quick-sighted, so are the most wicked to find fault with others. He speaks here in the singular number, to show the need of particular application.

*{the mote that is in thy brothers}* With what face can you tax your brother for small faults, when you bear with greater in yourself? Some think it should be read, a splinter, rather than a mote; because that answers best to a beam: but the Syriac reads, *לגלא*, *tegelto, festucam*, the mote.

**V. 4. ***{Or how wilt thou say, &c.}* You ought for shame, first to amend your own faults, before you censure another for the same; lest you hear, *Physician cure thyself*: sin and self-love blinds men so, that they can see no faults at home: and such persons are commonly censorious. See Gen. 38.24, 25, which renders a man unexcusable, and self-condemned, Rom. 2.1, 3.

**V. 5. ***{thou hypocrite}* Rash censuring smells very strong of hypocrisy; when men would seem to be so good, that they (as blameless) would be correctors of others, and are so bad, that they allow, or dispense with worse faults in themselves.

*{first cast out, &c.}* First amend yourself: and (because as *Hilarie* says, *Canon. 5. in Matth. Difficile quemquam — praestare quod exigat*; it is hard to perform the duty you require of others, and it is best, to teach rather by example, than words) suspend your judgment of others: and as they were wont to say of old, *ἀφ᾽* *ἑστίας* *ἄρχου*, *Aristoph.* Begin your censure at home, and amend your own faults first. How shall a man reprove another’s faults, *αὐτὸς* *ἐφ᾽* *ἑστίας* [*τὴν*?] *παῤῥησίαν* *οὐκ* *ἔχων*, *Gr. Nazianzen Caesario ep. 51*, himself having no confidence at home, or within his own conscience. First then, examine your life, whether you did never commit the same fault, whether yourself yet amended, and clear of it, lest else, as Nazianzen said, *Orat. 1*, ourselves being full of sores, we only endeavor, *ἄλλους* *ἰατρεύειν*, to cure others, not ourselves: in case you were never guilty of such sin, yet charity said, *Cogitemus & nos homines, & habere potuisse, Augustine l. 2. de serm. Domini in mont. c. 30*, let us remember that we also are men, and might also have had the same fault: it is too sure, we have many others. Lastly, if we have sinned so but do not now, let us remember the common frailty of men, that mercy, not malice, may usher in our reproofs. See Gal. 6.1.

**V. 6. ***{give not that which is holy unto the dogs}* Apply not the promises of the Gospel unto profane men: or speak no more of them, to such as persecute you for your message: or reprove not such, Prov. 9.8, for they are mischievous like dogs, Philip. 3.2, and will profane, but never profit, by holy things. By dogs and swine, he means the obstinate, professed, and impure enemies of the Gospel, and the ministers thereof, 2 Pet. 2.22. Rev. 22.15. Psal. 22.16, and by holy, and pearls, the excellent and unsearchable riches of Christ, Ephes. 3.8, opened in the Gospel, which Christ called, a pearl of great price, Chap. 13.46. See this precept in the type, Exod. 12.43. &c. Levit. 22.10, 25. Numb. 1.51.

*{pearls before swine}* Apply not the precious promises, of the Gospel to swinish men which regard them no more, than swine do pearls, of whose use they can have no sense.

*{trample them under their feet}* As swine would do the richest pearls; lest they scorn, and deride the promises and threatenings of God’s word, which is the certain fruit of unbelief, which Euthymius calls their feet, wherewith *ad perditionem gradiuntur*, they go to destruction.

*{turn again and rent you}* As beasts sometimes do to those that feed them; so impious hearers persecute their teachers, and hate them, 1 Kings 22.8. Jer. 18.18. &c. and 26.8. &c. Mark 6.17. Acts 7.52, 57, 58.

**V. 7. ***{ask and ye shall have}* Chap. 21.22. Mark 11.24. Luke 11.9. John 16.14. James 1.6.

*{seek}* Seek diligently, timely, and constantly: and you shall find eternal riches.

*{knock}* Pray earnestly, this threefold promise annexed to the threefold precept, must encourage us to instant, and constant prayer: if God had commanded some great matter, would we not have done it to be saved? How much more when he says *Ask and it shall be given you?* Were the means unknown to you, or lost, you ought to seek, see John 5.35, if it were locked up, and the door shut, *Knock and it shall be opened*: it may be we shall not find and understand all things that we would, yet must we continue knocking, as Peter did, Acts 12, and as Augustine said, *To. 6. contr. Priscil. l. 12*, we so believe in God, that, although *aliqua nobis pulsantibus non aperiat*, he does not open to us knocking, some things; yet we may by no means murmur against him; for he has revealed enough for our salvation: the old heretics, the Valentinians and Marcionites, would so wrest this text, as if they ought always to be to seek in religion, and not to rest in the Scriptures and doctrine of the apostolical churches, because Christ said, *Seek and ye shall finde*: it may be said to our modern seekers, as the Spirit says to the church of Sardis, Rev. 3.3, *Remember therefore how thou hast received and heard, and hold fast*: the reason of this present speech is in three things, in the matter, time and manner: that we first consider, what we must seek, when, and how far: we must seek Christ, and the knowledge of God’s will for our salvation in him; until we believe in him, that we may be saved: but as Tertullian says, *advers. Haeret. cap. 8. invenisti, cum credidisti*: you have found when you believe: we need not any curious search now after Christ, and the Gospel, *Cum credimus, nihil desideramus ultra credere*, when we believe, we desire not to believe anything beyond the truth: for this is that which we believed before, that there is nothing more (than what the Scripture speaks of God, and our salvation by Christ) which we ought to believe, *Ultra Dei verbum nihil invenitur; Hilarie de Trinitat. lib. 2*, there is nothing found beyond the word of God: let us remember, says Tertullian, when Christ said, *Seek and ye shall finde*: it was in the first fruits of his doctrine, when most doubted whether he were Christ, we now believing in him, must be no longer staggering, and to seek of our faith and religion.

**V. 8. ***{every one that asketh}* In faith and according to God’s will; for God will deny others, Jam. 4.3. Jam. 1.6, 7. Chap. 20.20. &c. Luke 9.54.

*{receiveth}* Though men be unkind, and deny to help the necessitous, God will give according to his gracious promise.

**V. 9. ***{what man is there, &c.}* Or, *if there were a man among you*, Chapter 12.11. & 24.45. Luke 11.5, 11. Is any of you so inhuman, that if his son should ask him for food, would give him poison?

**V. 10. ***{a serpent}* Would any man give his child mischievous things? How much less will God give us anything to hurt us?

**V. 11. ***{if ye then being evil}* Why doubt you of God’s good will toward you, who is perfectly good, when yourselves, who are so evil, and so ignorant, that you know not at all times, what is good for your children, and sometimes so harsh that you will not hear them, nor give them that which you know is good for them; yet your passion allayed, your natural affection prevailing, you will be sure to do them all the good you can.

*{your father which is in heaven}* See on Chap. 5.45.

*{good things}* The good gifts of his Spirit, Luke 11.13. The Holy Ghost, the author of all spiritual good: bodily goods which we ask, God does not always give us that he may give us something better; according to that which Augustine says, *non tribuit saepe quod volumus, ut quod mallemus attribuat, ep. 34. ad Paulin.* Many times he gives us not that which he would, that he may give us that which we had rather have. See on Luke 11.13.

**V. 12. ***{whatsoever ye would}* Luke 6.31, that is, do to others that, which you being well informed, advised, and freed from evil and discomposed affection, would have them do to you.

*{this is the law and the prophets}* This is required in the doctrine of the law and the prophets, and is the sum of that they teach, concerning the duty of man to man: the equal law, to do as he would be done unto.

**V. 13. ***{enter ye in}* Luke 13.24.

*{at the strait gate}* Laden travelers cannot pass through a strait and narrow gate: such is the way to eternal life full of difficulties: therefore must we strive to lay aside every weight, and the sin which does so easily beset us, that we may run with patience, Heb. 12.1. So full of dangers, obstructions, and lets is this way, that the Holy Ghost says, 1 Pet. 4.18, *If the righteous scarcely be saved* — &c.

*{wide is the gate}* It is easy to go to hell: our own corrupt nature leads us that way: the cause of so many and grievous persecutions, is sinners’ impatience to be pulled out of that path; there is nothing in the world so easy, as to become wicked, because we are born such.

*{many there be which go}* Example of others may not lead us, if we will not go to hell for company. See Exod. 23.2, there is no patronage for wickedness in multitudes, *ubi major est peccantium turba, major est divinitatis injuria, Salvian de gub. Dei, l. 3.*

**V. 14. ***{because}* Or, *how narrow, &c*.

*{straight is the gate}* We must be contented through many pressures and difficulties to go to heaven: for it is like a narrow way, which if we find not, and out of which if we fall, we cannot avoid utter and eternal destruction of body and soul: we have need of Solomon’s caution, as chiefly when we go into the house of God (where the way to life is opened to us) so in all the whole course of our lives, *Keep thy foot*, Eccles. 5.1. As there are many crooked lines between two points, but only one straight one: so are there many ways to error and damnation, but only one way to salvation, Acts 4.12. John 14.6.

**V. 15. ***{Beware of false prophets}* False teachers, 2 Peter 2.1, seducers: sometimes called false apostles, 2 Cor. 11.13, deceitful workers. See 1 Tim. 1.7. 2 Tim. 3.5, 6. This *προσέχετε* imports a diligent heed to be taken, as of a great danger: as Matth. 10.17. & 16.1. So here is a threefold danger described: 1. They come to you: they are bold intruders and run without sending, or calling. See Jerem. 23.21, 32. 2 Tim. 3.6. 2. In sheep’s clothing, that is, whatsoever is specious, fair in show, and apt to deceive the simple. See Chap. 23.14. &c. Chap. 24.24. Now as it is true, *turpiora sunt vitia quae virtutum specie celantur. Jerem. l. 2. ep. 22. Celant.*, more filthy are those vices which are hidden under a show of virtue: so indeed are they more dangerous to beguile others: of such men *Syricius* speaks, who (says he) under the veil of an holy name entering the house of prayer, pour out *sermonem serpentinae disputationis*, the speech of a serpent-like discourse, *inter ep. Ambros. l. 10. ep. 80.* 3. Within they are ravenous wolves: see Acts 20.28, 29. *Considerate quid de gregibus agatur, quando lupi pastores fiunt, Greg. hom. 17. in Evang.*, do but think how it will go with the flocks, when wolves are made shepherds.

*{which come unto you}* Which join with you: or pretending to show you a more ready and secure way to heaven, offer you their ministry.

*{in sheeps clothing}* Appearance of harmless innocence.

*{inwardly they are ravening wolves}* See Acts 20.29. Whatever shows of goodwill they make, they seek to destroy your souls.

**V. 16. ***{by their fruits}* By their false doctrine, self-seeking, and wickedness, verse 23. 2 Pet. 2.1, which at last shows what they are, as ripe fruits do trees.

*{do men gather grapes of thorns?}* Luke 6.43. Every tree brings forth its own proper fruit after its kind, Gen. 1.11. Jam. 3.12.

**V. 17. ***{every good tree bringeth forth good fruit}* As several kinds of trees bear several fruits; so of one kind there are some better than others, which the fruit discovers.

**V. 18. ***{a good tree cannot bring forth evil fruit}* Not generally and constantly though accidentally it may fail in some particular: so though the best of the sons of men do sometimes fall into sin, yet they live not in it, 1 John 3.9. Romans 7.15, 17, 20, 21, and these fruits of the flesh, are from the remainder of the old man, and unregenerate part; as sometimes wild and unsavory fruit, grows on the suckers of the old stock, which are not pruned off.

**V. 19. ***{every tree}* Chap. 3.10.

*{that bringeth not forth good fruit}* Chap. 3.10. We cut down unfruitful trees, though they bear no dangerous fruit. So men shall perish that live not godly, though they be not profane or notorious sinners: we must cease to do evil, and learn to do well, Isa. 1.16. Luke 13.7.

**V. 20. ***{by their fruits}* See on verse 16.

**V. 21. ***{not every one}* Not any that does hypocritically worship God in word, without a sincere heart; the bare profession of true religion, is not sufficient to save any man. What is a holy name without desert, but *ornamentum in luto? Salv. l. 4*, an ornament in the dirt? And again he says, Our very religion which we profess, accuses our errors.

*{Lord, Lord}* Rom. 2.13. James 1.22.

*{doth the will of my Father}* That is, who not only carefully endeavors to keep God’s law, but also truly believe in Christ; (*for this is the will of God, that every one who seeth the son, and believeth in him, should have eternal life*, John 6.40) and also that herewith bring forth the fruits of sanctification, and holy obedience; for otherwise faith is dead, James 2, and the differences of professors, are but testimonies, as Salvian said, *lib. 4. Mali servi & boni Domini*, of the evil servant of a good Lord; *qualis enim est illa confessio, quae sic Deo credit, ut pro nihilo ducat ejus imperium? Jerom. ep. 22*, for what is that confession which so believes in God, that it nothing values his commandments? This is the will of God, even your sanctification, 1 Thes. 4.3. To do the will of the Father, is therefore to believe, and walk conscionably, according to the rule of God’s word.

**V. 22. ***{many will say unto me}* Many shall be so much deceived in themselves, that we have need to beware of false prophets, and to look carefully to our own condition, before it be too late. See 2 Cor. 13.5.

*{in that day}* At the day of judgment.

*{have we not prophesied}* Verse 15. We never thought that we should have been rejected, we expected the chief places in heaven, in recompense of our service which we have done. The best gifts are nothing worth without true godliness, and some hypocrites and reprobates, have excellent gifts of knowledge and preaching.

*{in thy Name}* By your authority, or to manifest your power: for all the abused gifts of impostures and wicked men, they shall once hear *what have ye to do to declare my statute*, Psalm 50.16. It is a most abominable impiety which takes up the sacred name of Christ, to cloak and color it; O unspeakable and prodigious wickedness, what will not impious minds do? *armant se ad latrocinandum per Christi Nomen, auctorem quodammodo sui sceleris Deum faciunt, Salv. 5. l.* They arm themselves to rob by the name of Christ, and after a manner make God the author of their wickedness; whereas Christ is the interdicter and revenger of all evils, they say they do that wickedness they commit, in Christ’s name, and for his sake, and the advancement of his cause; the greater shall their damnation be.

*{wonderful works}* Properly, powers; now these excellent works above nature, are called powers; because therein appears the extraordinary power of God.

**V. 23. ***{I will profess}* Or *confess*, make it known unto them, and to men and angels.

*{I never knew you}* Luke 13.27. Though you thought so highly of yourselves, as in opinion thereof to condemn others; and though others magnified and admired your excellencies, yet I never approved of you, nor owned you for mine, Psalm 1.6. Rom. 7.15. 1 Cor. 8.3. Gal. 4.9. 2 Tim. 2.19.

*{depart from me}* Psal. 6.8.

*{ye that work iniquity}* That live in sin, notwithstanding the excellent gifts which I bestowed upon you, and the sacred profession, which you took on you.

**V. 24. ***{therefore}* This is the conclusion of our Savior’s sermon, upon the mount; wherein he shows by a similitude, the firm estate of those that practice the things he taught them in this sermon: and the failing condition of those that only heard them, lest any should think they must be saved, because they had heard such an excellent sermon, and that from Christ’s own mouth.

*{whosoever heareth these sayings, &c.}* Luke 6.47.

*{unto a wise man}* The first thing that a wise builder goes about, is to find a good foundation to build his house on.

**V. 25. ***{founded on a rock}* By rain descending, &c. he means troubles and persecutions, like storms and tempests, trying the constancy of Christians: others understand it by the terrible day of judgment: by founded on a rock, he understands those that do God’s will, being built upon Christ, who is our rock and foundation, 1 Cor. 10.4. Ephes. 2.20.

**V. 26. ***{to a foolish man}* An unwise builder, that lays no good foundation, though he may build curiously in the sight of men.

**V. 27. ***{and it fell}* He that only hears, will fall short of salvation: for he is not founded on Christ the rock. Jam. 1.22. Rom. 2.13. Luke 11.28.

**V. 28. ***{the people were astonished}* Mark 1.22. Luke 4.32. See note thereon.

**V. 29. ***{as one having authority}* Not coldly as the scribes, but boldly as with infallibility of spirit, and authority from God. Tit. 2.15, or not as scribes grounding on the traditions of men: but delivering what he spoke as divine oracles. See 1 Cor. 2.4. 1 Pet. 4.11. Or, not as a subject or minister, but as a spiritual king in his own kingdom: and therefore he said, *ye have heard, but I say, &c.* Chap. 5.21. &c.

## CHAPTER 8

**Verse 1. ***{When he was come down}* And entered into one of the cities. Luke 5.12.

*{great multitudes}* Those that heard the former sermon, Chap. 5.1. &c. and others coming in to them before they came into the city, where the former miracle was wrought.

**V. 2. ***{And behold}* Mark 1.40. Luke 5.12.

*{worshipped him}* By falling on his knees first. Mark 1.40, and then on his face. Luke 5.12, a sign of humility.

*{Lord if thou wilt}* He dares not ask to be healed, but leaves it to Christ, to do as he pleased; when he had declared his affliction to him: note his modesty; thus is called asking: Luke 5.12, he obtained what he desired, verse 3. See the like in Hezekiah, Isa. 38.3.

*{thou canst make me clean}* This shows his faith: though his disease was incurable by men, yet he is confident that Christ can heal it.

**V. 3. ***{touched him}* The law forbade to touch a leper, lest other should be infected by them. Lev. 5.3. But Christ was above the law and healed him by touching him: the lepers’ touching others defiles, Christ’s touching him cleanses.

*{saying}* Not out of ostentation, to show what he could do, but out of pity to the leper, Mark 1.41, and to show his beneficence and readiness to do good.

*{I will}* It is true as you say; I confirm it by healing you.

*{be thou clean}* A word of the effectual command, as Gen. 1.3. Let there be light.

*{immediately his leprosy was cleansed}* An argument of Christ’s divine power, that could heal him suddenly by his word. See Luke 5.12. &c. There is nothing between the work and command of God; *quia in praecepto est opus*, says *Ambros. in Luc l. 5*, his will is his word, and his word, his work: *voluntas ejus mensura rerum est; sermo ejus finis est operis. Ambros. Hexam. l. 3. c. 2. init.* His will is the measure of things, his word is the end, or finishing of the work. Here we see that believers, in afflictions, *non patiuntur ad poenam, sed subeunt ad coronas, P. Chrysol. g. serm. 15*, do not so much suffer to be punished, as undergo to reward, and to be crowned.

**V. 4. ***{see thou tell it no man}* Make no stay to tell it or talk of it to any, but hie you to the priest; as Luke 10.4, or, do not publish abroad, what I have done for you. Chapter 9.30. He might seem by his miracle to be ambitious of fame; but thus he prevents that opinion: and because, in his state of humiliation, he would not be manifested before the due time. See Mark 1.44. &c.

*{that Moses commanded}* Levit. 14.4.

*{for a testimony unto them}* That they may believe, chap. 10.18. 24.14. Mark 6.11, or for a testimony against them; to render them inexcusable if they believe not: (as Mark 13.9. Gal. 5.3. Jam. 5.3) and if they should calumniate him as an enemy to the law. See Mark 1.44, 45.

**V. 5. ***{And when Jesus}* Luke 7.1.

*{there came unto him}* Not in his own person, but by the elders of the Jews, Luke 7.3. (As John spoke to Christ by his disciples, chap. 11.3.) So he is said to beseech him; that is, by those he sent to beseech him: it seems, he first sent, and afterward came unto him himself.

*{a Centurion}* A commander of a band of soldiers, consisting of an hundred men, or more.

**V. 6. ***{lieth}* *βέβληται* is cast down, verse 14, as sick men use on their beds; being so feeble and weak that they can neither go nor stand: specially, they that are taken of the palsy, which is a *παράλυσις* and dissolution of the nerves, leaving the disaffected no strength to move themselves.

*{at home}* This heathen man’s care of his servant, keeping him in his own house, and looking out for cure for him, is an example for us, to tender our sick servants.

**V. 7. ***{I will come}* Our Savior refused to go to the ruler’s house, being twice entreated by the ruler himself. John 4.47, 49, but here he offers to go, being but sent unto by the centurion; possibly the other thought Christ could not heal except he came to him, but this man was confident he could.

**V. 8. ***{I am not worthy}* The best men have lowest thoughts of themselves.

*{speak the word only}* See on verse 7, and on verse 3.
