**V. 9. ***{For I am a man under authority}* If I being a man under the command of others, yet having others under my command, can thus do; much more can you who have [absolute?] command; thus men should take occasion by their own [command?] over others, and their ready subjection to them, to consider [God’s?] sovereign authority over themselves: and be ready to obey him in [all?] things.

*{go, and he goeth}* Not questioning why and wherefore, but without delay doing that which is enjoined; so will this sickness be gone at your command.

*{he marvelled}* As man, and showed some outward sign of admiration to others: as God, he knew the faith of the centurion, for he gave it; as man he knew not all, and so he marveled at a faith so rare and commendable.

*{no not in Israel}* That is among the Jewish nation who had been continually instructed in the Scriptures testifying of Christ: hence it appears, that the centurion was an heathen man, though probably a proselyte. Luke 7.5.

**V. 11. ***{many shall come}* Our Savior takes occasion from the commendation of the centurion’s faith, to speak of the calling of the Gentiles, and rejection of the Jews.

*{from the east and west}* From all parts, as Isai. 12.12. & 43.5, an allusion made to Jacob, Gen. 38.14.

*{shall sit down}* A metaphor taken from banquets or feasts, where men of different conditions sit down together, Isai. 25.6. Matth. 22.2. Luke 22.30.

*{with Abraham, and Isaac, and Jacob}* The Jews scorn to eat with strangers now, but the time shall come when strangers shall enjoy eternal happiness with their holy ancestors, and they themselves shall be excluded.

**V. 12. ***{children of the kingdom}* As Chap. 9.15, who were born of those parents to whom the kingdom was promised: and by virtue of the covenant, Rom. 9.4, it had still belonged to them, had they not forfeited it by their unbelief and wickedness.

*{into utter darkness}* The darkness of hell. 2 Pet. 2.4. Jude verse 6, compared to prisons, which were oftentimes out of the city. Acts 12.10, meaning, they should be in a state most remote from heavenly joy and blessedness. This utter darkness which shall be to all that are shut out of heaven, the kingdom of light they shall have no comfort in it; as that darkness has, in which men at least may rest, or flee from dangers pursuing them; which in utter darkness cannot be, there shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth; that is, extreme horror of soul, grief and anguish of the damned, in regard of the good which they have willfully deprived themselves of, and the tortures and bitter sorrows into which they have cast themselves; out of which they have no hope of coming.

*{weeping}* Isa. 55.14. For their sorrow and pain.

*{gnashing of teeth}* A sign of envy at the felicity of God’s people, Psalm 112.10.

**V. 13. ***{go thy way}* Take no further care, your request is granted. So, 2 Sam. 14.8. Mark 5.34. & 7.29. & 10.52. Luke 7.50. & 8.48. John 4.50. & 8.11.

*{as thou hast believed, so be it done}* &c. Matth. 9.29. He shows in these few words, the admirable power and efficacy of faith, which brings it to pass, that these things which according to God’s word, we believe, we receive accordingly: this centurion believed that Christ, though bodily absent, could yet by his divine power, everywhere present, cure his sick; and according to that faith, which Christ had given him, he also did restore his servant: he only can repair who made us, see how readily Christ pours out his mercy, where he gives a capacity to receive it: he will hear the power of faith, Jam. 5.16. The reason why we so seldom obtain, is because few have faith.

*{in the self same hour}* John 4.53.

**V. 14. ***{And when Jesus}* Mark 1.29. Luke 4.38. Went directly from the synagogue to Peter’s house, for the apostle had not left all in their following Christ, so as to retain no interest, they were ready to have left all for Christ, if his honor and truth required it: until then there was no cause why they should neglect father, mother, family, &c. As far as the service of Christ permitted, they held their proprieties, and supplied the necessities of theirs. See John 21.2. &c.

*{into Peters house}* Which was in Bethsaida. John 1.44. Unless he were born there, and lived at Capernaum: or removed his dwelling after he was called to be an apostle, that he might the better attend on Christ: or else that at Bethsaida was his own house, and this at Capernaum his mother-in-law’s house, which he used to repair to, when he came thither, which is most probable: for the house here specified, appears to have been at Capernaum, verse 5. Mark 1.21, 29. That was still Peter’s house, though he truly professed, that he had left all: that is, the present use, but not the propriety.

*{of a fever}* Gr. *Of a burning fever*, Gr. *πυρέσσουσαν*, which is of *πυρὸς* fire, and the Latin word *febris*, of which our English makes fever, seems to come *a fervore* heat, which Luke 4.38, it is called a great fever, the more dangerous for any person in years: all this sets forth the divine power of Christ, to heal all diseases, however dangerous, or desperate, to man’s reason.

**V. 15. ***{He touched her hand}* After he was entreated to heal her, Luke 4.38, and had rebuked the fever, as one that had authority over it. Luke 4.39. *non stat infirmitas ubi auctor salutis assistit, Chrysol. serm. 18*, infirmity stands not, where the author of health assists.

*{and ministered unto them}* To show her thankfulness, and that she was perfectly cured. See John 5.8, 9. First, she arose, which being sore sick she could not: next, she prepared meat for them; that is a work of the healthy.

**V. 16. ***{when the even was come}* Mark 1.32. Luke 4.40.

*{possessed of devils}* This mischief of possession, it seems, was then very common among the malicious Jews: no wonder, where strife, envy, and malice are, that wisdom is devilish, James 3.14, 15. Where love is, there God is, for God is love, 1 John 4.8. Where malice is, the devil easily takes possession of his own: if Christ own his disciples by their mutual love, John 13.25, Satan must needs know the malicious and revengeful for his own.

*{with his word}* Not as the blasphemous Jews said, through Beelzebub, Chap. 12, but by his own word and power, as being Lord of all the creatures. See Psal. 106.9. Therefore the devil neither would nor durst stay, when he said Go, verse 9.

*{all that were sick}* Of all sorts, or all that came to him to be healed.

**V. 17. ***{That it might be fulfilled}* It was in a sort fulfilled, by his having compassion on us, manifesting a kind of participation with us, in all our bodily infirmities, Heb. 4.15, or by taking away bodily diseases from men, which was a type of healing their souls: but it was principally fulfilled, by bearing all the punishment due to our sins: so were we healed by his stripes. Isaiah 53.4. 1 Pet. 2.24.

**V. 18. ***{great multitudes about him}* So many, that he could not conveniently converse with them, or attend that which was necessary for himself or his followers, Mark 3.9, 10, 20, which seems to have occasioned the commandment following.

*{to the other side}* For Capernaum was situate on the lake of Tiberias.

**V. 19. ***{a certain Scribe}* See on Luke 20.1. Syr. *ספרא* *חד*, *sophro chad*, one lawyer, or a certain scribe, it seems they were one and the same. See Ch. 13.52. Luke 10.25. 1 Cor. 1.20. & Tit. 3.13.

*{whithersoever thou goest}* I will leave all to follow you.

**V. 20. ***{foxes have holes}* He does not say, Kings have palaces, but I have none; or rich men have houses and lands, to entertain their followers, but I have none: but, the foxes and birds have, &c. As if he said, you are deceived, who think of following me for temporal preferments, who possess nothing secular, no not so much as an house.

*{nests}* Places of abode, or shelter, in trees, or other convenient receptacles, as Psal. 84.3. & 104.17.

*{the Son of man}* I that live in mean repute, and a contemptible estate among men, *hoc nomen imposuit, quo se familiarius appellari voluit*, &c. *Augustine Honorato ep. 120*, he gave himself this name, by which he would be more familiarly called.

*{hath not where to rest his head}* Either he lived in an hired house, or lodged with his disciples. He does not forbid him to follow him, but secretly points at his carnal intention in such his tendering of service: as P. Chrysologus says, *serm. 19. non repulit volentem sed fingentem prodidit*, he repelled not one willing to follow him, but discovered a dissembler.

**V. 21. ***{said unto him}* After that Christ had called him, Luke 9.59.

*{suffer me first}* Before I follow you, and leave my family and domestic affairs, to perform this work of piety to my deceased father.

*{to go and bury my father}* Let me stay till my father be dead, that I may perform the last office to him, and then I will wholly attend on you.

**V. 22. ***{Follow me}* Christ called him to preach the Gospel, Luke 9.60, in which case, parents and all were to be left, Chap. 4.20, 22. Christ will have his servants prefer his commands, before all the most necessary businesses of this present world; he will not stay until the affairs thereof will give them leisure to serve him, who ought first to be served. See Matth. 6.33.

*{let the dead bury their dead}* Let them that are dead in sin, bury them that are dead for sin, 1 Tim. 5.6. It seems to be an allusion to the rites of priests and Nazarites, who were forbidden to be present, at the funeral exequies of the dead. Lev. 10.6. Num. 6.9. *non pietatis obsequium negat*, &c. *Chysol. 5. s.* He denied not the obsequie of piety, but preferred the service of God before human affections.

**V. 23. ***{when he was entered}* Though he gave commandment to go over, verse 18, yet the disciples took not shipping, till he was gone aboard himself.

**V. 24. ***{and behold}* Mark 4.37. Luke 8.23, and here verse 2.

*{the ship was covered with waves}* She shipped much water in those overgrown seas, insomuch that she was ready to founder, and sink down.

*{he was asleep}* To show the truth of the human nature, and the weakness of faith in his disciples.

**V. 25. ***{we perish}* Thus they cried out in their fear and affrightment, and by way of expostulation, as Mark 4.38. *carest thou not that we perish?* And therefore he justly checks them, verse 26.

**V. 26. ***{O ye of little faith}* As Matth. 6.30.

*{rebuked the windes}* To show his authority over them; lest they might think the sudden allay of the winds, was either natural or accidental: he commanded them to cease, that they might know it was by his extraordinary power: and to show himself the Lord of sea and winds, he spoke so in his disciples’ hearing, to assure them that all the creatures are subject to his command.

**V. 27. ***{the men}* Which sailed in the ship, or in the other vessels in company. Mark 4.36. Luke 8.23.

**V. 28. ***{When he was come}* Mark 5.1. Luke 8.26.

*{of the Gergesenes}* Some think them to be a remnant of the Gergashites mentioned Gen. 10.16. They are called Gadarenes by the other evangelists, either because the city had two names: or else both these cities stood near together on the lake of Tiberias, and the country was called sometimes by the name of the one, and sometime of the other. See on Luke 8.26. See *Joseph. de bel. Jud. l. 2. c. 20.*

*{there met him}* So great was Christ’s power over them that they were enforced to come before him, who else would willingly have run from him.

*{two possessed with devils}* Mark 5.2. Luke 8.26, mention but one. See on Luke 8.27. *Augustine, To. 4. de consens. Evangelist. l. 2. c. 24* resolves that the one mentioned was, *persona alicujus clarioris & famosioris, quem regio illa maximè dolebat*, &c. A more eminent and more noted person, whom that country specially bewailed.

*{out of the tombs}* Or, *from among the tombs*: they were made out of the cities, and so, that some men might hide themselves therein, see on Luke 8.27. These were solitary places, whither the devils carried the possessed from the society of men, to make them in their sudden appearing, formidable to the passengers occasioned to go by them.

*{exceeding fierce}* The devil fills them whom he possesses with cruelty.

**V. 29. ***{they cried out}* Before Christ said anything to them they felt his divine power conventing them before him, of whom they were much afraid.

*{what have we to do with thee?}* Why do you come here to us? See 2 Sam. 16.10. 2 Chron. 45.28. John 2.4.

*{Jesus thou Son of God}* This confession of the devils, is either out of fear, or malice, to make the world suspect that to be false, because Satan spoke it. It is a true saying, liars so bring it to pass, *ut nec vera dicentibus credatur. Jerem. ep. Iulian l. 1. ep. 2*, that none believe them when they speak truth.

*{to torment us before the time}* As if he had said, This is our time to torment others: why do you come to torment us before the day of judgment? Either they feared that Christ would now send them to hell to abide there; or it was a torment, in their account, to be cast out of those they possessed, as Luke 8.28, 29. The word *βασανίσαι* here used, signifies to examine by torment, as racking, torturing, &c. importing a very vehement and painful tormenting.

**V. 30. ***{a good way off}* On a hill, as Mark and Luke testify.

*{a herd of many swine}* Some think these people were of those whom the king of Assyria had placed in Israel long before; others that they were a kind of Greeks, having a mixed religion, and so kept swine: others, that there was a colony of Romans in those parts. See *Isa. Casaubon. exercit. 13.34*, and on Luke 8.32.

**V. 31. ***{the devils besought him}* They could not go into swine without his leave, much less into men.

*{suffer us to go, &c.}* When they cannot hurt men, they desire to hurt their goods, see Job 1.10, 11. The reason why they desired to destroy the swine, was, first, that they might be doing some mischief, though they could not do all they would: next that they might have occasion of tempting the worldly Gadarenes to blaspheme Christ, for the loss of their swine: and lastly, that they might affect the Gadarenes’ carnal hearts with dislike of the Gospel, which brought them such loss, as it here fell out.

**V. 32. ***{he said unto them, Go}* Christ granted the devil’s petition, who sometimes, and in some things, denies ours; to teach us to submit our wills wholly to his, who sometimes grants in justice, and sometimes denies in mercy.

*{when they were come out}* Out of the possessed men.

*{the whole herd of swine ran}* About two thousand swine, Mark 5.13.

*{ran violently down}* Being carried by the power of the spirits possessing them.

*{into the sea}* Into the lake of Genezareth.

*{and perished in the waters}* Christ thus justly punished them who were the owners of these beasts, knowing their hearts, who preferred them before their own salvation by Christ; as many now do their pleasures, which God therefore both in justice and mercy takes from them.

**V. 33. ***{they that kept them, fled}* For fear, being partly terrified with the sight, partly to tell the news in the city, and possibly to excuse themselves to their masters, lest they should think their servants’ negligence was the cause of that their loss: yet God’s providence so wrought therein, that Satan, who would ever destroy and hinder the propagation of the truth of Christ, was foiled by his own endeavor, and the power and goodness of Christ, made more public and manifest in this miracle.

**V. 34. ***{they besought him}* They offer no violence unto him: they knew they could not that way prevail against him, by overpowering him, who could command the very powers of hell.

*{depart out of their coasts}* Lest they should suffer any further loss by his being there: thus they preferred their swine before Christ, as Esau, a portion of meat before his birthright. Genes. 25.34. It had been a singular blessing that Christ vouchsafed to visit them, had they made a right use thereof for their salvation, but mark how impious minds abuse and embitter all God’s favors: secondly, observe what wretched petitions the blinded worldly men sometimes present to God; how many desire that, which if obtained, would prove a severe curse unto them?

## CHAPTER 9

**Verse 1. ***{Into a ship}* The ship which had newly brought him thither, chap. 8.23.

*{into his own city}* Capernaum; for as *Chrysost. hom. 30. in Matth.* and Theophylact say, Bethlehem brought him forth, Nazareth brought him up, and Capernaum was his dwelling place. Matthew 4.3. Thither he went to abide for a time, after he had been endangered at Nazareth. Luke 4.29. It is called his city, because he was very often there; after he had gone through all Galilee preaching. In the first year of his ministry, he settled in Capernaum; whence it is called of the evangelists, his city.

**V. 2. ***{And behold}* Mark 2.1. &c. Luke 5.17, but both anticipate and alter the order thereof: all agree in the substance. 1. The faith of those that brought the sick man. 2. Christ his answer. 3. The Pharisees’ calumny. 4. Christ his defense. 5. The miracle and effect thereof.

*{a man sick of the palsy}* Which is a disease well known of many, so laxating and dissolving the sinews, that besides the pain, it renders one unable to help or move himself; and it is rarely cured by men.

*{seeing their faith}* Theirs that took such pains to carry him to Christ, as his also that would so be brought: he saw their faith, as man, by the fruits of it; in that they brought a man so incurable, let him down through the roof of the house, and laid him before him; saying nothing, as not doubting but that he would cure him if he saw him in that misery, Mark 2.4. Luke 5.9. As God, he knew it of his own omniscience, without these forementioned effects: as he knew the thoughts of the scribes. Mark 2.8. Luke 5.22.

*{son, be of good cheer}* Be comforted, and doubt not of the recovery 21of your health, now your sins are forgiven you, which were the cause of your sickness.

*{thy sins are forgiven thee}* Which he best knew, who forgave them, as God, and interceded for him as the only Mediator between God and man, 1 Tim. 2.5. Hence it appears, that sin is the cause of all diseases and miseries. Secondly, that Christ is that only physician, who can cure body and soul. Lastly, that the condition of believers is blessed, in that they obtain more than they could ask or think; as this paralytical man here did, whom he therefore called son; because faith made him a son of God. John 1.12. So good is our God, that faith obtains, before it can perceive it has obtained: so he hears us before we call. Isaiah 65.24.

**V. 3. ***{certain of the scribes}* Luke mentions the Pharisees also; they commonly joined (as wicked men use to do) to oppose Christ. However, they did dissent among themselves in respect of contrary opinions, or other private interests.

*{said within themselves}* *ἑαυτοῖς* Syr. *בנפשהון* *benaphshehun* in their souls: that is, they thought within themselves; they did not only murmur, but secretly thought impiously, expecting the opportunity of censuring, when they were among other company. See on Mark 6. Psal. 14.1.

*{this man blasphemeth}* *βλασφημεῖν*, is as it were *βλάπτειν* *τὴν* *φήμην*, to hurt another’s fame or reputation: Suidas interprets *βλάσφημος*, *ὁ* *εἰς* *Θεὸν* *ἐξυβρίζων*, one who injures God with contumelious words, which is either when men detract from God his honor due; or attribute any evil to him. So they thought Christ derogated from God’s prerogative to forgive sins, and arrogated it to himself: it is indeed in God alone to forgive sins, Isaiah 43.25. This shows therefore that Christ is God; which they would not understand in all his miracles which they saw.

**V. 4. ***{knowing their thoughts}* See on verse 2. Thus also he showed himself to be God, by showing what they thought: they dissembled their calumny in their hearts, he saw it lurking there.

*{Wherefore think ye evil?}* Of me, as a blasphemer: he does not doubtfully object, as one that suspected their thoughts by some whispering, countenance, or outward gesture; but openly charges, and reproves them for the sin of their heart which he saw, which none but God could do.

**V. 5. ***{Whether is it easier to say}* You think it harder to heal the palsy, than to forgive sins: you shall know then, that I have power to forgive sins, by my healing this man with my word; it is indeed more to remit sin, than to heal a sick man, but not so easy to convince another, by a thing not seen, as by a work discernable by the eye.

**V. 6. ***{The Son of man}* See on Chap. 8.20.

*{power}* Gr. *Authority*. *ἐξουσίαν*, which signifies either power or authority, so Matth. 7.29. John 17.2, or jurisdiction. Luke 23.7. Right Mark 3.15, dignity, John 1.12.

*{on earth}* Though he abide here on earth, and live here as man, conversant with men, and in a state of humiliation.

*{take up thy bed}* That all may know that you are perfectly healed. See on Chap. 8.25.

**V. 7. ***{and he arose}* Suddenly and perfectly cured, taking up his bed, who before, could not move himself. Luke adds, glorifying God, as verse 8. Christ having proved by this admirable work, that he, as God, had power to remit sin, as well as to take away the most desperate effects thereof: all diseases, (had the spectators been constant to their own principles, that none can forgive sin but God only) they might hence come have to believe in him the Messiah and Redeemer, but envy and malice had blinded them so, that they could be constant in nothing but sin and error.

**V. 8. ***{they marvelled}* To amazement and fear, as Luke expresses it.

*{glorified God}* That is, they divulged the power and goodness of God, showed in that miracle, which is the principal end of miracles; that in consideration thereof, we should believe Jesus Christ to be the Son of God manifested in the flesh: which though all did not hear, who marveled and feared, yet doubtless some did; by whom the operation of the Holy Ghost in them, truly apprehended him to be their Savior and Redeemer.

*{unto men}* That is unto a man: for such Christ appeared unto them, though the power working these things, was confessed to be divine.

**V. 9. ***{And as Jesus passed}* Mark 2.14. Luke 5.27.

*{named Matthew}* He is called Levi in the other evangelists, he had two names.

*{sitting at the receipt of custom}* At the custom-house: it seems to have been by the seaside, Mark 2.13. It may be he was to receive the customs due from merchants and passengers: then due to, and imposed by the state of Rome.

*{and followed him}* He advises not with flesh and blood, saying, how shall I live, whither shall I go; how shall I bear the malice of the Pharisees, who hate Christ and all his followers, but goes after him instantly. Gal. 1.16. Luke says, he left all: that is, his books, and businesses of the customs and that course of life; wholly addicting himself to the service of Christ: we may not conceive that he deserted or gave away his estate, personal or real; for he afterward entertained Christ at his house: verse 10, but so used them, as his calling to Christ, and following him, would permit: so Peter, Andrew, James and John followed Christ: they left their ships and nets, and fishing, yet Peter still retained his house, Chap. 8.14.

**V. 10. ***{in the house}* In Matthew’s house, who made a feast for him. Luke 5.29.

*{many Publicans and sinners}* *Qui vectigalia redimebant, Publicani appellati*, &c. *Volateran. de Magistr. & sacerd. Roman.* They that were here together, seem to have been the friends and acquaintance of Matthew, whom he therefore invited, that they also might hear, and be won to Christ and salvation: some here understand two sorts of men; by publicans Matthew’s fellow-officers: and by sinners, those that were of notorious, bad and loose lives, or such as the Jews had cast out of their synagogues; as, John 9.22, 34, infamous persons among them. (Of publicans see on Luke 3.12.) Others think them to be all one, and the publicans were called sinners, because they were employed by the Romans to gather their tribute, and for the most part did it with much rigor and oppression of God’s people.

**V. 11. ***{When the Pharisees saw it}* It is not likely that the Pharisees would go into the house of a publican to see it; but either they heard Matthew invite him, and saw them going along to the house, or else they knew it by report from others.

*{said unto his disciples}* They still catch up any occasion to calumniate Christ. See on Luke 19.7. They durst not ask Christ, because they knew they were not able to deal with him, but say this to his disciples, to alienate their affections from their Master; seeing that by their tradition, they were not to eat or commerce with publicans or infamous persons.

**V. 12. ***{When Jesus heard that}* From his disciples, or overheard the Pharisees speaking to them.

*{They that be whole, need not a physician}* I must converse with these, because they are spiritually sick, and I am the physician of souls; they are sinners, I am their Savior: others think they have not so much need of me: *bonus medicus qui infirmitates nostras accepit. Ambros. l. 10. ep. 82*, he is the good physician, who took on himself our infirmities.

**V. 13. ***{go ye and learn, &c.}* You that glory in your knowledge, and will be the only teachers of all others, are yet ignorant of the main; therefore go learn, for shame.

*{I will have mercy}* Hos. 6.6. Chap. 12.7. That mercy is better than sacrifice, see Hebr. 13.16. God by the prophet upbraids them with their hypocrisy, who pretended to much sanctity in their sacrifices and offerings, when indeed, they had within them, neither love to God, nor charity to their neighbor: the prophet put *חסד* *chesed*, benignity, or beneficence: the Greek *ἔλεος* mercy: Hosea 6.6. Chapter 12.7. For beneficence is a sure effect of mercy.

*{and not sacrifice}* More, or rather than sacrifice, as it is expounded. Hos. 9. See the like manner of speaking, Gen. 32.28. Prov. 8.10. Luke 14.11. 1 Cor. 1.17. Chap. 6.19.

*{I am not come to call the righteous}* That is, *illos justos, qui in sanctitate generis, & literâ legis gloriantur. Paulinus Augustino. ep.* Those righteous, who glory in the sanctity of their ancestors, and the letter of the law. Such as you Pharisees, who think yourselves righteous, and all others, contemptible sinners: I come to convert such as have not yet repented; and how shall I do that, if I converse not with them: though they that are converted are infirm, yet have they not so much need of my presence, as those who are yet in impenitency, and so desperately sick in soul.

*{sinners to repentance}* 1 Tim. 1.15.

**V. 14. ***{The disciples of John}* Matthew names these only, because these were the speakers. Luke names the Pharisees only, because they set them on work, that they might disgrace Christ, by that authority which John had with the people; Mark names both, because both had hands in it.

*{why do we and the Pharisees}* Mark 2.18. Luke 5.33. They propose themselves as examples: and the sense is, why do not you fast, as well as we? As reprehending his neglect therein; we have our set-fasts, why join you not with us therein, see Zach. 7.5. Besides, the Pharisees enjoined their disciples weekly fasts, Luke 18.

*{but thy disciples fast not}* Either your way, or ours taught us by John is not right: but we must know that John and Christ had divers schools and disciples, but not contrary: for John was to prepare the way of Christ, and that John’s discipline was much more strict and austere, than Christ’s school could conveniently bear: therefore the Pharisees liked John’s austerity better than Christ’s gracious affability, and commerce with sinners: so that they could have found in their hearts to have received John as their Messiah. John 1. Luke 3. Therefore in this question about fasting, the Pharisees and John’s disciples join.

**V. 15. ***{Can the children of the bride-chamber}* *μὴ* *δύνανται*, or as Nazianzen *orat. 26* reads *οὐ* *δύνανται*, they cannot, that (says he) is called impossible, which is not reasonable, or convenient, 22which the interrogation implies: can the, &c. that is, do you think it reasonable, or fit? Children of the bride-chamber, after the Hebrew manner, are those called, which are most conversant and familiar with the bridegroom. See on Luke 20.36. The whole sense is; John Baptist, your master, taught you that I am the bridegroom, John 3.29, that this time of my living here on earth, is the time of my espousals to my church, Ephes. 5.25, 27. &c. My disciples are children of the bride-chamber: how can they mourn, until these feasting days be over? That is, the incomparable joy which they now have, in gathering a pure virgin church unto me, and eternal salvation, by preaching of the Gospel.

*{mourn as long}* Can they be in heaviness while I, their Savior am with them? Is it reasonable that they should, in the very time of great joy, which should be to all people. Luke 2.10. In the day of salvation 2 Cor. 6.2, the time of rejoicing foretold by the prophet, Isaiah 35.1, 2. Jer. 31.13. Joel 2.23. Zach. 2.10. Zach. 9.9.

*{when the bridegroom shall be taken from them}* When I shall be taken from them into heaven.

**V. 16. ***{a piece of new cloth}* Or *raw or unwrought cloth*: *ῥάκους* *ἀγνάφου* *panniculi impoliti. Budaeus.* Some interpret thus, as new cloth is not fit to be put upon the old, nor new wine into old cask: so it is not fit to teach this doctrine of the Gospel newly manifested to you and the Pharisees, who stand too much upon old traditions: others better thus, as the old garment cannot long bear a new piece, nor the old vessels new wine, because of their weakness: so my disciples cannot yet bear this austere discipline, till I am ascended up into heaven, and send down the gifts of my Spirit in a more plentiful measure upon them.

**V. 17. ***{new wine into old bottles}* See on verse 16. He uses this manner of similitude, that he might show that he separated *Evangelii novitatem à legis vetustate — sicut fructus separatur à semine*, the newness of the Gospel from the oldness of the law, — as the fruit is separated from the seed. *Tertul. l. 4. c. 11. advers. Marcion*, so indeed is the Gospel to the law; *diversum sed non contrarium*, divers, not contrary: the Gospel was in the law, as the corn in the blade; understood, but not yet appearing, until maturity in the fullness of time; the harvest of which Christ spoke, John 4.35.

**V. 18. ***{While he spake these things}* While the Pharisees endeavor to calumniate Christ, God gives him a double occasion to convince them, and manifest his divine power.

*{a certain ruler}* Mark 5.22. Luke 8.41. Jairus, whom they call *ἄρχοντα* *τῆς* *συναγωγῆς* a ruler of a synagogue. See on Luke 13.14.

*{worshipped him}* By falling down at his feet, Mark 5.

*{lay thy hand upon her}* His faith was short of the centurion’s, though he were ruler of a synagogue: the centurion desired Christ’s word only, see Chap. 8.8.

**V. 19. ***{Jesus arose and followed him}* Graciously complying with his weakness, who could as well have raised her from the dead, by his word only, absent as present.

**V. 20. ***{with an issue of blood twelve years}* She had been a long time sick, and as it appears by the other evangelists, had tried many physicians, and was now in a wretched condition, having no more means to consult others: yet Christ heals her.

*{came behind him}* Ashamed to confess her disease: we are ashamed of the effects, not of the cause: of misery, but not of sin.

*{touched the hemme}* This was an admirable effect of faith, and modesty *pium latrocinium*, an holy theft. *Chrysol. serm. 33*, not wronging the owner, but relieving the taker; Christ was not hereby injured, but the helpless woman cured.

**V. 21. ***{I shall be whole}* The physicians could not cure me, only a touch of the garment of Jesus can do it.

**V. 22. ***{turned him about}* That he might manifest this work of faith unto the people, that God might be glorified: and that not only Jairus’ weak faith might be strengthened, and he the more confidently look for the raising up of his daughter: but that we also may be assured, that he (who now sitting at the right hand of God, has not less power or mercy, than when he was here in the form of a servant) will also heal and help us, if we come unto him in faith: *non Christus infirmantibus, sed fides deest; Chrysol. 5. s.* Christ is never wanting to infirm, if faith be not.

*{be of good comfort}* See on verse 2.

*{thy faith hath made thee whole}* See on Chap. 8.13. Christ, by faith apprehended, cured her: so ’tis most true, we are saved by faith in Christ Jesus, not by any merit of our faith, which is the free gift of God, (Ephes. 2.8) as is also justification by faith, and salvation by Christ, yet Christ would not here say, I have cured you, but *thy faith hath made thee whole*, that he might confirm the woman in that faith, and teach all others the only way to obtain health and salvation, that is, by faith in Christ, Mark 5.34 adds, *go in peace, and be whole of thy plague*. See Luk. 7.47, 48.

*{from that hour}* She was presently cured, and never troubled with that sickness more.

**V. 23. ***{saw the minstrels}* It was an usual thing among Jews and Gentiles to have formal mourners, and also musicians in their funerals. Jer. 9.17. & 48.36. So said *Jerom ad Heliod. l. 2. ep. 24. Hesiodus natales hominum plangens, gaudet in funere*: Hesiod bewailing the nativities of men, rejoices in their funeral.

**V. 24. ***{not dead, but sleepeth}* She is not so dead, but that she shall immediately be raised to life again. See on Luke 8.52.

**V. 25. ***{when the people were put forth}* He put them forth because they derided him, verse 24, and therefore were unworthy to see the miracle done: or because he would not yet manifest himself by such works.

*{took her by the hand}* As we use to wake them that are asleep, by shaking them by the hand, and calling aloud to them.

*{and the maid arose}* As one out of sleep: so death itself yields to Christ’s divine power, who is the resurrection and the life, John 11.25. It was he that said, *O death, I will be thy death*, or *thy plagues, &c.* Hosea 13.14. In his death you die, but we live: we give you thanks, O Christ our Savior, who are your creatures; *quod tam potentem adversarium nostrum, dum occideris, occidisti: Jerom 5. s. ep. 24*, that dying, you have overcome this our so powerful enemy; though he raise us not up now, yet shall he at the last day: thus faith obtains, while flesh and blood laughs at the promises of Christ, and is put out from his blessed presence.

**V. 26. ***{the fame thereof}* Or, *this fame*, verse 31. The fame or report of this his raising Jairus’ dead child was noised about through all Galilee, every one strives to tell it for a marvelous and new thing, though he charged them not to divulge it. Mark 5.43. Glory, like the shadow in the sunshine, follows those that fly from it.

**V. 27. ***{when Jesus departed thence}* Christ incessantly does good: and every place afforded him opportunity of manifesting his divine power.

*{two blind men}* The circumstances show that this was not the story recorded, Mark 10.46. Luke 18.35, but another healing the blind, which he did sundry times.

*{followed him crying}* He answered them not, until he came into the house, verse 28, that that little delay might the more kindle their desire of obtaining, and leave a deeper impression in their minds of the favor received: we too soon forget that which we quickly obtain.

*{thou son of David}* Chap. 15.22. & Chap. 20.30, 31. Meaning the Messiah of the posterity of David under which title they usually spoke of their expected Messiah. See Chap. 1.1. For now Christ’s frequent and admirable miracles, began to work in men an opinion that he was the Messiah, Chap. 12.23. John 7.31. Matth. 20.30, 31.

**V. 26. ***{able to do this}* To open your eyes; for so Christ understood their petition presented unto him for mercy, verse 27, though they named not this particular favor.

**V. 29. ***{then touched he their eyes}* He could have restored them sight only by his word: but thus he pleased sometimes to do, that they might understand that he was tied to no means or manner, who ruled all the courses of nature, and could dispense with them as he pleased.

*{according to your faith}* See on Chap. 8.13.

**V. 30. ***{their eyes were opened}* That is, they could see: for the Jews called blind eyes, shut. John 9.26, 30. Thus was that fulfilled which was spoken, Psalm 146.8. Isai. 35.5.

*{he straitly charged them}* He forbade them with an earnest commination. So the word *ἐνεβριμήσατο* here used, signifies, as it were, *graviter iis fuit interminatus*, or, *adhibitis minis interdixit*, he forbade them with threatenings, if they made the work done on them known, as if it were said, *cum fremitu interminatus est*.

*{see that no man know it}* See verse 25. Either Christ would not have his miracle published yet, or not to the inhabitants of those parts: for all things are not to be published at all times, nor to all persons. See Chap. 8.4.

**V. 31. ***{But they}* Contrary to his charge they published it all abroad. They could not conceal so great and admirable a mercy of God received.

**V. 32. ***{as they went out}* Luke 12.14. See on verse 18, 27.

*{a dumb man possessed of a devil}* Either he was natively dumb; or made dumb by the devil possessing him: that so he might deprive him of the use of his tongue, which is man’s glory, and wherewith he honors God: the latter is most probable; because Christ is not said to loose his tongue, but only to cast out the devil; and then he spoke, verse 33.

**V. 33. ***{it was never so seen in Israel}* None of the former prophets ever did the like things.

**V. 34. ***{But the Pharisees said}* These learned men blaspheme Christ, when the ignorant people had more holy speeches, and more reverend opinion of him: so easily does malice embitter and overthrow all excellencies.

*{he casteth out devils}* Chap. 12.24. Mark 3.22. Luke 11.25. It was a work so evident, that they could not deny it; yet they calumniate the means and manner.

*{the prince of the devils}* There seems to be an order among devils (the authors of all disorder) they rob God of his glory, and accuse Christ under the notion of a magician, as if he had entered into covenant with the prince of the devils, and so gotten power to cast out inferior devils.

**V. 35. ***{And Jesus went about}* Mark 6.6. Luke 13.22. See on chap. 4.23.

*{in their synagogues}* They had synagogues in villages, as well as in cities: so Christians had churches. Concerning the word synagogue, see on Luke 7.5.

*{among the people}* He was not like those covetous physicians, careful only of rich patients, but negligent of the poor; he healed all.

**V. 36. ***{But when he saw the multitudes}* Mark 6.34.

*{moved with compassion}* On their souls; because they wanted instruction. The scribes and doctors of the law, and the priests should have taught the people; but either they neglected to instruct them, or teaching, with their false glosses infected them; serving their own ambition, gain, and vainglory, they little minded their charge, wherein God had made them watchmen over his Israel: therefore Christ pitied their sad condition, who wanted better teaching.

*{fainted}* Or *were tired and laid down*.

*{as sheep having no shepherd}* See Numb. 27.17. Scattered abroad not knowing where to get food, and exposed to many seducements, as people without a pastor. See 1 King. 22.17. They had teachers, but none that tended their good.

**V. 37. ***{the harvest truly is plenteous}* Luke 10.2. It pitied him to see so many Galileans, like corn, ready to be gathered to God, by faith and repentance, and such want of *harvesters*, that is, ministers, to bring them in, John 4.35. The people’s minds were stirred up to expectation of better teaching by Jesus’ preaching and miracles: this seems to be the occasion of sending the apostles abroad, chap. 10.15.

**V. 38. ***{that he will send forth labourers}* Word for word, *ἐκβάλῃ*, is cast forth, or as the Syriac interpreter has it *thrust forth laborers*; for we are very slow in so holy and important a work.

## CHAPTER 10

**Verse 1. ***{And when he had}* Mark 3.14. Luke 9.1. He called them unto him now in private, as Mark 3.13, as to assign them their mission and commission of going out to preach that which he had taught them; so further to instruct them, what he would have them to do, and how to carry themselves therein; that as he pitied the scattered people, so he might also relieve, and lead them by these, as faithful pastors of their souls.

*{called unto him}* Not to be his attendants and hearers now, for that he had done before, Luke 6.13, but having kept them with him for some time, to fit them for their public ministry, and seeing many of the people willing to be taught, he now sends them forth among the Jews, to instruct them, Luke 9.1, 2. Christ had many disciples; the chief whereof were the twelve apostles, whom he would first send out as spiritual harvesters.

*{he gave them power}* And authority. Luke 9.1.

*{against}* Or *over*; no king could give his ambassadors power over devils, neither could any of the prophets give this power to his scholars; this only the Lord of all the creatures could do.

*{all manner of sicknesses}* See on Matth. 4.23. That such miracles wrought by them might, as confirm them in the truth of that they preached, and cause them to avow the same with all boldness and confidence; so also to assure the hearers, that it was indeed the, infallibly, truth, to which God vouchsafed to give testimony by these signs, exceeding all power and art of creatures, and so appearing to be divine. See 1 King. 17.24.

**V. 2. ***{the names of the twelve Apostles}* They are called apostles, because they were first sent by Christ to preach the Gospel: their names God would have recorded for their honor, who having been instruments of so much good, must not be forgotten: as also that we may imitate them; there were twelve in allusion to the twelve patriarchs, and relation to the tribes of Israel, chap. 19.28. Luke 22.30. Revelat. 21.12, 14. See *Is. Casaubon. exercit. 14. N. 5.*

*{The first Simon}* Theophylact says, that Peter and Andrew are named the first *διότι* [*και*] *πρωτόκλητοι* because they were first called: and *Chrysostom, hom. 33. in Matth.* You see how that *οὐ* *κατὰ* *τὴν* *ἀξίαν* *τίθησι*, &c. They are not placed, or reckoned according to dignity: for he thought John greater than James, after whom he is named. See chap. 4.18.

*{who is called Peter}* This name Christ gave him, John 1.42. chap. 16.18.

**V. 3. ***{Matthew the Publican}* Of publicans, see chap. 9.10. *Cael. Rhodigin. lect. Antiq. l. 20. c. 29* says out of Firmicus. *erant (inquit.) Publicani aut vectigalarii &c.* Publicans, or customers, officers of the custom-house, or farmers of customs: *Alexand. ab Alexandr. Genial. dier. l. 2. c. 29* says (with Volateran, forecited, chap. 9, verse 10) they that farmed customs were called publicans: they are deceived who think all the publicans among the Jews were heathens. See *Is. Casaubon. Exercit. 13.71, 73.* As also they who think it was, or is a contemptible office; it is true, the proud Jews despised them, as unholy and wicked, because they gathered customs and tribute of them, who were impatient of any subjection. But these were anciently among the Romans, men of good account; as may appear by many speeches of Cicero. Alexander ab Alex., forecited tells us, the chief of the order of knighthood, were in times past publicans, and the flower of the Roman knights, was contained in the order of publicans: it is true that afterward this office was conferred on manumissed servants, and meaner men: and that among the Jews these men were in disgrace: whence some observe his humility, in that though he were now an apostle, yet he tells what he had been; so does Paul 1 Tim. 1.13.

*{James the son of Alphaeus}* To distinguish him from the other James, the son of Zebedee, and the brother of John, Matth. 4.21. Mark 3.17. (Surnamed Boanerges, or sons of thunder) he was slain by Herod, Act. 12.2, but this James is he who was called, James the less, Mark 15.40, and the brother of the Lord, Galat. 1.19, because he was the son of Mary Cleopha, the Virgin Mary her sister: his wife was also named Mary, as Jo. Drusius thinks.

*{Lebbaeus}* Called also Judas, the brother of James, Luke 6.16, who writ the Epistle of Jude, as his brother did that of James.

**V. 4. ***{Simon the Canaanite}* Or *Canite*, that is *Zelotes*, Luke 6.15. This is added to distinguish him from Simon Peter, verse 2.

*{Judas Iscariot}* A man of Kerioth in the tribe of Judah, Josh. 15.25, or of Issachar, Judg. 10.1, or a man of reward, for he betrayed Christ for money, chap. 26.15. By this epithet and the words following, he is distinguished from the other Judas. See on verse 3. See also on John 6.71.

**V. 5. ***{into the way of the Gentiles}* Not yet, till after my death, then you must go preach to them, Act. 1.8, when the middle wall of partition shall be broken down (Ephes. 2.14) and the time of calling the Gentiles shall be come.

*{of the Samaritans}* A people mixed of Assyrians and Israelites, 2 King. 17, and hated of the Jews, (John 4.9) whom Christ would not have his apostles now offend, lest that might cause the weak elect among the Jews, to stumble, or dislike that religion which the Samaritans would receive: so God’s holy providence disposed lest anything of itself good, might, by reason of man’s weakness, prove an occasion of evil.

**V. 6. ***{But go rather}* You must first preach to the Israelites; of whom I come, and to whom I was promised, Act. 3.29. & 13.46, and principally sent, chap. 15.24.

*{to the lost sheep}* They who belong to God’s election, though yet they continue in their natural condition: he calls them sheep, *quos Deus olim pascebat*, whom God of old did feed, Psal. 80.1, but lost, because now dispersed and destitute of sound teachers. Euthym.

**V. 7. ***{preach}* Cry aloud, or proclaim like heralds, as Isaiah 58.1.

*{the kingdom of heaven is at hand}* Luke 10.9. Teach the same doctrine that John Baptist, and I have done before you. See on Matth. 3.2. & 4.17.

**V. 8. ***{heal the sick}* Confirm the doctrine you preach by these profitable miracles, which I now give you power to do.

*{Freely give}* Make no merchandise of the gift of miracles, freely bestowed on you: take no money for the cures you do, neither withhold the power I give you, whensoever you meet with occasion to do good therewith: for indeed, all earthly things, *servando totaliter amittimus, sed benè largiendo servamus. Greg. hom. 4. fin.* By keeping, we utterly lose, but by well bestowing we keep them: so is it with these gifts bestowed on us for others’ sakes.

**V. 9. ***{Provide neither}* Mark 6.8. Luke 9.3, or get neither, take none with you for this journey, that nothing may hinder you, and that you may have experience of God’s special providence over you: therefore he asks them, Luke 22.35, what they wanted by the way.

*{gold nor silver}* No money of greater or lesser value to buy provision: Christ forbade them not to possess that which was their own, but would not now have them solicitous for any money to bear their charge, lest they should not have been duly sensible of God’s special providence, which is never so well perceived as in the removal and absence of all ordinary means: here is an excellent document against avarice; no man is born rich, nor lives so poor as he was born: poverty (says *Ambros. l. 10. ep. 8.2*) is not of nature, but opinion: why do we desire an heap of riches, as if it were necessary? *nihil tam necessarium quàm cognoscere quod non sit necessarium*: there is nothing so necessary for us, as to know what is not necessary.

*{in your purses}* *εἰς* *τὰς* *ζώνας* — in, or into girdles; either because they used to have purses in their girdles, or because (as Euthymius says) travelers used by the way to carry their purses, *Zonis appensas* hanged on their girdles: Alexander Severus said, the soldier fears not but only when he is (well) clothed — and having *aliquid in Zonula*; some money in his girdle, that is, in his purse. *Ael. Lamprid.*

**V. 10. ***{nor scrip}* To put your provision in for your journey; 24such as travelers used to carry victuals in for their journey: knapsacks soldiers call them. Now he would have them in their journeys depend wholly on God’s providence, lest in the ordinary means, they might find some obstruction, and hindrance to their duty of preaching the Gospel; otherwise, this neither was, nor is to be taken for a prescript constantly binding.

*{two coats}* Two upper coats for change; and so of shoes, for he allowed them sandals, Mark 6.9.

*{nor yet staves}* For defense or offense: for they might carry a walking-staff for more ease or convenience. Mark 6.9.

*{the workman is worthy}* Luke 10.7. 1 Tim. 5.18. God who employs you, will give you food, if men of whom you deserve it, should neglect you.

*{of his meat}* That is all manner of necessary provisions.

**V. 11. ***{Into whatsoever city}* Luke 10.8.

*{enquire}* Of the inhabitants before you put yourselves into any man’s house; or try who is worthy.

*{who in it is worthy}* That is, who fears God, who is fit to have the Gospel preached unto him, and to have the honor to receive the ambassadors thereof.

*{till ye go thence}* Remove not from one house to another, that you may not offend him who first received you: or that you may not seem to wander from house to house, as vagrants, or give men suspicion that those were weary of you, by whom you were first entertained: tarry in that house, till you go to preach to another city.

**V. 12. ***{Salute it}* By wishing all happiness to the inhabitants thereof, as Luke 10.5.

**V. 13. ***{if the house be worthy}* If they courteously entertain you, like sons of peace, Luke 10.6, this is the token intimated verse 11, such a token of discovery Eliezer prayed for, Gen. 24.14, 18, 19.

*{let your peace come upon it}* Preach the Gospel of peace unto them; or it shall come upon them: as, Luke 10.6, that is, God will bring upon them all the good you wish them.

*{if it be not worthy}* If they show themselves unworthy to have the Gospel preached unto them, by refusing you who are the ministers thereof. Act. 13.46.

*{let your peace return unto you}* Carry away the Gospel of peace with you, to preach it to others, that shall receive you more kindly, or, God will bring that good on you, that you wished to them. See Psal. 35.13.

**V. 14. ***{Whosoever shall not receive you}* Mark 6.11.

*{shake off the dust of your feet}* As a token that God will shake them off, and destroy them: see Nehem. 5.13, or to signify that you will have no more to do with them: as, Act. 13.51. God accounts the reception of his ministers, as if himself were received: the good God, says *Salvian. de gub. l. 8*, makes one and the same honor or contumely common to himself and his servants: lest any man should think when he hurts a minister, *hominem tantum à se laedi*, that he hurts man only: without doubt the injury of God himself is mingled with the injury of his servants: God so witnessing, Zach. 2.8. *He that toucheth you, toucheth the apple of his eye.*

**V. 15. ***{it shall be more tolerable}* Gross sinners that never heard the Gospel, are not in so bad a condition, as they that refuse it, chap. 11.24. See 1 Pet. 2.21, and *Salv. 5. s. l. 4.* They do nothing in contempt of God’s law who know it not: if he say the Sodomites were less damnable than they who neglect the Gospel, what may they hereafter expect, who contemn it, and the faithful ministers thereof?

*{in the day of judgement}* Of the general account, and great assizes of the world, whereof, Act. 17.31.

**V. 16. ***{behold, I send you}* Luke 10.3. I shall send you after my resurrection, for in this journey they had neither want, nor opposition. Luke 22.35. It is likely that our Savior being now to send them forth among the Jews, intermixed divers things, that concerned their sending forth after his resurrection, as chap. 24, speaking of the destruction of Jerusalem, he adds divers things concerning the end of the world.

*{as sheep}* Altogether unarmed. He seems here to relate to that which was said, ver. 10. There is no creature more harmless, meek, or unable to defend itself, than the sheep: so were the apostles, unarmed, innocent, neither revenging injuries, nor able to protect themselves from them, but wholly depending on God’s providence.

*{in the midst of wolves}* Into great dangers, where you shall be compassed about with wicked men. See Psal. 57.4. Act. 20.29. Ezek. 22.27. Zeph. 3.3. Matth. 7.15.

*{wise as serpents}* See Gen. 3.1. Avoid dangers as far as you lawfully may: and give no just cause to men to persecute you.

*{harmless}* Or *simple*: be like serpents in the one, who avoid dangers by flying and hiding themselves; and like doves in the other, who do nothing to provoke: have the simplicity of the dove (says *Jerom lib. 2. ep. 16. Paulino*) that you may not machinate and imagine deceit against any man; and the wisdom of the serpent, lest other men’s plots supplant you: for matter of the fault there is not much difference, *vel decipere posse, vel decipi Christianum*, whether a Christian can deceive, or be deceived. Christ would have his, wise and innocent; religion and wisdom may not be severed. Lactantius says well, *lib. 1*, neither may any religion, without wisdom be received, or any wisdom approved without religion: *ib. l. 4. cap. 2, 3, 4.* Yet of the two, it is more secure to want wisdom, than innocency, as Tertullian says *adv. Valent. cap. 2. errare quam fallere*, it is better to err for want of wit, than deceive with too much. The wisdom of the world is eccentric to God’s, therefore the apostle says, 1 Cor. 3.18, *if any man among you seemeth to be wise in this world, let him become a fool, that he may be wise*, that is, says *Salvian. lib. 6. si vult esse sapiens, sit bonus; quia nemo verè sapiens, nisi vere bonus*; if he will be wise, let him be good; because there is no man truly wise, who is not truly good. Let the world count it foolishness, as 1 Cor. 1.18, or madness, as Act. 26.24, it shall appear at last to be the only wisdom. Let the impious Pharaoh say, *Let us deal wisely* — Exod. 1.10. All the depth of Satan can never make wickedness any better, than the most pernicious foolishness.

**V. 17. ***{beware of men}* Here it appears, that by wolves he meant cruel men: beware of those men that are like wolves, verse 16, bloodthirsty persecutors.

*{in their synagogues}* Some think that the synagogues were public places of meeting, as well for civil, as sacred affairs: others, that they did use to scourge men for offenses in matters of religion in their synagogues: others, that they had two sorts of synagogues, and did pray in the one, and scourge in the other: but concerning the last of these, we may more probably conceive, that they had their consistories, or council-houses wherein they executed the degrees of their council in way of discipline. See Act. 5.40. 2 Cor. 11.24.

**V. 18. ***{before governors and kings}* Such was Pilate, and after Felix, Festus, Act. 23.24, 25. Herod, Agrippa, Nero, and many succeeding persecutors of Christians. See chap. 27.2. Act. 23.24, 25. Act. 12.25, 26.

*{for my sake}* You need not be discouraged; seeing it is not for evil, but for your religion that they persecute you, Matth. 5.11, 12.

*{for a testimony}* I could keep you as easily from those dangers, as I will certainly keep you in them; but that you shall herein honor God, in testifying his truth, and leave your persecutors inexcusable.

*{against them, and the Gentiles}* You shall be persecuted both by Jews and Gentiles: to the one you shall be witnesses for their just rejection, who so judge themselves unworthy of eternal life, Act. 13.46, 47, to the other of the truth, by you constantly professed unto the death.

**V. 19. ***{but when they deliver you up}* Mark 13.11. Luke 12.11. When they shall convent you as malefactors, before magistrates, be not dismayed by their power or your own disability to plead for yourselves; God will then enable you.

*{take no thought}* Use no anxious and laborious preparation as orators and lawyers do, when they are to plead.

*{it shall be given you}* That is, to you apostles. See the like, Exod. 3.11.

**V. 20. ***{it is not ye that speak}* Not so much you, as the Spirit of God in you, dictating what you shall speak, as 2 Sam. 23.2. See also chap. 9.13. *neque enim & hoc ita fit de nobis, tanquam nihil facientibus nobis. Aug. ep. 105.*

**V. 21. ***{and the brother}* Luke 21.16. You shall be so hated for the truth of religion, that all bands of nature shall be forgotten, verse 35.

**V. 22. ***{hated of all men}* That is, of very many, or almost all, John 3.22. Phil. 2.21. Luke 21.17. Neither may this anything discourage us from speaking the truth, because it begets us hatred, but rather cause us to rejoice in our conformity to Christ: *Gratias ago Deo meo quod dignus sim, quem mundus oderit. Jerom. l. 1. ep. 45.* I thank my God that I am worthy to be hated of the world.

*{he that endureth to the end}* Mark 13.13. To the end of this mortal life, the limit and end of all our sufferings, Revel. 14.13, to the death, that calmest haven, where no breath of evil shall move us Revel. 2.10. & 22.3. The meaning here is, he that endures patiently, and with an unbroken mind, firmly relying on God for deliverance: it is not the beginning, but the end which crowns actions. See chap. 24.13.

*{shall be saved}* You cannot otherwise be saved, 1 Cor. 10.31. Do all to God’s glory, that is, do nothing to his dishonor. The constant perseverer to the end of trials, shall be saved, and be crowned as the most happy victors: they shall receive the end of their faith, the salvation of their souls, 1 Pet. 1.9. 2 Tim. 2.5. Rev. 2.10. Though God permit them power to put you to death, you that shall die for the testimony of the Lord Jesus, shall, even in death be saved, and crowned with martyrdom: he cannot perish who dies for Christ’s sake. The safety of this present life is never secure, nor can be long: neither is it entire or true, if out of Christ the life; the hour of death is ever imminent in this whole life, and must once come, and is ever 25coming on. But in the life to come there shall be perfect security, eternal blessedness, no more fear of death, — *there shall be no more curse* — Rev. 22.3.

**V. 23. ***{flee into another}* Christ gives liberty for flight, in case of persecution; partly for their own safety, and partly also for the further propagation of the Gospel into other places. See Act. 13.50, 41. & 14.5, 6, 7. & 8.4. Christ taught us this, not only by precept, but also by practice. In his infancy he was carried into Egypt: so also afterward, often hiding from persecutors, says Athanasius *apol. de fugâ suâ ad Imper. Constant. For it was meet that he should as well demonstrate by his hiding and fleeing, that he bare our flesh, and became man, as by hungring, and thirsting, and suffering*: see that whole apology, To. 1, as also Augustine on the same argument, *epist. 180*. Tertullian also wrote a book to Fabius concerning flight in persecution, though less orthodox. Augustine’s caution is here very requisite, *Ne quis temerè stationem deserat, nec gregem perfidè prodat, vel exemplum ignaviae praebeat*; lest any man should rashly desert his charge, betraying his flock, or give example of laziness: we may not flee without imminent danger, nor like false hirelings, for fear of the approaching wolf, leave our flocks to seducers: we may flee, where we are persecuted, and pursued to our destruction: so Paul escaped at Damascus, Act. 9.24. So fled other holy men in like cases: if our flock be all dispersed by persecution, we may also flee to secure our lives; lastly, if the church consent to our withdrawing, and they have others able to officiate for us: in such like cases not to flee is to tempt God.

*{ye shall not have gone over}* You shall not end or finish your preaching the Gospel to all the cities of Israel: this shows that they were not to flee only to secure themselves, but to hasten their work, to teach all the cities of Israel: or the meaning may be, you shall have some cities left to flee unto, and where you may preach.

*{till the Son of man be come}* This coming of the Son of man (that is Christ) is not to be understood of his incarnation, expected of old by the faithful, neither his manifestation to Israel: that was past, and this also performed by John Baptist, Joh. 1.31, but of the manifestation of his kingdom, whose person was already presented, and so manifested to Israel. This is to give them comfort in dangers, in assurance of his protection, according to that he says, Joh. 14.18. *I will not leave you comfortlesse; I will come unto you*: and Matth. 28.20. *I am with you alwayes, even unto the end of the world*; that is, to assist you (and your successors) in your ministry, and (as may stand with God’s glory) to deliver you from, or in dangers. See on chap. 16.28. They did not flee from city to city before, but after his resurrection: see on ver. 16. Act. 8.1. & 21.1.

**V. 24. ***{the disciple is not above his master}* Luke 6.40. John 13.16. & 15.20. If they persecute me your Lord and Master, you that are in a lower condition must not look to escape: the sufferings of Christ must teach us patience: *Jerom. ep. 45. lib. 1*, some talk that I am wicked: I being a servant own the title of faith: the Jews also called my Lord Jesus, magician: it is a great honor, and undoubted sign of goodness, to have the wicked revile and rail at us: they should condemn themselves, if they should speak well of any unlike themselves; their censures are your praises: but their praises call your goodness into question.

**V. 25. ***{Beelzebub}* It was the idol of the Ekronites, 2 King. 1.3, called so, either because there were store of flies in his temple, about the blood, and beasts sacrificed: or else because the people troubled with their swarms, sought to him for help: afterwards, as idols were called devils, Deut. 32.17. So the Jews called the chief devil Beelzebub, in detestation of this idol, Matth. 12.24, or it may signify a dunghill god, &c. See on Luke 11.15.

**V. 26. ***{for there is nothing covered}* Mark 4.22. Luke 8.27. & 12.2. Fear not what they can inflict upon you, for your innocency shall be made known here, or hereafter, Psal. 37.6, or fear not, but preach the Gospel freely, for it will be known and prevail in the end: this sense agrees best with the following words.

**V. 27. ***{in darkness}* What I teach you in private, when others are absent, as they use to be in the night: therefore he adds, *in the ear*, to show that he spoke of private instruction.

*{upon the house tops}* Openly, as in the highest places: for they could walk on their house tops, Deut. 22.8. Matth. 24.17. Act. 10.9, and from thence, publish things to multitudes below: they must publicly preach, not as the Valentinians, who cunningly veiled that they taught, lest the absurdity thereof should have appeared.

**V. 28. ***{fear not}* Fear not man more than God: or so, as to do any evil, either for fear of man (who can only take away a soon-fading life) to do that which God forbids, or to leave undone that which he commands: let us constantly remember that of *Cyprian. l. 2. ep. 6. qui pro nobis mortem semel vicit, semper vincit in nobis*: he that once overcame death for us, ever overcomes it in us: and in spite of persecutors, will raise us from death. See Act. 4.19. & 5.29. Dan. 3.16, 17, 18.

*{them which kill the body}* Luke 12.4. Though tyrants be never so cruel, they cannot hurt you so much for preaching the Gospel, as God can for not preaching it. Seeing therefore the dispensation thereof is committed unto you, and a woe pronounced against you if you preach not: sanctify the Lord your God, and let him be your fear, and your dread; but let not the fear of man shut your mouths, or deter you from your duties; but, whatever men design, threaten, or practice, to the contrary; preach the truth of Christ confidently.

*{hell}* He does not say, to destroy the soul and body simply or absolutely, so that they should be no more, (for that many men would be contented withal, rather than to run the hazard of present persecution) but to punish them eternally in hell, where the worm never dies; where it would be an unspeakable advantage once to die, that they might not ever live to torment.

**V. 29. ***{Are not two sparrows}* He does not instance God’s providence in the prophets, or famous men; lest they or we, should think God only took care for the excellent, but not for us; but in sparrows, little birds, of small price and account.

*{sold for a farthing}* The fourth part of our penny, but with them the tenth part of a Roman penny.

*{not fall on the ground}* When they are shot: nor can the most powerful tyrants kill you without God’s permission: some interpret it, a sparrow cannot light on the ground.

*{without your Father}* Without his permission, who governs all things in heaven and earth: fear not therefore, he will be more careful of you his children, than of the birds. See on chap. 5.45, and 6.32.

**V. 30. ***{but the very hairs}* See 2 Sam. 14.11. Act. 27.34. Not your hearts, hands, feet, and those parts which are (as they say) the *partes integrantes*; but even those which seem to have been made rather for convenience and ornament, than necessity: if God’s providence be over these, how much more will he preserve unto you your essential parts, and that, without which you cannot live?

*{all numbered}* Every one, not only known to God, but so kept, as it were by tale, that none can take away one of them without God’s permission; much more are the days of your life numbered: and not one of them, without God’s permission, can be taken away from you by the most powerful of the creatures.

**V. 31. ***{Ye are of more value}* More excellent creatures, of more esteem with God, as bearing his image, and redeemed with the precious blood of his holy Son Jesus; fear you not, he will not neglect you. See chap. 6.26.

**V. 32. ***{Whosoever therefore}* Luke 12.8.

*{shall confess me before men}* What more equal, than that servants which acknowledge and obey their masters in time of danger, should be owned by them in times of safety and honor: and that such as forsake their masters in troubles and dangerous conditions, should afterwards be turned out of doors? Thus will I deal with you, if you stand to me in times of persecution. I will own you publicly at the day of judgment, otherwise I will discard you.

**V. 33. ***{but whosoever}* See on verse 32, and on Luke 12.9. Mark 8.38. Luke 9.26. 2 Tim. 2.12.

**V. 34. ***{Think not that I am come}* See on Luke 12.51.

*{to send peace on earth}* He endeavors to correct the erroneous opinion of the Jews, and also the apostles, who thought that the times under the Messiah should be peaceable, prosperous, and glorious, misunderstanding the prophets, as if the things they spoke of spiritual peace with God, had been meant of worldly peace with men.

*{but a sword}* War, and divisions; as Luke 12.51. These are not proper fruits of the Gospel of peace; but accidental: while they, who will not be reclaimed from their errors, dissent from them that will, Acts 28.24, 25, 29. John 7.40. &c.

**V. 35. ***{against his father}* The times shall be as unquiet, as those spoken of, Mich. 7.6.

*{the daughter in law against, &c.}* No bands either sacred or civil shall restrain wicked persons from exercising their malice and cruelty against the professors of the Gospel. See verse 21.

**V. 36. ***{of his own house}* As if he said, such confusion shall come upon the world, that all laws of God and nature shall be trodden down: no humanity or fidelity shall be left among the impious: not that this should be at all times; but to arm the faithful, by forewarning them of these evils to come. See on verse 21.

**V. 37. ***{he that loveth father or mother}* See on Luke 14.26.

*{more than me}* As fear of evil, so likewise love of the world, and those things thereof, which are respectively to be loved (as parents, wife, children) may distract their affections from Christ: this is here forbidden.

*{is not worthy of me}* I count him not fit for my service. See on chap. 3.8. Luke 14.26.

*{he that loveth son or daughter}* This is added as a greater trial, because love rather descends than ascends; and some can more easily part with parents than children.

**V. 38. ***{he that taketh}* Chap. 16.24. Luke 9.23. Mark 8.34. 26That is, who does not willingly bear what God lays on him: it is an allusion to such as were condemned to be crucified, who used to carry their crosses. See on chap. 27.32.

*{his crosse}* That is yet a greater trial than before: a man may possibly be contented to lose worldly comforts, that would not endure pain (Job 2.4, 5), especially death, and such a kind of death, as was accursed: but we must suffer anything for Christ’s sake. By cross, is meant the heaviest and sorest afflictions and calamities, by alluding to the customs of Romans or Persians with whom that punishment was used and accounted most ignominious.

*{and followeth after me}* And with such an affection comes unto me: or is contented, if he be thereto called, to be crucified.

*{is not worthy of me}* See on verse 37.

**V. 39. ***{he that findeth his life}* John 12.25, that saves it, chap. 16.25. Mark 8.35. Luke 9.24, and 15.24, 32. So Prov. 24.10. He that thinks he has secured his life, by renouncing his religion: it is called finding, because worldly men account them all lost, who are dead.

*{shall lose it}* Shall go to hell, and so lose all comfort of life, as Tertullian said *adv. Gnost. c. 11. Perdet in Gehennam, qui se putat negando lucrifacere eam*.

*{he that loseth his life for my sake}* That dies for the testimony of the truth of religion, chap. 5.11, 12.

*{shall find it}* In heaven forever, as Tertullian also says, *Perdet eam ad praesens, qui confessus occiditur, sed inventurus eam in vitam aeternam*, he shall lose his life for the present, who confessing is slain, but shall find it to eternal life.

**V. 40. ***{he that receiveth you}* Luke 10.16. John 13.20. That hears your doctrine willingly, and entertains you: this is added to comfort them in distresses; for therein some should receive them, and relieve them.

*{receiveth me}* He shall have more divine guests. See Hebr. 13.2, me and my Father. See on John 14.23. He shall receive me, whose apostles and messengers you are; whose embassy you are to deliver: I will take all that which is done to you, on mine account, and as done to mine own person: as a prince esteems that which is done to his ambassadors, as if it were done to his own royal person, by them represented (as in the example of David and Hanun appears, 2 Sam. 10.2, 4. &c.) so will it be between Christ the King of kings, and men, honoring, relieving, abusing, harming, or persecuting the ministers, or any other servants of his. See Matth. 25.40.

*{in the name of a prophet}* Not for kindred or civil respects, but because he is God’s messenger.

*{shall receive a prophets reward}* Such a reward as a prophet shall have: or, as is fit for him that has received a prophet, and so furthered God’s service in an extraordinary manner.

*{a righteous man}* A man fearing God; a religious man, Matth. 13.17.

**V. 42. ***{whosoever shall give to drink}* Mark 9.41.

*{unto one}* How much more, if he give, and be beneficent to many?

*{of these little ones}* Who newly made profession of the true religion: and were as babes in it, Mark 9.42. Luke 17.2. Matth. 25.40, 45. 1 Cor. 3.1. 1 John 2.12, 14.

*{a cup of cold water only}* The least and cheapest courtesy, that can be showed: how much more if he provide with greater charge to relieve him?

*{he shall in no wise}* He shall be sure of it.

*{his reward}* The phrase is altered; he said before, the reward of a prophet, and of a righteous man, verse 41. Here he does not say, the reward of a little one, lest it should be thought to be a small reward; but, his reward: that is, not a reward proportionable to his work, and affection to the cause of God, (see chap. 25.34. &c. Luke 14.14) but incomparably more, because God is infinite in mercy and beneficence, and so the reward which he will freely give at last, must needs exceed all proportion of man’s service done for his sake, and shall be infinitely more than all that which we can ask or think: hence it appears how dear Christ’s servants are to him, in that for the least office of love and kindness done to the meanest of them, he will bountifully, and eternally requite and reward it; in which his bounty excels the beneficence which all the greatest of this world’s princes can show: they can only reward deserts with temporal gratuities and goods, which are not always such to the receivers, neither long constant to any: but God’s bounty makes the receivers good, and so happy, because eternally so.

## CHAPTER 11

**Verse 1. ***{Of commanding his twelve disciples}* Of giving them precepts how to carry themselves in their journey and preaching, chap. 10, and had sent them away furnished with those instructions.

*{in their cities}* In the cities of Galilee. See chap. 9.35, and 12.9.

**V. 2. ***{John had heard}* By his disciples, as Luke 7.18.

*{in the prison}* *ἐν* *τῷ* *δεσμωτηρίῳ*: the word comes of *δεσμόω*, *ligo, vincio*, to bind, and signifies the place where prisoners are bound, or kept in ward. Suidas interprets it by *φυλακὴ*, a keep, or prison: Syr. *בית* *אסירא*, *in domo vinctorum*. So the Hebrews said, *בית* *הסהר*, *beth hassohar*, Gen. 39.20, in the house of the prison, or the place where the king’s prisoners were bound. So Judg. 16.21 *בבית* *האסורים*, *bebeth haasirim*, in the house of the bound, that is, the prison. John was cast into prison, because he reproved Herod [unclear]. See Mark 6.17.

*{the works of Christ}* That is, his miracles, [of] which verse 4, 5.

*{he sent two of his disciples}* Not for his own information, who was assured by God’s Spirit, and a sign from heaven that Jesus was the true Messiah, John 1.33, and bare record thereof, John 1.29, 34, but to instruct his disciples, who, out of zeal to their master, stood ill affected to Christ, as appears, John 3.26. chap. 9.14, and very likely did not now acquaint John with Christ’s great works, out of love to him; but out of envy and respect of their master’s credit.

**V. 3. ***{and said unto him}* By his disciples whom he sent, for John was now in prison, 2 verse. See chap. 8.5, 6.

*{art thou he that should come}* The Messiah promised in the prophets, and expected by the Jews, Gen. 49.10. Isai. 35.4. Acts 26.6, 7.

**V. 4. ***{again}* G. *πορευθέντες*, as Luke 7.22, going, or returning show, &c. Which in effect is, whereas John has by you sent his message to me; tell him again from me those things which you do hear and see.

*{those things}* He does not answer John’s message otherwise than by his works done, whereof John’s messengers were eyewitnesses, that he might demonstrate himself to be the Lord’s Christ, by those marks which the prophets had foretold.

**V. 5. ***{the blind receive sight}* This is one of the marks which was foretold, Isaiah 35.5, 6. See John 10.25, 37, 38.

*{the dead are raised}* This they related to John, Luke 7.12, 18, whether they saw it, or heard it from some eyewitnesses.

*{the poor have the gospel preached}* Isaiah 61.1. Christ does not (as worldly princes use) bestow his favors on the eminent and great, but most commonly on the obscure, poor, and despised of the world, 1 Cor. 1.26. &c. In the prophecy it is, *God sent me to preach good tydings*, *ענוים* *hanavim*, *to the meek*: the meaning is, the poor in spirit, as chap. 5.3. Those who know themselves destitute of grace and true happiness thence ensuing.

**V. 6. ***{whosoever shall not be offended in me}* At my mean estate and sufferings, chap. 26.31. 1 Cor. 1.34, or at my familiarity with sinners, differing from John’s austere course, verse 18, 19. The Jews expecting a worldly king for their Messiah, glorious, rich and victorious, would not receive nor believe in him; because they were offended at the form of a servant and man of sorrows: and it seems by this part of Christ’s answer, that John’s disciples were offended at Christ, because he lived not apart from the multitude, nor in that external austerity of life, which they admired in their master, John Baptist, chap. 9.14, which Christ so secretly reprehends in them, that he might not shame them before the multitude, nor let them go away in their error. See note on Luke 7.23.

**V. 7. ***{as they departed}* Christ would not commend John before his disciples, lest they should still err in preferring him before Christ; or the people should think he flattered John before his disciples, in hope to ingratiate with him, who had so high authority in the people’s opinion: but after John’s disciples were gone, Luke 7.24, he commends him to the people, who now might possibly have too mean an opinion of him in respect of his imprisonment.

*{into the wilderness}* Of Judea, where John preached, chap. 3.1.

*{a reed shaken with the wind}* Why went you into the wilderness by such multitudes? Surely you would not have gone to see reeds that are shaken with the winds, or such other unconsiderable things, which the wilderness affords: and men in fine clothing you could hardly see there; they are usually in kings’ courts: nothing was there to be seen worth your labor, but only John, whom you then esteemed as a great prophet: and you have no reason to think more meanly of him now, in respect of his imprisonment: some by reed, understand an unconstant person: a reed, as the wind blows, bends this way or that way: but John was no such light person, but a true and constant servant of God, as in his unchangeable testimony concerning me, so in his life and doctrine.

**V. 8. ***{a man clothed in soft raiment}* In silk, or courtly apparel. See on verse 7. John was far from affecting any delicacy of clothing, or diet. See chap. 3.4. Hereby he intimates that the testimonies, which John formerly gave concerning him, could not be for favor of any: for, the prison in which he 27was now, might sufficiently testify, that he could not flatter kings or princes: and therefore notwithstanding his message, which was for his disciples’ sake; he changed not his testimony for all his bonds. See on Luke 7.25.

**V. 9. ***{A Prophet}* See on verse 7. A teacher of the truth. See Chapter 21.26, an holy and reverent doctor sent of God.

*{more than a Prophet}* A man preferred by God to an higher office, than any of the former prophets was — *shewing him now present, who from the beginning was but typically foretold of, Clem. Alexandr. strom. l. 5*, and *Ireneus l. 3. c. 11. adventum vidit & demonstravit*, he saw and demonstrated his coming, persuading many to believe in him; so that he *& prophetae & Apostoli locum habuerit*, had the office both of a prophet and apostle: it is the office of a prophet *ventura praedicere, non autem demonstrare*, to declare things to come, not to show them present, as John did, and was therein greater than a prophet, *Gregor. hom. 6. in Evang.*
