**V. 10. ***{Behold I send}* See on chap. 3.1. Mal. 3.1.

*{my messenger, &c.}* He shows a reason why John was greater than the prophets: he was the harbinger designed by God to prepare the way of Christ, and the herald to proclaim him now come into the world, who is the King of his church. See Luke 1.15, 16, 17, 74, 75.

**V. 11. ***{among them that were borne of women}* He prefers John not only before the prophets, as verse 9, but also before all others that were born before him. Job 14.1, and 15.14, and 25.4.

*{there hath not risen}* Or *been raised*, as chap. 9.25. Luke 7.16. John 7.52. None greater than he has appeared among men. The sense is, God has not by his divine vocation prepared, designed, fitted and brought any more excellent of the sons of men, than John Baptist, to the ministry of prophet or apostle; the word *ἐγείρω* here used, is sometimes transitively put; as Luke 7.16, as also *ἀνίστημι*, Act. 3.22, *προφήτην* *ὑμῖν* *ἀναστήσει* *a Prophet shall the Lord your God raise up to you*. So Act. 2.30, as it were intimating a stirring up him, who of himself, had as little thought thereof, or ability thereto, as a sleeping or dead man, or as if the promise long before made to the fathers seemed to sleep, but now in the fullness of time, in his exhibition to Israel it was awakened, remembered, and performed.

*{a greater than John}* In dignity of office, and clearness and fullness of doctrine, in publishing the entire performance of all things foretold of Christ; as his death, resurrection, ascension, calling of the Gentiles, which John saw not.

*{he that is least in the kingdom of heaven}* The least in the ministry of the Gospel has a more excellent function, in regard of the clearness of doctrine to be taught by him. See Luke 7.28. John 1.29.

**V. 12. ***{from the days of John}* Luke 16.16. The scribes taught the people coldly before, but John has kindled in their hearts a fervent desire of hearing the Gospel.

*{until now}* This zeal lasts in them still, although John be cast into prison, and cannot now preach unto them.

*{suffereth violence}* Or, *is gotten by force*, as the Syriac expresses his *βιάζεται*: men run to hear the Gospel preached, as soldiers run upon a rich town besieged, to get the wealth thereof, Isaiah 60.4, 8, 11.

*{and the violent}* Only they, who with ardent affections, overcoming all that stands in their way, press into it, do, and shall attain possession thereof: the violent (says *Ambros. in Luc. lib. 8*) *qui vim facit, vehementi studio properat*: he that uses violence, hastens with vehement desire: the violent, or they that thrust on men, as in storming a town, one thrusts another, to get before him into it: so the people were so encouraged by John’s preaching to come to Christ, that every one was ambitious to come first.

**V. 13. ***{prophesied until John}* The things they foretold of Christ, and the types of him in the law, began to be fulfilled in the time of John, there needed now no more predictions, but a manifestation of the Messiah to Israel, which was John’s office to do, John 1.31.

**V. 14. ***{If ye will receive it}* If you can endure to have the truth told you.

*{this is Elias}* Mal. 4.5. This is he who should come in the spirit of Elias, Luke 1.17, not Elias himself, whom the Jews vainly expected, John 1.21. By this prophecy of Malachi he again shows the dignity of John Baptist, recalling to their minds (who had heard it) the word of the angel, Luke 1.17. This was Elias, not in person, but in office. See John 1.21.

**V. 15. ***{he that hath ears}* See on chap. 13.9. By this epiphonema he excites us to believe his doctrine (for *to hear*, is here put, for *to believe*) intimating, that although we all have external ears to hear, we are commonly deaf within: and this he speaks, as to make us consider our great defects herein, that we may pray the Lord to open our hearts and ears to attend unto his voice, that we may no longer be deaf with them that shall perish; *aures cordis requirit, Greg. hom. 15. in Evang.*, he requires the ears of the heart.

**V. 16. ***{But whereunto}* Luke 7.31.

*{this generation}* The men of this age, as chap. 24.34.

*{it is like unto children}* An allusion to children’s sports, who find fault with the surliness of some of their companions, who would not comply with them in their pastimes, though they assayed all ways they could to allure them thereto; one while singing merry songs, otherwhile elogies, or sad funeral ditties. See verse 18, 19. See on Luke 7.32.

**V. 17. ***{we have piped}* He taxes the stupidity of hearers, moved neither with the gracious comity of Christ, nor the strict austerity of John, or those who went before him: with those who preached the gladsome tidings of salvation by faith, or the severity of the law, to answer God’s promises or threatenings with faith and repentance.

*{but ye have not lamented}* *ἐκόψασθε*, this word expresses the gesture of mourners, and those who are deeply affected at that they hear or see: *κόπτειν*, amongst other things, signifies to smite and strike the breast: and also to mourn, as chap. 24.30. So the penitent man smote his breast, Luke 18.13. So did the spectators of Christ’s passion, Luke 23.48.

**V. 18. ***{neither eating nor drinking}* He ate locusts and wild honey, chap. 3. The meaning is, he used a most hard and austere diet, far from the common use of other men, abstaining from feasts and luxury.

*{he hath a devil}* By whose assistance he can endure such hardship: because many of those who are possessed, will endure much more hardness than others ordinarily can, without much sense thereof: John’s austere course was so far from winning many of them, that it incensed them the more: they thought he hated mankind, as Satan does. Thus does malice turn all that to occasion of calumny and blasphemy, which God appointed for their conversion and salvation.

**V. 19. ***{eating and drinking}* In a more free use of the creatures, and graciously conversing with all sorts of men in a more familiar course of life: his way differed from John’s, but both came to the same end: but the Jews were censorious of both, being won by neither.

*{a man gluttonous}* Given to excess, and abuse of the creatures keeping company with men of loose life.

*{wisdom is justified of her children}* Though there be many that scoff at all means of grace, yet the wise counsel of God, Luk. 7.30, in calling sinners home by John’s austerity, and Christ’s familiarity, is approved (as Psal. 51.4. Rom. 3.4. Luk. 7.29) by the followers thereof, as Luk. 10.6, which he says, to show that neither Christ’s, nor John’s labors had been fruitless to the elect hearers, though (as it commonly comes to pass) the wicked grew worse thereby, and their judgment more heavy, by their contempt of God’s mercy offered them.

**V. 20. ***{then began he}* Calling to mind the malicious and invincible ingratitude of the Jews: and particularly of certain cities where himself had taught, and done great miracles. See Luk. 19.13.

*{because they repented not}* Not because they did not commend him and his preaching, but because they did not amend by it. For the end for which God showed his power in miracles, was to confirm the doctrine of the Gospel, repentance, amendment of life, faith in Christ, regeneration, &c. To which, when they were not persuaded, they did not make a right use of the power of God, in so many miracles evidently showed them, neither should they finally advantage them, but more aggravate their judgment. Chap. 7.22, 23. Luk. 13.26, 27.

**V. 21. ***{wo unto thee Chorazin, &c.}* These were cities upon the lake of Genezaret, where Christ conversed much, and taught often.

*{Bethsaida}* The city where Philip, Andrew and Peter dwelt. See John 1.44.

*{the mighty works}* The great miracles, in curing all diseases, &c. Which being joined to the doctrine which our Savior preached unto them, might have assured them that it was the word of God, and therefore they ought to have obeyed it, calling them to faith and repentance.

*{in Tyre and Sidon}* Cities of great traffic by the sea, and of very impious manners: therefore, as the prophets of old denounced miseries to come upon them, Isaiah 23.1. Ezek. 26, 27, 28. Amos 1.8, so does our Savior here pronounce a woe to them, and threaten great and fearful judgments: to Corazin and Bethsaida who were much more hard-hearted, and less apt to repentance than the impious Tyrians and Sidonians. Suidas says that Tyrus of old was a metropolis: these were ancient, great, populous, and rich cities of Phoenicia. Sidon fell into the lot of the tribe of Asher, who yet possessed it not, because (says *Jerom de loc. Hebraic.*) they were not able to beat them out: so says he, *Tyrus in tribu Nephtalim*.

*{In sackcloth and ashes}* Repenting and manifesting their repentance by such outward signs of humiliation, as of old, and then were in use, with those who repented and humbled themselves for the diversion of any imminent or threatened judgments; and that not only among the Jews; but also among the Gentiles, Job 42.6. Jonah 3.6. Hester 4.1. Jer. 6.26. Lam. 2.10. The meaning is, that those miracles might have been sufficient, to have overcome their malice, and moved them to repentance; which the Jews had seen done, and profited not by them.

**V. 22. ***{it shall be more tolerable}* As there are diversities of punishments in hell appointed for impenitent sinners; so you, that have refused the Gospel, shall at the day of judgment, be 28more freely handled, than they that never heard it, though they lived more profanely than you do, Heb. 2.2, 3, and 10.28, 29. So he teaches here: 1. That there shall be a future judgment, chap. 25.31. &c. Act. 17. **2.** That it shall be at the resurrection. 3. That as there are degrees of sins, so shall there be of punishment to the damned. 4. That the heaviest punishment shall be to those impenitent sinners, on whom God had bestowed the largest means of conversion, and would not be reclaimed. See 2 Pet. 2.21.

**V. 23. ***{which art exalted unto heaven}* By my abode in you, frequent preaching and great miracles wrought. It was a marvelous dignity that Christ the Son of the living God, and King of kings, would among them auspicate his kingdom, chap. 4.13, so that it was honored with the name of his city, chap. 9.1, that he vouchsafed to make it illustrious by many divine and eminent miracles.

*{brought down to hell}* *ἕως* *ᾅδου*, *עדמא* *לשיול*, *unto the pit or grave*, Syr. See note on Luke 16.23, and Act. 2.27. *שאול* *sheol* with the Hebrew signifies the grave, which the Greeks give by *ᾅδης*, as Psal. 16. So Gen. 37. *I shall go down to my son mourning*, *εἰς* *ᾅδου*, *into the grave*: the same word is also taken for *Hell*, the place of the damned, Luke 16.23. Speaking here of the glory of Capernaum, he says, it was lifted up to heaven, (that is, very highly exalted in dignity and privileges, as also in her own opinion) and speaking of her ingratitude, he presently says, that she should be thrust down to hell, that is, your glory shall depart, you shall be destroyed and ruined, you shall be afflicted with extreme and pitiless ignominy (which probably came to pass, when the Romans destroyed and wasted the rest of Galilee) and you shall at last be damned in hell, who contemn so great means of salvation brought home to you.

*{in Sodom}* Which was so wicked, that it was destroyed by fire from heaven, that sad monument of God’s just indignation against sins and sinners: see Gen. 19.24. 2 Pet. 2.6. *super impium populum Gehennam misit è Coelo, Salvian de gub. Dei. lib. 1.* He sent upon an impious people, hell from heaven: that is, showers of fire and brimstone, wherewith the damned shall be tormented, Psal. 11.6. Isa. 30.33. Rev. 19.20, and 20.10, and 21.8.

*{it would have remained}* The Sodomites would not have been so wicked, as to bring that judgment on themselves: either we must understand this with relation to man’s apprehension and sense, considering only second causes; as if it were said, in man’s reason so great miracles would have converted them, though grievous sinners, and so God would have spared them, as he did Nineveh: or he regarded his own omniscience, as God, foreseeing and certainly foreknowing what effect every cause shall and will work *in subjecto determinato*, or the particular subject: and if any ask, if God, who cannot be deceived, did indeed foresee, that *Sodom and Gomorrah* would have repented? If he had given them as much means, as he did other sinful cities; why did he not give them, so that they might repent? We must know, that God being debtor to no man, both mercifully gives the means and the effect to repentance, amendment and salvation where he pleases (Rom. 9.18, 21), and justly denies the means, or the effect thereof, where he will execute his justice, and not show mercy, as Augustine says (*ep. 107*) to *Vitalis; Scimus eis quibus datur misericordia Dei gratuita dari: Scimus eis quibus non datur, justo judicio Dei non dari*; we know that to those to whom it is given, it is given by the free mercy of God: we know that to those to whom it is not given, it is, by the just judgment of God, not given: in this particular the Sodomites were grievous sinners, Gen. 13.13. Gen. 18.20, so that their sin is said to have cried to God: indeed, God had given them a righteous Lot, whose example might have been to them a lively doctrine to have restrained them from such impiety: but their wicked conversation vexed his righteous soul, 2 Pet. 2.8. They being nothing amended, but left more unexcusable hereby: therefore it was an evident justice of God, not to give them more means of grace, who had buried that talent of the light of natural reason which they had received.

**V. 24. ***{it shall be more tolerable}* See on verse 22. It shall be more easy damnation to the Sodomites in the world to come (though the righteous God made them examples of his indignation against such sins for future times, 2 Pet. 2.6) not because the Sodomites were less sinners than the Capernaïtes, but because the Capernaïtes despised, or neglected greater knowledge and means of salvation than was offered, or afforded the Sodomites. Consider well you whom it now concerns, what damnation awaits the neglecters of the Gospel, and the administration thereof.

**V. 25. ***{at that time}* This is said to be when the 70 were returned, and had told our Savior what good success they had, Luke 10.17, 21.

*{Jesus answered}* To the report of the 70 disciples; or answering is put for the beginning of a speech, as often in Scripture.

*{I thank thee}* Or, *I confesse unto thee*; *ἐξομολογοῦμαι*, imports either, or *I acknowledge before thee*, so *ידה* *jadah in Hiphil*, signifies among the Hebrews; *projecit, seu emisit vocem, liberè fassus est*, or *confessus est*, Psal. 118.1. &c. Confession, and free acknowledgment of God’s mercy, is a praising and thanking him for the same.

*{these things}* The mysteries of your kingdom.

*{and prudent}* From the scribes, Pharisees, and worldly wise men.

*{and hast revealed them}* By my ministry, and those whom I have sent to teach.

*{unto babes}* *Νηπίοις*, to simple men and women, who know no more in such things, than children do in worldly affairs. (See *Clem. Alex. Paedag. l. 1. c. 1.*) That which the Hebrew expresses by *פתי* Psal. 19.8, and 116.6, in both which places the 70 give it *νήπια* and Prov. 1.4, 22. & 8.5. & 14.15: (which Symmachus gives *νηπίοις*) they render by *ἀκάκοις*, harmless persons: but Prov. 1.32, the 70 give the same word *νηπίους*: which Greek word is used sometimes to express young brutes, plants, or that which has small strength, as an infant: what these here mean may be gathered, chapter 18.3. Rom. 16.19. 1 Cor. 14.20.

**V. 26. ***{even so}* *Ναί*, yea, Syr. *אין*, *etiam, certè, profectò*, yea, surely, truly.

*{for so it seemed good}* God’s pleasure being known, we must rest satisfied; there needs no other reason, where he will have it so; because his judgments are ever just, though many times unknown to us: and he is Lord of heaven and earth, and may do what he will with his own. Here we see that of *Prosper de vocat. Gent. l. 1. c. 24*, to be true, *Ex Deo esse principium, & profectum, consummationemque*, &c. That the beginning, advancement, and consummation of our salvation is of God.

**V. 27. ***{all things}* Joh. 3.35. Not only persons, but things; so great is the power of Christ. See chap. 28.18. Others understand it of the knowledge of those mysteries spoken of here, 2.25.

*{are delivered}* Christ then, as man, had not this power and knowledge of himself.

*{no man knoweth the Son}* His divine nature, and eternal generation: or, no man knows what he is to do, or suffer, but the Father, who has designed him to both.

*{neither knoweth any man}* John 6.46.

*{the Father}* That is his essence, or divine substance: or how he will have the calling of the Jews, or Gentiles to embrace the Gospel, to be dispensed or disposed: the prophets saw but little hereof, 1 Pet. 1.10, 11, 12.

*{to whomsoever the Son will reveal him}* None else can know, nor can any know any further than Christ will reveal him. chap. 13.11. Mark 4.11. Luke 8.10. 1 Cor. 2.7. Eph. 3.5. Col. 1.26. Joh. 1.18. Joh. 4.12. 1 Tim. 6.16.

**V. 28. ***{Come unto me}* It seems an allusion to Isaiah 55.1, where God calls all that spiritually thirst to him, with promise freely to refresh them: the meaning is trust in me, you that are troubled in conscience for sin, and I will bring you to true rest, God’s favor, which by other means you can never obtain: he calls them who alone can give both will and ability to come: as Ambrose says, *l. 7. ep. 54. Syricio*, he gives strength to those that follow him, so that the nearer you are to virtue, the stronger shall you be: observe here, 1. Whom he invites, all the laboring and heavy laden; 2. To what, *I will give you rest*: 3. With what condition; that they submit to the just and easy yoke of Christ.

*{all ye that labour}* Or *are ill at ease*; Christ calls all to faith and repentance, excluding none that are thereto prepared by a deep sense of the intolerable burden of their sin.

*{and are heavy laden}* With the sense of your sins, and a guilty conscience, of all burdens in this world, the most importable, Prov. 18.14. The more heavy we feel our sins, the nearer we are to God’s mercy to relieve us. See Psal. 38.4.

*{I will give you rest}* Peace of conscience, in the free remission of guilt and punishment, the assurance thereof, and freedom from the servitude of sin. Rom. 6.14.

**V. 29. ***{take my yoke upon you}* Obeying my commandments.

*{learn of me, for I am meek}* Imitate me in meekness and humility: which you will do if you love Christ: for as Chrysostom says, you love not him whom you will not imitate, and him whom you will not follow, you do but feign to love.

*{ye shall find rest}* Jer. 6.16. Your sins remitted, you shall have peace of conscience, in which your souls shall rest assured of God’s mercy and your salvation in me; and so shall you being discharged of the heavy burden of your sins, which shall not be imputed to you to condemnation, find rest to your souls. See Rom. 5.1. & Rom. 8.1, 2.

*{unto your souls}* Your outward man may be exercised with many troubles, but you shall have inward peace.

**V. 30. ***{my yoke is easy}* That which I command you is good for you and easy. See John 15.12.

*{my burden is light}* The word *φορτίον*, primarily signifies the freight or ballast of a ship, which glides through the waves, as if it had no burden, and indeed cannot go safe without ballast: now the regenerate consent to the law of God, Rom. 7.22, and therefore it is not grievous to them, 1 John 5.3. Not through any strength or will of their own, but by the free grace of God, who gives faith Ephes. 2.8, love, patience, perseverance, and all those things which they have need of, for the accomplishment of their salvation: the burden of Pharisaical 29traditions was heavy, chap. 23.4, and of oppressors, Isaiah 9.4, and 10.27. Hos. 8.10. The law was a burden, which neither we nor our fathers were able to bear (said Peter, Acts 15.10). It was figured in Moses’ hand, Exod. 17.12. *Moses’ hands were heavy.* *Lex manus graves habuit*, Gregor. *hom.* 33 *in Evang.* The law had heavy hands, which struck sinners with a heavy judgment: but Christ’s burden is light, who requires only of us, that which is best for us: as to love, fear, and obey him, in which there is true happiness, out of which there can be none: he exacts of us only faith, charity, sobriety, mercy, sanctity, all which (says Salvian *l.* 7 *de gub. Dei*) *non onerant nos sed ornant*: do not burden, but adorn us: he commands his to believe in him, that they may be saved, John 14.1. John 3.16; that they love one another, John 13.34,35. John 15.12. What easier burden than love? What sweeter yoke than that which God’s love makes easy? Every commandment of God is easy to him, who loves; because God is with him, and sheds abroad his love in his heart, that he may cheerfully and willingly obey him: as Prosper notes after Augustine, he that does anything only out of fear, servilely does it: nor is he a friend of the right, who if it could be, had rather not have that which is right commanded. Christ requires (to make us blessed) that at least we do hunger and thirst for righteousness, that is, that we have a will to be holy. *Vide si labor est, ubi velle satis est*, Augustine, *ep.* 45. See whether that be a labor, where ’tis enough to be but willing.

## CHAPTER 12

**Verse 1. ***{Jesus went on the sabbath day}* Mark 2.23. Luke 6.1. *שבתון* (of *שבת*, *shabbath of shabath, cessavit, quievit*) signifies a rest, or cessation; as God rested the seventh day from his work of creation, Gen. 2.2, sanctified and enjoined the same for a weekly seventh-day rest, Exod. 20.10, and 35.2,3. So it signifies the Lord’s day, wherein we are to keep a holy rest from all servile work. Secondly, all other solemn festival days, enjoined by the Levitical law to be kept holy, and set apart from all servile work, were called *שבתון* *Shabbathon*, Levit. 23.24,39. *Sabbathum*. 70. *ἀνάπαυσις*. So Ezek. 20.21, *they polluted* *שבתותי* *Shabbethothai, my sabbaths*, that is, my appointed feasts: so Col. 2.16, these are called *Sabbaths*, that is, such rests as were appointed some day of the week to be kept holy, by the ceremonial, not the moral law: for these feasts were appointed by a positive law, only for the time that the Levitical law and priesthood lasted; sometimes this word imports the whole week, as Luke 18.12.

*{began to pluck the ears of corn}* Which was lawful for them to do, Deut. 23.25, though in another man’s field.

**V. 2. ***{when the Pharisees saw it}* Who commonly went along with the multitudes which followed Christ, not with any intention to learn of Christ, but to observe all that he did, or said, that they might cavil and calumniate: as here, it seems they watched for the same purpose: and vainly thought, they had now found a just occasion to accuse him of the breach of God’s laws.

*{thy disciples}* It is your fault who teach them no better, or suffer them in your presence to do these things; he that connives at others’ sins, makes them his own; they so blame the disciples, that they might cast some impious aspersions on their Master, concerning breach of God’s law, though never so far from the mind of the lawgiver.

*{which is not lawful}* Because dressing of meat on the Sabbath was forbidden in the wilderness, Exod. 16.23, in regard of the Manna (their then present food) which was forbidden to be gathered on the Sabbath; they conceived that the very plucking, and rubbing of the ears was also unlawful, and a servile work forbidden, Lev. 23.7: and a capital crime, at that Num. 15.23. Thus hypocrites are over-scrupulous in the smallest, but careless in the greatest matters of the law, as charity and equity, as if they were of no consequence.

**V. 3. ***{have ye not read}* You that glory in the knowledge of the Scriptures, consider what the man after God’s own heart (1 Sam. 13.14) did in case of necessity; if he have read this, you might here have found that which might free you from this error.

*{what David did}* 1 Sam. 21.6. You must either blame your patriarch David, or free my disciples.

*{and they that were with him}* David then had some attending on him in his flight, though he be said to come alone to the priest, 1 Sam. 21.1. It seems they stayed for him in some place nigh, and he brought of the showbread thither to them. See 1 Sam. 21.5. For he being loath then to be seen in Nob, went alone to the tabernacle that so, little or no notice might be taken of him, and that he might have the better convenience to confer privately with the priest.

**V. 4. ***{the shewbread}* *τοὺς* *ἄρτους* *τῆς* *προθέσεως*, as if we should say bread of apposition, or setting before: Syr. *ולחמא* *דפתורה* *דמריא* *velachema dephathureh demorio. The bread of the Lord’s table*: the Hebr. called it, *לחם* *פנים*, *lechem phanim*, bread of faces, or presence, Exod. 25.30, because it stood before the Lord all the week, upon the golden table appointed for that service, Levit. 24.8,9, and placed in the foremost part of the tabernacle, to the north side, next after the holy of holies: there was none other use thereof, but to be furnished with twelve loaves or cakes: some say, that thereby the twelve tribes of Israel might testify and acknowledge, that God did perpetually feed them, and prepare a table for them, or to mind them of the poor: these loaves were made of two tenths of an Ephah, or bushel of fine flour, and were set, by six in a row, upon this golden, or pure table before the Lord: the old bread which had stood there all the week, was taken away, and eaten by the priests, Levit. 24.5,6, and new set in the place every Sabbath day; because they were ever set before the Lord, they were called *panes propositionis*, and *לחם* *התמיד* *lechem hattamid, perpetual bread*, which we call *showbread*, because these loaves did ever show, or present themselves to the Lord upon that table instead of Israel; for as the Ark signified the presence of the Lord in his Church, so this table with the twelve loaves, signified the multitude of the faithful, presented unto him in his Church, under his view, care, and providence, continually serving in his presence; and lastly, his continual feeding them with the bread of life, Christ. See John 6.32. Eph. 5.2.

*{which was not lawful for him to eat}* According to the letter of the law: but according to the intent thereof, it was lawful.

*{neither for them which were with him}* It appears hence, that they did eat of the showbread, as well as David: and this is added, lest the Pharisees might think, that David might do it by some extraordinary warrant, being a prophet: they that were with him were no prophets, yet they also did eat it, and are not blamed, because it was in a case of necessity, to which ceremonial laws must yield.

*{but only for the priests}* Exod. 29.32,33. Lev. 8.31, and 24.9.

**V. 5. ***{in the law?}* In the ceremonial law ordained in Mount Sinai. Numb. 28.6,9.

*{the priests in the temple profane the sabbath}* That is, in your sense, who judge any labor on the Sabbath to be profanation of the law: but there are Sabbath-days works; therefore the priests labor bodily, killing cattle for sacrifices on that day, and that in the court of the temple, and yet sin not, because this was their duty enjoined them by God.

*{are blameless}* Such as you have no cause to complain of; blameless, not only by mercy or connivance, but according to the law. So the Greek word *ἀναίτιοι* signifies unreprovable, or without crime: they do not violate the Sabbath, because they do that which appertains to the service of God: some note, that the Fathers, though rigid observers of the Sabbath; yet held this maxim, that necessity, or danger of life put by the Sabbath: that is, in case of necessity, there was no breach of the Sabbath, neither was necessary labor therein, any servile work. See Tremel. note on this place.

**V. 6. ***{one greater than the temple}* More holy, more excellent; Christ the Lord of the temple, *ἡ* *ἀλήθεια* *οὐχ* *ὁ* *τύπος*, Chrysost. *hom.* 40 *in Matth.* 12, the truth, not the type only, which preceded in the priest: if they were blameless, though they cut wood, kindled fires, slew beasts, and sacrificed on the Sabbath day, because all this was subordinate to the service of the temple, how much more are my disciples in doing that which enables them to attend on me in a greater business than sacrifices. That is, propagation of the Gospel of Christ, who alone can save us, which the law, and sacrifices therein appointed, could never do. Rom. 8.3. Heb. 10.4.

**V. 7. ***{I will have mercy}* See on Hos. 6.6, and chap. 9.13. God prefers relieving man’s necessities, before the performance of his own external service: so he concludes works of mercy to be Sabbath-days works and no profanations.

**V. 8. ***{for the Son of man}* I who am the Lord of the Sabbath, declare, that the Sabbath which was appointed for man, must yield to man’s safety and welfare; especially when either of them are in jeopardy, Mark 2.27,28. Hereto it is observable, which Athanasius *To.* 1 *de semente*, and others note, that Christ wrought his most illustrious miracles on the Sabbath days, not breaking the law, or introducing any new thing therein, as Tertul. *advers. Marcion. l.* 4 *c.* 11 excellently notes; for (says he) he here fulfilled the law, while he interprets the condition hereof, while he does those things which the law excepted from the Sabbath day’s rest, while he makes the Sabbath day itself (by God’s blessing holy from the beginning) more holy by his doing good thereon.

**V. 9. ***{when he was departed thence}* Out of those cornfields mentioned, verse 1. Mark 3.1. Luke 6.6, or from the place where the Pharisees calumniated him by those words, verse 2, *thy disciples do that which is not lawful to do, &c.*

*{he went into their synagogue}* See on chap. 11.1. On another Sabbath he went into the synagogue of the same town, Luke 6.6.

**V. 10. ***{his hand withered}* His right hand, Luke 6.6, the want whereof made his condition more calamitous.

*{they asked him}* They stay not till Christ had done this miracle; but by their speech, first put him upon it, that they might pick a quarrel with him.

**V. 11. ***{what man shall there be among you}* Will any of you that are so strict for the Sabbath, let a sheep perish thereon for want of help?

**V. 12. ***{a man better than a sheep}* All creatures on earth were 30made for man, it were therefore a shame to save a sheep, and suffer a man to perish: it is lawful by your own tradition and practice, to save a beast on the Sabbath day, and should not I heal on the Sabbath?

*{it is lawful to do well, &c.}* Legal ceremonies ought not to hinder works of charity: *Nulla necessitas major est charitate*, Jerem. *l.* 1 *ep.* 37, *Chromatio*, there is no necessity greater than charity.

**V. 13. ***{Stretch forth thy hand}* That withered hand: he commanded, and withal gave him ability so to do; by healing it on an instant, without which he could not have stretched it out: so when God gives us strength to do what he commands us, it is our duty, but his grace and mercy that we can in some good measure do it.

*{it was restored whole}* So Christ showed his divine power, as in many other like miracles.

*{as the other}* Which was sound and well.

**V. 14. ***{held a council}* The Pharisees went out of the synagogue, leaving the multitude, before whom they durst not for shame calumniate him, for this work of divine power and mercy: and took counsel to destroy Christ.

*{how they might destroy him}* Here appears the invincible malice of these adversaries: he healed all their infirmities and diseases, and they consult how to destroy him: to them belonged that which Moses said, Deut. 32.5,6, *they are a perverse and crooked generation: do ye thus requite the Lord, O foolish people and unwise?* The wicked so abuse the mercies of God, that even the good which he does for them makes them worse.

**V. 15. ***{when Jesus knew it}* By his divine knowledge.

*{he withdrew himself}* Though he could by his power have defended himself against all their attempts, yet he would deport himself as man, and by ordinary means keep out of their hands, till his time of suffering came. See Joh. 4.1,3.

*{great multitudes followed him}* The multitude, whom in their pride of heart the Pharisees contemned, as ignorant, and impious (John 7.49), were more sensible of God’s mercy than they; and therefore followed him withdrawing himself: they envied not his glory: consider what a dangerous thing envy is.

*{he healed them all}* That had need of healing, and desired it. See chap. 4.23. Mark 3.10. The malice of some shall not overcome and intercept his mercy toward others.

**V. 16. ***{that they should not make him known}* Lest the Pharisees, should find him, and endeavor to destroy him before the appointed time, or till after his resurrection, as chap. 17.9. See also ch. 8.4.

**V. 17. ***{spoken by the prophet}* Isaiah 42.1. See notes on that place: how little reason the Jews had, to expect a victorious, pompous, and externally glorious Messiah, the prophets by whom the Lord promised him to the fathers, evidently make it appear, they describing him to be a peaceable, meek, and humble person, so far from contention, quarreling, oppressing, or injuring of any that he was the man of sorrows. Isai. 53. Not so much as requiting injuries, with any clamor or complaint, but doing good to all, healing his enemies. Luke 22.51. Praying for them. Luke 23.34.

**V. 18. ***{whom I have chosen}* To be a Savior of the word. See Hebrews 5.5.

*{my beloved, &c.}* Chap. 3.17.

*{I will put my spirit upon him}* Isaiah 11.2, and 61.1. John 3.34. Coloss. 2.9.

*{judgment to the Gentiles}* *ἔθνεσιν*, by judgment he means the true worship, preaching of the Gospel, the doctrine of salvation by Christ: by Gentiles, the people which were not of the Jews, such were the Galileans and Tyrians now present, such are we. So was the prophecy then in part fulfilled: also hereby may be meant a settled estate of the Gospel, because Christ was to preach (as here by himself to these Gentiles, so by his Apostles the word) the truth of the Gospel, whereby to cast out superstition; which thing, wherever it is done, the Lord does reign and judge there.

**V. 19. ***{shall not strive}* He shall do his office meekly and humbly, and not manage his spiritual kingdom by violence, nor unnecessarily contest with the Pharisees, who consulted how to destroy him, verse 14. He shall not strive with violence, vociferations and clamor, as those, who having a bad cause in hand, think to make it good, by importunity and railing.

*{nor cry}* As men enraged, Ephes. 4.31: as those, who do all by tumultuous outcries. See John 18.40.

*{his voice in the streets}* As those who clamor in the ears of the people abroad and in public, for vain ostentation, and to be heard of men, out of the house where they teach or pray; but shall teach the Gospel with all meekness and modesty.

**V. 20. ***{a bruised reed}* By these two similitudes are described Christ’s gracious clemency in dealing with the most infirm; as those who had only a desire of grace, and some slender beginnings of conversion and faith, as many in this multitude following him, seemed to have: the meaning also may further be, that he shall not condemn such as labor under the burden of sin, but rather quiet their consciences, chap. 11.28,29. Ezek. 34.16.

*{smoking flax}* Alluding to the wick of a candle put out, that only smokes and gives no light, or very obscurely burning, it seems to be a proverbial manner of speaking among the vulgar: Christ would here show, that he will not extinguish, or cast away the weakest and slenderest measure of grace in any, but foment and cherish it to salvation; as it were distilling and secretly infusing oil into the lamp, the spirit of illumination into the infirm soul: of which clemency of our Savior we have daily experience, who spares us, and follows us continually with new accessions of grace, who have in ourselves a very obscure measure of knowledge and strength to do good, but as a bruised reed.

*{judgment unto victory}* Till he shall bring under, or confound all his enemies, and until the truth of the Gospel do prevail and overcome, throwing down principality and powers of darkness, in the Hebrew it is *לאמת* *Leemeth*, to truth, such shall the Gospel be acknowledged, in spite of all powerful enemies; though this victory cannot be achieved without hard and dangerous conflicts, the restless adversary doing all the mischief he possibly can, to oppose and overthrow it. See Rev. 12.7,13.15,17.

**V. 21. ***{in his name}* In him, when they know him aright, lest any should think that this appertained to the Jews only, he names the Gentiles to be converted, and to believe in Christ: in citing this prophecy, the Evangelist like a faithful interpreter, gives the full sense, though he omit some words less pertinent to his purpose; or vary others, as they best might serve to express the matter which he historifies.

**V. 22. ***{was brought unto him}* Luke 11.14. Very like it is, that he was made blind and dumb by the devil that possessed him. See on chap. 9.32. And now he was brought unto Christ by some friends; when he had in his recess and withdrawing from the people, showed himself to be the Messiah, by healing many.

**V. 23. ***{all the people were amazed}* The word and works of Christ have variable effects, as hearers and spectators, these admire the power and goodness of God, but the Pharisees blaspheme.

*{is not this the son of David?}* The Messiah. See on chap. 9.27.

**V. 24. ***{when the Pharisees heard it}* Chap. 9.34.

*{this fellow}* This man; so it is likely they might here call Christ in contempt, not vouchsafing so much as to name him; though *οὗτος* among the Greeks, did not always import contempt of the person to which it related: but sometimes it was a note of honor and eminency.

*{By Beelzebub}* See on chap. 9.34, and on Luke 11.15.

**V. 25. ***{Jesus knew their thoughts}* They are charged in the foregoing verse to have said so, what need Christ then know their thoughts? The answer may be that they said it in their hearts only; or that he knew it the same way, by which he knows men’s thoughts without any man’s relation, though as man he heard it not himself.

*{every kingdom divided}* As division overthrows houses, cities and kingdoms; so must it destroy Satan’s kingdom: if one devil should give power to men to cast out another. See Luke 11.17.

**V. 27. ***{by whom do your children cast them out?}* Some understand this of the Apostles, who were Jews: others of exorcists among them, Act. 19.13. Others of such Jews as did by Christ’s name cast out devils, but did not follow Christ, as Mark 9.38. Luke 9.49. Yet none of these were blamed by the Jews: why then should Christ be so taxed? The first interpretation is most probable.

**V. 28. ***{by the Spirit of God}* By power from God.

*{the kingdom of God}* The kingdom of the promised Messiah under the New Testament. See Luke 11.20.

**V. 29. ***{or else how can, &c.}* If God’s powerful kingdom were not come among you, how could so many devils be cast out? They are strong, and will not be driven out of possession by any, but a stronger hand.

**V. 30. ***{he that is not with me, is against me}* As in war, he that fights not for his prince, is an enemy: and as they which are to gather in their master’s harvest, if they neglect it, are scatterers and destroyers; so they that will not defend me against blasphemies (wherein they can) betray my cause and are enemies.

**V. 31. ***{wherefore I say unto you}* He speaks this to show, that either the Pharisees had committed this sin, or to warn men to take heed of it, by them who had come near unto it.

*{all manner of sin}* Great and small. Mark 3.28. Luke 12.10. John 5.16.

*{shall be forgiven unto men}* To some men, or any sin else may be forgiven.

*{blaspheme against the Holy Ghost}* This is conceived to be a malicious opposing the truth of the Gospel, made known to us by the Holy Ghost, joined with persecution of those that profess it, (and that merely because they do so) with final impenitency: See August. *Macedonio ep.* 50 *paulo ante finem*, and Augustin. *in Evang. Matth. serm.* 11 *post init.*

**V. 32. ***{against the Son of man}* Against Christ by way of disgrace as he is man, or against any man; for it is a grievous sin, to calumniate or wrong any man, yet pardonable: but to sin with so high a hand, as verse 31, against God himself, as has been said, is unpardonable, Numb. 15.30. Heb. 10.29.

*{neither in this world, nor in the world to come}* That is, never: but shall be punished both here, and in the life to come, Mark 3.29. 1 John 5.16.

**V. 33. ***{Either make the tree good}* Either confess that I am good, because you see me cast out devils, and good, or else convince me of any evil action, before you prejudicate my person; or either bring forth good fruits, that you may appear to be good; or else confess yourselves to be evil, as indeed you are: be what you seem, or seem as you are.

*{tree is known by his fruit}* So must you and I, by our words and actions.

**V. 34. ***{O generation of Vipers}* See on chap. 3.7.

*{how can ye, being evil, speak good things?}* Hypocrites will speak good things to deceive men: yet sometimes the evil of their hearts breaks out into their tongues; let your heart agree with your tongue.

*{for out of the abundance, &c.}* Luke 6.45. The heart is the fountain of words and actions; according to the veins and quality thereof, the words do commonly relish.

**V. 35. ***{a good man}* A man of a sanctified heart and affection, will speak to God’s honor and edification of others, Psalm 37.30,31, but a wicked man’s heart (like the unhealed waters of Jericho, 2 King. 2.19) sends out only streams of death and barrenness.

*{the treasure}* That is, the abundance, and indeficient store, of the Spirit of sanctification. See John 7.38,39.

**V. 36. ***{every idle word}* Every vain word, as Ephes. 5.6, or of every false word: much more then for every injurious calumny, profanation and blasphemy.

*{give account}* A metaphor from accountants, as Rom. 14.12. Phil. 4.17. Eccles. 12.14. We have need therefore to set a careful watch before the doors of our lips; it is a good advice of Jerom. *ep.* 22 *lib.* 2. Consider long what you must speak, and being yet silent, take heed you speak nothing to be repented of; let your thoughts ponder words, *et linguae officium animi libra dispenset*: and let the balance of your mind weigh out the task of your tongue.

**V. 37. ***{for by thy words, &c.}* See Luke 19.22. This also seems a proverbial and vulgar manner of speaking, that every man should be condemned or justified by his own mouth, or words thereof; but Christ here uses it to a different sense; to show, that perverse words, as being interpreters, otherwise of the secret malice of the heart, should be enough to condemn them; it relates to that he said before: idle talk condemns men of levity of mind: so in all other sins of the heart expressed by words.

*{and}* Or, *as*, Exod. 21.15,17. Num. 30.4,5,6.

**V. 38. ***{then certain of the Scribes}* Chapter 16.1. Luke 11.29. These would pretend a willingness to believe, might they but be satisfied, and convinced with such a sign as they required, Luke 11.16.

*{a sign from thee}* A miracle, or sign from heaven, that we may be ascertained that God has sent you to teach us, chap. 16.1. John 6.30,31. For they had seen many miraculously healed, but yet could not believe, or would not acknowledge the hand and power of God therein.

**V. 39. ***{adulterous generation}* A bastardly brood, Hos. 1.2, no true children of Abraham, John 8.39, because so much degenerate from his faith and sanctity. See on chapter 16.4. He notes their vain confidence in being the posterity of Abraham, in such their defection from the love and true worship of God.

*{no sign be given unto it}* No sign shall be given them now at their request, but afterward, one shall be given them in the death and resurrection of Christ, which shall be to the shame and confusion of unbelievers.

*{the sign of the prophet Jonas}* Whose lying so long in the whale’s belly, was a type of Christ’s burial, or such a miracle as was wrought upon Jonas.

**V. 40. ***{For as Jonas}* Jonas 1.17.

*{the Son of man be three days}* Some part of three days; for he was buried towards the end of the sixth day, lay in the grave the whole seventh day, and rose not again until about the dawning of the first day of the week, of which the night was a part; so Hierom. *in Jon.* 2.1 says, that *συνεκδοχικῶς*, *synecdochically*, a part is put for the whole.

*{in the heart}* Within the body of the earth, *ἐν* *τῇ* *καρδίᾳ*, in the heart; so the Hebrews say *לבב* *ימים*, *lebab iammim*, Ezek. 28.2, *the heart of the sea*, Jonah 2.4. 70. *βάθη* *καρδίας* *θαλάσσης*, *the midst of the seas*, or *the deeps*, so Deut. 4.11. *לב* *השמים*, *lev bashamaim, the heart of heaven*. 70. *ἕως* *τοῦ* *οὐρανοῦ*, *up to heaven*. Targ. *עד* *צית* *שמיא*, *ad summitatem coeli*. Exod. 4.14. *בלבו*, *belibbo*. 70. *ἐν* *ἑαυτῷ*, *within himself*. And so Hilary interprets this *ἐν* *καρδίᾳ*, *interiora terrae*, and Mark 2.6, *ἐν* *καρδίαις* *αὐτῶν*, the Syriac gives there, verse 8, according to the Greek, *ἐν* *ἑαυτοῖς*, *בנפשהון*, *benaphshehun*, in their souls, or within themselves, or among themselves: so does it among other things import the midst, or the inward part of the earth: so was Christ’s body being buried within the earth, that is, in the grave.

**V. 41. ***{shall rise in judgment}* Alluding to the manner of human courts of judicature; in which witnesses are said to arise, or stand up, to give in witness or evidence, Deut. 19.15,16. Mark 14.57. Acts 6.13. The very example of the Ninevites, in the day of judgment, shall be an argument of condemnation to the obstinate Jews.

*{this generation}* See on chap. 11.16.

*{shall condemn it}* Shall cause it to be condemned, as being against the men thereof an argument of condemnation.

*{because they repented}* Jon. 3.5. They repented at Jonas’ preaching, who was but a prophet, a mere infirm man; but the Jews would not repent at the preaching of Christ, God and man.

**V. 42. ***{The Queen of the South}* 1 Kings 10.1. The Queen of Sheba, whose nation bare south from Jerusalem, and the land of Israel.

*{shall rise up, &c.}* See on verse 41.

*{from the uttermost parts of the earth}* For Sheba was in the utmost parts of Arabia the Happy: and on the mouth or entrance of the Arabian Sea. Jerem. 50.26. And in those times there was not much more of those parts of the world discovered unto them, so that they knew no more beyond that.

*{to hear the wisdom of Solomon}* In things natural and human, 1 Kings 10.3, whereas here is another manner of wisdom, that is, unto salvation. 2 Tim. 3.16.

**V. 43. ***{When the unclean spirit}* Luke 11.24. He could as easily have said, The devil will more and more violently carry you into greater sins, until at last he destroy you: but he uses this parabolical admonition, that the penitent might learn caution, and the rest be less exasperated against him.

*{walketh through dry places}* The devil is compared here to a man cast out of his dwelling, and finding no contentment in dry places, and deserts, incommodious for habitation.

*{seeking rest}* That is, a place to settle in. Truly and properly, the devil’s rest, is in unrest, disquietness, and in doing all the mischief he may; there is no rest to the wicked: they are like the troubled sea, when it cannot rest: you may easily know, whose influence governs those restless spirits, whose sleep departs from them, except they cause some to fall: except they do some mischief, Prov. 4.16.

*{and findeth none}* For he rests not, except where he can do some mischief: he is like a malecontented exile, so long as he cannot infest man.

**V. 44. ***{I will return}* When he finds not some more wicked heart, or some others to pervert to their destruction, he advises with himself, how he may make them more impious and damnable whom he has already corrupted, and set in the way to hell and damnation.

*{into my house}* Here appears the miserable condition of the wicked, however great, glorious, or happy they appear to the sense of the world; they are but the devil’s houses, wherein he claims and holds a perpetual interest, yea, though he sometimes permit them to some more sober fits and appearances of repentance and amendment, as he did Pharaoh, Ahab, &c.

*{findeth it empty}* And none to hinder his reentering. Continuing in this allegory, he shows their desperate condition who are not guided by the good Spirit of God: they are ever ready, on all occasions, (as in study of revenge, anger, &c. Ephes. 4.26,27, envy, James 3.15, and in incentives to intemperance, lust, &c.) to entertain the devil in their hearts; in that they desire to serve him, by doing more evil than possibly they can, or dare do: that which he says in the following words, further expresses this:

*{swept and garnished}* Either by some formal repentance, or secure, void of all fear of God, living in pleasure, for this is Satan’s garnishing, whereby the sinner is made ready for him to entertain him again.

**V. 45. ***{then goeth he}* Finding not the means to do mischief to others, which he desired and expected he repossesses himself of that heart he had before, by carrying the sinner into some more desperate and damnable condition than before he was in.

*{seven other spirits}* That is many, as Revel. 1.4. Seven a definite number is put for an indefinite.

*{more wicked than himself}* To keep the more secure possession, and do the more mischief there.

*{and the last state, &c.}* See Hebrews 6.4, and 10.26. 2 Peter 2.20.

*{to this wicked generation}* Which seeks a sign, verse 38, the Jews having been in some sort freed from Satan’s dominion, by Christ’s abode among them, his doctrine and miracles: so that being now willing to destroy Christ, and to entertain Satan again, he intimates that they should be like a repossessed man, tenfold more children of the devil, than before: consider here the inevitable danger, that you relapse and turn again to the filthy vomit of your old sins.

**V. 46. ***{while he yet talked to the people}* This shows a reason, 32why he would not presently go to his mother and brethren; because they came unseasonably to interrupt him in his Father’s work. See Luke 2.49.

*{behold his mother}* Mark 3.31. Luke 8.20.

*{and his brethren}* His kinsmen, (as Gen. 13.8. See 13.55) where four of them are mentioned, James, Joses, Simon, Judas.

*{stood without}* Either in modesty, because he was instructing the people: and they could not but know, how unseasonable it must needs be to interrupt him then, or because they could not get in for the press.

*{desiring to speak with him}* Concerning what is not recorded: some think to acquaint him with some dangers towards him by the Pharisees: as Acts 23.16.

**V. 47. ***{one said unto him}* One at first, and afterwards many, Mark 3.32.

*{thy brethren}* See on verse 46.

**V. 48. ***{who is my mother?}* Not that he did in any measure despise his mother and brethren; but that he preferred the service of God, and salvation of the people, and because he would not be unseasonably troubled, See John 2.4.

**V. 49. ***{behold my mother}* He declares hereby how he loves and esteems the righteous, even as his dear mother and brethren: he is the firstborn among many brethren; and our brother, the Word became flesh, John 1.12,13,14. See also Rom. 8.17, and such as we are (as Hilary notes *de trin. lib.* 11) sin only excepted. The spiritual kindred he prefers; so should we the household of faith, Gal. 6.10.

**V. 50. ***{Whosoever shall do the will, &c.}* Matth. 7.21. Not every one of you that hears me, but he that faithfully does my Father’s will.

*{and sister}* It may be there were present some of his kinswomen also, though they are not mentioned, or else he adds it for the comfort of those religious women, that followed him, and ministered to him, Luke 8.2,3.

*{and mother}* The holy are near and dear unto me, as those to whom the laws of nature and flesh and blood oblige me; herein grace is better than nature.

## CHAPTER 13

**Verse 1. ***{The same day}* On that day: which phrase although in some places of Scripture it signify not precisely the very same day, but about that time, as in the prophets, Isaiah 4.1, and 26.1, yet in a historical narration as this is, it signifies the same day, and here appears our Savior’s unwearied endeavor in preaching salvation to the people.

*{went Jesus out of the house}* His going into the house is mentioned, Mark 3.19, as it seems to refresh his disciples, who by reason of the multitudes’ importunity, had not time to eat bread, Mark 3.20. Therefore Christ leaves his meat and the house, to go into a larger place where he might teach the people: so was it his meat and drink to do his Father’s work, John 4.34.

*{and sat by the sea side}* Mark 4.1. By the sea of Tiberias, John 6.1, which lay near unto his own country, whither he went, when he had finished the ensuing parables, v. 53,54.

**V. 2. ***{great multitudes were gathered, &c.}* The malice of the Pharisees, desiring a sign from heaven to confirm his authority and doctrine, chap. 12.38: though Christ would not satisfy, yet the people flock to him.

*{so that he went into a ship}* The multitude pressing him, as Mark 5.31. Luke 5.1, also that being freed from the throng, he might the better be heard and seen of his auditors, being at some distance from him, and all their faces towards him; therefore the ship was put off a little from the shore.

*{and sat}* This was the usual gesture of our Savior’s teaching; as also of their doctors, Matth. 23.2. See chapter 5.1. Luke 4.20. Although he stood when he publicly read the Scriptures, Luke 4.16, these things are of their own nature arbitrary, and therefore are most safely regulated by the customs of time, place, and persons, so as that we make no division in things indifferent; but there follow the custom of that Church whereof God’s providence has made us a part.

*{multitude stood on the shore}* That they might more conveniently see and hear him.

**V. 3. ***{He spake many things, &c.}* He spoke many parables, according to the several capacities of his auditors and their various employments. Some of them possibly were husbandmen; some merchants, others fishermen: he teaches them all in their several elements: such speeches are sometimes called parables, although there be no similitude in them: as Psalm 49.4. (See on John 10.6.) But here the word signifies a setting forth of spiritual things, under certain representations, and similitudes, taken from things secular and worldly: in his Sermon on the Mount, wherein he taught concerning blessedness; the sense and meaning of the law, &c. he speaks more literally and plainly, or in parables very easy to be understood: but here he speaks only in parables, not expounding them to the people: the reason is given, verse 11,34,35. Yet parables expounded (as some of these are afterwards) much help both understanding and memory, as showing us the face of heavenly things in earthly glasses: also they work much upon our affections, when they are taken from things which we either love or hate. (See on Luke 5.36.) And indeed, as the sun is more easily viewed under some thin cloud; so is divine truth under some similitudes taken from things familiar and nearer to our understandings. See concerning allegories, Jerom. *on Amos l.* 3 *c.* 9. Augustin. *To.* 6 *contr. Faust. Manich. l.* 12 *c.* 27,28,29, &c.

*{A sower went forth to sow}* Luke 8.5. This is the first of the seven parables in this chapter; wherein is intimated, that the seed of eternal life, which is sown in preaching of the Gospel, thrives not so well in one, as in another: and the reason is, for that men, for the most part, either do not receive it, or lose it or suffer it not to ripen: therefore he admonishes his hearers, that their flocking after him, would not profit them, except they kept it in faithful hearts, and brought forth the fruits thereof in newness of life, which in likelihood a fourth part of them only would do: the Lord sows largely, (says Athanasius, *de semente*) but the fruit bearing is *πρὸς* *τὸ* *ὑποκείμενον*——according to the subject receiving it; when it withers on the stony ground, &c. it is not *ἀδυναμίᾳ* *τοῦ* *σπόρου*, *ἀλλὰ* *ἡ* *καχεξία* *τῆς* *γῆς* in any impotency of the seed, but in the fault of the earth.

**V. 4. ***{Some fell by the ways side}* Some highways go through plowed fields, not enclosed in champaign countries, such as it seems that was where our Savior conversed, chapter 12.1. There the seed could not enter, because the ground was hardened by the feet of passengers; so the seed lying on the ground, was not received into the earth at all: as some hearers receive no benefit by the Word preached, either because they hear regardlessly, or understand not what they hear, verse 19.

*{the fowls came, and devoured}* Here is mentioned another cause why they grew not: what the earth received not, the fowls carried away; what these fowls are, see verse 19.

**V. 5. ***{Some fell upon stony places}* Where the grains might fall in between stone and stone, but could find no good rooting: so some are a little affected at the hearing of the Word, as Herod was; but it can find no firm ground in such stony and hardened hearts, as the flocks of Padan Aram could not be watered, until the stone was removed from the well’s mouth, Genesis 29.3. So neither can the waters of life, (whereof the waters running from the sanctuary of God, Ezekiel 47.1, &c. were a figure) in the holy Scripture contained, be tasted, until that heavy stone, the hardness of the auditors’ hearts, be rolled and removed away.

*{where they had not much earth}* Not much care to receive, not much understanding to apprehend, not much faith to believe, not much will to obey, or not much love to retain it.

*{forthwith they sprung up}* They could not root downward, because of the hardness of the earth; but shot up suddenly, because, as Euthymius says; *Quae modica terra occultantur, velociter exoriuntur*: seeds covered with little earth, soon spring up.

**V. 6. ***{when the Sun was up, they were scorched}* *ἐκαυματίσθη* Syr. *It felt the heat*: the heat dried up all the moisture which should nourish them, and so they perished.

*{because they had no root, &c.}* Another cause of their decaying, is want of root which should receive moisture to nourish it, as the mouth does food for the body: though the blade wither being blasted, yet if the root be in any depth of earth, it may shoot out again; so though a Christian be under some scorching temptations, dangerously blasted in the outward man, yet if he continue in the faith, and love of God, *grounded and settled*, (Colossians 1.23) he shall be like the tree planted by the rivers of water, (Psalm 1) but if this root fail, all perishes.

**V. 7. ***{Some fell among thorns, &c.}* This had some root and growth; but is choked up with thorns: so the Word in worldly hearers finds some love to it, and purposes to obey it; but the cares and pleasures of this world overgrow all, and make it fruitless.

**V. 8. ***{fell into good ground}* In the application, we may not think, that the heavenly seed found any good ground, but made it such. Lydia indeed, like good ground, attended unto that which Paul preached, Acts 16, but God opened her heart thereto: the heart must be prepared, or the word shall never prosper, and profit: therefore the prophet says *Break up your fallow ground, and sow not among thorns.* Jeremiah 4.3. That is, repent and root out your sins: but the Lord says also: *I will take the stony heart out of their flesh, and will give them an heart of flesh*, Ezekiel 11.19. See 2 Timothy 2.19. Which except he do, no art, power, wisdom, or counsel of man can do it.

*{and brought forth fruit}* The first sprung not up: the second sprung up, but had not sufficient root: the third grew up, but brought forth no fruit: but this grew up and fructified.

*{some an hundred fold, some sixty fold, &c.}* God’s Word does not fructify alike in all; the most fruit is best pleasing to God, John 15.2,5, yet he despises not the least, chapter 12.20.

*{ears to hear, let him hear}* As if he said the parable is hard, and requires diligent attention and consideration. See chapter 11.15, and Revelation 2.7. Let them whose hearts and ears God has opened, be attentive: all are deaf, until God open. See Psalm 40.6. If God have given you an ear to hear, receive not the grace of God in vain; make good use of it, and hearken diligently to his Word.

**V. 10. ***{the Disciples came}* To wit, after he had done teaching the people, and was gone out of the ship: see Mark 4.10. For it is not likely that our Savior would leave teaching the people, to confer with the Apostles, until he had uttered all he had to say unto the people. (See verse 36.) At that present time.

*{and said unto him}* Two questions were now moved to our Savior: the first recorded here: why he spoke to the people in parables; the second, Mark 4.10,13, what was the sense of the parable? To the first our Savior answers, verse 11, to the second verse 18.

*{Why speakest thou unto them in parables?}* In speeches more obscure than usual, and such as neither they nor we understand: this shows a pious charity in the Apostles, in that they not only desired to understand these parables themselves, but that the people also might understand them; and it is probable that they apprehended from Christ’s conclusion, verse 9, that themselves also, wanted something of the *ear to hear*, and understand. See Mark 4.10. So also it may appear in that Christ reproved them, Mark 4.13.

**V. 11. ***{Because it is given unto you to know, &c.}* To you that fear God I will interpret the parables: see Psalm 25.14, which not being unfolded, are hidden mysteries; and like the cloudy pillar, dark toward the Egyptians, but light to Israel, Exodus 14.20. Prosper says well, *de vocat. Gent. l.* 1 *c.* 24. *ut Deus cognoscatur, ex Deo sit*, that we may know God, it is of God. See Ezek. 11.19. Jer. 24.7. Prov. 8.9. Prov. 14.6.

*{to them it is not given}* The understanding of things divine and heavenly, is to the faithful: the rest either know them not, or to their further condemnation, who knew their Master’s will and would not do it: the free grace of God makes the difference: see Psalm 25.9,12,14. Prov. 3.32. 1 Cor. 4.7.

**V. 12. ***{For whosoever hath, &c.}* Chapter 25.29. It is the custom of the world to give to rich men, and to take away from the poor; this they unjustly do: but God will justly reward those that use his ordinances well, and punish them that abuse them.

*{even that he hath}* That which he seems to have, Luke 8.18, or those common graces which he did not well use when he had them.

**V. 13. ***{Therefore spake I to them in parables, &c.}* The reason why these mysteries are no more plainly delivered unto them, is for their foregoing obstinacy: Isa. 6.9,10. Compare the words here following, and see also Mich. 3.5,6,7.

*{they seeing, see not}* Though they daily see the miracles wrought by me, yet will they not take any notice of my divine power, that they may believe, and be saved, John 6.36. See the like, Deut. 29.2,3,4.

*{hearing they hear not}* Though they hear with their outward ears, that which might convince their errors, and inform them of the truth, yet they receive it not inwardly, but obstinately withstand it.

*{neither do they understand}* Nor conceive aright, what is delivered unto them, John 2.19,20, and 8.27,43.

*{in them is fulfilled the Prophecy, &c.}* This is no new thing, neither did it seem strange unto you that they are so hardhearted: so was it with their fathers, and so it was foretold that it should be with them, Acts 7.51,52.

*{By hearing ye shall hear}* So Mark 4.12. Luke 8.10. John 12.40. Acts 28.20. Rom. 11.8. Isa. 6.9.

**V. 15. ***{for this people is waxed gross, &c.}* As in the body, when the heart is overgrown with fat, both that, and all the parts of the body, thence deriving vital spirits, grow heavy, dull and inactive: so it is in the inward man; when the heart is grown sensual and obdurate, men cannot apprehend God’s truth for their sanctification and salvation. See Isaiah 6.10.

*{Their eyes have they closed}* This shows a reason of that heavy judgment denounced against Israel, Isa. 6.9,10: the obstinate people would not hear, nor understand; but as those who willfully stopped their ears, and shut their eyes, that they might the more securely run upon their own destruction, and neither hear nor see that which might stay them, and have made for their conversion and safety; deafness in a sharp fever (say physicians) is a sign of madness: more miserable are obstinate sinners: the lunatic has some excuse; the will is not in fault, where men are not themselves, said Salvian *de gub. lib.* 6. The more condemnable are these, *qui sani insaniebant*; who are wittingly mad, where there is willful maliciousness *ἐκεῖ* *ἡ* *ἀποχὴ* *τῆς* *χάριτος* there is absence of graces Cyril. Hierosol. *Catec.* 6. Therefore God justly deserts those, who unjustly first deserted him, following their obstinacy, by giving them up to spiritual blindness; withdrawing the common light which he had given them: his judgments ever follow, they never precede man’s sins: where his eternal decree, and omniscience precede, it is to appoint impenitent sinners to punishment, not to sin.

**V. 16. ***{But blessed be your eyes}* You are happy who understand these mysteries of salvation, chap. 16.17. For as Ireneus says, *l.* 4 *c.* 6. *nec enim lumen deficit propter eos qui semetipsos excaecaverunt*, the light fails not for their fault who have blinded themselves.

*{for they see}* You not only see with your bodily eyes, as these wicked men do; but also with the eyes of your mind: see John 6.40.

**V. 17. ***{Many Prophets and Righteous men}* Your condition is better not only than these wicked men’s, but also than that of the fathers, who lived in former ages; for you know more of me, and heavenly mysteries, than they did; and where you doubt, you have one present to resolve you, and plainly and infallibly expound unto you the mysteries of eternal life, and salvation; which the fathers saw but darkly, through the clouds of the law, and far off: see Hebrews 10.1. Hebrews 11.13.

**V. 18. ***{Hear ye therefore the parable}* I will tell you the meaning of it, though I conceal it from them, who willfully stop their ears, and harden their hearts; their obstinacy shall not hinder you. See on verse 3, and chapter 15.15, and on Luke 5.36, and here on verse 16.

**V. 19. ***{The word of the Kingdom}* The Gospel preached, which brings men into the kingdom of grace here, and shows them the way into the kingdom of glory, to be possessed hereafter: this is the seed before spoken of, called also the Gospel of the kingdom, chap. 4.23, and the kingdom of heaven, chapter 3.2.

*{understandeth it not}* Or *regards it not*.

*{the wicked one}* The devil, who is wicked himself, and endeavors to make all others so: here are the ravening birds, which devour the seed: by wicked one, understand one or more unclean spirits; who are ever ready, watching the *oscitancy*, neglect, sleepiness and carelessness of hearers; whereby they easily steal away the Word preached from the heart, and memory of the hearers: look how hungry birds watch the sown fields, to pick away the seed, so do the devils watch auditors while they hear; this the wicked one does usually.

*{That which was sown}* The Word preached; which would else bring forth according to the kind and season: as without good seed sown, the earth brings forth only its own curse, briers, thistles, thorns, and weeds; at the best, but grass for the beasts: so is it with the soul of man, without this heavenly seed of God’s Word, we bring forth nothing but sin and a curse.

*{in his heart}* Which he had heard, but regarded not in his heart.

*{this is he which received the seed, &c.}* This is he who is likened to that portion of earth, which being near the uttermost furrows, receives some overcast seed: who though he be joined in place and number, to and with the people of God, hearing the Word, (as the highway is somewhere near the furrows of corn) yet indeed he belongs not to the good earth, but the highway, so hardened with treading (that is customary sinning, and obduration of heart thereby) that no good can there take root, or prosper.

**V. 20. ***{He that received the seed}* He that is here likened to such earth.

*{anon with joy}* *εὐθὺς* presently, the Syrian gives it *בר* *שעתה* *bar shahatheh*——the same hour, as chapter 8.13, and here verse 5. *Forthwith*, that which the Hebrews express by *רגע* *אחד* *regah echad*——Exodus 33.5, *in a moment*; *μία* *ὥρα* Revelation 18.17,19, *in one hour*: such is the appearance of any good, in the rootless hypocrite, *as a morning cloud, and as the early dew it goeth away*, Hosea 6.4. As those, *fructus horarii*, soon ripe, soon rotten: like Jonah’s gourd, *בן* *לילה* *ben laila* Jonah 4.10, *the sonne of a night*, which came up in a night, and perished in a night: such was Ahab’s, Saul’s and Pharaoh’s repentance, 1 Kings 21.27, &c. 1 Sam. 15.30,31. 1 Sam. 24.16,17. 1 Sam. 26.1, &c. Exodus 9.27,34,35, &c. Such was Herod’s attention to John’s preaching, Mark 6.20. So are many carnal hearers affected with the Word, the same instant or hour they hear it, and thence it withers away, and is no more thought of. By this, examine your condition, whether the word you hear, and the affection it works in you, in that hour you hear it take root in your heart, until it bring 34fruits of sanctification, in the course and practice of your life.

**V. 21. ***{yet hath he not root in himself}* As corn not well rooted may make some show, till some blast or scorching drought comes, but then it withers, and wilts away: so, they that have not God’s Word rooted in their hearts, may make a fair profession, till trouble come, and then they fall away.

*{persecution ariseth}* When unexpected troubles arise, which frustrate the vain hopes he conceived of temporal peace, and prosperity, by the profession of the Gospel; or those forementioned promises.

*{offended}* Or *made to fall*. Gr. *scandalized*.

**V. 22. ***{and the care of this world}* The care of getting and managing worldly riches, and enjoying pleasures, are compared to piercing thorns choking up the seed; that is, good intentions wrought in the heart by the Word preached. See 1 Tim. 6.9,10. Luke 21.34. 2 Tim. 4.10.

*{choke the Word}* As overshadowing briers, thorns, or weeds, keeping the sunbeams from the corn, and with more strong and rank roots, drawing away the fatness of the earth (which otherwise should nourish the corn) and thereby hinder it from all maturity of fruit: so do cares and pleasures in the soul: indeed such a severe justice of God follows the contempt and neglect of his Word, as that where it mends not, it leaves the hearer much more condemnable. Hence comes it so often to pass, that when men for the present having their consciences convinced by the Word they have heard, and being moved by the evident power thereof to repent, and resolve to become new men; yet they presently fall back again into their old wicked courses: so that (as Salvian. *de gub. l.* 5 well says) a man might probably think that they had rather repented of their repentance, than of their sins: *nec tam prius poenituisse quod male vixerint, quam postea quod se promiserint bene victuros*; and that they had not before so much repented, for that they had lived wickedly; as afterward for that they had promised that they would live well: consider this you foolish *libertines*, who think it enough if you frequent good sermons; though you presently after return to your filthy sins: indeed God’s Word is the soul’s antidote, and sin its poison; but (says the same Salvian) *quid prodesse poterit antidotum, cui superfunditur venenum?* What can the antidote profit, which has more poison poured down upon it?

*{becometh unfruitful}* The hearer, or the Word in the hearer becomes unfruitful.

**V. 23. ***{into the good ground}* But one ground of four is fruitful; most hearers are fruitless.

*{He that heareth the Word, &c.}* Here are three properties of a good scholar of Christ; he must hear with his ear, understand with his heart, and practice in his life.

*{some an hundred fold, &c.}* As before, there were three sorts of bad hearers, so now also of good ones, all bear not fruit alike, some are richer grounds than other; so is it here in matters spiritual.

**V. 24. ***{The Kingdom of Heaven}* See on verse 19.

*{is likened to a man which sowed}* Christ shows in this parable of the wheat and tares, or weeds, that the visible Church, should ever unto the end of the world, have in it some good, and some wicked hypocrites, united only in external communion and profession of religion, but no sound members of the Church, or body of Christ; which is necessarily to be observed, lest either the faithful should be scandalized, or the truth of religion dishonored, and called into question, by the evident apostasy of those who go out from us, but indeed were no more of us, than the tares of the envious man’s scattering, of the Lord’s good wheat.

*{good seed in his field}* In the former parable, the seed was the Word preached, here it signifies good men, raised up by God in his Church: except we will also understand hence the doctrine of truth (which instrumentally makes good men) on the one part; and heresies, doctrines of devils, &c. 1 Tim. 4.1 (which evidently render men and women unhappy tares in the Lord’s field) on the other part.

**V. 25. ***{While men slept}* The devil goes so cunningly about his nightworks, that he may not be descried, or takes occasion by the negligence, incogitancy, or slothfulness of those that have the charge of others, to sow mischief in God’s Church. Isaiah 56.9,10.

*{his enemy came}* The devil, called *שטן* *Satan*. So 1 Samuel 29.4, *lest in the battle, he be to us* *לשטן* *lesatan an enemy*, or *adversary*. 2 Samuel 19.22, *that you should this day be to me* *לשטן* *an adversary*. See chapter 16.23. Revelation 1.20. It imports here, God’s enemy and ours; for as Chrysostom. *Hom.* 47 *in Matthew* notes, his endeavor is all against us, for the malice he bears to the holy majesty of God. See 1 Peter 5.8.

*{sowed Tares}* *ζιζάνια* signifies that same *infelix lolium* (as Suidas interpreter also gives *Ζιζάνιον*) ours render it *ray darnel, linetare, tares*, or something very hurtful to the corn, (as any of these are among wheat; hereby are meant, (as Theophylact. Euthymius, August. *de verb. Domini. s. Mattheum, serm.* 18 clearly gather from verse 38) *the children of the wicked one*, that is, heretics, schismatics, hypocrites, wicked and profane men, living in the Church. Pet. Chrysolog. *serm.* 96 says also *haereses inter fideles, inter sanctos peccatum: inter pacificos lites*, &c. Heresies among the faithful (that is in the Church) sin among the saints, dissensions among the peaceable: indeed all these are tares of the envious man’s scattering, whereby he makes men wicked, and disturbs the peace of the Church, and flourishing of the truth.

*{among the Wheat}* Not in the world abroad, but in the Church of God: *ut periret dominica messis* (as Chrysolog. *serm.* 97 says) only to endamage the Lord’s crop: for being filled with the spirit of envy, he esteems the destruction of men his gain: it is a strange temerity to desert a Church for some wicked men therein being: where did the enemy sow his tares, but among the good wheat? *perplexae quippe sunt istae duae civitates in hoc seculo invicemque permixtae*—these two cities in this world, are folded together and mingled the one with the other. Augustin. *de C. D. l.* 1 *c.* 35, and a little before he gives a good caution against uncharitable censure, *memineris sane in ipsis inimicis suis latere cives futuros*, &c. Let them remember that indeed among their enemies, some good citizens for the time to come, lie hidden.

*{and went his way}* He would not stand to it, nor have it thought to be his doing; because he can no otherwise deceive, than under some color of good: if he should appear, and show himself with the impious and heretical, all would as much abhor them, as fear his stratagems: therefore he transforms his ministers into angels of light. 2 Corinthians 11.14. That is, into seeming godly, religious, and zealous men, without which dresses and disguises, he could never deceive the world by such instruments.

**V. 26. ***{When the blade sprung up}* When such men first profess the true religion, they so cunningly hide their principles in obscure terms, and veil their wickedness with shows of holiness, that it cannot presently appear, who are good, and who evil: but afterwards, when good men are nearer their maturity, and the wicked to their height of maliciousness; an evident difference appears. So Chrysostome notes the manner of heretics, at first, they overshadow themselves until they gain more confidence and affiance of popular favor and opinion; but then *τὸν* *ἰὸν* *ἐκχέουσι* they pour out their venom with impudence undaunted; and so are known at last.

**V. 27. ***{the servants}* Either this is added to fill up the similitude, and need not be applied, as not being after expounded by our Savior, or else the servants are ministers and good men, who wonder how these tares came, and desire to root them out, (see Revel. 17.6) but may not do what they desire: the minister must handle the cause of God, with arguments, not stripes: *fides non est cogenda, sed docenda*: faith must be taught, not forced. Lactantius said it well; *Religio cogi non potest: verbis potius quam verberibus res agenda est, ut sit voluntas*, &c. *l.* 5 *c.* 20. Religion cannot be compelled: this cause is to be handled with words, not stripes, that the will may have a part, without which can be no sanctity.

**V. 28. ***{an enemy}* *ἐχθρὸς* *ἄνθρωπος*, the devil and wicked men. (See on verse 25.) For both cooperate in broaching heresy: the devil prompts, and his ministers act.

**V. 29. ***{Nay}* This is not any prohibition, of excommunicating or executing malefactors, by the civil power, but a prediction that such shall be in the Church, till the end of time, as our Savior expounds it, verse 39,40, as Augustin says, *Ep.* 146. *donec diluvium transeat, ubi Arca continet corvum et columbam*. Until the deluge pass away, wherein the Ark (the Church) contains both the raven, and the dove:—

*{let both grow together}* For as Gregor. says *Hom.* 31 *in evang. boni soli, nusquam sunt, nisi in coelo*, only good men (in any one society) are nowhere but in heaven; where Christ will make his Church glorious, not having spot or wrinkle; but holy, and without blemish, Ephesians 5.27.
