Why God suffered wicked men to continue in his Church; there are many reasons: as, that he might leave the impenitent more unexcusable, whom the society and example of the holy could not reclaim: that he might show on them an example of long-suffering and mercy, that also they might justly perish, who abuse such mercy, to security of sinning: that he might hereby exercise his elect, and make their constancy more glorious, whom neither Satan, nor the mischievous examples of men so nearly united to them, could pervert; among other, Gregory Nazianzen gives this reason, *Orat.* 4 *post med.* Though his judgments are ever ready, yet he patiently expects, until sin, like some putrid and malignant humor, do quite break out, and show itself; then is his justice evidenced in striking: it should teach us a charitable patience in judging inferiors; though it is certain, that there is much difference, with what mind, and to what end one spares who should correct: for as there is sometimes a *misericordia puniens*, so also is there a *crudelitas parcens*, a punishing mercy, and a sparing cruelty, Aug. *ep.* 54 *Maced.nio*.

**V. 30. ***{gather ye together}* Though both sorts continue mixed together unto the end, yet at the day of judgment a separation shall be of the one from the other, Mal. 3.18. Chap. 25.32.

**V. 31. ***{the kingdom of heaven}* Mark 4.30. Luke 13.19. The Church is here the seed.

**V. 32. ***{the least of all seed}* One of the lesser sort of ordinary seeds, for some may be less. God raises his Church out of very small beginnings; that, it growing up beyond all men’s expectation, his mighty power therein may appear.

*{greatest among herbs}* *Μείζον* *τῶν* *λαχάνων*, greater than herbs, one of the greatest sort of herbs. The Greek word *λάχανον*, Lat. *olus*, signifies either potherbs, or any other herbs to be beaten or chopped——as——*herbas contundit olentes*——Virgil, or otherwise eaten, though Varro Plin. &c. sometimes take *olus pro brassica*, the cabbage, or colewort. Theophr. *hist. plant. l.* 7. *καλοῦμεν* *λάχανα* *τὰ* *πρὸς* *τὴν* *ἡμετέραν* *χρείαν* we call them *olera* which we use to eat. So Athenaeus *l.* 2. So also the Syriac here gives it; *maximum omnium* *ירקונא* *olerum*: the same word *ירק* Syr. *יורק* is used, Luke 11.42, where the Greek also has *λάχανον*, and we give it herbs; as also 1 King. 21.2, where the Hebrew has also *ירק* *τὸ* *εἰς* *κῆπον* *λαχάνων*—*for a garden of herbs*. Plin. *l.* 19 *c.* 8 takes the *olus*, to be of no esteem with the Grecians (as Ambros. says—*vile olus*) of the *Sinapi*, he says, *coquuntur et folia sicut reliquarum olerum*—on which account possibly it was reckoned among potherbs.

*{a tree}* It grows high, and puts forth branches as a tree: it seems it was of a great growth in Palestine. See Tremel. on this place. It becomes the greatest or tallest of the plants which are accounted *olera*, or *λάχανα* as the Greek has it here, so that the birds may sit upon the branches, or under the shadow thereof, which the Greek word *κατασκηνεῖν* of *σκηνὴ* a tabernacle or tent, may well import.

**V. 33. ***{another parable}* Luke 13.20. The scope of this parable, is the same with the former.

*{three measures}* *σάτα* *τρία*. That is, some say, an Ephah; this contained about a peck and a half, wanting a little more than a pint.

**V. 34. ***{All these things}* Mark 4.33. Forementioned, he spoke all these parables, whereby the godly auditors might be excited to a further inquiry after the precious and veiled sense thereof, and might the more esteem it found out, and discovered unto them; and that the profane, touching only, as it were, the outward rind or shell, might not have occasion to despise the hidden truth, which they knew not.

*{without a parable}* See on verse 3.

**V. 35. ***{I will open my mouth}* Psal. 78.2.

*{in parables}* The Psalmist’s parables or proverbial sayings, had some resemblance to Christ’s manner of teaching here by similitudes and parables.

**V. 36. ***{went into the house}* Though the disciples were very desirous to know the meaning of the parable, yet they stayed until he came to the house; either because they were ashamed to ask him before the people, or unwilling to interrupt him in his teaching.

**V. 37. ***{The Son of man}* Christ himself, the most excellent Son of man, as verse 41.

**V. 38. ***{children of the kingdom}* God’s elect, appointed to be heirs of the kingdom of heaven. See on chap. 9.15, and Luke 20.36.

*{children of the wicked one}* Not by nature, but imitation, children of the devil, verse 19. Hereby are not meant all wicked men, but such as live in the Church; see verse 25,41,47,48.

**V. 39. ***{The harvest}* See Joel 3.13. Rev. 14.15. *The reapers, the Angels* are as the harvesters. This name is accommodate to the present cause, John 4.34. The Apostles in relation to the prophets, were also accounted harvesters, or reapers, to which then appertained that which is there said, verse 35, *the regions are white to the harvest*: and in this Gospel, chap. 9.37,38, *the harvest truly is plenteous, but the labourers are few*, &c. and in chap. 3.12. Christ with the fan in his hand, began to purge his floor, by preaching of the truth, and cleansing it from the false and corrupt glosses of the Scribes, priests and Pharisees; but here in the last day, he will perfect that work; then Christ the Judge and King of glory, shall sit on his throne of majesty, convent all the living and the dead raised again, and shall separate them to his right hand, and left; those to the kingdom of glory and blessedness, these to fire and torments unspeakable, Matth. 25.31, &c. In these acts he will use the ministry of his angels, as here is declared, and verse 41.

**V. 40. ***{in the end of this world}* The end of time, and this state of mortality, then the wicked men shall be cast body and soul into hellfire.

**V. 41. ***{The Son of man}* He mentions his human nature, because therein he will judge us, chap. 25.31. John 5.27. Act. 17.31.

*{his angels}* As verse 57. Chap. 14.11. Which he made ministering spirits, Hebr. 1.14, of admirable strength, power, and vigor, to do that which he commands them.

*{things that offend}* Or *scandals*: scandalous persons as chap. 16.23. See the words following, and verse 42, such as depart from truth of doctrine, Rom. 16.17. Compare Matth. 18.7 with 1 Cor. 11.19, or such as are hypocrites and evil livers; who offend good men by their sins; or by being mixed with them. See verse 27.

*{them which do iniquity}* Who customarily sin, chap. 7.23. Psal. 6.8. Which do wickedly, and so would do: so would not the regenerate. See Rom. 7.15.

**V. 42. ***{into a furnace of fire}* The pains of hell are described by that which is most painful and terrible to us. Mark 9.45,46. Mal. 4.1.

*{wailing}* For helpless and hopeless grief.

*{gnashing of teeth}* Out of indignation, and impatience, as Act. 7.54. See chapter 8.12.

**V. 43. ***{Then shall the righteous, &c.}* Dan. 12.3. They shall shine as the sun, Jude 5.31. Chap. 17.2. That is, be exceeding glorious.

*{the kingdom of their Father}* 1 Cor. 15.24. The kingdom of God is the inheritance of his adopted children, and is here mentioned by our Savior in opposition to earthly inheritances, in which he would have them ever remember that they are but pilgrims and sojourners, that they might aspire to that which shall be their own eternally. See Coloss. 3.1, &c. Psal. 39.12. Psal. 119.19. We are indeed now in our regeneration the *sonnes of God: but it doth not appear what we shall be: but we know, that when he shall appear, we shall be like him*, 1 John 3.3. See also Coloss. 3.4. Phil. 3.21. The kingdom of God is in us now (Luke 17.21) who walk in the Spirit; but the light thereof (as Gideon’s lamps in those earthen vessels, Jud. 7.20) shall not clearly shine out until they be broken, and this earthen tabernacle of our body be dissolved. Meantime our state, as to the world is calamitous; we have many failings, and an odious remainder of sin in us, which deforms the best of the sons of men, and the wicked cast dirt in our faces, by several calumnies, making us seem more odious to men; but in spite of their malice, we shall once be glorious, when our God shall be *all in all*, 1 Cor. 15.28, when the wicked shall be clothed with shame.

*{who hath ears}* See on verse 9.

**V. 44. ***{The kingdom of heaven}* The word of God which shows the way to heaven; and opens unto us the inestimable riches of God’s grace and mercy, hid from the world’s beginning, until the preaching of the Gospel.

*{unto a treasure}* Of great value: as gold and silver, in great store laid up.

*{hid in a field}* Ireneus hereby understands, Christ hidden in the Scriptures, *per typos et parabolas* (*l.* 4 *c.* 43 *Init.*) by types and parables: it is rather the truth of Christ therein, which few find, and so understand not how great and excellent these spiritual riches are, verse 45, as men know not the value of a hidden treasure.

*{he hideth}* Not lest others also should find it, but lest himself should lose it: lest men, or Satan should beguile him of it: also good men, hearing the doctrine of God’s mercy in Christ, take care to lay it up in their heart, that they may not be deprived of it. See Luke 2.15. Psal. 119.11.

*{selleth all that he hath}* This is to show, that no man can be partaker of this treasure, unless he value it above all worldly possessions, and be willing to part with all that he has, rather than miss thereof. Luke 14.33. Philippians 3.8,9.

**V. 45. ***{seeking goodly pearls}* This parable shows, that men should seek heavenly knowledge with as much affection, labor, cost, and peril, if need be, as merchants do seek the choicest pearls; in the former, the treasure was found unlooked for; here it is sought: the first seems to relate to God’s free mercy, found of them that sought him not. Rom. 10.20. The second, to man’s duty, chap. 7.7.

**V. 46. ***{one pearl of great price}* The knowledge of Christ, which far exceeds all other sciences, Phil. 3.8, and is the only necessary thing, Luke 10.42. Phil. 3.13,14.

**V. 47. ***{like unto a net}* This parable is to the same purpose with that of the tares: the word *σαγήνη* is that which we call a seine, probably from thence coming, *a draw net*, that sweeps along with it all that comes into the way, as well trash as fish: it was a fit similitude for some of the Apostles, whom of fishermen, he made fishers of men, chap. 4.19.

**V. 48. ***{cast the bad away}* There are many in the Church, which are not of the Church, 1 John 2.19, and therefore in the end shall be cast out: but the full and perfect cleansing of the Church from them, is deferred unto the last day. *οὐ* *γὰρ* *πᾶσα* *παραβολὴ* *ἐν* *τῇ* *γραφῇ* *ὅλη*, *λαμβάνεται*. No similitude in Scripture is wholly applicable. Epiphanius. *haeres.* 77 *N.* 23.

**V. 49. ***{So shall it be}* Our Savior applies only the end of the parable: so that we need not inquire what is meant by the sea and shore.

**V. 50. ***{and shall cast them}* See verse 42. He tells not what shall become of good men in the Church: none may doubt but they shall be happy.

**V. 51. ***{all these things}* Do you understand all these, &c. the parables I have not expounded, as well as those I have expounded? Their desire of exposition might occasion this question. See 36verse 10,36. Hereby we are taught, that all we hear is lost, if we understand it not.

**V. 52. ***{Therefore}* Seeing you have profited so well, I admonish you to teach others that which I have taught you: you must not know only for yourselves, but for others also: the sun shines not for itself: the trees bear not fruit for themselves; the fountains send not forth sweet streams for themselves, but others’ use. See 1 Cor. 12.7. Chap. 5.14,15.

*{every scribe}* Ministers are compared to scribes, who use to teach the people, (chap. 7.29. Ezra 7.6,11) as well as to write. See on Luke 20.1.

*{instructed in the kingdom of heaven}* Instructed or learned in the Gospel, and fit to be a minister of the Gospel.

*{things new and old}* Who lays up provisions of divers years, and brings them forth for the use of the family, according to their several occasions, and appetites. See Cant. 7.13. So must a minister not only teach truth, but so, as may fit all men’s capacities, and conditions, 2 Tim. 2.15.

**V. 53. ***{He departed thence}* Out of the house mentioned, verse 36. Christ being the Savior of all the elect, would not spend all his time and strength in one place, Luke 4.43.

**V. 54. ***{And when}* Mark 6.1. Luke 4.16.

*{into his own country}* Nazareth, where his mother dwelt, Luke 1.26, and he was bred, though not born there, Matth. 2.23.

*{whence hath this man this wisdom?}* He being never brought up to learning. See John 7.15.

*{these mighty works}* That is, power to do such works: there shined in Christ, both an incomprehensible depth of wisdom in his speeches, and a divine power in his works: which should have persuaded them to have believed in him: it seems he wrought some miracles at Nazareth, though few, verse 8. Mark 6.5.

**V. 55. ***{Is not this the carpenter’s son?}* John 6.41. He is of mean parentage, and unlearned: not brought up in the ordinary schools of the prophets; whence has he this profound and admirable knowledge? Verse 56.

*{his brethren}* His kinsmen, Genes. 13.8. See chap. 12.46,47.

**V. 56. ***{his sisters}* His kinswomen, chap. 12.50.

*{whence then hath this man}* Thus men are ready to lay stumbling blocks in their own way, (as oft as anything proposed, or considered, exceeds their carnal reason) to bar them from heaven.

**V. 57. ***{they were offended in him}* Or *at him, or with him*; they should have blessed God, who gave such gifts to him, for their profit and advantage, but they were offended at it: so, when in the works of God, men look no higher than second causes, they usually stumble, and the good he does them, makes them worse, and so more damnable.

*{a prophet}* Mark 6.4. Luke 4.24. John 4.44.

*{save in his, &c.}* They who know him will not be reproved by him: we easily contemn, that which is familiar and known to us.

**V. 58. ***{not many mighty works}* See on verse 54.

*{because of their unbelief}* Our unbelief keeps many mercies away from us, and hinders us of many blessings. Mark 6.5 speaks more expressly: the present sense is, that albeit Christ gave them some tastes of his power and goodness, in that there he healed some that were infirm and sick, yet their incorrigible ingratitude stayed his hand, and left no place for greater mercies, which otherwise he was ready to give them. The incredulity and contumacy of sinners, do hinder many blessings from descending on themselves and others therein to have shared with them. Christ’s mercy as it were strove with their iniquity; but he is just as well as merciful, therefore when they would not be amended by that he had done, he justly leaves them to their own hardness of heart, to perish therein.

## CHAPTER 14

**Verse 1. ***{At that time}* When Jesus went about Galilee teaching, and doing miracles for the confirmation of that which he taught.

*{Herod}* The second, here mentioned, was called Antipas. See Mark 6.14. Luke 9.7. There were three Herods, the first was Herod the Great, Antipater’s son, who is also called Ascalonius, in whose reign Christ was born; he caused the children to be slain, Matth. 2. The second was called Antipas, son of Herod the Great, whose mother’s name was Malthaca, or Martace; he was tetrarch of Galilee, Luke 3.1. The third, was Herod Agrippa, nephew to Herod the Great by Aristobulus: he slew James, Acts 12.2. So were they all three murderers.

*{the Tetrarch}* He had a fourth part of the kingdom. See on Luke 3.1. His father, Herod the Great, divided his kingdom by will, to his three sons: to Archelaus, he assigned the middle part, Judea and Idumea: to Philip Iturea, and the region of the Traconites: to this Herod Galilee; but Augustus the Roman Emperor, (because the very name of kings were in suspicion with them) otherwise divided the kingdom (that he might the more easily distract their powers, and keep them in subjection) into *tetrarchies*, or governments of four parts. Joseph *Antiq. l.* 17 *c.* 10,13. See Plin. *nat. hist. l.* 5 *c.* 18. The name is Greek, though of Roman use: *τετραρχία* says Suidas, is, *οἱ* *τέσσαρες* *λόχοι* four bands of sixty-four soldiers, whose captain was called *τετράρχης*, a tetrarch: so happily Demosthenes called each fourth part of Thessaly, which was called *τετρὰς* a fourth, *τετραρχίαν* a tetrarchy, the word being compounded of *τετρὰς* and *ἀρχὴ*.

*{heard of the fame of Jesus}* That is, (as in Luke 9.7) all that Jesus did; which being so much noised abroad, even while John Baptist was yet living, it may seem strange, how Herod should be so long ignorant thereof; but that we may consider his carelessness in religion; the too common policy of courtiers, unwilling to commend or speak of good men (specially to impious princes) lest seeming to favor them, they should endanger their favor at court: and lastly, Herod’s great distractions; yet through all these obstructions, now at last the fame of Jesus breaks into Herod’s ears: which the Evangelist therefore mentions, that it may appear that the preaching of the Gospel was not so obscure, but that the knowledge thereof came to the ears of kings and princes, that none might pretend ignorance.

**V. 2. ***{said to his servants}* It was said so first of some others, and then Herod terrified with a guilty conscience, easily believing what he feared, followed their vain opinion. See on Luke 9.7.

*{he is risen from the dead}* Syr. *from among the dead*; *מן* *בית* *מיתא* *men beith maithe*. It may be translated, as some give it; *ex domo mortuorum*, from the house of the dead (in and to the same sense, (or——*ab inter mortuos*——so *בית* the preposition signifies——some note here Herod’s opinion of John’s sanctity as concurring with the Pharisees, who thought that the holy did easily return to life again: see Joseph *Antiq. l.* 18 *c.* 2. The meaning is, as if Herod had said, he has more power now than he ever had, for John wrought no miracles, John 10.41. Possibly Herod might now fear some great practices in way of revenge, of his late murdering John Baptist. See on Luke 5.7.

*{mighty works}* Force and power, whereby great works are wrought. See chap. 12.54.

*{do shew forth themselves}* Or *are wrought by him*. Which like some divine light declare him risen from the dead, and that God is with him, to do some great and strange works by him. Herod could fear, but not repent. Note what childish fancies involve and befool men, when once they cast off the fear of the Lord: what panic and irrational fears terrify those, whom God, for their sins, smites with folly and madness, Deut. 28.28.

**V. 3. ***{For Herod}* See chap. 4.12. Luke 3.19,20.

*{laid hold on}* Or *caused him to be apprehended*, by his officers: how long that was before this time, is not certain.

*{his brother Philips wife}* Many faults did Herod commit: first, that he took another man’s wife, while he was yet alive. Secondly, that he put away his own wife. Thirdly, that he took his brother’s wife. Fourthly, in that when his brother had children by her, he yet took her to wife, which had not been lawful, if his brother had been dead: so blind and precipitate is filthy lust, such concatenations have sins, they seldom go alone. Herod’s last sin was his worst, he being reproved, *added yet this above all, that he shut up John in prison*, Luke 3.19,20.

**V. 4. ***{It is not lawful for thee to have}* Levit. 18.6, and 20.21. Observe John’s confidence; he does not say, it may be dangerous unto you, but speaks home, *it is not lawful for thee*; though you are a king or governor, you may not dispense with God’s laws.

**V. 5. ***{when he would have put him to death}* This shows that he was willing to kill him, before Herodias provoked him: so hateful is reproof to impious great men.

*{he feared the multitude}* He feared man, but not God. Mark adds, that he feared John, (whom he then had in prison) because indeed his conscience told him, that John was guiltless: the truth is, he feared the vengeance of God on the persecutors of God’s ministers, otherwise he would not have feared the people, who feared his tyranny. It is clear, that at first, Herod heard and reverenced John: and though possibly Herodias solicited him to that murder, to which himself was inclined too much, yet fear did for a time restrain him; yet at last, the sin broke out, thus this court-fox, Luke 13.32, made semblance of one thing, but acted another: and surely he desired to see Jesus, (Luke 9.9) with as good devotion as his father did, when he pretended a desire to worship him, Matth. 2.8.

*{because they counted him a Prophet}* Chap. 21.26. He feared the multitude in respect of their general good opinion they had of John, whom many suspected to be their Messiah. See Luke 3.15. John 1.20,25. And there had now been no prophets since Malachi, who prophesied of this very John Baptist, the second Elias, to be the immediate forerunner of the Messiah, Christ.

**V. 6. ***{When Herod’s birthday was kept}* The birthdays of kings used to be kept with great solemnity, feasts and triumphs, Gen. 40.20. That which some conjecture here, is not improbable; 37that all this tragedy was but an impious compact between Herod and Herodias, that John’s death should be deferred, only to Herod’s birthday; when many peers and great men being present, the daughter of Herodias should dance, Herod promised anything which she should ask: she being fore-instructed, must ask John’s head, Herod must seem very sorrowful, but at last (for a pretended conscience made of performing his oath) do what he had promised, were it never so damnable.

*{the daughter of Herodias}* By Philip her former husband, not by Herod. Josephus *Antiq. l.* 8 *c.* 7 calls her Salome.

*{danced before them}* Those lascivious gestures of a damsel, alone, at a feast, before so many men; may give us to consider of immodest dancing, to which few come sober, or go from it without some evil incentives: here was like mother, like daughter.

*{and pleased Herod}* Mark adds, and those that sate with him: these were composed to Herod’s mind, and knew how to applaud anything which the king commended: though indeed however it seemed to them, yet the circumstances considered, we may say with Theophylact *ὅσῳ* *καλῶς* *ὠρχεῖτο*, *τοσούτῳ* *κακῶς* the better she danced, the worse it was. Behold here the miserable depravation of impious minds: Herod is displeased with holy and wholesome preaching but pleased with the lascivious and impudent behavior of Salome: you may know what men are, by their delights.

**V. 7. ***{he promised with an oath}* Here is set down a mischievous effect of malice and intemperance; a rash and dangerous oath, with a promise so perniciously large, whatever she should ask; to the half of his kingdom, which he yet held at Caesar’s pleasure, and not long after lost. The word here interpreted, *promised* is properly used in mutual bargaining, covenanting, &c. wherein one demanded an oath, *do you promise thus or thus to do?* He is said *ὁμολόγειν* to assent or confess, and by such promise to oblige himself to perform: so, says Euthymius, the promise here made, he calls *ὁμολόγησιν*, which word often signifies an assent, bargain, &c.

*{Whatsoever she would ask}* He excepted not that which was inconvenient or unlawful. So, many in their jollity, rashly promise, what they repent of afterward, verse 9: or should indeed do so.

**V. 8. ***{before instructed}* Perhaps not before the feast: her mother possibly might be afraid to trust such a secret to her, long before it was to be acted: and Mark 6.24, she went out to ask her mother, after the king had promised.

*{give me here John Baptists head in a charger}* The mother was not content that John should be killed, but must have his head in a platter, as the most desired dish of that feast, that she might be sure of his death, and satiate herself with the sight of it: so devilish a sweetness is in revenge unto a harlot’s appetite: so quickly will those whelps of a fierce kind learn to be bloody, specially against faithful ministers who reprove them.

**V. 9. ***{the king was sorry}* Not so much for that he knew John was to be murdered, as because he feared an insurrection, and the just judgments of God following so impious an act; for it is not very likely that he was sorry to commit sin, who could not repent when it was committed: but fear of his own safety made him sorry that he had such occasion to hazard himself, or the opinion of constancy and keeping his word or performing his promise, made before so many witnesses then present.

*{for the oaths sake}* He made no conscience of incest and murder, yet would be thought he made conscience of an oath, unlawfully made, and more unlawfully performed, as if the same God which forbids perjury, did not also forbid incest and murder. See Jam. 2.10,11.

*{and them which sate with him, &c.}* Either he feared they would account him a perjured man; or they in favor to Herodias pressed him to perform his oath.

**V. 10. ***{and beheaded John in prison}* Not in public, for fear of the people, verse 5. John’s trial was the more, because he had the means to clear his innocency before the people, at his death, not any man to comfort him; this God permitted, that he might the more gloriously crown this just man, and leave an example of comfort, to those who suffer unjustly, Euthym. Yet observe, that God, who will have the memory of the just sacred, leaves John’s justification, and Herod’s time-outlasting infamy upon a record, so public, as shall be remembered while the Gospel is preached: and so certain, as is the testimony of the Holy Ghost, the Author of the Scripture, 2 Pet. 1.19.

**V. 11. ***{his head was brought in a charger}* A sad sight at a feast, but that which the revengeful most hunger for; there is no command of great men so impious, but it shall find servants to execute it.

*{she brought it to her mother}* This execrable reward of her dancing, is presented according to command to her: a chaste and holy virgin would have startled at the sight, but she carries it boldly, as insulting over her mother’s enemy, rendering up to her, these bloody gains: what just revenge followed all these actors, see Josephus *Antiq. l.* 18 *c.* 9. See verse 12.

**V. 12. ***{and told Jesus}* Either that he might prevent the like danger; or that they might be taught by him what course to take, now their master was gone: this was the end of John’s former sending to Christ, chap. 11, that they might at last be entered into his school: the disciples of John give him an honest and civil burial; either taking up the body cast out, (the Gr. *σῶμα*, the Syriac gives *שלדה*——*Sheladeh* the carcass, trunk, or body, as some say without a head, if we may believe Nicephorus: and Euthymius seems of the same opinion) or else by permission of the tyrant, as Joseph and Nicodemus afterwards did to the sacred body of Jesus: this last honor contributes nothing to the happiness of the dead; yet is it a testimony of our charity; and hope of the future resurrection: the end of Herod’s cruelty, was first the slaughter of his army by King Aretha, revenging the injury and dishonor of his daughter Herod’s lawful wife, whom he put away, for a harlot’s sake. Secondly, his own deposition and banishment to Lyons in France, with his incestuous Herodias, where, after much misery endured, he made away himself.

**V. 13. ***{When Jesus heard it}* Mark 6.32. Luke 9.10. Of John’s death, or of Herod’s knowledge of him and his miracles.

*{he departed thence}* From his own country; chap. 13.53, or from the place where he was told of John’s death.

*{into a desert place apart}* From the parts of Galilee, more frequented, to a more solitary place, to shun Herod’s laying wait for him: for the time of his death was not yet come. So chap. 4.12, and 12.14,15.

*{they followed him on foot}* For both places were on the same side of the lake: he only went over a creek by ship, and the people went about on foot: note their zeal, they go as soon as they hear he was gone: they go a great way about on foot; leaving businesses, families, and cities of good accommodation, to follow Christ in desert places.

**V. 14. ***{And Jesus went forth}* Out of the ship, and saw the people there before him, Mark 6.33. Such haste made they.

*{he healed their sick}* They carried sick folk also with them, which could not be without much trouble: nothing hindered them from Christ.

**V. 15. ***{When it was evening}* Mark 6.35. John 6.5. That is, the first evening, when the sun was much declined, about three of the clock in the afternoon, mentioned, 1 King. 18.29, and the second evening, verse 23, which began at sunset: between these two evenings, they used to kill the Passover, Exod. 12.6.

*{This is a desert place}* Neither time nor place are fit for provisions.

*{The time is now past}* Dinner time, or the usual hour to provide, is past. There may be many reasons why Christ, who could have done this miracle at any hour, would thus long defer it: as first, because of this delay, he might raise the disciples’ minds to a more serious attention and marking so clear and express a remonstrance of his divine power. Secondly that it might appear, that he did not rashly, but on urgent necessity put forth his power of doing miracles: for indeed he was not at all desirous of gain, or ambitious of human applause. Thirdly, that the hungry multitude might more deeply apprehend the greatness of the miracle, by their own sense of necessity in which they now were. Fourthly, that this miracle, might appear so much the more illustrious, by how much greater the supply must be to satisfy so many, so hungry stomachs. Lastly, that he might plainly demonstrate to them and us, that he has power ever ready, and providence ever watching to relieve those that depend on him: which he will do when he knows ’tis most opportune and best.

*{send the multitude away}* They were tied by the ears, in so much that they neglected their food: therefore the disciples solicit Christ to dismiss them: but nowadays men think long till the sermon be done: who take it to be a great merit, and point of religion, patiently to endure a sermon of an hour.

**V. 16. ***{give ye them to eat}* Christ by feeding such a multitude with so little food, intimates that they shall want nothing, which lay aside all businesses for God’s service, and first seek the kingdom of heaven, chap. 6.33.

**V. 17. ***{We have here but five loaves and two fishes}* Comparing their short provision, with the multitude of people, they marveled why he should command them to feed them.

**V. 18. ***{bring them hither}* They say not, what shall we then eat ourselves? But readily obey him: so the woman of Sarepta, 1 King. 17.12,13,15.

*{to me}* You cannot make a little sufficient to feed such a multitude; but I can.

**V. 19. ***{to sit down on the grasse}* That they may more conveniently be served, and their number more easily appear. See on Luke 9.15.

*{looking up to heaven}* To show whence his power was to do this miracle: (and whence we must look for supplies. See 38Psal. 145.15) says Hilary. *in Matth. canon.* 14.

*{he blessed}* He gave thanks to God for that provision, and prayed for a miraculous blessing upon it that it might be sufficient for the multitude, and that God’s divine power and mercy, beneficence and glory might appear to them therein.

*{and brake}* As the master of a family, or chief person in the company used to do, that it might be distributed. See on Luke 24.35.

**V. 20. ***{They did all eat and were filled}* This is added lest any (to extenuate the miracle) should think that they did not all eat, or that they were not all satisfied: so admirable an increase was here of a little food, that a great multitude of guests were so well satisfied, that they left abundance more than they found at first. See on Luke 9.17.

*{took up of the fragments}* The remainder of the fragments: this showed the truth of the miracle, as also the greatness.

*{twelve baskets full}* See on chap. 15.37. For each Apostle one basket: these baskets were such as they used to carry bread, and other victuals in, chap. 16.7. Mark 8.14. So admirably did these viands increase: I know not (says Hilar. *s. s.*) whether on the place of the tables, or in the receivers’ hands, &c. but admirably were they increased: how should it teach us confidence in God, and contentment in a little?

**V. 21. ***{they that had eaten}* He says not, they that were present, but they that had eaten, were about five thousand: they are numbered that the miracle might be the more illustrious.

*{beside women and children}* Which probably were a very great accession to this multitude.

**V. 22. ***{Straightway Jesus constrained his, &c.}* That is, his attendants in ordinary; not all that came to learn of him. See chap. 12.14, and 15.36, and 17.25. They were willing to have stayed with Christ, and not to leave him alone in a desert, near night: but yet to prevent their joining with the people to make him a king, John 6.15, he compelled them to go before.

*{unto the other side}* See note on verse 13.

**V. 23. ***{when he had sent}* Mark 6.46. For that present, taking leave of them, blessing and praying for them, wishing them now to depart. See Joh. 6.22, &c.

*{into a mountain apart to pray}* Christ choosing solitudes for prayer, shows us the danger of distraction and deviation of thoughts in prayer; and how necessary it is to choose convenient places to private prayers.

*{And when the evening was come}* John 6.16. The second evening at and after sunset. See on verse 15.

**V. 24. ***{in the midst of the sea}* Many things concurring, make this trial the greater: it was a great storm, it was dark, John 6.17. The storm continued till the last watch of the night: Christ was absent, they knew not when he would come to relieve them: thus says Chrysostom *hom.* 51 *in Matth.* 14, he would by degrees accustom them to endure great matters: in the first danger at sea, chap. 8, he was present, though asleep, they could quickly awake him: but now absent, and that very long.

*{tossed with waves}* *βασανιζόμενον*, tormented, laboring with contrary winds, and rough seas; it is a metaphorical expression taken from men tormented (for *βάσανοι* are torments, as Luke 16.23, the rich man being *ἐν* *βασάνοις* *in torments*) tumbling up and down for ease; such is a laborsome ship, heaving and setting in a storm.

**V. 25. ***{in the fourth watch}* They divided the night into four watches. See Mark 13.35. For so often in the night were the watches in garrisons, or armies in the field relieved, by fresh watchmen coming in their stead: now the fourth watch was the three last hours of the night (until sunrising) at which the morning watch began, mentioned Exod. 14.24. Christ who well knew in what a stress of wind and weather they were all night, and in how great danger of being all cast away, would not presently relieve them, as he could if he would, but so long exercise their faith and patience, as he knew should best conduce to God’s honor, and their future comfort in troubles and dangers.

*{Jesus went unto them}* He came unto them walking on the water, showing himself to be Lord of the sea also; as he did Exod. 14.22. We may endure sore stresses, and mighty tempests; yet Christ will never forsake us, if we go when and whither he commands us.

**V. 26. ***{saw him walking on the sea}* Walking up and down, as on dry ground: all the creatures own, and in their several kinds, obey that word of their Creator, which at first gave them being and their laws of nature: the deaf sea is sensible of his power and presence yielding and submitting her swelling waves, even themselves for him to walk on; only man, is unthankfully rebellious against him, and will not acknowledge him. See Isaiah 1.3. Jer. 8.7.

*{they were troubled}* For they had never seen the like before: they feared this apparition, as they did the storm.

*{it is a spirit}* *φάντασμα* of *φαίνεσθαι*, to appear or seem, a *spectrum*, that which a man sees, or vainly imagines that he sees: an apparition: thus fear and forgetfulness of Christ’s power discomposes and troubles our minds: they could not know Christ approaching.

**V. 27. ***{Straightway Jesus spake unto them}* Christ quickly hears the cries of his servants; and speaks comfort to them, except the cause of his delay be in themselves.

*{be of good cheer}* *Be confident*: fear often arises out of want of confidence rather than the greatness of the danger, which others endure with less trouble of mind.

*{it is I}* He does not say, *I am Jesus*, as to persecuting Saul, Act. 9.5, but *it is I*: as speaking to those who well knew his voice.

**V. 28. ***{bid me come unto thee on the waters}* If Christ commanded, he was confident that he also could enable him thereto: true believers are soon comforted with Christ’s voice.

**V. 29. ***{he walked on the water}* This shows an admirable confidence, and strength of faith in Peter, that upon one word of Christ, durst adventure to walk on a cursed sea.

**V. 30. ***{when he saw the wind}* That is, perceived it, by hearing and feeling the effects thereof: for otherwise he could not see it, John 3. So dangerous is it, to look too much on second causes, and neglect the first: here our faith begins to fail, and we to sink.

*{boisterous}* Strong, or vehement.

*{Lord save me}* By faith he trod under feet the seas, by diffidence he began to sink, and found what need he had of Christ’s assistance to save him.

**V. 31. ***{why didst thou doubt?}* What cause have you to doubt, seeing I am with you? This shows your faith weak: the strongest faith has need of help: it nothing advantages to be near Christ in place, if you are not so in faith, says Chrysostom *hom.* 51 *in Matth.*

**V. 32. ***{the wind ceased}* Christ not only comforts his in trouble, but also puts an end to their troubles: yet the storm did not presently cease when Jesus came in sight, and spoke to them: but when he came to them into the ship: their faith was not presently awaked, and exercised enough; it is not without just cause, that God does so often delay our deliverance from fears and troubles, though he knows them before we cry unto him.

**V. 33. ***{they that were in the ship}* The rest of the Apostles, other passengers, or the seamen that carried them.

*{came and worshipped him}* They blame not Peter for attempting, nor commend him for walking on the sea; but come directly to worship him, who had delivered them: they had seen lately a great miracle in his feeding the multitude, yet did they not worship him then: affliction taken away, sooner brings men to acknowledge Christ, than prosperity given.

**V. 34. ***{when they were gone over}* Mark 6.53.

*{the land of Gennesaret}* This lake of Gennesaret was nigh to Capernaum, which is also called the sea of Galilee, and Tiberias, so that the country itself took denomination from it. See on John 21.1.

**V. 35. ***{had knowledge of him}* By fame, report, or such of them as had seen his miracles.

*{they sent out into all that country}* They would not engross Christ to themselves, but inform others of his coming, that they might obtain health of body and soul of him also: this was their charity, that they wished their neighbors the same good they had attained themselves.

**V. 36. ***{the hem of his garment}* The lower part or skirt thereof: this was a sign of modesty, Matth. 9.20. The word signifies a selvage, or fringe.

*{they besought him}* That which the poor woman did not, chap. 9.20,21. Herein they show their faith and humility, as they had done their charity.

*{made perfectly whole}* By the divine power of Christ: in that he heals the sick, we are taught to seek help of him, in all our distresses: and not only to run to him ourselves, but also to bring others to him, that they may be saved: there is no malady of body or soul so great, but that by his power he can cure it; nor so little, but in his mercy he will. Some think there was a tincture of superstition in that they desired to touch the hem of his garment, that they might be cured, as restraining Christ’s power and grace thereto; but this seems not so probable as to be avowed, seeing that Christ’s power to heal (the woman of the bloody issue, Matth. 9.21, who thought, as these here) concurred with such a desire: one thing is certain, that lest he should have quenched the smoking flax, he mercifully complies with their weakness, healing their sick, and curing their infirm.

## CHAPTER 15

**Verse 1. ***{Then came to Jesus}* After these forementioned miracles: the more famous God’s ministers grow, the greater is the envy of the adversaries: the Scribes and Pharisees left Jerusalem, to pursue Christ into Galilee, Mark 7.1, 39which shows their unwearied and vain malice against the truth of Christ.

*{Scribes and Pharisees}* In these great pretenders to the law of God, it appears, that none more dangerously wound religion, than those, who would before men, seem the most zealous patrons, and professors thereof.

*{which were of Jerusalem}* Not of lower towns, or less villages: the more learned and honorable hypocrites are, the more dangerous enemies they are to Christ and the Gospel; though there were Scribes and Pharisees in every tribe of Israel, yet they of Jerusalem being more proud, and injurious to Christ (as trusting on their wealth, greatness and popularity) were the most impious. Isai. 1.21. That faithful city was become a harlot.

**V. 2. ***{Why do thy disciples transgress?}* They come not to dispute the case, or to tax the disciples only, but to quarrel with Christ himself: as if they said, you so teach or tolerate them.

*{tradition of the elders}* Which we have received from our ancestors, who were holy men: or which the governors of our Church (whom we ought obey) require of us: they saw the great zeal of the people following Jesus, which the more their envy labored to quench, the more they enflamed it; so that all their artifices, and plots turned to their disadvantage, nor could they find anything justly to object against Jesus, therefore they cavil at his disciples.

*{for they wash not their hands}* The law of God enjoined no such necessity of often washings; but the elders had brought in this tradition. See Mark 7.

**V. 3. ***{Why do ye also}* Christ answers the Pharisees by way of recrimination, satisfying the multitude, verse 10,11, and the disciples more plainly, verse 15,16,17. So he repels their malicious calumny about things of no real moment, by detecting their hypocrisy, who would seem to make conscience of transgressing man’s laws, but not God’s.

*{transgress the commandments of God}* *παραβαίνω*, the word here used, signifies to go beyond one’s bounds, which are indeed the precepts of God’s law, the one straight line of sanctity, from which if any man decline to the right hand or to the left, he sins; concerning which, God said in the mystery on Mount Sinai: Exod. 19.21——*Charge the people that they break not their bounds*, Heb. *פן*——[*יהרסו*?], *ne forte destruant*——*הרס* signifies *diruit, destruxit*, &c. Targ. *ne——irruant ad Dominum*, as presuming transgressors use to do, until they meet with God’s judgments to destroy them——as Balaam with the angel, Numb. 22.22, &c. Most damnable is the transgression of God’s laws, under pretence of godliness.

*{by your tradition}* *διὰ* *τὴν* *παράδοσιν*, which the Syriac gives *מטל* *metul, propter traditiones vestras*, that you may maintain your tradition, which they called their *קבלה* *cabbala* which was says V. Schindler. *Pentagl. doctrina patrum quam* (*קבל* *cibel*) *accepit unus de ore alterius, et tradidit posteris*, the doctrine of the fathers, which one received from the mouth of another, and the same delivered to posterity; this was that contained in their *תלמוד* *talmud*, which was *doctrinalis farrago*, a great collection of all their doctrines and traditions: these he called theirs, though received from the elders; because they superstitiously held them against God’s law: and with that gross pride, that therein they were the most holy and unreprovable in the world in their own conceit; being of foolish Ticonius’ mind (Augustin. *ep.* 48, and *lib. To.* 7 *contra epist. Parm. l.* 2 *c.* 13 *fin.*) who said, *quod volumus, sanctum est*, that which we will, is holy.

**V. 4. ***{God commanded saying}* In this particular, our Savior proves the charge he gave them, concerning their transgression of God’s commandments.

*{honour thy father, and &c.}* Exod. 20.12. Deut. 5.16. By honor is meant all kind of duty, obedience, reverence, love, beneficence, protection, maintenance: by father and mother, natural parents, princes, governors, magistrates, ministers and superiors.

*{he that curseth father or mother}* Exod. 21.17. Levit. 20.9. Prov. 20.20. Gr. *κακολογῶν*, which imports not only execration, cursing or imprecation of some evil; but also any evil, reproachful, undutiful or irreverent speaking, to the parents’ injury, or dishonor.

*{let him die the death}* Our Savior instances in a precept which has the promise to the observers thereof, Ephes. 6.2, and an annexed curse to the transgressors, to show them, and all men, the odiousness of this sin before God. *θανάτῳ* *τελευτάτω*, Syr. *ממת* *ימות* *memath iemuth* (Tremel.) let him certainly, unpardonably, or unexcusably be put to death: with them this ingemination imports a vehemency and certainty, as Gen. 2.17, *in the day thou eatest thereof*, *מות* *תמות*, *moth thamuth*, 70. *θανάτῳ* *ἀποθανεῖσθε*, *dying thou shalt die*; that is, surely die. Unworthy is that unnatural creature of life, who is injurious to those of whom he received life, who so impiously requites their tender care and pains for him.

**V. 5. ***{it is a gift}* Mark 7.11,12. You teach that a man may vow to give all that he has to the temple, although he have poor parents wanting relief; and after that, he need not, or ought not to relieve them: or to say to their parents, that which I have offered to God, will do you as much good, as if I had therewith relieved you. See on Luke 21.4.

*{by me}* *ἐξ* *ἐμοῦ*, *out of me*, that is, out of my estate.

**V. 6. ***{and honour not}* Though he honor not father or mother. Though he perform not that duty of a child toward his parents, which the law of God enjoins him, Exod. 20.12, &c.

*{of none effect}* You neglect it, as if it were of no force; they did not deny it to be a commandment, but upon this matter absolved children from obedience thereto. Gr. *ἠκυρώσατε* *ἀκυρόω*, is properly, *omni authoritate et imperio spolio; irritum reddo*, I deprive of all authority, command and power: I make void, or of no binding effect: so did they God’s commandment, which by imposition of their traditions, they declared to be of no force, as to a necessary obedience.

**V. 7. ***{ye hypocrites}* He condemns them of hypocrisy, because they made the kingdom of God to stand in outward things; and because under a pretence of worshiping God, they overthrew the power of true godliness. See on Luke 13.15.

*{prophesy of you}* Not of them in particular, but of all hypocrites in general: as if he had said, to you may those words of the prophet fitly be applied.

**V. 8. ***{this people}* Isa. 29.13. The Jews, the then peculiar people of God; amongst them the priests and the prophets, the teachers especially, were dissemblers in religious acts. See Isa. 28.7. Hos. 9.8. Jer. 2.8.

*{draweth nigh unto me with their mouth}* He describes their hypocrisy by two characters. 1. That in words, countenance and gesture, they made great show and profession of a reverend and most religious serving of God; not that all these external things were not convenient and requirable in the worship of God; but because these only were so far from being enough, that they were double iniquity, in that they made them mere cloaks of sin; 2. That in these fair external shows, they wanted in their hearts, true faith, repentance, love and fear of God.

**V. 9. ***{in vain do they worship me}* Christ renders here the true sense though not the words of the prophet, for albeit (*in vain*) be not in the Hebrew text; yet God’s denunciation of judgments to come upon these pretenders to sanctity, easily concludes that they worshiped him in vain: that is not all: but rather depraved and polluted his worship and service; by making it serve as a disguise to their filthy sins. *quomodo Deum violat, qui hoc modo placat?* Minuc. Fel. *Octav.* How does he violate a holy Deity, who thus endeavors to pacify him? Doctrines and traditions of men are not the oracles of God, neither is the observation of them his worship.

*{teaching for doctrines the commandments of men}* Teaching their disciples and hearers, to observe and keep their precepts, however inconsistent they were with God’s commandments.

**V. 10. ***{and he called the multitude}* Mark 7.14. He lets go the malicious Pharisees, and informs the better affected people, that might possibly be offended at the fact of his disciples, or corrupted by the false doctrine of the Pharisees: the Pharisees he saw were incurable, and applies himself to the people, who were flexible and inclined to learn.

*{hear and understand}* We must at all times open our hearts and ears to receive God’s word; but this preface is here most necessary, because men are hardly taken off from old traditions, and common and received opinions.

**V. 11. ***{not that which goeth into the mouth}* Your meat and drink moderately taken, and thankfully used do not hurt your soul, (for every creature of God is good, &c. 1 Tim. 4.4) though you do not with superstitious Pharisees so often wash your hands; but your evil thoughts and words do hurt you.

*{defileth a man}* *κοινοῖ*, that is, renders one unclean or impure: so is it expounded, Acts 10.15. Christ takes this word from the custom of the law, to apply to the spiritual estate of man: by the law, a man was to be esteemed, unclean and defiled, who had touched anything, which the law counted unclean, as a dead body, &c. Now, a thing which is *common* (in relation to God’s service) is opposed to sacred; therefore, as they reckoned all things sacred (or set apart to the service of God) holy: so they esteemed all things of common use profane, and such as defiled them: hence were their frequent washings for their pretended purification. Mark 7.1, &c.

**V. 12. ***{knowest thou, &c.?}* We see in their countenances, and hear by their words, that they are vexed at you, or it is certain they are displeased, &c. See Exod. 10.7. 2 King. 2.3,5.

*{were offended}* More alienated from you and your doctrine: as John 6.60,66.

*{after they heard this saying}* Or, *when they heard*, as Ephesians 1.13.

**V. 13. ***{Every plant}* John 15.2. All doctrines, traditions or persons.

*{shall be rooted up}* By a metaphor taken from plants (which being plucked up by the roots, quickly wither and come to nought) he shows what shall become of hypocrites which abide not in Christ, John 15.4. Matth. 3.10. Their hopes shall vanish, and shall come to nothing: or if it be understood of doctrine, the sense may be, it 40ought to be rooted out by bringing men to the knowledge of the truth: this agrees best with the scope of the place.

**V. 14. ***{Let them alone}* If they will not be reformed, or better informed, let us take no further care for them but endeavor to undeceive those whom they have misled; lest, they perish with them: or, do not you hearken to them, lest like blind guides, they lead you hoodwinked to hell. See Rev. 22.11. A dreadful doom, Hosea 4.14. *I will not punish your daughters when they commit whoredom.*

*{blind leaders of the blind}* Luke 6.39.

*{both fall into the ditch}* It is a misery to be blind, but to be led by the blind, or to be a blind leader of the blind, as Euthymius says well, is certainly *duplex malum*, a double mischief: a mischief to those poor souls, who have such ignorant leaders, and a greater misery to such wretched guides as those, who, for secular gains, or honors, take upon them to teach others, and to lead them in the narrow way to salvation, who have not well learned the same themselves: these are (as Cyprian said, *l.* 1 *ep.* 7) *non fidei duces, sed perfidiae magistri*, &c. Not guides to faith, but masters of perfidiousness. See 1 Tim. 1.6,7. Deut. 27.18. Isaiah 42.19, and 9.10, and 59.10. Lam. 4.13,14. Ezek. 3.17,18, and 14.9,10.

**V. 15. ***{then answered Peter}* Mark 7.17.

*{this parable}* That sentence, verse 11. See chap. 13.3. That which you said concerning the difference of meats to be taken away: this he understood as figuratively spoken (whereas it was indeed literal) in which he was afterward more fully instructed by an express vision. Acts 10. He calls it here *παραβολὴν*, a parable, according to the Hebrew manner, who called anything obscurely and prudently spoken, *משל* *maschal*; so are Solomon’s sayings called, *משלי* *שלמה* *mischle Schelomo*, the parables or proverbs of Solomon. See on Luke 5.36.

**V. 16. ***{are ye also without understanding}* No marvel if others understand me not, when you, whom I have so often instructed, do not.

**V. 17. ***{whatsoever entereth in at the mouth}* He does not say all; but whatsoever; that is, all kinds of meat, for of every kind some remains, in a good concoction, to nourishment; our meat comes not into the heart, where sin is hatched: nature keeps something good for nourishment, it sends away the rest; and therefore the species of wholesome meat cannot defile us.

**V. 18. ***{which proceed out of the mouth}* The heart being the fountain of thoughts, words and actions, is taken (after the custom of the Hebrews) for the understanding, will, and affections; and so being the principal seat of sin, it is that evil treasury whence all wickedness issues: and because the greatest part of sin, (as blasphemy, profaneness, lies, false witness, slandering, reviling, railing, obscenity, heretical doctrines, &c.) comes through, or is fomented by the tongue, all which render sinners polluted, and sinful before God and man: therefore, our Savior says, that those things which proceed out of the mouth (the interpreter of a wicked heart) defile the man, and make him guilty before God.

**V. 19. ***{out of the heart proceed evil thoughts}* Gen. 6.5, and 8.21. The Jews misled by the Pharisees, thought these to be no sins, Mat. 5.21,22,27,28. These therefore are put first to show, that all sins, even in thought only, defile the man.

**V. 20. ***{which defile a man}* That is, make him guilty before God: out of the premises he infers this conclusion; seeing these are things which pollute a man, the Pharisees’ maxim, (that to eat with unwashed hands is a sin) is a false calumny: so that Christ here teaches, 1. That the true worship of God consists in keeping his commandments: 2. That human traditions are but vain worship, save only where they are agreeing with, and subordinate to the worship of God commanded in his word: 3. That real pollution of a man is not in unwashed hands, or any such external matter, not forbidden in God’s word, but in the impurity of the heart, and the proceeds thereof, which ought to be cleansed by true faith and repentance, and otherwise cannot. See Acts 15.9.

**V. 21. ***{Then Jesus went thence}* Mark 7.24.

*{coasts of Tyre and Sidon}* Which bordered on Tyre and Sidon: that is, in that quarter where Palestine bends towards Phoenicia, and the sea of Syria.

**V. 22. ***{of Canaan}* Of the stock of the Canaanites which dwelt in Phoenicia, near the sea, Numb. 13.29, such were the Sidonians, Judg. 1.31, one of the posterity of the Canaanites which Joshua did not drive out. See Judg. 1.30,31.

*{have mercy upon me}* She counts her daughter’s misery her own; so strong was her motherly affection. And who knows not, that it is an evident mercy of God to parents, when he hears them for the recovery or relief of their sick, or otherwise afflicted, children? 1 King. 17.18. 2 Sam. 12.16.

*{thou Son of David}* You are the long expected Messiah, of the seed of David: the promise of a Messiah and redeemer, being much spoken of among the Jews, it is likely that the report thereof came to the ears of the neighboring nations: as also how Jesus had with so many admirable works, declared himself to be the Messiah: hence were such confluences of people to him, from those parts, Mark 3.8. Luke 6.17, which this woman also hearing of, comes on the behalf of her afflicted daughter.

**V. 23. ***{he answered her not a word}* When Christ delays, and seems not to regard the elect that cry to him, he does it, for his glory, their advantage and the good of others. Christ answers her not with his mouth, but speaks unto her by that sweet and secret voice of his Spirit to cry louder.

*{for she cryeth after us}* They plead for her, not so much out of charity, as to be freed from her importunity. So Luke 18.5.

**V. 24. ***{I am not sent, &c.}* Chap. 10.6, and 9.36. To whom the promise was specially made, Rom. 9.4, to whom he was first to be revealed, Acts 3.26, and 13.46, and with whom he did personally converse in his ministry, Rom. 15.8.

*{unto the lost sheep}* Who went astray and erred through ignorance and negligence of their pastors; who were dear unto God as his peculiar. See Psalm 74.1, and 79.13, and 95.7, and 100.3, and 119.176.

*{of the house of Israel}* Of the people of Israel, the remainders whereof were now divided into tribes; but all those tribes came out of Jacob’s house, who was called Israel.

**V. 25. ***{and worshipped him}* Showing her humility by her gestures; and the constancy of her faith, by renewing her petition. She will not be laid nay, or sit down either with silence or a sad answer believing, as a man may say with reverence, whether Christ will or no.

*{Lord help me}* Few words, but very forcible, Eccles. 5.1. *When thou comest before God, let thy words be few*, says Solomon. St. Peter calls us to be sober in prayer, 1 Pet. 4.7, without trifling or vain babbling, which the wise man calls the sacrifice of fools, the word properly signifies to run at one’s cries that calls for help, as the tender mother does to her hungry child when he stirs up his voice and cries lustily.

**V. 26. ***{it is not meet}* Before, he would not vouchsafe her any answer, verse 23; then he gave her a mild one, verse 24, but now he gave her a harsh denial with disgraceful terms, yet further to exercise her faith and patience.

*{the children’s bread}* I must do wonders for Israel, the children and peculiar people of God, not for the unconsiderable Canaanites, that were as dogs, in respect of Israel. The Israelites were before all nations of the earth, adopted God’s children, Exod. 4.22. Psal. 147.19,20. Isa. 1.2. Aliens from the covenant of grace, he calls dogs, as Phil. 3.2. See Psal. 22.16. Rev. 22.15. The sense is, that the Gentiles must be content to wait until God had refreshed the Israelites with grace and mercy.

**V. 27. ***{truth Lord, yet the dogs eat the crumbs}* There is enough falling from the table to feed and relieve us, the bread need not be cast unto us: your power and mercy is so great, that the healing my child can be no wrong to your Israel: though by your free covenant and promises to the Jews, the first place for mercy belongs to them; yet when the children are filled unto satiety, your mercy is sufficient and will extend itself to us, yet unconsiderable Gentiles also. The Syriac adds *וחאין*, *vechoin, and they live*: that is, that they also may live: the good master suffers not his dogs to starve; much less will you, the most contemptible creatures which wait on you: you will not deny me that which is not denied to dogs: so she compares the cure of her child, to the crumbs which fall from the table of mercy and beneficence, prepared for Israel, continuing her reply in the allegory which Christ began: as if she had said, that which I beg, in respect of the full and continual favor bestowed on Israel, is but as crumbs from a full table: you bestow on them spiritual and eternal blessings, the temporal you deny not the heathens: seeing all taste of your bounty, blessed Jesus, send me not away empty.

**V. 28. ***{then Jesus answered}* Having received her reply, declaring her admirable confidence in the mercy of Christ, and seeing her holy importunity, which would receive no nay; he would now no longer hold her in suspense, nor conceal his mercy, neither exercise her patience with any more dilatory answers.

*{O woman}* Thus he speaks, as it were admiring the victory of her faith, and applauding it to the multitude: therefore he had all the while seemed to repel her petition, that the consequences might suit to this word; that (says Chrysost.) *στεφανώσῃ* *τὴν* *γυναῖκα*, he might crown this woman.

*{great is thy faith}* He commends her faith, because that was the ground of this constancy of hers, which himself had given her: so accepts he and crowns in us, not our merits, but his own mercies.

*{be it unto thee}* This voice was, *συγγενὴς* *ἐκείνῃ* *τῇ* *φωνῇ*, of the same kind of voice, which said at first, *Let there be a heaven*, and it was made. Gen. 1.1. Chrysost. *s. s.*

*{even as thou wilt}* She intimated her daughter’s misery, but had not made her petition in particular, verse 22; now he bids her make her own choice. God will not deny such as be constant and fervent in prayer. Chap. 9.29.

**V. 29. ***{Jesus departed thence, and came nigh unto the sea of galilee}* Mark 7.31. Christ ceases not to be beneficial, even there, where he is contemned.

*{and sate down there}* Either to rest himself; or as chap. 14.23, that he might there pray: and also for the more conveniency of receiving them, whom he foresaw coming unto him with their sick and infirm.

**V. 30. ***{great multitudes came}* Isaiah 35.5,6. For as there were many sick and disaffected persons among them, so had they heard of his unwearied power and mercy curing all that came or were brought unto him for help.

*{those that were lame}* By disease, or inward, and inbred weakness, this shows their great charity and pains, that would carry these up the mountain to be healed, verse 29.

*{maimed}* *κυλλοὺς*, maimed: or those who had lost the use of their limbs, as by the palsy, or some disaffection of body, shrinking up the sinews, or weakening them to a kind of dead and helpless impotency: for we read not of any to whom our Savior restored such limbs, as formerly were by any casualty cut off or lost: though he could as easily have done that also, had it so pleased him.

*{cast them down at Jesus feet}* Note their humility and faith: we read of nothing they said, but only that they laid them before him, not doubting but he would heal them, who well knew the thoughts and desires of their hearts.

*{and he healed them}* So the Evangelist contracts the large story of his admirable power and mercy, here showed in curing all diseases into a few words: so chap. 8.16, and 14.35, that we may consider Christ’s immense power, and inexhausted goodness: but why he did so quickly cure all these, and so long delay the Canaanite if any ask, they may first, therein see how he did indeed give bread to the children to the full: and next how he teaches us, 1. That he most tried her faith, to whom he had given the greatest measure of faith: God proportions our trials to the measure of faith, which he gives us. 2. That when he delays his favors; it is to the final advantage of his, whose patience he will abundantly recompense at last.

**V. 31. ***{the multitude wondered}* Because those cures exceeded the course of nature, and the power of art.

*{and glorified God, &c.}* They were far better than the high-crested Pharisees, who attributed all Christ’s miracles to Satan: so bitterly injurious is malice and envy; they had rather the devil should have the honor of these great works, than Christ.

*{of Israel}* That is, the God whom Israel worships, as Euthymius well interprets; for otherwise he is not only the God of Israel, and the Jews, but of the Gentiles also. Rom. 3.29.

**V. 32. ***{then Jesus called his disciples}* Mark 8.1. See also chap. 14.22. By the following miracle, Jesus shows that he will never be wanting to them that follow him, no, not in a barren wilderness.

*{I have compassion on the multitude}* My bowels yearn towards them, the like he did before, chap. 14.14,19, which he now does again, to declare his indeficient mercy and power, never weary of supplying them who follow him; therefore he gives them food, whose sickness he had cured: he is still our high priest, who is touched with the feeling of our infirmities; Hebrews 4.15.

*{they continue with me now these three days}* If they had brought any food with them in that time it might be spent: neither did they leave Christ to buy them any: such was his unwearied mercy to instruct and cure them, and such their zeal and patience, as that it overcame their care for domestical affairs, and bodily supplies.

*{I will not send them away fasting}* Or, *I am not willing to send them away fasting*: the words express rather an affection, than a resolution to feed them: as chap. 8.23. See Mark 8.3, but the effect shows both.

*{lest they faint in the way}* Which intimates that some of them were so weakened with fasting, that they could not get home again without some refection: thus did Christ leave this following miraculous supply, to an extreme exigent; to teach us constancy in our dependence on him: though his help come sometimes slow, it will come surely and opportunely in our greatest need, and to our best advantage: so did it here for their present relief and comfort, as also for the future confirmation of their faith.

**V. 33. ***{whence should we have so much, &c.}* Or, *shall we have*: it seems they had forgot their former supply, chap. 14, where our Savior fed a greater number with fewer loaves and fishes, and more was left. See verse 34,37,38.

*{in the wilderness}* No food is here to be sold: see the like diffidence in Moses, Numb. 11.21,22, where, to man’s reason, there is the least appearance of supplies from the creature, there is God’s power and goodness most opportunely manifested in our supplies.

**V. 34. ***{how many loaves have ye?}* He knew before, but asks, that they might consider the greatness of the miracle, and the more clearly see it in the remoteness of all probable means to supply.

*{they said seven}* Because Christ withdrew himself, and his disciples often into the desert, where no victuals were to be bought or procured, the disciples used to carry some with them, (see chap. 16.7) though very frugal and slender.

**V. 35. ***{to sit down on the ground}* That they might expect refection from him, and be the more attentive to the miracle. See chap. 14.19.

**V. 36. ***{he took the seven loaves}* In sight of the people; that they might know there were no more, chap. 14.17. There were only five loaves and two fishes: but now seven, and few small fishes: the power of God to relieve us is tied to no means: much or little, few or many, are all one to him. See 1 Samuel 14.6.

*{and brake them}* That they might the better be distributed.

*{and gave to his disciples}* That they might see that done in their hands, which they thought impossible. See 2 Kings 7.2.

**V. 37. ***{of the broken meat}* See chap. 14.20.

*{seven baskets full}* It was a kind of basket wrought with twigs, of common use among the Jews, *σπυρίδας* the Syriac gives *אספרידין*, *asperidon*, which word both in the Greek and Syriac is used, Acts 9.25, for that basket, in which the disciples at Damascus, let down Paul by the wall.

**V. 38. ***{they that did eat}* See chap. 14.21. Herein was the majesty of the miracle, that there was no proportion between the men and the meat.

**V. 39. ***{and sent away the multitude}* Not without a blessing and a great deal of good counsel, *Labour not for the meat that perisheth*, &c. Now that you have eaten and are full, beware that you forget not the Lord your God, Deut. 8.10,11.

*{into the coasts of Magdala}* Whence some think Mary Magdalene had her name. See on Luke 8.2. Mark 8.10. It is said he came into the parts of Dalmanutha which was the name of a neighboring tract of land. Euthymius thinks that region had both these names: most probably, say others, they were so joined, as that they had fields, or commons lying together, so that when Christ was between them, or on the coast of either.

## CHAPTER 16

**Verse 1. ***{The Pharisees}* These would be one constant party against Christ. See Mark 8.11. Luke 12.54.

*{with the Sadducees}* With the Sadducees: of these two sects, see on chap. 3.7. Though they were at a great distance, in point of principles, and opinions one from another, yet they agree together against Christ, as Herod and Pilate, Luke 23.12. Acts 4.27. Yet against all their power and conspiracy Christ prevails: all unity is not good, but that which is in Christ.

*{tempting him}* Their purpose was to find some occasion to calumniate, and accuse him, chap. 19.3, and 22.18. Some think it was only to satisfy their own curiosity: but all other their attempts against him concluded them more seriously malicious.

*{a sign from heaven}* Such a one as Moses did, who fed our fathers with Manna from heaven, John 6.31. Thus they endeavored to obscure our Savior’s glory, as if he had not already showed them his divine and heavenly glory and power, in many former miracles exceeding all power of the creatures.

**V. 2. ***{when it is evening}* You are very careful, and skillful out of experience, to observe the usual signs of fair or foul weather to come.

**V. 3. ***{red, and lowring}* *στυγνάζων* sad: it is a metaphor borrowed from the countenance; we are said *στυγνάζειν*, when we are troubled at anything odious or grievous: so the sky is called sad or lowering, when it is overcast with clouds and threatens rain or storms. So the Latins said, *tristis hyems*, sad winter: so the Syriac gives it, *שמיא* *כמיראית* *Shemaio chemirioth, coelum triste*; so it translates *σκυθρωποί*, Matth. 6.16, and Luke 24.17, by the same word, which we gave, sad.

*{hypocrite}* See on Matth. 6.2, and on Luke 13.15.

*{ye can discern}* *γινώσκετε* *διακρίνειν* you know how to discern, the word *διακρίνειν*, signifies to *discern*, or *judge of*, also, among other significations, *probare, explorare*, by which the sense of this place may appear.

*{the face}* He puts here *πρόσωπον*, as the Hebrews used *פנים*, *panim*: for the outward show or appearance of anything (as Gen. 1.2. 1 Sam. 14.25. 2 Sam. 17.19, &c.) whereby one thing may be known from another.

*{can ye not discern}* Or, *ye cannot discern*: you set not your minds to it. See Luke 12.56.

*{the signs of the times}* So to discern, what is like to fall out, by reason to foresee events, and so to dispose of your affairs accordingly: or, *the signes of the seasons*; that is of the choicest times, such as this is; the time of the Messiah’s coming long expected, Acts 26.6,7: the accepted time, and day of salvation; whereof the miracles by me wrought are signs evident and undeniable; which were foretold by the prophets, Isaiah 35.4, &c. Matthew 11.3,4,5.

**V. 4. ***{adulterous generation}* See on chap. 12.39. *spuria soboles* a bastardly brood, so he calls them, because utterly degenerate 42from the forefathers’ faith and holiness.

*{seeketh after a sign}* It was their malicious impudence, who seeing many evident miracles daily done by him, which themselves could not deny, John 11.47, would yet require signs, as if none had been showed them.

*{but the sign of the prophet Jonas}* Such a one as was showed on Jonas, the article added in the Greek, intimates, that was a notable sign. See on chap. 12.39,40.

*{and he left them}* Not only that he might go thence to preach to others, as Luke 4.42,43, but as being justly offended at the hardness of their hearts, Mark 3.5. They came to tempt him, and not to learn of him, and therefore he leaves them. Euthim.

**V. 5. ***{when his disciples were come to the other side}* They forgot it before they came into the ship, and did not until now remember it; possibly when the want thereof minded them of their neglect to provide as they were wont to do.

*{forgotten to take bread}* Probably because of our Savior’s sudden taking ship.

**V. 6. ***{take heed}* *ὁρᾶτε*; the word among other things, signifies, to weigh, examine or foresee, as chap. 8.4. Mark 8.15. So the Syriac word *חזו* *chesau*, of *חזא*, is interpreted by *prospexit, consideravit*, Mar. Trostius *Lex. Syr.* See chap. 18.10, examine and try whether it be sound or no; as 1 Thess. 5.21. 1 John 4.1. The next word interprets it, as the Syriac gives it, *diligenter cavete à*, &c. Which caution being doubled, shows it is a serious and most necessary admonition.

*{of the leaven}* Of their corruptions in doctrine: error taints sound doctrine, taking away the sweetness, and sincerity thereof: false teachers are not to be heard, Deut. 13.3. Jerem. 23.16. Matth. 24.26. And if unawares we light on such who corrupt sound doctrine by mingling falsehoods therewith, as Ireneus *lib.* 3 *c.* 19 says, giving us *gypsum pro lacte*, lime instead of milk, deceiving by the similitude of color; therefore we must take heed *what we hear*, Mark 4.24. And as Bede says in *Cant. exp. lib.* 1 *exord.* concerning the books of the heretical bishop, *Juliani ita carpat botrum, ut et spinam caveat*: so let one gather the grapes, that he also take heed of the thorns: *in dictis ejus sanos sensus scrutetur et eligat, ut non minus vitet insanos*; this our Savior teaches his disciples by a similitude; and an occasion taken from their present forgetting to take bread, verse 5.

*{of the Pharisees and of the Sadducees}* The doctrine of these two sects differed very much, and were both contrary to Christ’s doctrine, and would, like leaven, spread to the infection of the hearers; therefore Christ warns his disciples to beware of both: the Pharisees were much given to the observation of human traditions even to the annulling and making void the holy law of God, chap. 15.3,4,5. They made fair shows of devotion and strictness of life; whereas they were indeed but mere hypocrites, chapter 23.5. Luke 12.1. They were so besotted with the opinion of their own righteousness and merit, that thereby they became the most unreconcilable enemies of the Gospel, which teaches free justification by faith in Christ, these two being altogether inconsistent: for if righteousness can be by the law, Christ died in vain. Gal. 2.21. See Gal. 5.4. Rom. 9.30, &c. The Sadducees maintained such points, as overthrew all grounds of religion, Acts 23.8.

*{Sadducees}* See on chap. 22.23, and Luke 20.27.

**V. 7. ***{among themselves}* One said so to another privately: or *within themselves*, every one thought so in his heart.

*{it is because}* As if they had said, Christ reproves us for our forgetting to buy bread.

**V. 8. ***{When Jesus perceived}* Not by relation from them, but by his divine omniscience.

*{O ye of little faith}* The root of diffidence, care and thoughtfulness about worldly provisions as if they were our main interest, is want or weakness of faith. See chap. 6.30,31.

*{why reason ye among yourselves}* As if I could not feed you unless you brought bread with you, or should be beholding to the Pharisees for it.

*{because ye have brought no bread}* As if the occasion of my speech were only the want of bodily food.

**V. 9. ***{Do ye not yet understand?}* See chap. 14.17. Having seen two such miracles of feeding so many, with so very little food, do you not yet understand that I can if need be do the like for you?

*{neither remember}* This is the third fault objected: the first was unbelief, verse 8, the second ignorance, the third is forgetfulness. See Mark 6.52.

*{five loaves of the five thousand}* That is, wherewith the five thousand were fed.

**V. 10. ***{neither the seven loaves}* Chap. 15.34.

**V. 11. ***{how is it that ye do not understand?}* This is a question implying some kind of admiration, and reproof of their dullness, and inconstancy in matters of such great moment.

**V. 12. ***{then understood they}* They might well understand that he spoke not of bread: and it seems that they gathered by our Savior’s last words, that he meant the doctrine of Pharisees and Sadducees, the rather because he used to teach spiritual things by occasion of the temporal.

**V. 13. ***{when Jesus came into the coasts}* Hereby it appears that the former discourse was by the way, or some byplace, whither Christ had withdrawn himself, ere he came to that journey’s end, as Luke 9.18.

*{of Caesarea Philippi}* Built by Herod the Great, at the foot of Lebanon, in honor of Tiberius: whereas the other called Caesarea Stratonis, stood by the Mediterranean Sea, built in honor of Octavius, Josephus *de bello Judaico, lib.* 1 *cap.* 6.

*{he asked his disciples}* Not out of ignorance, as if he knew not what men said of him; nor out of ambition, as desiring to hear himself commended; but out of love to his disciples, lest they not rightly knowing him, should fail of those blessed fruits of faith which he intended them.

*{whom do men say}* Mark 8.27. Luke 9.18.

*{that I the Son of man am}* Do they think me but an ordinary man? Are they offended at my low estate? Or do they believe me to be the Messiah, promised by God, and expected of the faithful?

**V. 14. ***{some say, &c.}* They repeat not the blasphemies of Pharisees, but say what the better sort said of him; though they thought too meanly of him also, verse 19,20.

*{John the Baptist}* Some of the Jews thought that prophets and eminent men either did rise again, or else that their souls came into other bodies; and that they then became more famous than ever before: hence it might possibly be, that they thought John Baptist was raised up again, see chap. 14.2. This must be understood of those that knew not Christ till after John’s death, for others might know them both together; and so that the one could not be the other.

*{some Elias}* Because it was foretold that he should come as the forerunner of Christ, the Messiah, Mal. 4.5,6.

*{others Jeremias}* Because he was as eager against the priests, and as much hated of them, as Jeremias was, and so might seem to have his spirit in him. Some think that the Jews expected the return of Jeremias, to find the ark which he had hidden, 2 Mac. 2.5.

*{one of the prophets}* Or, *one of the other prophets*; for Jeremias was a prophet also.

**V. 15. ***{but whom say}* Christ looks for confession of the mouth, as well as faith in the heart, especially of such as are to teach others.

*{ye}* You Apostles, who have seen and heard more than others, and ought also to know me better.

*{that I am}* He examines them not concerning his doctrine but his person: it much concerns us to have a good opinion of our teachers: but Christ was not only a teacher but the Savior of the world: to know and believe in him is life everlasting, see John 17.3. John 3.16.

**V. 16. ***{Simon Peter answered}* He in the name of the rest, made this confession of Christ.

*{thou art Christ}* God’s anointed to be king and priest over his Church, John 6.69, and 1.49. Heb. 2.17, and 3.1, and 4.14, and 5.6, and 6.20. Luke 4.18. Acts 4.27, and 10.38.
