*{the Son}* The Son of God by nature, not by adoption, as we are: to declare his eminency and excellency, the article is added to every word in the Greek: he had called himself the Son of man, verse 13, and Peter here confesses him the Son of God, one and the same person consisting of the two united natures, being both the Son of God, and the Son of man; very God and very man. See chap. 1.23.

*{of the living God}* This distinguishes him from false gods which were the works of men’s hands, neither able to give life to others, not having life themselves. See Gen. 16.14. Chap. 26.63. Romans 9.26. 2 Corinth. 3.3, and 6.16. 1 Thes. 1.9. 1 Timothy 3.15, and 4.10. Heb. 3.12, and 9.14, and 10.31, and 12.22.

**V. 17. ***{blessed art thou}* Who believe and confess to salvation, see Rom. 10.9,10, and Mat. 13.16,17.

*{Bar-Jona}* This distinguishes him from the other Simons: Simon was his own name before his calling, chap. 4.18. John 1.42. The Evangelist writing in Greek, uses here the Chaldee and Syriac word, then in common use among the Jews, instead of a surname; for *bar* in the Syriac, as *בן* *ben* in the Hebrew, is a son, *of Jona*; it seems a contract of *Ἰωαννᾶ*, whereof see John 1.42. So we read Bartimaeus, the son of Timaeus, &c. Mark 10.46.

*{flesh and blood}* That is; neither man, nor the wisdom of man, no carnal reason; but God’s Spirit. See John 1.13. Gal. 1.16.

**V. 18. ***{Thou art Peter}* John 1.42. That is, a stone: you have glorified me by your confession of the truth, and I will honor you, by making you a pillar in my Church, Gal. 2.9. This name our Savior gave him in his calling; Mark 3.16. John 1.42. To intimate his constancy.

*{upon this rock}* That is, the rock confessed, not confessing; upon the rock of your confession that I am the Messiah: for as Hilary says (*de Trinit. l.* 2) *Petra est fidei confessio*, this confession of faith is the rock, or Christ confessed is the rock, Eph. 2.20. 1 Corinth. 10.4. See on John 1.42. See also Hilary *de Trinit. l.* 6.

*{will I build}* Christ is both the builder, and the upholder of his Church.

*{the gates of hell}* That is, all the power and policy of hell: the gates of cities being in ancient time, not only parts of special strength, but places also of meeting for advice in matters of greatest concernment. Genes. 34.20,24. Deuter. 3.5. Psalm 127.5.

*{shall not prevail against it}* Either to destroy it, or any of the elect: see John 5.24, and 11.25,26. Revel. 20.6. 1 Corinth. 15.55. Or utterly to extinguish the light of truth, or to extirpate the professors thereof unto the world’s end, maugre all the power, malice, and machinations of Satan, and those instruments which he shall employ against it. See Acts 5.39. Peter in few words had confessed the main sum of the Gospel, the truth of God, against which no falsehood shall finally prevail. God’s gifts are neither *incerta, nor mutabilia* (Prosper *de vocat. Gent. l.* 1 *c.* 24), uncertain, or mutable.

**V. 19. ***{I will give}* The authority of God’s ministers is from God, see John 20.23.

*{unto thee}* With the rest of the Apostles: not only to you; but also to others; for as the confession which Peter made, was the confession of all the holy Apostles; so also the power here given him, was given to all the rest respectively, as may appear, John 20.23, where the same power is given to them all. See chap. 18.18, and on chap. 10.2, and on chap. 20.24.

*{the keyes}* A metaphor from stewards or officers of great houses to whom the keys are entrusted. The keys of ecclesiastical power; that is, preaching the Gospel, and administering the holy sacraments (which open unto us the kingdom of heaven) and whatsoever else appertains to faithful stewards of the mysteries of God. See Luke 11.52. Isaiah 22.22.

*{of the kingdom of heaven}* To let in, and shut out of the Church here, and of heaven hereafter, for here in this life salvation is gained or eternally lost: therefore here, the word of the kingdom is to the hearers thereof, a savor of life, or death, 2 Cor. 2.16. *To them that believe, it is the power of God to salvation*, Rom. 1.16, and *the just shall live by faith*, Rom. 1.17, and the ordinary external means thereto, is *by hearing*, Rom. 10.16, and hearing; by the word of God, by the ministry of man, which is God’s own ordinance. So that, as the effect of men’s hearing proves in this present life, either by opening the kingdom of heaven to them by faith and repentance, or shutting them out of all hope thereof, by their own unbelief and hardness of heart; so shall it be to eternity: for as in the rearing of the temple of Solomon, *there was in neither hammer nor ax, nor any toole heard in the house, while it was building*, 1 King. 6.7. For the materials were made ready before they were brought thither, so shall it be with us, lively stones for the spiritual house of God, 1 Pet. 2.5. Here we must be framed, for in the life to come prophecies shall cease, 1 Corinth. 13.8, and faith end in enjoying.

*{thou shalt bind}* To bind and loose, is to retain and remit men’s sins, John 20.23. This properly belongs to God, as to save, Ephes. 4.32. Coloss. 3.13. Matth. 9.6. Mark 2.10. Luke 5.24. To man only ministerially, as they have the embassage of reconciliation to them committed, 2 Cor. 5.18,20, so they remit to those that believe, and retain to those that are offended and gainsay, by the ministry of the word, and execution of the Church censures, see Acts 2.38, and 10.43.

*{on earth}* Here among men.

*{shall be bound in heaven}* That is, in God’s judgment; for here life is gained or lost, and that according as we believe and obey the word, or otherwise.

**V. 20. ***{that they should tell no man}* Not that it was not true which Peter had confessed, verse 16, or that it was not needful to be known: because the time was not seasonable until after his passion, and resurrection, wherein he was declared the Son of God, verse 21, and chap. 17.9. Rom. 1.4. Then were the Apostles to publish the same everywhere, Acts 1.8. Yet, as occasion served, he taught before, that he was Christ, the Son of God, John 5.18. Mark 14.62.

**V. 21. ***{to shew his disciples}* He acquaints them before with his passion, that they might not be scandalized at those things which they should see him suffer.

*{go unto Jerusalem}* Or *go thence unto, &c*. Christ was born at Bethlehem in an obscure place; but he was to be crucified in Jerusalem the chief city: that the accomplishment of our redemption might be the more public, and the more certainly divulged.

*{suffer many things}* Shame, contumelies, slander, reproach, pains of death, &c. Which, not the righteous, but wicked men ought to have suffered.

*{of the elders}* Which the Hebrews called *זקנים*, *Zekenim*, Deut. 21.2, which was not so much a name of age, as dignity: of these consisted the Jewish Sanhedrin, such as the Latins called senators; styled usually in this sacred story *Elders of the people*, judges, rulers: this sets off the bitterness of Christ’s passion: it had been less dishonor to have suffered the rage of an ignoble vulgar; but here authority, and countenance of religion in the high priests, and chief men condemning him, made the aspersions injuriously cast on him, far more opprobrious.

**V. 22. ***{then Peter}* Peter is here permitted to temptation, and sin, that he might not be proud of that which Christ said before, verse 17. Here we see, as in a glass, the weakness of the best men; and that the good they have is of God alone, and nothing of or from corrupt flesh and blood, see Rom. 7.18. 1 Cor. 4.7. John 1.13.

*{took him}* Took him by the hand and led him aside, as they use to do, who intend to talk privately and familiarly with a friend.

*{to rebuke him}* Out of carnal affection, to reprove him, for not having more care of his own safety.

*{be it far from thee}* *ἵλεώς* *σοι*, *spare thyself*: you shall not go: no doubt but he spoke this out of good affection to Christ; but here we see what good intentions are without a due knowledge of God’s revealed will: *nihil interest, quo animo facias, quod fecisse vitiosum est*, Lactant. *l.* 3 *c.* 15. It skills nothing, with what intention you do that which is evil to be done.

**V. 23. ***{he turned}* Away from Peter toward the other Apostles, as displeased with Peter.

*{get thee behind me}* Lead not me, nor give me counsel, but follow me, and learn of me, as verse 24.

*{Satan}* *Σατανᾶ*, the word generally signifies an adversary, chapter 4.10. He called him so, either because he had spoken like an enemy, or had showed himself an instrument of Satan, set on work by him to dissuade Christ from suffering for our salvation: this Christ takes ill, though Peter spoke ignorantly, and out of affection to the person of Christ. The wisdom of the flesh is enmity to God, Romans 8.7. So was this preposterous zeal in good Peter: how easily do we fall into sin, seeing our best affections are subject to such error?

*{an offence unto me}* You lay a snare, or stumbling block before me; you offend, and displease me in this your motion made to me in the course of my calling, as mediator wherein say some, he sinned more grievously than afterwards he did in denying his master.

*{the things that be of God}* Although God has revealed unto you that I am his Son, verse 16,17, yet he has not acquainted you with my passions, and therefore out of carnal affection, you dissuade me from that, which is the end of my coming into the world, the fulfilling my Father’s will for man’s salvation. See 1 Corinth. 2.14. So much differs frail man, borne up by the grace and assistance of God’s Spirit, from himself, left to himself and his own wisdom of flesh: there Peter was pronounced blessed, verse 17. Here he deserved to be called Satan, and offense: therein Peter went rejoicing from the council, that he was counted worthy to suffer for the name of Christ, Acts 5.41. Here he would have that done, which would have hindered the great work of our redemption, for which the Son of God became the Son of man, for our salvation.

**V. 24. ***{then said Jesus}* Mark 8.34. Upon occasion of Peter’s failing, he shows them, that not only himself must suffer, but all his also, if God shall call them, or as many as God would call of them to suffer for the truth of the Gospel.

*{unto his disciples}* He called the people also to hear it, Mark 8.34. For it concerned them likewise, to be acquainted with the doctrine of the cross; the Apostle saying, 2 Tim. 3.12, *all that will live godly in Christ, shall suffer persecution*.

*{will come after me}* Will follow me as a disciple, and professor of the Gospel, Luke 14.26, and 22.39. Or desires to come to heaven, whither I am going.

*{let him deny himself}* Let him not have any regard of himself which may in any measure hinder him from those things which he owes to the service of God. Set aside all worldly respects, so far forth as they impeach or hinder the revealed will of God: Gregory *hom.* 32 *in evang.* It is not enough to leave that which we have, except we leave ourselves, and yet *quo ibimus extra nos?* Whither shall we go, out of ourselves? *relinquamus nos ipsos, quales peccando nos fecimus: et maneamus nosmetipsi quales per gratiam facti sumus*; let us leave ourselves in respect of that which we have made ourselves by sin: and let us remain ourselves in respect of that which we are made by grace. We may here see how hard it is to follow Christ aright: *grandis——virtutis est, et sollicitae diligentiae superare quod natus sis*, says Hieronym, to Furia, *ep.* 21 *lib.* 2. It is a point of great virtue and careful diligence to overcome that which you are born.

*{take up his cross}* Not bear it unwillingly, murmuring at the burden laid on him: but cheerfully, as he that willingly and readily takes up that burden, of which he is assured he shall make great advantage.

*{his crosse}* That portion of affliction which God has appointed for him, and calls him to suffer, chap. 10.38, wherein he must (what human infirmity will permit) imitate Christ’s willing submission to his Father’s will, who in his bitter agony cried—*not as I wil, but as thou* (O Father) *wilt*, Matth. 26.39.

*{and follow me}* When he has resolved so to do, he is prepared 44to be my disciple: or, it is not sufficient that he deny his own will, and suffer for me, unless he also imitate me in piety, and all holy conversation, chap. 11.29. 1 Cor. 11.1. Ephes. 5.2. Or, let him not think much to deny himself and suffer for me, who suffer death for his salvation. See chap. 10.24,25. Or, let him make account to follow me through afflictions into eternal glory, Luke 24.26. Acts 14.21,22. Heb. 13.12,13. This makes much for the setting out the certain truth of Christian religion; that Christ never proposed secular riches, honors, or ease to those who followed him, thereby to allure them to adhere to him; but contrariwise, the malice of an evil and injurious world, persecutions for his name’s sake, shame, poverty, contempt, and death itself: and yet for the certain reward in the life to come, grounded on his word, they have in all ages since, so constantly professed the Gospel, as that Satan by all his rages against them, could never deter them from Christianity: nay, never more did the Gospel flourish, than in the midst of bloody persecutions: never were we less overcome, than when we could not by many years’ slaughters be overcome. Reason have we to follow Christ patiently, whatsoever we endure: had he not suffered more for us, than we can suffer for him, we had not been saved.

**V. 25. ***{for whosoever}* Christ’s propositions for salvation are general: he is no accepter of persons; whosoever believes, and obeys him, shall be saved, John 3.16. Galatians 3.28,29.

*{his life}* *Ψυχὴν* *αὐτοῦ*, his soul: it is a synecdoche familiar with the Hebrews, thereby importing life; or it may signify (as oft it does) the whole man; for he that saves, or loses soul or body eternally loses both.

*{for my sake}* That he may glorify me in a constant profession of the truth which I teach in the Gospel.

*{shall find it}* That is, shall be saved, and find eternal joy and blessedness. See chap. 10.39. So gainful a loss is it to suffer for the truth: so lossful a gain is it, by time-serving to provide for present peace, and ease, with denying or betraying the truth: the whole sense Gregory excellently expresses by a simile: *frumentum si servas, perdis: si seminas, renovas*, *hom.* 32 *in Evang.* As if one should say to the husbandman, if you keep your wheat, you lose it; if you sow it, you renew it.

**V. 26. ***{for what is a man profited, &c.}* As men desire rather to preserve their lives, than their goods, Job 2.4, so do you much more endeavor to save your souls, which are of greater value than all the world; which being once lost can never be redeemed by all the riches of the world.

*{his own soul}* Or, *his own life*, as verse 25. Or rather body and soul himself, to eternity: for the one of these cannot be left without the loss of the other also.

**V. 27. ***{for the Son of man}* I, who seem now mere man, the man of sorrows, contemptible; shall yet judge the living and the dead, wherein I will reward my constant servants, and punish others, who feignedly cleave unto me.

*{in the glory of his father}* As God, equal to the Father: or in the glory given him in his human nature by the Father, like a glorious king. *The prince of the kings of the earth*, Rev. 1.5, when all power is given him, *in heaven and in earth*, Matth. 28.18.

*{and then}* Not now presently: here is our place of trial and suffering: we must patiently expect satisfaction for all our wrongs, at Christ’s second coming, and repair of all our temporal losses, in his eternal kingdom.

*{he shall reward}* And save good men, but condemn the wicked, John 5.29. And indeed, though life eternal, be the free grace and gift of God, which we merit not, yet death is *ὀψώνια* *ἁμαρτίας*, Rom. 6.23. *The wages of sin*, the sinner’s just desert.

**V. 28. ***{there be some}* He names them not, but means those disciples, who saw his glory in the mount, and should see him after his resurrection: for the kingdom of God is here *praesens ecclesia* Gregor. *hom.* 32, the present Church. See Mark 9.1. Luke 9.27.

*{shall not taste of death}* Shall not die, as Heb. 2.9. See on Mark 9.1.

*{come in his kingdom}* As chap. 17.1,2, where they saw a shadow of his glory, wherein he shall come to judge: or in his resurrection, ascension, sending of the Holy Ghost, and propagation of the Gospel by signs ensuing, see chap. 28.18,19,20. Acts 2.30,32, &c. Or coming in power by execution of judgments upon the Jews, to the utter overthrow of that nation, chap. 24.3,26,27,34. Luke 21.6,7,24.

## CHAPTER 17

**Verse 1. ***{After six days}* Mark 9.2. Luke 9.28. See note thereon.

*{Jesus taketh Peter}* He took with him Peter; the prime Apostle in order; James who was first to suffer for him, and John the beloved Apostle; who was to outlive the rest, and when he raised the damsel, he took with him these three only, haply as best beloved and more zealous than the rest. See Mark 5.37. See on Luke 9.28.

*{into an high mountain}* A fit place for his transfiguration, for height and secrecy. Some think this was Mount Tabor, others are of other opinion: Mount Tabor being (as Hieronym. *de loc. Hebraic.* says) in the midst of a field of Galilee, but verse 22, it appears that this was done out of Galilee, *vid.* Mark 9.30. Is. *Casaubon. exercit.* 15 *N.* 26 defended the ancient tradition of the Church, that it was Mount Tabor. St. Peter calls it the holy mount, 2 Pet. 1.18. It is not expressed in the Gospel, and therefore we may be safely ignorant hereof: Luke 9.28, it appears that he went up to the mount to pray: secret prayers are more ardent, the mind being more attentive thereto, from distractions by external things: therefore he says, *when thou prayest, enter into thy chamber, or closet, shut the door*; Matth. 6.6.

**V. 2. ***{was transfigured}* The substance of his body was not altered; but his countenance was far more glorious than before; (as Moses’ face shined, Exod. 34.29,30. 2 Corinth. 3.7) so were his garments, that the glory then appeared, *qua venturus erat postea in regno suo*, Hieronym. in which he would afterwards come in his kingdom.

*{did shine as the Sun}* In a most glorious manner. See the like manner of phrase, chap. 13.43. Dan. 12.2. Judg. 3.31.

*{his raiment was white as the light}* It was glorious also, to an extraordinary excellency, and heavenly and divine majesty shining on his humanity.

**V. 3. ***{Moses and Elias}* Some conceive that Moses’ body was now raised up again; others are of other opinion: Moses was the prophet by whom the law was given, and Elias a principal prophet; these were fittest to speak concerning Christ’s passion; for as Hieronym. says, the law (represented here by Moses) and the prophets (by Elias) did frequently declare the passion and resurrection of Christ; these show that Christ was the Lord of the law and prophets; the scope of which was to declare him the only Savior, Acts 10.43. These chief witnesses, appear now to strengthen the disciples’ faith, against the scandal of the ensuing cross: if any ask how the disciples knew Moses and Elias; let them consider, that besides Christ’s talking with them, whereby they might be informed in this his admirable preludium of light and heavenly glory they might as easily have a special revelation, as Paul a divine, rapt to hear unspeakable things, 2 Cor. 12. In the state of innocence, Adam knew Eve presented to him, to be flesh of his flesh, &c. Gen. 2.23, and here the Apostles knew Moses and Elias whom they never before saw; it is most likely that in the state of glory, wherein God shall be all in all; (our knowledge subordinate to the happy communion of saints) shall be more excellent, than in the state of innocency, or regeneration here begun.

*{talking with him}* About his passion; as Luke 9.30.

**V. 4. ***{It is good for us to be here}* How delectable will heavenly glory be, when this glimpse thereof did so ravish Peter, that he desired to abide here.

**V. 5. ***{While he yet spake}* 2 Pet. 1.17. That is, while Peter was speaking the forementioned words,—*let us make here three tabernacles*, &c. It was an inconvenient motion of Peter, in their fear, not knowing what to say, Mark 9.6. And therefore Christ answered nothing to it; but God, as it were by a new scene entering that holy theater, covered with a glorious cloud, breaks off that speech.

*{a bright cloud}* Not now a black dark one, as on Sinai in the giving of the law: this cloud was to convey Moses and Elias away from them. God sometimes descending to inform man’s capacity, and speaking more familiarly with him, showed his presence from the clouds of heaven: (Exod. 16.10. Exod. 40.34. Numb. 11.25) as it were the curtains of his most glorious throne, who dwells in the unaccessible light of divine majesty, see Psal. 104.1,2,3. Job 36.29, &c. Psal. 68.34,35. Now this was not a dark cloud in which God manifested his glorious presence, with dread and terror, as in the giving of the law, which had but dark shadows of things to come. Exod. 19.9. Heb. 10.1. But now in a bright and shining cloud, portending and showing the manifestation, of a most comfortable, unveiled, and clear truth of Christ our Savior.

*{This is my beloved Son}* Chapter 3.17. Not a servant as Moses, Hebrews 3.5,6, and the prophets; but a Son; also the double article here again differences Christ from other sons: he is a Son by nature, but we by adoption: therefore he is called the firstborn among the brethren; because though he be of right, the only Son, yet is he chief among many, in that he is the fountain and head of our adoption, Coloss. 1.13. Ephes. 1.6.

*{in whom I am well pleased}* See on chap. 3.17. *duo grata vocabula, filius et dilectus* (says the author of that tract, *de baptism. Christi; apud Cyprian.*) these are two words which are pleasing to God; Son, and beloved.

*{hear ye him}* He is the prophet of whom Moses spoke, Deuteronomie 18.15. Acts 3.22,24. He is the truth and eternal Word of the Father, who spoke by the prophets: he only can reveal the counsel of God, concerning our salvation, which is in the bosom of the Father. John 1.18, God speaks by him, Hebrews 1.2. If we must hear him, and his *ipse dixit* must be our rule; then it is of no 45great concernment what men say; except their assertions be according to that which he says, we may securely neglect them: God has said, *hear him*. See Cyprian *l.* 2 *ep.* 3. If Christ only is to be heard (who now speaks to us by his ministers, Luke 10.16. Matth. 28.20) we must not so much attend, what others before us taught, or thought was to be done, as what Christ, who is before all, did; for the customs of men we may not follow, where the same cross the truth of God.

**V. 6. ***{They fell on their face}* For fear, as by the words following it seems they did. John 18.6.

**V. 7. ***{touched them}* To raise him up, and to comfort them against exanimating fear.

**V. 8. ***{save Jesus alone}* To assure them that the voice from heaven concerned him alone, for Moses and Elias were gone: so the veil of the law and the prophets taken away, we see Christ the end of the law and the prophets to them that believe, 2 Cor. 3.13, &c. See Hieronym. *in loc.*

**V. 9. ***{The vision}* Which they saw with their eyes: sometimes the word *חזון* *chazon* (which the seventy commonly give by *ὅραμα* a vision) is used for that which men see in a dream, but here it was a vision of waking men. See Mark 9.9,11.

*{to no man}* See on verse 20. (And chap. 16.20.) No not so much as to your fellow disciples, lest it might either seem incredible, to them, that Moses, many ages past dead, and Elias taken up into heaven, had now appeared to them on the mount: or possibly lest after so great a glory of Christ showed unto them, the scandal of his cross might in them prove incurable: yet to these three appointed witnesses, he showed it: first to perform his promise mentioned, chap. 16.28. Secondly that these and others by their example might more steadfastly adhere to Christ their head, the Son of God, so avowed by Moses, Elias, and God the Father from the cloud of heaven: thirdly to help their infirmity against the scandal of the cross, when they should consider that he died not by human compulsion, from which he could easily have delivered his sacred body, but that he offered himself up a voluntary and satisfactory oblation for sinners.

**V. 10. ***{Why then say the scribes}* Hearing of his resurrection from the dead, which they so little understood with relation to Christ (of whose restoring a temporal kingdom to Israel they dreamed) as that they reasoned among themselves what he meant thereby, Mark 9.10. It is probable that they remembered what the Scribes in their synagogues used to teach concerning the return of Elias, to go before Christ the Messiah, whom having seen now talking with Christ, they demand, how these things agree together. See chap. 11.14. Mark 9.11. These scribes abused that prophecy of Mal. 4.5, to make the people believe that the Messiah was not yet come.

*{that Elias must first come?}* Before Christ come: how can you be our expected Messiah, seeing Elias your forerunner, is not yet come, except this be his coming which we have seen, and that he shall now go and manifest himself and you to all Israel? And if he must declare you, why do you forbid us to declare, and testify to the people what we have seen and heard concerning him? See Augustin. *de C. D. l.* 20 *c.* 29, and Tertullian *de anim. c.* 35.

**V. 11. ***{shall first come and restore}* Gr. *ἔρχεται* *comes*, that is, the Scribes say true, that Elias must come before the Messiah: but they understand not what they speak of.

*{restore all things}* See Mal. 4.6. Luke 1.17.

**V. 12. ***{Elias is come already}* That is, John Baptist in the spirit of Elias, verse 13. Chap. 11.14. Luke 1.17.

*{they knew him not}* Because they looked for Elias in his own person. The Scribes and doctors of the law would not acknowledge John Baptist to be the forerunner of Christ, spoken of by the prophet, Mal. 3.1, because they would not acknowledge Christ to be the Messiah and Savior, but contrariwise decreed to excommunicate any that confessed him to be so, John 9.22.

*{of them}* Of the Jews.

**V. 13. ***{Of John the baptist}* See on verse 12.

**V. 14. ***{And when they were come}* Mark 9.17. Luke 9.38.

*{kneeling down to him}* In a humble manner, as men that tender petitions use to do, with an earnest desire to obtain relief for his afflicted child, he would omit no means or importunity thereto probably conducing.

**V. 15. ***{He is lunatick}* He means troubled at certain times of the moon, with the falling sickness or some like disease: whereby the devil probably took advantage about those times, to torment him more, verse 18, by joining himself to second causes. See chap. 4.24. For that is a murderer from the beginning, takes all occasions he possibly can, to annoy and afflict men, except God’s providence (which preserved, and at last cured this child) restrain his malicious endeavors.

*{he falleth into the fire}* When the evil spirit drives him.

**V. 16. ***{They could not cure him}* Though they had cast out some devils, yet these they could not. See chapter 10.8, and verse 21.

**V. 17. ***{O faithless, &c.}* He blames the want of faith in the father, who doubted of Christ’s power, Mark 9.22, the unbelief of the people which hindered Christ’s doing of miracles sometimes, chap. 13.58, and the unbelief, or weakness of faith in his disciples, verse 20.

*{how long shall I be with you?}* Teaching you, and confirming my doctrine by miracles, and yet you believe not.

*{bring him hither to me}* Though he were offended with them, yet he would heal the child; that they might know that he could do what he would, which belongs to God alone: so refelled he the cavils of the Pharisees in curing this wretched young man.

**V. 18. ***{rebuked the devil}* *ἐπετίμησεν* *αὐτῷ* He rebuked him: that is, the evil spirit in him: as, Mark 1.23,24. See Luke 9.42.

**V. 19. ***{Then came the disciples}* To wit, when they were come into the house, Mark 9.28. Understand this of the nine Apostles which were not on the mount with Christ, who having the occasion offered them by the father of the child, verse 16, could not cast out the devil that afflicted him.

*{why could not we cast him out?}* They were ashamed that the Scribes and Pharisees had seen their inability: and possibly they were also afraid lest they had lost the power which Christ had given them, chap. 10.1, and therefore ask this question.

**V. 20. ***{because of your unbelief}* That is weakness of faith, not an utter want of it: they had very little, as the words following import.

*{if ye have faith}* Luke 17.6. Understand this of faith of miracles: we may have a good and sufficient measure of justifying faith unto salvation, and yet not have any faith of miracles.

*{a grain of mustard seed}* In a very small measure. See chap. 13.32.

*{ye shall say unto this mountain}* This expression is used to set out the greatest miracles, as, 1 Cor. 13.2. No prince with his army could ever remove a ponderous mountain: that is more than to lay it plain, which yet is a great work. Zach. 4.7. See on Luke 17.6.

*{nothing shall be impossible unto you}* Nothing needful for the confirmation of the doctrine taught by you, and to procure authority thereto. Nothing which may promote God’s glory and propagation of the Gospel, yours, or others’ salvation in believing the same, for faith apprehends and lays hold on the truth of almighty God, to whom nothing which he will do, is impossible. Luke 1.31.

**V. 21. ***{this kind}* God possibly at some times for causes best known to himself, gives more power to some evil spirits than to others: they that had long possession, were very unwilling to part with it, Mark 9.21,29.

*{and fasting}* Which is used on extraordinary occasions as a means to render our prayers more fervent, 2 Cor. 20.3. Ezra 8.21. Dan. 9.3. Jonah 3.5,7,8, and so, much more prevalent (Jam. 5.16) by how much more fervent.

**V. 22. ***{While they abode in Galilee}* Chap. 20.17. Mark 9.31. Luke 9.44.

*{The Son of man shall be betrayed}* He often puts them in mind of this, lest they should be offended at the ignominy of the cross; and to draw them from their opinion of a temporal kingdom, which they so much dreamed of, Mark 10.35,37. Luke 24.21. Acts 1.6.

**V. 23. ***{They were exceeding sorry}* Now resenting that which our Savior so often spoke of, they were very sad.

**V. 24. ***{tribute money}* *δίδραχμα*. Mark 12.14 calls it *κῆνσον* and Luke 20.22 *φόρον*——the *didrachma* was half the shekel of the sanctuary, which the Israelites were by the law, Exod. 30.13, to give for every man’s offering: but after the Romans subdued them they exacted the same for poll money, or tribute; as they did also that *censum* which was a kind of levy on their fields, or houses. See Joseph. *Ant. l.* 19 *c.* 15. Some value this *didrachma*, at fifteen pence: some at a little less: the silver shekel was half an ounce in poise; this tribute money says Master [B?]eza, has on it, Samaritan letters; and on the one side, that sacred pot in which the Manna was kept, with the letter Aleph on the top, signifying that it was a single shekel: the double being as much more, in value four of their drachmas, with this inscription *שקל* *ישראל* *shekel Israel*, the shekel of Israel: on the other side Aaron’s rod which budded, with this inscription *ירושלם* *קדשה* *Jerusalaim kods-chah. Jerusalem the holy*: it had this image. *vid. [B?]eza in loc.*

**V. 25. ***{Jesus prevented him}* He knew what he would ask; and by preventing him, showed his divine omniscience.

*{Of whom do the kings}* Secular princes take it not of their sons, how much less ought they to exact it of the Son of God? Or, the emperor is but God’s deputy; therefore he ought not to take tribute of his only Son.

**V. 26. ***{then are the children free}* And much more I, who am the only Son of God by nature.

**V. 27. ***{lest we should offend them}* Lest we should give them occasion to think that we despise authority, or lest we should give an example to others so to do, to the disturbance of peace and order; thus would he teach us obedience to superiors, though in our personal wrongs, and rather, in things lawful, to recede from our right, than to disadvantage our religion, by administering any matter of offense unto those who are in authority.

*{Go to the sea}* Here he shows Peter indeed what he spoke in word, that he being the Son of God, ought to be free; for as much as (beyond the power of all the kings of the earth) he both knew the secrets of the deeps, and could command the creatures therein to do his will, and the fish of the sea to become tributary to him: this shows, as Christ’s power and omniscience, so also Peter’s admirable faith, obedient in things so remote and distant from the reach of carnal reason.

*{a piece of money}* A *stater* answering the Hebrew *shekel*, in weight, it is half an ounce of silver, in value two shillings six pence, after five shillings the ounce. See on verse 23.

## CHAPTER 18

**Verse 1. ***{At the same time}* *ἐν* *ἐκείνῃ* *τῇ* *ὥρᾳ*, *in the same hour*, at that time when he was at Capernaum in the house whence he had sent Peter to the sea of Gennesaret, to fish for tribute. Mark 9.33. Luke 9.46. *ὥρα* an hour, properly was put for the twelfth part of their day, Matth. 20.6. John 11.9. Yet does it also signify a certain time appointed, Luke 22.14, and sometimes an age, 1 John 2.18.

*{came the disciples}* After they had disputed by the way, which of them should be greatest; and were ashamed to tell Christ what their controversy was, though he asked them (as appears in the other Evangelists) at length they were bold to propound the question to him: yet somewhat reservedly; for whereas they controverted which of them should be greatest, here they propound a general question, neither limiting it to themselves, nor any other particular; and they add, *in the kingdom of heaven*: either in their own sense (who expected a secular kingdom of Christ) or else to palliate their ambition with pretence of their desiring nothing but heavenly things.

*{Who is the greatest}* *τίς* *ἄρα*, *Who then is the greatest*; as if the occasion arose from Christ’s pronouncing himself free from tribute, as being the Son of the heavenly King: this was a dangerous weakness of the Apostles; for as Tertullian says truly; *de baptism. c.* 17, *aemulatio schismatum mater est*; emulation is the mother of schism.

*{in the kingdom of heaven}* The kingdom of heaven imports the kingdom of grace here, and the kingdom of glory in the life to come: but seeing ambition is a stranger and exile to these, it is most likely that they enquired concerning the temporal kingdom which they expected.

**V. 2. ***{a little child}* A young child whom he might take in his arms. Mark 9.36. So the prophets often taught the people by outward signs, and actions; as Jer. 27.1.

**V. 3. ***{except ye be converted}* The Hebrews expressed repentance by conversion, intimating a change in the course of life, and going another way, as men that have gone wrong, turn about in the right path: it is likely that our Savior here intends, that except they left the dangerous precipices of ambition, and became as little children, in humility of mind, it could not be well with them.

*{become as little children}* Not in ignorance and infirmity, but in lowliness of spirit. 1 Cor. 14.20. He never said, Peter, or James, or John shall be greatest (which doubtless he would not have left undetermined, if he would have had any one of them primate, and above the rest, in authority) but equally dehorts them all from vain and pernicious ambition; therefore he set the child in the midst of them, as an emblem and pattern of humility appertaining to them all.

*{Ye shall not enter into the kingdom, &c.}* No man can be saved without humility: ambition cast the angels thence; except you learn humility, you shall be so far from being greatest in heaven, that you shall not come there at all.

**V. 4. ***{Whosoever therefore shall humble himself, &c.}* Whosoever does not think highly of himself, no more than this little child does; humbleness of mind is the right way to preeminence.

**V. 5. ***{one such little child}* One like such a child, in the quality before mentioned: see Psalm 131.2, and 119.141, receives me, that is, I will take it as done to myself. So chap. 25.3.

**V. 6. ***{But who so shall offend}* Mark 9.42. See on Luke 17.2.

*{one of these little ones}* One of a humbled soul: as verse 5. See on Luke 17.2. Or the sense may be, whosoever shall injure, or cause to fall one of the little ones of them that believe. See verse 10, and Mark 9.42.

*{it were better for him}* If he might so end his punishment, it were better for him to be cast into the most helpless condition temporal, than to endure eternal damnation in hellfire.

*{that a millstone}* Possibly some great malefactors were so executed, to which custom Christ might allude, or it may intimate the most desperate condition, as to any hope of escape, concerning this present life.

**V. 7. ***{Wo unto the world}* Many mischiefs will fall on mankind, by reason of offenses; for of them are both the offendants, verse 8,9, and the offended, verse 10.

*{because of offences}* Lets and hinderances which stop the course of good works: the Greek word *σκάνδαλα* signifies things which we stumble at. See on Luke 17.1.

*{it must needs be}* *Necessitate consequentiae*: because of the wickedness of men, it will certainly be so. See on Luke 17.1. And God justly permits the same, for causes to him best known. See 1 Cor. 11.19. Offenses are inevitable.

*{but woe be to that man}* This is added to show, that God’s permission, neither enforces man’s will, nor excuses any evil act.

**V. 8. ***{Wherefore if thy right hand}* See on Matth. 5.30. Mark 9.45.

**V. 9. ***{It is better for thee, &c.}* *καλόν* *σοι* &c. Understand here *μᾶλλον*, *rather* good for you; that is, *ἀνεκτότερον*, as, chap. 10.15, more tolerable.

**V. 10. ***{Take heed}* *ὁρᾶτε*——*See*, as chap. 16.6, and 24.4.

*{that ye despise not}* See on verse 6.

*{one of these little ones}* Not in years, but in humility; as verse 3. God takes care of every one of them, that are of a humble spirit, and so, so little in their own eyes, as 1 Sam. 15.17. Saul once was.

*{their angels}* God sets his angels to guard and take special charge over those that are least able to defend themselves, see Psal. 34.6,7, and 91.11.

*{behold the face of my father}* Wait for God’s command, to be employed for the good of poor infants and humble believers, Heb. 1.14. They behold the face of God, *non sicuti est, sed latente sua natura in specie qua voluerit apparet*, Augustin. *ep.* 112 *Paulino*, not as he is (in his incomprehensible being) but his nature being concealed, he appears in what form he pleases: not as he is in himself, *ἀλλὰ* *καθόσον* *ἂν* *αὐτοὶ* *χωρῶσιν*, &c. As much, or, as they are capable. Cyril. *Hierosol. Cat.* 6.

**V. 11. ***{to save that which was lost}* No not only men contemned of the world, but such as were dead in trespasses and sins, Ephes. 2.1, and so of themselves lost, see chap. 15.24. Isa. 27.13. Luke 19.10.

**V. 12. ***{how think ye}* Luke 15.4. Be you yourselves judges, and weigh the matter by common rules of experience: thus God sometimes seems to appeal to men’s consciences. See Isa. 5.3. 1 Corinth. 11.13.

*{one of them gone astray}* As Jer. 50.6; see Psal. 119.176. Ezek. 34.6. Matth. 9.36.

*{doth he not leave ninety and nine}* So many are much more worth than one; yet such is his love and care of that one, that he leaves them all to seek it, see on Luke 15.4.

**V. 13. ***{He rejoiceth more of that sheep}* Because the other were safe before, but this was in a manner lost: see on Luke 15.5,6,7,9, &c.

**V. 14. ***{It is not the will of your father}* It is not pleasing unto him, that any of them should perish. Ezek. 18.32, and his eternal and unchangeable decree is, that they shall all be saved, see Isa. 54.10, and 61.8. John 10.28,29. Rom. 8.38. 2 Tim. 2.19. Heb. 6.19.

**V. 15. ***{If thy brother shall trespass}* Levit. 19.17, see on Luke 17.3.

*{against thee}* By doing you a personal injury: or by saying, or doing something impiously, or irreligiously against the law of God, whereby you are justly scandalized or offended.

*{Go and tell him his fault}* Tarry not till he come to you, if he neglect his duty go to him: *ὕπαγε* go, seems to prohibit delay; as wounds of the body, so sins, which are the wounds and sickness of the soul, grow more uncureable by too much delay; why says he not here; as chap. 5.24, *go and be reconciled to thy brother?* (See also chap. 6.14.) The answer is, that there he treats of reconciliation only, and the necessity of forgiving one another, but here he speaks of brotherly correption; where remission of the offense commonly amends not, but indulgence often makes the offender worse; therefore he says, tell him of his fault; for as Chrysostom *de Lazar.* says, *reprehensio makes good men better*. See on Luke 17.3.

*{between thee and him alone}* That he may evidently perceive that you do it not to disgrace him, nor to insult over him; but out of desire of his spiritual good, and the reparation of the wrong done.

*{if he hear thee}* If upon your admonition or reproof, he shall confess his faults, satisfy you, and amend. See on Luke 17.3.

*{thou hast gained thy brother}* You have got a friend of him: or you have been an instrument to save his soul. Jam. 5.20. The end of 47our reproving must be, the gain of our brother’s soul: the Apostle proposed this end of his whole ministry; see 1 Cor. 9.19.

**V. 16. ***{take with thee one or two more}* That he may the more respect your words and you may have witness of your brotherly proceeding with him, if you shall be forced to complain publicly: what if my brother, being reproved, grow more obstinate and pertinacious, must I give him over to himself, as desperate and hopeless? By no means, but take witnesses with you, that your candor and fair dealing may appear before men, as it does before God the searcher of hearts.

*{in the mouth}* A Hebrew manner of speaking who said *על*——*פי* *hal pi, in the mouth*, Deut. 19.15. That is, by the word or testimony. So is *פי* *mouth*, put for *עדה* *heda* or *עדות* *heduth witness* or testimony. See Deut. 17.6. See V. Schindl. *pent. in* *עוד* *et in* *פתה*. See John 8.17. 2 Cor. 13.1. Hebr. 10.28.

*{every word may be established}* Every matter or thing testified (as Luke 1.37) may be accounted certain and out of further question.

**V. 17. ***{if he shall neglect to hear}* If he do not vouchsafe to hear them, persuading him to confess his fault, and to repair you, and give you satisfaction: if he continue obstinate, and will not hearken to good and peaceable advice.

*{tell it unto the Church}* Unto the ministers, or governors of the Church: or to the public congregation, or multitude (as Vatablus interprets) he spoke here to the Jews, to whom he presently opposes, *an Heathen or Publican*: so that, because the power of judicature concerning those things, was then in their Sanhedrin, or college of elders, he remits them in such case, to complain to them for redress: so he calls those who had the authority in matters ecclesiastical, the Church which Luke 22.66 calls *πρεσβυτέριον* *τοῦ* *λαοῦ* &c. *the elders of the people*, and the chief priests, and Scribes.

*{let him be unto thee, &c.}* You may then carry yourself towards him, as towards a profane stranger, and contemner of government and religion: so the Jews accounted all heathens, and shunned their company. See chap. 9.10, and 1 Cor. 5.9,10,11. 2 Thess. 3.14.

**V. 18. ***{Whatsoever ye shall bind on earth}* John 20.23. 1 Cor. 5.4, &c. By convincing men of sin before God, or of wrong done to men. Some understand it of just excommunication of obstinate sinners.

*{in heaven}* Before God.

*{whatsoever ye shall lose}* By reducing to repentance through your ministry and preaching the word: others understand it of absolution, or freeing from the sentence of excommunication, upon good and just ground.

**V. 19. ***{If two of you}* Here he speaks to them, as to private men met together to make peace, and to accord differences between party, and party; or for any work of piety and charity: or it may be understood of the party wronging, and wronged; or of the wronged and parties taken in to assist, though they be but two.

*{shall agree on earth}* This word *συμφωνήσωσι*, is transferred from things bodily, to the spiritual; *symphonia* is properly in musical notes and cords: signifying harmony, or consent: but it is here used to import a consent or agreement of mind and will.

*{touching any thing}* Any good thing well carried, or well desired, and to a good end; or about the reconciliation before mentioned.

**V. 20. ***{Where two or three}* He promises his gracious assistance to, and presence with his Church, be it great and numerous, or small: thus he confirms the authority of ecclesiastical censure, and comforts them who unite in truth, and holy duties.

*{gathered together}* In prayer, or any other religious works, agreeing together therein, as verse 19.

*{there am I in the midst of them}* This is an argument of Christ’s divine power, and special presence with his, as chap. 28.20.

**V. 21. ***{How oft}* The question is moved by occasion of our Savior’s former speech, v. 15. Luke c. 17.4 is repeated the occasion of Peter’s *quaere*, which this Evangelist omits.

**V. 22. ***{Until seventy times seven}* As oft as he shall repent, and ask forgiveness: here is a definite number for an indefinite; Peter’s seven is multiplied into seventy times seven, Luke 17.4. See Gen. 4.24.

**V. 23. ***{Therefore}* To this end I do tell you the ensuing parable, that you may be willing to forgive them, who have had so many sins forgiven you.

*{the kingdom of heaven}* God’s spiritual government in the Church, reestablished by the Messiah.

**V. 24. ***{ten thousand talents}* Of a talent, see on 1 Chron. 22.14, and Tho. Godwin. *Moses and Aaron l.* 6 *c.* 10.

**V. 25. ***{And his wife and children}* As Tertullian *de resur. carn. c.* 37 shows the sense is to be taken *ex materia dicti*; from the matter of the speech: in parables, we are to consider the scope and intention of the speaker, and not over-curiously to discuss every particular: so here we must know, that God does not always show extreme rigor, until the vindication of his justice, or the compulsion of a sinner to repentance (which light afflictions do not always effect) necessarily require it: only he shows, that if God should deal with us according to the rigor and severity of his justice, we could not answer him one of a thousand. See Psal. 143.2. And therefore our only sanctuary is his mercy, and the way thither by humble supplication, and charity in forgiving each other: this was the course of old, when men had not to pay, to sell them and theirs, for slaves, Levit. 25.39. 2 King. 4.2. See on chap. 20.7.

*{to be made}* *ἀποδοθῆναι* *to be given from thence* to satisfy the creditor.

**V. 26. ***{worshiped him}* Or *reverently saluted him*: this was a civil reverence, which was very usual in those eastern parts, Gen. 27.3, and 33.3.

*{have patience with me}* God is said in Scripture to be slow to anger, Exod. 34.6. That is, patient, not easily provoked, and not to stir up all his wrath, Psal. 78.38.

**V. 27. ***{Forgave him the debt}* Did more for him than he asked; he desired but days of payment, and the king remitted the whole debt. See Luke 7.42. God’s mercy (which can have no cause but itself) is the sole cause of our sins’ remission in Christ, we can thereto contribute neither merit nor satisfaction.

**V. 28. ***{an hundred pence}* The Roman penny, is the eighth part of an ounce, which after five shillings the ounce, is seven pence halfpenny, a small sum in comparison of that which was forgiven him: teaching that if God forgive us a debt incomparably greater; than we can have to forgive one another: then must we forgive, or else we shall be unexcusable: concerning their penny, see Godwin, *Moses and Aaron*, cited verse 24.

**V. 29. ***{his fellow-servant fell down at his feet}* This was a great degree of humiliation before his fellow servant: but nothing can move that heart to pity which is hardened to destruction.

**V. 30. ***{he would not}* He that had found greater favor from a superior, denies a less courtesy to an equal: the king had forgiven him the whole debt of 10000 talents, and he denies his fellow servant time to pay a hundred pence.

**V. 31. ***{told unto their Lord}* Out of their compassion; they tell all, with the circumstances of the cruelty: these words are not to be strained, as if God were informed by any; for the evidence of every fact cries to him: only it teaches us, that we have (besides our own consciences) so many adverse witnesses crying to God against our cruelty, as are acquainted with our sins: because they cannot but be sorely offended at our injustice or cruelty.

**V. 32. ***{I forgave thee all the debt}* This aggravates his fault, who would not forgive his brother: and sets off God’s admirable mercy, who forgives all, where he forgives any one sin.

*{because thou desiredst me}* Upon your entreaty without further satisfaction: *ἐπεὶ* *after that*, or *because* you entreated me: this *because* in the application, does not import a cause, but a condition of obtaining mercy: the sense is, seeing that at your entreaty, I was contented to forgive so great a debt; you ought to have spared your fellow servant at his entreaty.

**V. 33. ***{on thy fellow-servant}* You had reason to pity him: he was your fellow servant, and might have satisfied you; you could never satisfy me, nor merit any mercy, yet I forgave you.

**V. 34. ***{tormentors}* Those that keep men committed to prison, in much hardship: some say, the Romans (under whose laws the Jews now were) used not only to imprison, but also caused them to be beaten there: the meaning is, God delivers the unmerciful to perpetuity of torments, which they justly suffer, who would not be amended by mercy. Compare this here with that which is said, v. 27, and it may be demanded, whether God ever recalls his pardon granted, or finally condemns that sinner, whom he had once forgiven: I answer,

1. As before, and elsewhere, parables are not over-curiously to be strained so as to make every particle to agree in the moral and explication thereof: similitudes they say, run not on four feet, they will go current if they agree in one, or few points according to the scope thereof, and intent of the speaker. So here this parable is to show; 1. The uncharitable temerity of men, who would find mercy at God’s hand, but show none to men for God’s sake, and at his command. 2. The vain hopes of malicious persons: without mercy shall they be judged, who show not mercy before they are judged.

2. God seems to remit the sin, where he defers execution of the punishment, or where he diverts a plague or punishment denounced, in things concerning this life; as we may see in the several plagues in execution on Pharaoh and his people, or threatened as in the example of Ahab, 1 King. 21.29, and others, whose sins God forgave not; however the punishment temporal was either so diverted, or deferred to the next life, that it became not exemplary in this.

3. The gifts and graces of God are *ἀμεταμέλητα*, Rom. 11.29, such as he never repents of, nor wholly recalls; as in election, sanctification, remission of sins. Where the Scripture speaks of God’s repentance, as for giving the kingdom to Saul, or the like, they descend to man’s capacity therein: for properly God 48cannot repent, because he cannot err who is omniscient, or mistake in his election so as to repent, or recall his own grant, who foresaw the event of his giving. So that, as where God remits the guilt of one sin, he remits all; so to whomsoever he forgives sins, he forgives them forever; yet here, where the punishment is not presently (or possibly all the sinner’s lifetime) remitted and taken off; as in David may appear, 2 Sam. 12.13,18. 2 Sam. 15. 2 Sam. 23. But God never finally condemns that sinner, whom he had once forgiven.

**V. 35. ***{so likewise}* This parable informs us, that they shall find God severe and implacable, that do not forgive their brethren, although they have been diversely and grievously injured by them.

*{from your hearts}* Sincerely, not in show only; neither seeking revenge, nor affecting it, no nor so much as remembering injuries, Levit. 19.18. Rom. 12.9. For so would you have God forgive you, Ephes. 4.32. Coloss. 3.13.

*{every one}* Of what rank or degree soever: superiors must as well forgive inferiors, as look for forgiveness from them, or from God, who says upon the matter, as Joseph, Gen. 43.3,5, *ye shall not see my face, except your brother be with you*.

*{his brother}* Not his fellow servant, as before, but his brother, verse 15. That very name carries a convincing argument of forgiveness in itself.

*{their trespasses}* Of all sorts, great or small.

## CHAPTER 19

**Verse 1. ***{When Jesus had finished}* Mark 10.1. When he had performed his ministry in Galilee: this was the last journey which Christ made to Jerusalem. See Luke 9.51. Hereof Christ spoke chap. 16.21, and 17.22, to show his constancy to do God’s will to the death. Luke says chap. 9.51, *τὸ* *πρόσωπον* *αὐτοῦ* *ἐστήριξε*, *he strengtheneth*, or *set his face to go to Jerusalem*: it is a Hebrew manner of expression, signifying a fixed resolution to do something, as Ezek. 4.3. *והכינתה* *את* *פניך* *vebachinotha eth phanecha*——see also 2 King. 12.17. *et obfirmabis facies tuas*: the circumstance of time is here mentioned, to assure us, that Christ now set himself in the way with a constant resolution to die for the elect. See on Luke 9.51.

*{he departed from Galilee}* He passed over the water out of Galilee into Judea.

**V. 2. ***{healed them}* See on chap. 12.15.

**V. 3. ***{tempting him}* Seeking to entrap him: as chap. 22.16,36. If he should pronounce all divorces unlawful, he should seem contrary to Moses and so expose himself to the odium of the Jews, who religiously observed the law: if he should allow of all divorces, he should contradict his own doctrine, chap. 5.32, and allow of the perverse and uncharitable practices of the Jews, who on every slight occasion put away their wives.

*{to put away his wife}* To give her a bill of divorce, and so dismiss her. See on v. 7,8.

*{for every cause}* Or *for any cause* *κατὰ* *πᾶσαν* *αἰτίαν*; *αἰτία* imports, both a crime, and a cause: *vid. Budaeum* *αἰτία*:——Col. 2. Here it better accords with the latter, seeing that the Jews used to put away their wives for deformity also, Deut. 24.1, which if it were a cause, could not be a crime: and here *πᾶσαν* may be translated *any*, (according to the Hebrew expression, Psal. 103.2, and 147.20) specially if we understand, cause, distinct from crime: none doubting but that some crime might be a just cause of divorce.

**V. 4. ***{Have ye not read}* He reduces them to the first institution of marriage set out in Scripture. See on chap. 12.3.

*{that he which made}* Gen. 1.27.

*{male and female}* He made but one wife for Adam, though he could have made him many, that if one had not pleased him, he might have put her away, and have taken another: but God, the universal parent and Creator of all, in the beginning, made only one man and one woman, Mal. 2.15. Therein declaring his holy will concerning matrimony, between one man, and one woman. Polygamy was never lawful: for God never dispenses with any moral law.

**V. 5. ***{And he said}* God inspired Adam to say so, or God said so by Moses.

*{For this cause}* Gen. 2.24. Ephes. 5.31. Because in the beginning God joined one man, and one woman, in holy matrimony: and for as much as the woman was taken out of the man, being flesh of his flesh, and bone of his bone, Gen. 2.22,23,24.

*{leave father and mother}* This is not to be understood simply; for God cannot be contrary to himself, in prohibiting duty to parents, which is the first commandment with promise, Ephes. 6.2. But to declare the strict bond of unity between man and wife; the sense, is rather forsake father and mother, than your wife: you must dwell with your wife, 1 Pet. 3.7. You are not bound so to dwell with your parents; except in case of their necessity.

*{shall cleave to his wife}* He shall dwell with her: (as 1 Pet. 3.7) in holy conversation, comfortably, in loving and individual society of life: they mutually esteeming themselves so bound to cherish, assist, and comfort one another, as if they were members of one and the same body; to the uttermost of their power, defending themselves from the attempts of Satan, and his agents; as also the advantages which he, too often makes, on men and women’s inordinate passions; lest he should thereby get any occasion of dividing them; or in the least measure alienating, or embittering their affections towards each other.

*{they twain shall be one flesh}* 1 Cor. 6.16. They which were two, became one: this word flesh, is taken for the whole man, or the body, after the manner of the Hebrews: the meaning is, they who before their marriage were two; in their marriage become one in respect of the bond, and their conjunction. See 1 Cor. 6.16,17. 1 Cor. 7.4.

**V. 6. ***{no more twain}* For the man is the wife’s head, Ephes. 5.23, and the head and body are no more twain, but one flesh. See concerning this argument, Augustin *To.* 7 *de nupt. concup. l.* 1 *c.* 9,10,17.

*{God hath joined together}* Those who are joined together according to God’s ordinance, and by his providence.

*{let no man put asunder}* On any other terms than God has appointed. See on chap. 5.32, and here v. 9.

**V. 7. ***{Why did Moses}* Deut. 24.1.

*{a writing of divorcement}* Which Moses calls, *ספר* *כריתות* *Sepher cherithuth*, *βιβλίον* *ἀποστασίου*, the seventy give it, *librum excisionis*, that is divorce or parting away; which they in after-times wrote, according to this or the like tenor. “J. N. willingly, and without constraint, have dismissed and cast out this N. who has hitherto been my wife: and do grant her licence and power, to go at her pleasure, whither she will: and any man may marry her, neither let any presume to prohibit her: in witness whereof I have given her this bill of divorce, &c.” V. Schindler. *Pent. in* *כרת*: see also a more large form. Henr. Ainsworth. on Deut. 24.1, and D. Godwin. *Moses and Aaron l.* 6 *c.* 4.

*{and to put her away}* *ἀπολῦσαι*, *to loose her from*, to wit, the bond of marriage: thus the Jews did when their wives displeased them, though they were chaste, and they conceived that they had authority from Moses so to do.

**V. 8. ***{because of the hardness of your heart}* The common fault wherewith this people was branded, Exod. 33.3, and 34.9. Deut. 9.6, and 31.27. Isaiah 48.4. Ezek. 2.4, and 3.7. Acts 7.41. Lest when once they displeased you, you should use them cruelly, Moses permitted you to put them away; not allowing such divorce, but preventing a greater mischief.

*{suffered you to put away, &c.}* They say Moses commanded, verse 7. Christ says, *Moses suffered it*, to wit, in his political ordinances (wherein he could not but comply somewhat with the disposition of that hardhearted people) not as being a minister of the moral law; or Moses winked at it, but gave no law so to do; for Moses’ words, Deut. 24.1, may be read thus, *and he hath written her a bill of divorcement, and given it, &c. and she hath departed; and gone and been another mans wife*; and the words of the 4th verse shows the main scope of that sanction to be, not a precept of putting her away, but a prohibition of a future reception and taking her again, *for that is abomination before the Lord*——see Jer. 3.1. Though Moses suffered this to those who could not be persuaded to keep their wives, and commanded such to give a bill of divorce, for the future impunity of the wife, against the husband’s implacable rage, yet he never gave a law, that the man should therefore put away his wife, for any less cause than adultery; neither were they any better than adulterers before God, who (except in case of adultery) put away their wives (verse 9) and married another. See chap. 5.32.

*{from the beginning}* From the first original and institution of this ordinance concerning marriage.

**V. 9. ***{and I say}* Or, *but I say*, as chap. 11.19. See chap. 5.31. Mark 10.11. Luke 16.18. 1 Cor. 7.11.

*{except it be for fornication}* The bond of marriage cannot be broken by a divorce, except it be for adultery; yet that needed not, had they duly executed the law concerning punishing adultery with death: but it seems that in the neglect thereof, our Savior adds this caution: as to tax that neglect, and perverse indulgence to adulterers, and adulteresses; so also to condemn their rash judgments, and unwarrantable practices, who devise other causes of annulling marriages: let all take heed that they presume not to control the wisdom of their heavenly Master, by assigning other causes of parting them whom God has joined, than God has ever declared in his word.

**V. 10. ***{If the case, &c.}* If a man be so strictly and inseparably tied by this ordinance: thus flesh uses to reason.

**V. 11. ***{all cannot}* Cannot live continently without wives, nor all wives without husbands.

*{receive this &c.}* This the Greek word *χωρεῖν* properly signifies: and metaphorically, as the Syr. interpreter gives it, *non quivis est par huic rei*: all are not so continent, as to live chastely single. See 1 Cor. 7.2,8.

*{To whom it is given}* By some peculiar means verse 12.

**V. 12. ***{Which were so born}* Unfit for marriage by nature, or not desiring it.

*{made eunuchs}* Gelded: such were appointed to keep the concubines of great men.

*{made themselves eunuchs}* Not by dismembering themselves, but *τῶν* *πονηρῶν* *λογισμῶν* *τὴν* *ἀναίρεσιν*, &c. *Anastas. Sinait. q. 66. ex Chrysostom.* by taking away evil thoughts: the meaning is, that to be abstemious, and chaste, *Βασιλείας* *οὐρανῶν* *ἐστι* *δῶρον*, as *Epiphan. haeres. 21. 5.* interprets, is a gift of the kingdom of heaven: a gift of grace fitting them for the kingdom of heaven.

*{he that is able}* If any man has received the gift of continency, that he can in single life keep himself an undefiled member of Christ’s mystical body: otherwise, take heed that temporal advantages cast you not eternally into hellfire. See 1 Cor. 6.9, 10. 1 Cor. 7.9. Heb. 13.4.

**V. 13. ***{Then there were brought, &c.}* Mark 10.13. Luke 18.15. After they saw how he helped and cured people of ripe years; the pious mothers began to hope that he would also bestow some blessing and heavenly grace on their children, if he would but lay his hands on them (which was the accustomed form of blessing, as appears in the story of Isaac, Gen. 27.21, 22. and Jacob, Gen. 48.14, 17. therefore they brought their children to our Savior).

*{and pray}* They who were excellent in gifts, and eminent in authority, used to bless inferiors, by imposition of their hands. (See Heb. 7.7.) which was a *symbolum*, and sign of blessing: so the priests were appointed a form of blessing, which (as representing God) they were to pronounce to the people, Numb. 6.24. &c. therefore these parents desired this solemn ceremony, and Christ’s prayers for their children, who were presented unto him, *in fide parentum*, in their parents’ faith, as Euthymius speaks: this shows the great desire they had of their children’s spiritual good.

*{The disciples rebuked them}* That is, those that brought them, Mark 10.13. It may be because they brought their children *ἀκόσμως* *δὲ* *καὶ* *τεθορυβημένως* disorderly, and tumultuously, as Theophylact thinks: they thought it a thing troublesome to Christ, and not worthy of his greatness and excellency, to meddle with children, who were not yet of capacity to learn the high mysteries of the kingdom of heaven, and needed no miraculous healing.

**V. 14. ***{of such is the kingdom of heaven}* You have no cause to blame them for bringing their children to me; for they have interest in the kingdom of heaven, as well as those of riper years: and unless you be like them, you shall never come there, chap. 18.3. They have no appetite of revenge against those that hurt them; when the parents correct them, yet they cleave to them, and prefer them before all others: if a queen in a diadem and purple robes should be offered (says *Chrysostom. hom. 63. in Matth.*) they would rather see their mother though sordid and poorly clothed, than another in the most princely attire: they desire nothing beyond necessity; when they are satisfied, they let go the breast: they are not distracted with cares of this world; nor know they many snares, wherewith years and maturity acquaint the rest of the race of men: if we will be saved, let us imitate these harmless patterns, which come nearest to our lost state of innocency.

**V. 15. ***{He laid his hands on them}* It is not said, that he prayed, as they that brought the children desired verse 13. but only that he laid his hands upon them: whereby he showed that he had power of himself to bless them: so Mark 10.16. it is said, *he put his hands upon them and blessed them.*

**V. 16. ***{And behold one came}* See Luke 18.18. Mark 10.17. He is said in Luke, to be a certain ruler, a chief man in eminency or authority, not a vulgar plebeian: it seems he came not to Christ treacherously, as the scribes and Pharisees used, but with a desire to learn of him, whom he reverenced; yet in his story appears, how dangerous an obtrusion and let to salvation, love of riches and the world is.

*{good Master}* This preface, with other circumstances show, that he meant willingly to embrace the doctrine of Christ; and how little, fair intentions profit, where anything is loved more than God, and our salvation!

*{What good thing shall I do?}* Mark the question, you that will understand Christ’s answer to him; he says not by what means must I be saved? to which undoubtedly Christ would have answered, believe and you shall be saved, (see Acts 16.31.) but *what good thing shall I do, &c.?* He knew it was not in saying or professing good, but yet he dreamed of merit of works; whereby he (as the Pharisees) thought he must be saved. See Rom. 9.30, 31.

**V. 17. ***{Why callest thou me good?}* Seeing you suppose me to be mere man: he understood his hypocritical spice, or presumption on his works: therefore he here intimates that which he said, Luke 6.46. *why call ye me Lord, Lord, and do not the things which I say?* He denies not his goodness, *sed magistri absque Deo excludit errorem*, *Jerom.* but excluded his error, who took him to be a teacher without a deity.

*{none good but one}* None good so as God; that is, originally, essentially, absolutely, immutably good, 1 Sam. 2.2. James 1.17.

*{keep the commandments}* He speaks not this as if any man might be justified, and saved by the works of the law; but in answer to his question, verse 16. and to draw him from ceremonies to moral duties. See on verse 16. Sanctity is *via regni*, the way to the kingdom, though not *causa regnandi*, the cause of reigning. *Bernard.* See Heb. 12.14. and compare it with Rom. 3.28. Rom. 4.14. Gal. 2.21.

**V. 18. ***{Thou shalt do no murder}* Exod. 20.13. He mentions not the ceremonial law, which the Jews strictly observed, nor the duties of the first table, whereof they seemed very zealous; but of the second table, wherein they were very negligent, chap. 23. and wherein they commonly fail, who hypocritically make fair shows of devotion in the first table. See Isa. 1.11. &c. and 66.3. Jer. 7.9, 10, 21. Micah 6.6, 7, 8.

**V. 19. ***{Thou shalt love thy neighbour as thyself}* This is the sum of the second table, Rom. 13.9. Luke 10.27. Mark 12.31. Matt. 22.39. Gal. 5.14.

**V. 20. ***{All these things have I kept}* In the literal sense, and externally, he could not keep them in the large extent of the law, which reaches to the thoughts, and secret corruptions of the heart: he might be unreprovable before men (as Paul was Phil. 3.6.) he could not be so before the all-seeing God, *vid.* Ps. 143.2. 1 Kings 8.46. James 3.2. The best on earth are but partly spirit, Gal. 5.17. Rom. 7.17, 21, 23. Matt. 5.22, 28.

**V. 21. ***{If thou wilt be perfect}* If you will indeed show yourself to be such as you say you are; Christ shows not here, wherein eternal life consists (for this precept is not universal) but hereby he detects, and convinces the false opinion he had of his own righteousness; and wherein he had hitherto securely deceived himself: therefore Mark and Luke say, *yet one thing is wanting*: as if he said; you are not yet perfect, as you vainly dream.

*{sell all thou hast}* Christ well knowing his covetousness, thus touches the secret sore of his mind, to show him how imperfect he was: and as God, gives him this special commandment, which he disobeying, discovers a breach of the first precept of the law; and so indeed, kept none.

*{thou shalt have treasure in heaven}* You shall not part with your goods so much as change them for those that are incomparably better: instead of transitory riches which you cannot long keep, you shall have eternal, which can never be lost; then riches become our goods when to God’s honor we bestow them on the poor.

*{follow me}* Be my disciple, and abide with me, that I may employ you in the ministry of the Gospel. See chap. 4.19.

**V. 22. ***{He went away sorrowful}* It troubled him much that he had moved the question, not finding Christ’s answer according to his desire and expectation.

*{for he had great possessions}* Possibly he would have parted with some part for Christ, but he would not leave so great an estate for him, or his sake, to wit, that he might become a disciple and follower of Christ; or for the hope of that which Christ had conditionally promised, treasure in heaven: see what dangerous lets to salvation are in great riches and possessions of carnal men.

**V. 23. ***{To his disciples}* Not that they were rich and so in danger of losing heaven thereby; but that they should not in aftertimes be offended, if they could not bring rich men to God by their ministry. See 1 Cor. 1.26. Neither should attain to riches themselves.

*{a rich man shall hardly enter}* Because it is hard for them not to trust in riches (Mark 10.24.) not to set their hearts on their increases; to love them too much, to keep them from distracting them from God’s service, and from choking up the seed of God’s word, and all good desires and intentions of the heart: lastly, because it is hard for them, not to be covetous which exposes them to the snares of Satan, 1 Tim. 6.9, 10. The sum is not in the possession, but in the possessor’s mind: Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, &c. were both rich and holy: good men become so much better, by how much more means they have to be good. Barnabas will sell his lands to advance the propagation of the Gospel by relieving the poor brethren. Dorcas will provide coats for those that cannot provide for themselves. Obadiah will hide, and feed the prophets of the Lord, in time of persecution. So the saints lay up treasure in heaven; as chap. 6.20. *a good foundation against the time to come, that they may lay hold on eternal life*, 1 Tim. 6.19.

**V. 24. ***{It is easier for a camel}* Theophylact says that some say that *κάμηλον* signifies not here the living creature (that is a camel) but *τὸ* *παχὺ* *σχοινίον*, &c. a great rope, &c. That is, a cable, but indeed this is a proverbial manner of speaking, which Caninius notes out of the Talmudists; though they the elephant; and Christ mentions the camel, a beast better known in Syria, and likely according to their usual proverb, of their opinion which Theophylact cites is *Eli. Cretens. com. in Gr. Nazianzen. orat. 11.* but Suidas says, *κάμηλος* is *τὸ* *ἀχθοφόρον* *ζῶον*, a burden-bearing beast; but *κάμιλος* is *τὸ* *παχὺ* *σχοινίον*, a great rope, or cable. See *Joh. Drus. praeterit. lib. 1.*

**V. 25. ***{They were exceedingly amazed}* Understanding the way to heaven to be much harder than they formerly conceived it had been: and no wonder: for, before their calling to the school of Christ, they having been hearers only of the scribes and Pharisees, 50must needs be seasoned with some ill relishes of their leaven and doctrine, as in the expectation of the Messiah’s temporal monarchy; the coming again of Elijah the Tishbite, &c. So also of salvation by works of the law, free will, &c. these and the like principles, caused their amazement at the doctrine of Christ, concerning the strait and difficult way to life eternal.

*{Who then can be saved?}* What rich man can be saved? or who can be saved? for how little soever a man has, he is naturally unwilling to part with that all, and to be left destitute, to the mercy of an unkind world. Now if the rich man (who according to the Pharisees’ opinion might by good works make him an easy way to heaven) shall so hardly be saved; what shall become of others who have not so much means to do good?

**V. 26. ***{Jesus beheld them}* As intimating that he knew their thoughts: so the disciples probably did not speak out, but whispered among themselves, Mark 10.26.

*{with God all things are possible}* Be not troubled at what I said; for God can and will save some rich men; such were Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Job, Zaccheus; and all his elect: not for their merit, but for the sole merit of Christ.

**V. 27. ***{Then answered Peter}* Mark 10.28. Luke 18.28. In the name of all the rest, as often on other occasions.

*{we have forsaken all}* We were not commanded to sell all; and if we had, in what condition should we have left our families? yet have we left all: for they had left their trade, which was their livelihood.

**V. 28. ***{in the regeneration}* You have followed me in preaching the Gospel to the regeneration of the elect: or he means that in the absolute renovation in the resurrection, they should sit, &c.

*{When the Son of man}* Though your reward be not in this world, you shall be sure of it in the world to come, chap. 16.27.

*{ye shall also sit}* Luke 22.30. You shall have a prerogative above others in this behalf.

*{judging the twelve tribes}* He alludes to their manner of judgment, when their twelve tribes were entire and flourishing; he means the universal Church consisting of the carnal and spiritual Israel: and he speaks of twelve thrones, according to the number of the apostles, that they might know, that they should all equally share in dignity and honor; Judas excepted, into whose honor and office Matthias succeeded: you shall sit judging either by your doctrine, or by attending on me in a special manner.

**V. 29. ***{every one}* Not you apostles only; but all others; no man shall lose by obeying me.

*{or father, or mother, &c.}* That is, in case you cannot cleave to Christ, except you forsake them: honor your parents in God; and as far as his honor and service will permit; but where it is otherwise, *praeferenda est religio necessitudini, pietas propinquitati* says *Ambros. l. 7. ep. 56. Romulo.* Religion must be preferred before friendship, and godliness before kindred: that, says he, is true godliness, which esteems things divine, before human; and eternal, before temporal. *Sanctis omnia est Deus*; God is all in all to the saints.

*{for my name’s sake}* See on chap. 5.11.

*{an hundredfold}* In secular goods, Mark 10.30. For a little which the righteous have, is better than great riches to the ungodly: or in spiritual riches, and content, which is far better; for godliness with contentment is great gain, 1 Tim. 6.6.

**V. 30. ***{But many that are first}* Chap. 20.16. Mark 10.31. Luke 13.30. The Gentiles whom the Jews now despised, shall be preferred before them; and among these again, some, that in regard of former evil lives, now truly repented of, shall be preferred before some others who basely esteemed them, and thought highly of themselves: or many of highest esteem and reputation for religious life here, shall be shut out of heaven, as the Pharisees, chap. 5.20. when despised ones shall be saved; or, the last converted shall be rewarded as much as the first, chap. 20.1–16. See Luke 13.28, 30. & 7.29, 30. and 18.9, 14. chap. 8.11, 12. & 21.31. &c. See also Wisd. 5.1, 2. &c.

## CHAPTER 20

**Verse 1. ***{For}* This causal particle, and the repeating of the end of the last chapter here, verse 16. shows that this parable is inferred to expound the former conclusion.

*{the kingdom of heaven}* The equity of God’s dispensation in the kingdom of heaven, is as if a householder, &c.

*{early in the morning}* *ἅμα* *πρωῒ* *together with the morning*: so they said, *ἅμα* *τῇ* *ἡμέρᾳ* *with the day*, and the Latins, *cum prima luce*: with the first light; that is, as soon as it was day: God from the beginning of the world had a care to provide workmen for his vineyard, the Church, Isaiah 5. whom he sent into it with the first light, the knowledge of him, and his will concerning man’s salvation: the vineyard is the Catholic Church, which brought forth so many vine-branches, *quot sanctos, &c.* from Abel the just, unto the last-born elect: *Gregor. hom. 19. in Evang. mane mundi fuit ab Adam usque ad Noe*: the morning of the world (says the same) was from Adam to Noah: the third hour, verse 3. was from Noah to Abraham: the sixth hour was from Abraham to Moses; verse 5. the ninth hour; verse 5. was from Moses to Christ: and the eleventh verse 6. to the end of the world. See here also, *Hilary canon. 20. in Matth.* Theophylact and Euthymius, understand this of the divers ages of man, childhood, youth, middle age, old age, wherein they are called to the faith in Christ: *Augustin de verb. dom. secundum Joh. serm. 59.* proposes both these interpretations.

**V. 2. ***{When he had agreed}* *συμφωνήσας*. See on chap. 18.19. When he had made a contract or covenant.

*{for a penny}* The Roman penny, is the eighth part of an ounce, which, after five shillings the ounce, is seven pence halfpenny, chap. 18.28.

**V. 3. ***{About the third hour}* This shows the continuance of God’s care for his Church unto the end; because he is compared to a husbandman, that still looks out for workmen. See on verse 1.

*{standing idle in the marketplace}* Whither idle persons used to resort, chap. 11.16. Acts 17.5. and where they who expect, or desire employment, are wont to wait.

**V. 4. ***{whatsoever is right, I will give you}* God promises a reward to his servants, to encourage them to the work, Psalm 19.11. & 58.10, 11. 1 Cor. 15.58. In the application of this parable, we may not understand that any man can merit the least good at God’s hands; that which he gives is of free grace, not of man’s desert, as with men, the workman is worthy, or deserves his wages or hire, Luke 10.7. Matt. 10.10. but between God and man the case is thus — *if by grace, then it is no more of works; otherwise grace is no more grace: but if it be of works, then it is no more grace* (or a free and undeserved gift) *otherwise, work is no more work*, Rom. 11.6. Inconsistent therefore are man’s merit, and God’s grace and free mercy in Christ. What then? consider well, and choose which of the two you will stick to for your salvation.
