The apostle tells you, that *Israel attained not to the law of righteousness, because they sought it not by faith* (in him who freely, and without any merit of man, justifies sinners) *but as it were by the works of the law*, Rom. 9.31, 32. Let him therefore boast of his merit, who will renounce the grace and free mercy of God in Christ: for, *if righteousness came by the law, then Christ is dead in vain*, Gal. 2.21. See *Aug. Enchirid. ad lau. c. 3.*

**V. 5. ***{About the sixth, and ninth hour}* See on verse 1.

**V. 6. ***{about the eleventh hour}* An hour before sunset, or somewhat more; for the first hour began at sunrising, in the *aequinoctial*: that is, at six of clock with us, for their day was twelve hours, John 11.9.

**V. 7. ***{Because no man hath hired us}* This might import their excuse, who make no good use of those excellent parts which God has given them; under pretense that they have no calling to the ministry: but (as has been formerly noted) parables may not be over-curiously stretched to answer in morality to every particle, which they seem to point at: but the scope of the speaker, is chiefly to be observed: and so here, as Euthymius notes after Chrysostom, those things are to be expounded only, *quae opportuna videntur — caetera vero non sunt curiose scrutanda*; which are opportune, but the rest may not be curiously examined. See on chap. 18.25.

**V. 8. ***{So when even was come}* This need not be applied, as being rather to fill up the parable, than otherwise: yet it is true, that God will in like manner appoint Christ, in the end of the world, to reward all his ministers and people, for all their service done here, in his Church, chap. 16.27.

**V. 9. ***{they received every man a penny}* That is, (says *Hilari can. 20. in Matth.*) *obedientiae ipsius praemium*, the reward of his obedience, salvation; says Euthymius, as much as was promised to the first, and more than the last could expect.

**V. 10. ***{They supposed}* We must not think, that any that go to heaven will complain, that any have too much, or themselves too little: (there is no envy or repining there, where all are full) but this is added, to give occasion, in the words following, to show; that God’s liberality in rewarding some that have labored less than others, is no prejudice to his truth or justice, in rewarding others.

**V. 11. ***{They murmured}* See on verse 10. The Jews murmured, that God had equaled the Gentiles with them in their interest in Christ, (see also, Luke 15.29, 30.) but there can be no such discontent in heaven.

**V. 12. ***{have wrought but one hour}* Or, *have continued one hour only, and thou hast given them a reward equal with ours.* So began the Lord with the last, *quia ad Paradisi requiem, prius latronem quam Petrum perduxit.* *Gregor. hom. 19. in Evang.* For he brought the penitent thief into the rest of Paradise before Peter.

**V. 13. ***{I do thee no wrong}* See on verse 10.

**V. 14. ***{Take that is thine}* By agreement, verse 2, 13. God’s people shall receive all that God does promise them; yet still we must remember, that God freely justifies sinners, and that through grace, not any merits of those whom he justifies.

**V. 15. ***{to do what I will with mine own}* I give only that which is mine own: I have paid you according to my agreement with you; and you have no cause to complain of me, for giving to other what I please, out of mine own estate.

*{is thine eye evil}* Do you envy them for my free liberality, 51wherein I do you no wrong? The Hebrews by *רע* *עין* *rah ben* mean an envious eye, Prov. 23.6. So the seventy *eat thou not with ἀνδρὶ βασκάνῳ*; *Jerom. cum homine invido*, with an envious man; according to this, Christ says, *an evil eye proceedeth from within, out of the heart of men.* Mark 7.21, 22. So also Deuteronomy 15.9. *ורעה* *עינך*. 70. *καὶ* *πονηρεύσεται* *ὁ* *ὀφθαλμός* *σου*. So here, *ὀφθαλμὸς* *πονηρὸς* is an evil or envious eye. See Eccles. 14.7. Contrary hereto is, *טוב* *עין* *the good eye*, which Solomon says, *shall be blessed*. Proverbs 22.9. According to which, Syrac says, Eccles. 35.10. *ἐν* *ἀγαθῷ* *ὀφθαλμῷ*, *with a good eye give the Lord his honor, and diminish not the firstfruits of thy hands*; that is, with a heart free from envy. See on chap. 6.23.

**V. 16. ***{So the last}* See on chap. 19.30.

*{for many}* Some come short of them and their reward, who were much superior to them in the world’s esteem; because they are only outwardly called by the word, but are not elected to eternal life; or the last shall have reward, when the first shall fail of; because *plerique Deum vocibus sequuntur, moribus fugiunt.* *Gregor. hom. 19. in Evang.* Most follow God in words but in their manners fly from him; they *profess that they know God, but in works they deny him.* Tit. 1.16. But why (say some) does not God show the same mercy to all those whom he calls? We know his judgments are just, though in many things we cannot understand the cause; let us say therefore with *Prosper. de vocat. Gen. l. 1. c. 15. quid calumniamur justitiae occultae, qui gratias debemus misericordiae manifestae?* Why calumniate we his secret justice, who owe (all possible) thanks to his evident mercy?

**V. 17. ***{And Jesus}* Mark 10.32. Luke 18.31.

*{going up to Jerusalem}* Up, either in regard it stood somewhat higher than the plain about it, Jer. 17.3. and 21.13. or, as men are said to go up to some chief place in a country, or head city of a kingdom: howbeit the word ascending, is oftentimes put simply, for going or traveling to a place, 1 Kings 22.4, 6, 12. Jer. 34.21. and 37.5.

*{apart in the way}* To acquaint them privately with his death and resurrection, who were to be special witnesses thereof, Acts 10.41.

**V. 18. ***{we go up to Jerusalem}* Christ foreshows his readiness to suffer for us, by his words here, and by his going before them, Mark 10.32.

*{the Son of man shall be betrayed}* He had acquainted them with his passion twice before, chapter 16.21. and 17.22, 23. yet now he mentions it again, because they understood not, why he would wittingly go among his enemies; and afraid what would become of themselves, they willingly declined that journey, Mark 10.32. therefore he repeats this hard lesson to them again, that, when, they saw one part of the prediction come to pass in his death, they might be confirmed concerning the other part his resurrection from the dead. The first time he told them of his death in general: the second time he declares the mean, by treason: now he tells them of the manner, by condemnation and crucifying: and in his last supper he reveals the traitor to them; all this did he, that they might not be dejected nor scandalized at that which he should suffer; considering, that nothing came to him by chance, nor the power and will of man, but the determinate counsel of God, unto which, he wittingly and willingly submitted himself, chapter 26.39, 42. This, in the present perturbation of mind through fear and sorrow, though they did not now understand, yet they afterward confessed: see Acts 4.27, 28.

*{unto the chief priests}* Who, though they were ordained of God to be pillars of the Church, guides, and comforts to the people, yet in their corruption, became the most violent suppressors of religion, and persecutors of the ministers and children of God, Jer. 20.1. and 26.8. Acts 4.1. and 5.17.

*{shall condemn him}* Shall cause him to be condemned: for they had no power to do it themselves, the Romans having taken from them the power of judicature, in matters capital, and concerning life and death.

**V. 19. ***{and shall deliver him}* See on verse 18.

*{to mock}* They shall afflict him with words, stripes, and death. See chapter 27.27, &c. John 19.2. Thus they as chief instruments, delivered him to the Roman power: but still we must consider the supreme mover, and the ultimate end, without which none of the rest could have prevailed. God the Father delivered him for our redemption, Rom. 8.33. Christ delivered up himself, Gal. 2.20. John 10.11, 13. Judas the betrayer delivered him: the chief priests and scribes delivered him, for they persuaded the people to cry crucify him: and Pilate delivered him to death: yet in this delivery God is just and merciful, but man unjust and impious, because in one and the same thing, there was not one and the same end proposed: God delivered his Son to death for us, that he might deliver us from eternal death: Judas betrayed him, because Judas was covetous: the Jews delivered him up, because they were envious: Pilate delivered him to be crucified, to gratify the Jews; the end and impulsive causes make the difference of actions. See on verse 18. Acts 4.27, 28. and Augustine *ep. 38. ad Vincent.*

*{to crucify him}* After the manner of the Roman execution of the malefactors: so did he *make his grave with the wicked*: so was he *numbered with transgressors*, Isaiah 53.9, 2. So became he obedient to the death of the cross, Philippians 2.8.

*{the third day}* By this he comforts them, and instructs them against their sorrow, and his ignominy, lest they should be scandalized at his sufferings. See on chap. 12.40.

*{he shall rise again}* See chapter 16.21. It was a great trial of their faith, that he, whom they believed, and professed to be the Son of the living God, the Messiah and Savior of the world, should be permitted to such impious hands, such ignominy, and a death so shameful; but in his resurrection he was declared the Son of God with power, Romans 1.4. By his resurrection the third day, death and hell were conquered, and we freed from the sting and power thereof.

**V. 20. ***{then came to him}* A little time after, in the same journey: it is likely they came to him apart, and privately. See verse 24.

*{the mother of Zebedee’s children}* Mark 10.35. says, *James, and John, the sons of Zebedee, came unto him, saying, &c.* It seems their mother came with them, and solicited for them: these were James the elder, and John the beloved: the mother’s name was Salome, Mark 15.40. This James Herod slew, Acts 12. and John was the beloved disciple, and apostle, who lived long after.

*{worshipping him}* Adoring him, Syriac: either kneeling down to him, or with such reverence, as might import, and present a humble and hearty petition.

**V. 21. ***{what wilt thou?}* It seems hereby that she petitioned at first indefinitely, as some use; will you grant me one thing? not presently saying what: but Christ well knowing what she would ask, would yet have her express her request: to teach us, that no wise man may grant indefinite petitions, as impious and foolish Herod did; and also that he might have the more evident occasion of reproving their ambition.

*{these my two sons}* Probably, she had heard from the two apostles, her sons, what our Savior had said, chapter 19.28.

*{the one on thy right hand}* Let them have the highest degree of honor in your kingdom; which they (not yet understanding the spiritual kingdom of Christ) did suppose should be with external honor and greatness. See 1 Kings 2.19. Psalm 45.9.

**V. 22. ***{ye know not what ye ask}* You think that they who are principal in my kingdom shall have much secular honor and ease, with all affluence of worldly accommodations; but you will find it quite contrary, for they must be more exposed to troubles, and sharp encounters, than others.

*{of the cup that I, &c.}* A metaphor taken from a man condemned to die by drinking poison. See Psalm 11.6. Isaiah 51.17. John 18.11. Ezek. 23.32. Can you suffer persecution for the confession of the truth of the Gospel? It is inopportune, and preposterous which you desire: you must first taste of my sufferings, and then talk of honor after the victory: this question has the value of a deliberation; as if he said, consider first, how you will be able to suffer, which is the present condition of my kingdom, (chapter 16.24.) and do you already talk of rejoicing with me? Such is our natural affection, we would fain be crowned before the conflict: and they are most ambitious, who are most impatient of the cross, or performing their duty: the only bridle of ambition, is meditation of the cross; no man shall share with Christ in glory, who does not first in sufferings, 2 Tim. 3.12.

*{to be baptized}* Shall you be able to endure those overflowings of afflictions, which like deep water-floods must compass me about; see Psalm 69.2, 15. Psalm 124.4. and 116.3. Hence he takes an expression from the manner of baptizing them, by immersion or dipping over head and ears in the water. See on Luke 12.50. which in these hot countries was more safe for infants, or weak persons, than in these northern and cold parts it can be.

*{we are able}* Taking it literally; they knew they were able to do it; for they had often drunk of his cup, and had been baptized as he was.

**V. 23. ***{of my cup}* You shall endure afflictions as I shall; so they did both; for James was slain, Acts 12.1. and John was banished, and suffered many sad things, Revelation 1.9. See *Jerom. catal. script. eccles. & Adricom. Act. Apost. Anno Christi, 98. 99. 129.*

*{but it shall be given to them, for whom, &c.}* Or, *save to whom*; so the word is used, Mark 9.8. Christ’s power of disposing of it, is not denied; but he shows only for whom it is to be given.

**V. 24. ***{when the ten}* All the other apostles, among whom was Peter; yet we read of no peculiar exception made by him, concerning his primacy; probably they all promised themselves high dignities. Ambition troubled the Churches’ infancy; but much more in the growth thereof; as may appear in the several contentions of bishops, especially that which was between those of Rome and Constantinople, concerning the 52title of *universal priest or bishop*, Mauritius the emperor, favoring John bishop of Constantinople, granted him that prerogative; Gregory bishop of Rome much stomaching it, inveighed sharply against it; styling that same John, *proud, wicked, foolish, antichristian, &c.* as appears in several epistles of his, *Ep. 188. l. 1. ib. ep. 19. ib. Ep. 194.* he says, *ego autem fidenter dico, quod quisquis se UNIVERSALEM SACERDOTEM vocat, vel vocari desiderat, in elatione sua ANTICHRISTUM praecurrit, quod superbiendo se caeteris praeponit*, I say confidently, that whosoever calls himself *universal priest*, or desires so to be called, he foreruns *Antichrist* in his pride; in that, in being arrogant he prefers himself before others. Boniface the third, having at last obtained that primacy, by the favor of Phocas, the then emperor (which Phocas had murdered his sovereign lord, the emperor Mauritius) it was continued in the woeful effects thereof, which are mentioned in many histories. In the Council of Constantinople, *it was ordained, that every bishop should keep within his own jurisdiction*: *To. 1. Canon. 2.* So *To. 1. Concil. Nicen. Can. 6.* Over the banks of which channel, when ambition of prelates once swelled, it overflowed the Christian world, with many sad calamities.

*{against the two brethren}* Because they sought to be above them, who were too much inclined to the same fault, Mark 9.33, 34.

**V. 25. ***{Jesus called them unto him}* That he might appease this emergent contention, that he might reprove the ambition of one party, and the envy of the other, he would thus hear the matter, and reconcile them.

*{Ye know}* See on Luke 22.25. As before, chapter 18. upon the like occasion, where he would quench those unhappy and dangerous sparks of ambition; he pleaded not any *primacy* granted to Peter: so neither does he here, which doubtless he would not have omitted, had he intended any such right by his own donation, and right by himself formerly placed in Peter.

*{that the princes of the Gentiles}* He speaks this to his disciples, intimating thereby, that they were not to expect secular honors and dignities, by following Christ; much less, ambitiously to seek after them; but to carry themselves humbly one towards another, according to his example. Primacy and dominion is proper to secular princes; therefore whatsoever subject affects that, would usurp that which God has not given them. See on verse 26.

**V. 26. ***{it shall not be so among you}* Another manner of life and state must you look for, such as I have undergone before you: you must not domineer (1 Peter 5.3.) but teach and be subject to princes, and labor in the Gospel to save souls. See on Luke 22.26.

**V. 27. ***{let him be your servant}* Let him carry himself humbly towards his fellow servants: or let the most ambitious be least esteemed: let him that most faithfully labors in the word, and is of most holy conversation, be had in most honor: this is the chief dignity of ecclesiastical persons, rather to be good than great.

**V. 28. ***{as the Son of man}* Here he proposes his own example, as John 13.13. I who am the Lord of all, yet have so humbled myself, as to take on me the form of a servant, Phil. 2.7. and to lay down my life for the redemption of the elect; how much more ought you to serve in the ministration of the Gospel, for the saving of your fellow servants?

*{not to be ministered unto}* Not to be attended on as a king in this world; but in the form of a servant and man of sorrows, to work and accomplish your salvation, Phil. 2.7. yet as God, to be worshipped in spirit and truth, John 4.23. 1 John 5.23.

*{to give his life}* John 10.11.

*{a ransom}* *λύτρον*, a satisfactory price of redemption. The apostle calls it *ἀντίλυτρον*, to free us from God’s wrath due unto us for our sins.

*{for many}* Sufficiently for all in regard of the worth and merit of it; though not efficiently in respect of the issue and event thereof: or they are many that are saved by Christ, chap. 26.28. Rom. 5.15, 19. though they be few in comparison of them that perish, chap. 7.13, 14.

**V. 29. ***{as they departed from Jericho}* Mark 10.46. Luke 18.35. John 6.26. 1 Kings 16.34.

**V. 30. ***{two blind men}* But one of better note than the other, called Bartimeus, in St. Mark: these two were healed when Christ came out of Jericho: some think St. Luke mentions another healed before he went into it: and that is most probable. See on Luke 18.35.

*{sitting by the wayside}* As beggars use to do: it seems they were very poor, as well as blind.

*{when they heard that Jesus passed by}* By the multitude going with him, and discoursing of him; or it may be they had asked some of them, and they told them.

*{have mercy on us}* See on chap. 9.27.

**V. 31. ***{the multitude rebuked them}* Either disdaining their condition, as not fit to speak to Christ: or thinking Christ could not heal them: or it may be they were offended at the high title which they gave Christ: or misdoubting they would but disturb and retard his passage; or supposing they meant to beg something of him.

*{but they cried the more}* They were persuaded that Christ could heal them, and would not be dismayed, because the multitude would not give them leave to pray: true faith (the effect whereof is fervent prayer, Psalm 116.10. 2 Cor. 4.13.) like as in the conflicts between the wind and the fire, the more it is opposed, the more it is inflamed.

**V. 32. ***{and called them}* Speaking himself to them, and not sending others to call them.

*{what will ye}* He knew what they did desire, and would ask; but he would have them to declare it, that the people might be the more attentive to the ensuing miracle.

**V. 33. ***{that our eyes may be opened}* The eyes of them, who by reason of any defect, cannot see, are as it were shut up, therefore they would have them opened, that is, their sight restored. See on chapter 9.30. They beg that which no mere man could have given them: it is a short prayer, but the effect of great faith, by which they apprehended both his power to do it, and mercy to perform it.

**V. 34. ***{Jesus had compassion}* He granted both their suits: that which is specified, verse 30. by pitying them, and that which is mentioned, verse 33. by restoring them their sight. The fountain of this miracle was the mercy of Christ, whereby he pitied them: the external means, his touching their eyes: sometimes he only said, *Receive thy sight*, Luke 18.42. Sometimes he anointed them, John 9.6. to teach us, that he can help by ordinary means, without means; and contrary to the ordinary means.

*{presently their eyes}* This is a certain argument of Christ’s divine power: God only can in an instant by his word, and touching them by his repairing hand, give sight to the blind, heal every infirmity, or repair any decay or damage of men.

*{and followed him}* They became his disciples: this was an evident proof of their recovery, bodily and spiritual: and of their gratitude, who did not as many, after they had obtained their petition, go away home; but they followed Christ, that they might be further edified and confirmed: and for others’ sakes, that they might confirm them, by testifying the power and mercy showed on them, which Luke 18.43. is called glorifying of God; true faith is ever thankful: this also is the end of our illumination and spiritual healing by Christ; that we may follow (not our own darkness any more, but) Christ our light. See Eph. 1.17, 18. &c. Eph. 5.8.

## CHAPTER 21

**Verse 1. ***{When they drew nigh}* Mark 11.1. Luke 19.29.

*{to Bethphage}* See on Luke 19.29. It was a sabbath day’s journey from Jerusalem: the valley of Jehoshaphat (divided by the brook Cedron) being between that and Jerusalem. See *Joseph. Antiq. lib. 20. cap. 6.*

*{mount of Olives}* A little hill on the east side of Jerusalem, planted with olive trees, Zech. 14.4. Acts 1.12.

**V. 2. ***{over against you}* This it seems he spoke *δεικτικῶς*, pointing to it with his hand: therefore he named it not, it being then in sight.

*{straightway ye shall find}* You shall not need to look up and down for them, you will soon see them: this he says, lest they should think their finding it so accidental or by chance.

*{a colt}* Not yet weaned from the dam, or a young one.

**V. 3. ***{If any man say unto you ought}* He furnishes them with this answer, lest they should be put off, or retarded in that which he determined should be done.

*{The Lord hath need}* I who am Lord of all, and have more right to them, than they have use of them: therefore he would not have them say, *our Lord*, or *your Lord*, but *the Lord* of this universe; the Lord of all would thus use this man’s goods, being made poor for us; though the earth and the fullness thereof be his. See 2 Cor. 8.9. Yet he would not have them taken up for his use against the owner’s will; but would have him persuaded by two arguments of his just dominion and present necessity.

*{he will send them}* He made the owner willing to let them go: thus he declares, as his omniscience, who foreknew, and told them the event; so also omnipotence, who could rule the wills and affections of all men, to that which he would have done: or *he will send them*, may import, the Lord will presently restore them, or send them back again; the former agrees best with Mark 11.6. and the Syriac interpreter, *& confestim dimittet eos huc.*

**V. 4. ***{all this was done}* Christ did not send for the ass to ride into Jerusalem, for ease of his journey, or ostentation, but that he might declare himself to be the Messiah, whose coming in this very manner, was foretold by the prophets.

**V. 5. ***{Tell ye the daughter of Zion}* Isaiah 62.11. Zech. 9.9. John 12.15. The citizens of Jerusalem, which was built on mount Zion: it is a Hebrew phrase often used in the Lamentations. See the like Psalm 45.12. and 137.7.

*{sitting upon an ass}* As eminent persons, or judges use, Judges 10.4. and 12.14. or to show the peaceableness of his kingdom.

*{and a colt}* He rode on the one, though both be mentioned, as a mark of evidence, showing the fulfilling of the prophecy.

*{of an ass}* *υἱὸν* *ὑποζυγίου*, the foal of an ass under the yoke used to draw in the team.

**V. 6. ***{and the disciples}* Mark 11.4. They made not excuses, as unwilling to undertake that office, either as unworthy of them, or uncertain of the event; but resting assured of the success they find it as they expected.

**V. 7. ***{put on them}* Probably on the one, though both are named. See verse 5.

*{their clothes}* Their upper garments, which being worn loose, were large and long, and so might the more commodiously be cast over beasts to be rid on.

*{set him thereon}* Upon their garments: so did the captains, when they proclaimed Jehu king, 2 Kings 9.13.

**V. 8. ***{spread their garments in the way}* They covered the way with them for him to ride on: as kings at their coronations, or at some solemnities of state, use to have their way upon cloth.

*{cut down branches}* Possibly such as had no loose garments to spare, cut down boughs of trees, and laid them in the way: these were signs of festivity and solemnity: whereby God’s all-disposing providence gave them to understand, that the end of the law was now come, and the true tabernacle in which God dwelt, now present, Revel. 21.3. Col. 2.9.

**V. 9. ***{cried, saying}* Thus they used to cry in the feast of tabernacles, or booths, made of boughs, Nehem. 8.15. and it may be at other solemn times also.

*{Hosanna}* Save now, or *save we beseech thee*: taken out of Psalm 118.26. which psalm, as some observe, was wont to be sung often in their synagogues, and to be expounded concerning Christ’s coming: and was as a solemn form of acclamation (and therefore left by the evangelists in the Hebrew, as a thing commonly known among them) specially in their feast of tabernacles, wherein they were to carry boughs in their hands, by the commandment of God, Levit. 23.40. Here their *הושענא* (a word compounded of the two words *הושיעה* *נא* *hoshiha na* save or help I pray) imports as much as, *Lord prosper*, or give good success to our Messiah which the next words import. See notes *Tremel. in loc.* See on John 12.13.

*{to the son of David}* They desired God to preserve their new king, the son of David, and so acknowledge Christ to be their king by this solemnity. See on chap. 9.27. Though possibly the generality of this people, thought not of Christ’s spiritual kingdom; but these popular acclamations might be in order to their consent unto his secular kingdom, which the most knowing among them, dreamed of.

*{blessed be he, &c.}* Psalm 118.27. Well be it to him that comes in the name of the Lord, that is to say, whom the Lord has promised, and now given us for our king.

*{in the name of the Lord}* This is a Hebrew manner of speaking, importing him, who is, as it were the peculiar ambassador of the Lord.

*{in the highest}* Blessed is he that is in the highest, Luke 2.14. Or, save him, that he may save us, not only here, but in heaven also, Psalm 20.9. which might be the vote of the faithful and true believers in him, confident that Christ Jesus was the very promised Messiah, and Savior of his people.

**V. 10. ***{And when}* Mark 11.15. Luke 19.45. John 12.13.

*{he was come into Jerusalem}* The noise began at the descent of the mount of Olives, and the company (specially by such unusual acclamations) might probably increase in the way: but at his entering the city, the people thereof, must needs take notice thereof.

*{all the city was moved}* All the citizens of Jerusalem were affected, as to strange a noise, confluence, and postures of the people: see chapter 2.3. Ruth 1.19. Possibly the malicious scribes and Pharisees, did either foment and put on this design among the people, to cry him up as their king, that they might destroy him by the Roman power, as aspiring to a kingdom: or that they took occasion at least from these voices of the people, to accuse him hereof to Pilate: though we must still remember, that those things which men do wickedly, God, who creates light of darkness, turns to that which shall advance his glory: as here these acclamations (whoever moved them) gave the faithful knowledge of their Messiah now exhibited; and left the unbelieving so much the more unexcusable.

*{who is this?}* They do not generally do this, as if they knew him not, who had done so many and such unparalleled things among them, as drew all eyes and thoughts to admire, but they speak as amazed, what this sudden pomp should mean, what these acclamations, and the noise of the multitude, as at the inauguration of a king: so was Adonijah troubled, 1 Kings 1.41, 45. and Athaliah, 2 Kings 11.13.

**V. 11. ***{This is Jesus, &c.}* Probably some took him for the Messiah, and others esteemed him as some great prophet; who called him the prophet of Nazareth, in regard of his education, and his parents’ abode there, chapter 2.23. Luke 14.16, 24.

**V. 12. ***{into the temple of God}* Not to towers, or fortifications, but to the temple which might have satisfied them, what manner of kingdom his was; whereof also some show appeared in his harbingers, taking up the ass for his service: by the temple is here meant, that court of the temple, wherein the people prayed, Luke 1.10.

*{he cast out}* He showed himself to be the high priest, and Lord of the temple, by purging it. He had done this once before, John 2.15. but does it now again, because they had polluted it again. The first purging thereof seems to have been about the beginning of his public ministry; and this about the latter end of it: this was done the second day. See Mark 11.12, 15.

*{all them}* He spared none, great nor small.

*{that sold and bought}* Cattle, or doves for sacrifice. See on John 2.14.

*{overthrew the tables}* Showing thereby that they ought not to be set up again. See on John 2.14. There, for any such use as money-changing.

*{of the money changers}* Which changed foreign coin into money there current, that those which dwelt far off, might there according to the law buy sacrifices, and pay their oblations. See on John 2.14.

*{that sold doves}* Poor men’s offerings, Levit. 5.7, 11. and 14.22. Luke 2.24.

**V. 13. ***{it is written}* Partly Isaiah 56.7. partly Jer. 7.1. which he cites to show us how we must deal with the adversaries of truth by Scriptures; and how reformations of abuses in the Church, ought to be according to Scripture, not human inventions, which many times make the cure worse than the disease.

*{my house}* God calls the temple his house, Isa. 56.7. not that he can be included, or dwell in temples made with hands (Acts 7.48, 49.) but because it was a *symbolum*, and pledge of his gracious presence with his people: hence by a metaphor it is called his rest (Psalm 132.14.) and here Christ appears Lord of the temple, in that he reforms it.

*{but ye have made it}* Jer. 7.11. Mark 11.17. Luke 19.46.

*{a den of thieves}* A place to exercise your traffic and fraud in: whereby under color of promoting God’s service there, your care and study is to enrich yourselves, and so to make my Father’s house like such a den or cave, where thieves lie in wait to spoil men passing by, making it a shelter for themselves, and a receptacle for their purchases, or stolen goods, Hosea 6.9. He reproves not only the money changers, but the priests also, who made gain by them *τὸ* *γὰρ* *φιλοκερδὲς*, *λῃστρῶν* *πάθος* *ἐστί*, says Theophylact, for desire of gain is a thievish affection: in that he said, *my house shall be called the house of prayer*; he shows what by right it should be: but in that he says, *ye have made it a den of thieves*, he declares the impropriation and abuse thereof. So Mal. 2.8. There is much difference between that which is, and that which should be.

**V. 14. ***{and he healed them}* To show his divine power in his temple, into which he was to come, Mal. 3.1. and to stop the mouths of Pharisees, if they should again question him for purging the temple, or meddling with matters (as they conceived) being peculiar to them and their office and jurisdiction; and none other person whatsoever, except by their permission and leave.

**V. 15. ***{saw the wonderful things}* The miracles which he wrought on the blind, lame, &c. which were presented to him to be healed as verse 14. &c. They take no notice of them, though they saw them; but calumniate him for suffering the children to express his honor due.

**V. 16. ***{hearest thou what these say?}* As if they said, their blasphemy is so palpable, that you could not for shame but rebuke them, if you heard them.

*{have ye never read}* See on chap. 12.2. Thus he reproves their ignorant malice, and approves the acclamations of their children, by the testimony of David.

*{out of the mouth}* Psalm 8.2. The word *νηπίων* here used for, (of infants) signifies not only a child in years (coming of *νη* a privative particle, and *ἔπος*, a word, as if we should say, one that has not a word to say, or can hardly speak) but also a simple man, or one contemptible before men (see on chap. 11.25.) unlearned, rude, ignorant; of slender understanding, 1 Cor. 3.1. Here *θηλαζόντων*, of sucklings is set as Psalm 8.2. for the more full expression of the sense; which is to show the admirable power of God to set forth his glory, in and by the weakest and most improbable means.

*{thou hast}* These children do not blaspheme, but God has stirred them up, to acknowledge me to be the Messiah, to his glory.

*{perfected praise}* Psalm 8.3. *יסדת* *עז* *jissadata hoz*, *thou hast founded strength*, which here is called praise: that is, strong praise. See Psalm 29.1. and 96.7. and 118.14. Therefore the seventy, which this evangelist follows, gives it *αἶνον*, praise: the word *κατηρτίσω*, here used, imports a firm decreeing, or appointing a thing which in God’s counsel is a perfecting that which cannot be altered, nor miss effect, Esther 1.8. and mouth is here taken for testimony, as chap. 18.16. Theophylact interprets it, that the Spirit of God spoke by them.

**V. 17. ***{went out of the city}* He would not stay in the city in the night, to avoid all suspicion of his affecting a temporal kingdom, and to have the better convenience of solitude for prayer and private instruction, necessary for those whom he would send to preach the Gospel to all nations.

*{to Bethany}* A little village near Jerusalem, John 11.18. about two miles off, that he might further confirm and comfort Lazarus (whom he had lately raised from the dead) and Mary and Martha his sisters.

**V. 18. ***{he hungered}* Showing thereby that he was subject to human infirmities; and also that he might have occasion to work the ensuing miracle. See chapter 4.2. Possibly he had eaten no supper, but spent the night in teaching those present, or praying, as he often had done.

**V. 19. ***{and when he saw}* Mark 11.13.

*{Let no fruit grow on thee}* See on Mark 11.13. Some conceive that our Savior intended hereby, to foreshow the destruction of Jerusalem, because they brought forth nothing but leaves of ceremonies: others think it was only for an occasion of the ensuing exhortation, verse 21.

**V. 20. ***{when the disciples saw it}* On the morrow, as they passed by again, Mark 11.20.

*{how soon is the fig tree withered}* They wondered at the event, who did not so much consider the cause: it was more to be admired, why Christ cursed this tree, who was ever wont to bless and preserve his creatures. See Mark 11.20, 21. *Ξηραίνει* *οὖν* *τὸ* *δένδρον* *ἵνα* *σωφρονίσῃ* *ἀνθρώπους*, *he made the tree wither, that he might amend men*, says Theophylact: hereby showing them the condition of the Jewish synagogue.

**V. 21. ***{If ye have faith}* See on chap. 17.20.

*{and doubt not}* *καὶ* *μὴ* *διακριθῆτε*, the word signifies a doubting, wherein a man does, as it were with opposite opinions, contend with himself, recounting such difficulties as render him diffident and fluctuant, as not knowing what to resolve, or which way to incline. See Romans 4.20. and on Acts 10.20. But herein he means that effect of faith in prayer which asks *τὰ* *συμφέροντα*, things profitable, and convenient, as Theophylact well notes.

*{ye shall say unto this mountain}* As if he had said, there shall be nothing (which conduces to God’s glory, and your salvation) so hard or seeming impossible to man, but steadfastly believing, you shall attain it. See on chapter 17.20. and on Luke 17.6.

**V. 22. ***{all things}* Agreeable to God’s will, 1 John 5.14. Mark 11.24.

*{believing}* Prayers without faith are fruitless, *πιστεύοντες*, this participle includes a condition of obtaining; (see James 1.6.) believing that God can, and will give. See on chapter 7.7, 8. Yet when God does not give to the prayer of faith, Prosper’s rule is ever to be held, *nec est iniquitas apud Deum, qui saepe postulata non tribuit, quae postulare donavit*, *contr. Collat. cap. 26.* Neither is there unrighteousness with God, who oftentimes does not give those things desired, or entreated, which he gave (faith and a heart) to entreat, that he may give something better, as more conducing to his glory, and the petitioner’s salvation: see Deuteronomy 3.23, 24, 25, 26. 2 Sam. 12.16, 18. 2 Cor. 12.8, 9.

**V. 23. ***{when he was come into the temple}* To teach the people, and to perform this part of his priestly and prophetical office. See Mark 11.27. and Luke 20.1.

*{the chief priests and elders of the people}* These men seeing the glory of his miracles, the people following him, his casting out the buyers and sellers out of the temple, where the priests permitted them to be, they stomached him, as concluding, that if he thus proceeded without their leave to meddle with the government of the temple, and teaching therein; their authority and honor, must soon suffer a total eclipse, and come into contempt: therefore they question his authority. Envy ever calumniates good things (as Euthymius notes): it is the black shadow which ever waits on those, on whom prosperity, or any popular opinion of excellency shines.

*{by what authority}* They question not now his doctrine, which they knew they were not able to gainsay; nor his miracles, which clearly enough appeared to be the works of God; only they move a captious question: if he should have said, by divine authority, they would have accused him of blasphemy; if he should have answered by human authority, themselves were the rulers, who in an ordinary calling, had care and charge of the temple; if he should have said, without authority, they would have accused him of sedition.

**V. 24. ***{I also will ask you one thing}* As they asked not with any mind to learn, but to find some calumny against him; so neither does Christ answer them so directly as they expected: for as Theophylact says well, *τοῖς* *κακούργως* *ἐρωτῶσιν*, *οὐ* *δεῖ* *ἀποκρίνεσθαι*: we must not answer them, who malignantly inquire, or question; but in that he answers by his question concerning John, they might have understood, that his authority was supreme, he being the very God and Lord of the temple; if they had indeed believed John, who openly testified him to be the only Son of God; so therefore Christ answers them by a dilemmatical question, verse 25, 26. So he takes the subtle in their subtlety, 1 Cor. 3.19.

**V. 25. ***{the baptism of John}* That is, John’s ministry and doctrine, who preached the baptism of repentance, Mark 1.4.

*{reasoned with themselves}* They beat their heads about it, and mused or laid their heads together, well perceiving their unavoidable error, and that they must be convicted in their answer to either part of Christ’s question.

*{from heaven}* From God (as appears by the opposition. So Dan. 4.28. Luke 15.18, 21.) that is, of divine authority, and institution.

*{he will say unto us}* Thus the hypocrite’s conscience is self-convicted: they feared that Christ by saying thus, would make their unbelief and obstinacy appear to the people, whose good opinion was a great part of their ambition.

**V. 26. ***{If we say of men}* See note on Luke 20.6.

*{we fear the people}* Lest they should stone us.

*{all hold John as a prophet}* Chap. 14.5.

**V. 27. ***{we cannot tell}* They could, but durst not. See verse 25, 26. See on Luke 20.7.

**V. 28. ***{But what think ye}* He thinks it not enough to stop their mouth; but, by a parable, he makes them to condemn themselves, as Nathan did David, 2 Sam. 12. and the disguised prophet Ahab, 1 Kings 20.38. &c.

*{a certain man had two sons}* These are expounded to be publicans and Pharisees, verse 31, 32. by some interpreters.

**V. 19. ***{afterward he repented}* Thinking more seriously of the matter, he undertook and went about that work, which he formerly declined and refused.

**V. 30. ***{and said likewise}* As verse 28.

*{I go}* *Ἐγὼ*, I, he understood, *ἀπέρχομαι*, will go, or will do as you command: hereby he noted the Pharisees, who professed much readiness and willingness to serve God, but did not: the speech is *elliptical*, usual in case of answering commands, or offering service, wherein they said, *הנני* *hinnem*, behold I, or I am ready: so Israel promised, Exod. 24.3, 7. See Gen. 37.13. 1 Sam. 3.4, 5, 6. Isaiah 6.8.

**V. 31. ***{the first}* Hereby they confess, by just consequence, that the repenting publicans and sinners were better than themselves, with all their fair pretenses and shows of good works.

*{into the kingdom of God before you}* They made haste to the kingdom of God, and you are slow and remiss; you should at least have followed their example: this word, goe before, is improperly taken in this place, where none follow: they shall go to heaven, rather than you; or, so shall not you: or these whom you despise, as unholy, might be guides and patterns for you to follow, but that your pride and malice blind you.

**V. 32. ***{John came unto you, &c.}* Chapter 3.8. By way of righteousness; he means, living uprightly, being of a holy and honest conversation, and persuading you to do so: as the Hebrews use the word, *דרך*, *derech*, for life and manners, Psalm 119.1.

*{repented not}* Of your evil deeds, or of your unbelieving John.

**V. 33. ***{Hear another parable}* In the former parable, he had showed them their neglect of John’s doctrine: in this he sets forth their cruelty, shortly after to be exercised on himself; and ushers it in by their fathers’ cruelty to the prophets.

*{which planted a vineyard}* Isaiah 5.1. &c. Jer. 2.21. Mark 12.1. Luke 20.9. God planted the Jewish Church, as his vineyard, Psalm 80.9. &c.

*{hedged it}* Herein is implied God’s care of the safety and prosperity of it, Psalm 35.27. The meaning is, he defended, and preserved it from the incursions of the enemies, which otherwise would have destroyed it.

*{digged a winepress}* Furnished it with all things necessary.

*{built a tower}* Made the place strong; for a tower, between the curtains, is as a buttress and strength of the wall.

*{let it out to husbandmen}* To the priests.

*{went into a far country}* Left it wholly to their care; in outward appearance, as if he had been absent; whereas indeed, as Theophylact says, *ἡ* *ἀποδημία* *τοῦ* *Θεοῦ* *ἡ* *μακροθυμία*, *the peregrination of God, is his long-suffering*: he seems as if he slept, or were gone far off, when he forbears long, and does not presently punish sinners; or does not manifest his presence to men of this world.

**V. 34. ***{sent his servants}* The prophets whom the Jews slew.

**V. 35. ***{beat one}* See on Luke 20.10. So Pashur the priest beat the prophet Jeremiah, Jer. 20.2. and Zedekiah, Michaiah, 1 Kings 22.24.

*{another they killed}* Many others they slew. Which of the prophets have they not persecuted? Acts 7.52.

*{stoned another}* As if they had been blasphemers: so Joash used Zechariah the son of Jehoiada, 2 Chron. 24.21, 22.

**V. 36. ***{more than the first}* They had more prophets a little before the captivity than formerly. See 1 Sam. 3.1. with Isaiah 1.1. Hosea 1.1.

**V. 37. ***{sent unto them his son}* Christ himself, who here foretells his own death. See on Luke 20.14.

**V. 38. ***{come let us kill him}* See chap. 26.3. John 11.53. Gen. 37.16, 20. See on Luke 20.14.

*{let us seize on his inheritance}* Let us give laws, and rule the Church according to our own will, as we have many years done.

**V. 39. ***{they cast him out of the vineyard}* Delivered him to the Romans to be condemned: and slew him without the city, Heb. 13.12. See on Luke 20.15.

**V. 40. ***{What will he do?}* He makes them judges, having spoken the parable concerning them; meaning to condemn them out of their own mouth, as verse 31. See on Luke 20.15.

**V. 41. ***{He will miserably destroy}* *κακοὺς* *κακῶς* *ἀπολέσει*, it is a kind of Greek proverb, meaning he will with grievous punishments consume those impious tenants, and provide him better.

**V. 42. ***{Did ye never read}* Psalm 118.22. Acts 4.11. See on chap. 12.3.

*{which the builders rejected}* Those whom before he compared to husbandmen, here he compares to builders.

*{the head of the corner}* The chief stone in the corner is called the head of the corner: which being the strength of the walls, the builders usually make choice of some principal stone for that place. So Christ is to the Church, though refused of the priests, Eph. 2.22. He is as Euthymius says, *conjunctio populorum*, the stone which united the (two walls) the people (of the Jews and Gentiles). See Acts 4.11. *In unam fidem Abrahae colligens eos, qui ex utroque testamento apti sunt in aedificationem Dei*, *Irenaeus lib. 4. cap. 42.*

*{This is the Lord’s doing}* That is, God appointed him to this office, though the Jews through malice and emulation rejected him. See Heb. 5.4, 5.

**V. 43. ***{the kingdom of God shall be taken from you}* That is, the Gospel, the scepter of his kingdom; the word and ministry, whereby men are gathered into his kingdom, and his protection, as Euthymius interprets. See Acts 13.46. Rom. 11.8. &c.

*{and given to another nation}* As he foretold the rejection of the Jews, so also the calling of the Gentiles. See places fore-cited.

*{the fruits thereof}* Every kingdom has its own proper fruits: the fruits of God’s kingdom are the fruits of the Holy Spirit, not of corrupted flesh. See Gal. 5.22. Eph. 5.9. The fruits here spoken of, must be, not only meet for repentance, but also beseeming God’s kingdom; not only leaving evil, but also doing good. See Isa. 1.16, 17.

**V. 44. ***{Whosoever shall fall on this stone}* Isaiah 8.14, 15. Rom. 9.33. 1 Pet. 2.7. The meaning is, as Theophylact after Chrysostom interprets, *σκανδαλιζόμενοι* *ἐπὶ* *τῷ* *Χριστῷ*, they who are offended in Christ; as contemners and unbelievers, who blasphemed him: or as taking offense at my humility; he shall not hurt me, but destroy himself.

*{shall be broken}* As a man falling upon a rock, breaks not it, but his own bones.

*{it will grind him to powder}* Which Chrysostom and others understand of temporal and eternal destruction. See Daniel 2.44.

**V. 45. ***{his parables}* These two fore-mentioned, verse 28, 33.

*{they perceived he spake of them}* Their own consciences interpreted the parables concerning themselves: yet were their hearts so hardened, that they could not repent.

**V. 46. ***{they feared the multitude}* Wicked hypocrites abstain from sin: only for fear of punishment: so for the present, these enemies of Christ (though in great authority) are awed by fear, lest the people who honored Christ should fall upon them. Evil magistrates are sometimes kept from cruelty, for fear of inferiors; their breaking God’s laws, gives example to the people to break theirs: this is just with God, though the people sin in so doing.

*{took him for a prophet}* The people deservedly took him for a prophet, whom the priests unjustly, and impiously accounted as an impostor: chap. 12.24. Mark 3.22. See Deut. 18.18. John 1.45. Acts 1.45. Acts 3.20. &c. Acts 7.37. &c.

## CHAPTER 22

**Verse 1. ***{Jesus answered}* To the thoughts of Pharisees, who were offended at the former parables: or he added more parables to the former: it is usual with the Hebrews to say, he answered, instead of, he said, or began to speak. St. Luke recording this parable, shows the occasion of Christ’s speech was, that which one said unto him, who sat at meat with him in the Pharisee’s house, to wit, *blessed is he that shall eat bread in the kingdom of God.* Luke 14.15, 16.

*{by parables}* See on chap. 9.17. and 13.3, 18. Luke 5.6. In the former parable he had threatened destruction to the Pharisees, here to all the people: he shows the manner also, to wit, by sword and fire; and that after his death the Gospel should be preached to the Gentiles, &c.

**V. 2. ***{the kingdom of heaven is like}* See on chap. 20.1.

*{a certain king which made a marriage for his son}* That is, prepared a marriage feast so magnificent as became a kingly state. That which the Greek calls here *γάμους*, the Syriac interprets by *משתותא* *meshtutho*, (that which the Greeks called *συμπόσιον*) *a bibendo*; for *שתה* *shetho*, *bibere*, is properly to drink: but by a synecdoche, to celebrate and keep a marriage feast: so Gen. 29.22. *Laban made* *משתה* *mishte*, which the seventy give as here, *ἐποίησε* *γάμον*, he made a wedding feast: that is he sent the tidings of Christ’s incarnation, or the Gospel; which is compared to a marriage feast full of joy, made by a king, therefore full of state; not for a servant, or friend, but for his son. God makes this marriage between Christ and his Church of Jews and Gentiles. *Jerom.*

**V. 3. ***{his servants to call}* His prophets to foretell the coming of Christ into the world, and invite men to receive him with joy (see Luke 14.17.) signifying the hour or instant time, that they might prepare.

*{them that were bidden to the wedding}* Those who were formerly invited: that is the Jews, who had been before certified, and instructed concerning the coming of Christ our Savior.

*{and they would not come}* He sent John Baptist, the twelve apostles, the disciples to invite the Jews to repentance, Matt. 3.1, 2. Mark 6.12. Luke 3.3. and 9.2, 6. but would not. See on Luke 14.18.

**V. 4. ***{other servants}* The apostles, &c. as verse 3. After the prophets he sent the apostles, and ministers of the Gospel to invite men to him, that they might believe and be saved. See 1 Cor. 5.20.

*{I have prepared my dinner}* This is another call, to let the guests understand, that on God’s part all things are ready, and he expects their coming to him.

*{fatlings are killed}* *τὰ* *σιτιστὰ*, *altilia*, or *saginata*, as divers of the ancient Latins translate it: it is of *σιτίζειν*, to feed, as they use fatting beasts, or fowls. The word *τεθυμένα* killed, of *θύειν*, properly signifies things sacrificed; but is used also to signify things killed for a feast: the reason whereof might be, for that even profane princes used to auspicate their solemn feasts with sacrifices. See Isaiah 25.6.

**V. 5. ***{one to his farm}* So worldly men look after secular riches, not valuing the eternal: so pleasures and avarice keep men from Christ and salvation.

**V. 6. ***{entreated them spitefully}* Calling them profaners, pestilent fellows, movers of sedition, imprisoned, scourged, and put them to death. See Acts 5.40. and 21.32. 2 Cor. 11.24. Acts 12.2. Acts 7.58. Heb. 11.37, 38. See also *Joseph. Antiq. l. 20. c. 8.* What more spitefully could be done, than so ungratefully, and unworthily to requite them with injuries, who had none other end but to advance God’s honor in their salvation: we must not think much of our sufferings; Christ foretold us, the world shall hate you: see Phil. 1.28, 29, 30. and John 12.2.

*{and slew them}* So did the Jews, Stephen, Acts 7. and Herod, James, &c.

**V. 7. ***{he was wroth}* See Luke 14.21. God’s anger is the just execution of his judgments on the wicked and impenitent.

*{sent forth his armies}* The Romans (as Theophylact, Jerome, &c. interpret) called God’s armies, because employed by him to destroy Jerusalem, as was foretold Dan. 9.26.

*{those murderers}* The Jews who slew the apostles, verse 6.

*{and burnt up their city}* This was done to Jerusalem by the Romans, wherein (as *Josephus de bel. Judaic. l. 7.* and others report) perished eleven millions of men, women, and children; the rest being sold for slaves, or otherwise scattered among all nations; their temple and city were fired and demolished.

**V. 8. ***{the wedding}* The wedding feast.

*{were not worthy}* The Jews for the most part were unworthy. Acts 13.46. See also on chap. 10.13.

**V. 9. ***{go ye therefore}* Jerome and Theophylact understand by these second messengers, the apostles sent to the Gentiles: which being true, we must also understand the ministers sent after them until the fullness of the Gentiles be come in.

*{into the highways}* The meaning is, Go preach the Gospel where greatest confluences of people ought to be in God’s service; or use to be in the Churches of the Gentiles: or preach to the most abject, and those whom the Jews despise. See Luke 14.21.

*{as many as ye shall find, bid}* God calls us, when we think not of it, Isaiah 65.2. Rom. 10.21. and invites all without exception, in the rule, Mark 10.15. Matt. 11.28. though in 56some cases, according to his just judgments, for causes best, and only to him known, he sometimes restrains, either in the general, as Matthew 7.6. or the particular, as Acts 16.6, 7. See on Luke 14.23.

**V. 10. ***{both bad and good}* High and low, rich and poor, profane and civil; for men of all sorts were to be found among the Gentiles. See chap. 13.38, 47, 48. Only remember that God finds none good to be invited, but makes them such by his invitation.

*{the wedding was furnished}* Literally, the place where the wedding was furnished, or the tables were furnished with guests; spiritually, the Church was, by the preaching of the Gospel, full of those who gave their names to Christ, though of these, some were true believers, and some hypocrites.

**V. 11. ***{when the king came in}* Some understand this of the day of judgment, others of the time of the Gospel here: wherein God, observing the hypocrites in the Church, takes them away by death, and sends them to hell: the sense is when God comes to judge: and this which follows seems partly inferred to propose an objection, which the Jews might have made; does God reject us to call the Gentiles? are there not as wicked among them, as us? Yes: but he will come and examine who has the wedding garment, and who not, and judge them accordingly: by this one without the wedding garment, are understood all those, *qui sociati sunt malitia*, *Jerom.* who are combined in maliciousness. And here is a grave admonition to us, that we may not think it enough for us to take up rooms in the Church of Christ, if we walk not worthy of our high calling in him, God will find us out.

*{had not a wedding garment}* Conversation becoming the calling of a Christian. See Eph. 4.1. Phil. 1.27. 2 Pet. 1.10. Revel. 19.8. and 3.18.

**V. 12. ***{Friend, how?}* He calls him friend, because he had invited him to the wedding, *Jerom.*; yet has it the sense of a sharp reproof of him, that so unworthily requited God’s gracious calling, in that he, who had obtained so much favor in a friendly invitation, would so dishonor the company with an unseemly habit, that is, such impious and wicked course of life, as became not the Gospel.

*{how camest thou hither}* As if he had said, *qua fronte?* how could you for shame come hither, into such company, so sordidly habited (under this similitude God reproves the secure impudence of those, who continuing in their filthy impurity of sins, yet dare associate themselves with true Christians).

*{not having a wedding garment}* Divers diversely express this, but all to one purpose: obedience to the moral law, and Gospel — *quae novi hominis efficiunt vestimentum*, *Jerom.*; which make the garment of the new man. *Prosper de promiss. & praedict. part. 2. cap. 1.* says it is, as 1 Tim. 1.5. charity out of a pure heart, and of a good conscience, and of faith unfeigned: *Hilary can. 22.* seems to understand it of the gifts of the Ghost, and heavenly candor of holy conversation, as Phil. 3.20. Chrysostom, Theophylact, Euthymius, &c. understand by the sordid garment, sinful uncleanness, and want of mercy and charity. *Greg. hom. 38. in Evang.* says also, that the wedding garment is charity. Note from all this, 1. that God’s all-seeing eye of justice easily finds out hypocrites: 2. that a bare profession of Christianity, and assembling in holy duties with them, shall never save any man, except he adorn that profession with newness and holiness of life; without peace and holiness no man shall see the Lord, Heb. 12.14. 3. that no man is called for the wedding garment’s sake, neither accepted without it, that is, saved for his good works, nor without them; we are called *χάριτι* *μόνῃ* *πάντες*, all by free grace, as Chrysostom, Theophylact, Euthymius, &c. here note.

*{he was speechless}* *ἐφιμώθη*, he was muzzled, 1 Cor. 9.9. He could say nothing for himself; his own conscience condemned, and silenced him: so shall it be to all those, who now under the name of Christians, dishonor the Gospel, and profession of Christ.

**V. 13. ***{to the servants}* That attended on the guests, that is, to the angels and ministering spirits.

*{bind him hand and foot}* Make him sure, as they do condemned men: it imports that the damned shall have no power to resist, nor possibility of flying from God’s severe judgments.

*{cast him}* *ἐκβάλετε* *cast him forth*, so the Syriac interpreter *ejicite*.

*{into outer darkness}* See on chap. 8.12. That is, as Prosper understands it, *de vit. contempl. l. 3. c. 11.* expulsion from the blessed presence of God, who is *mentium lumen*.

*{weeping and gnashing of teeth}* This imports their bitter sorrows, who shall be condemned to eternal death, *non videndi sensum habituri sunt, sed dolendi*, *Prosper q. 5.* They shall not have sense of seeing, but of sorrowing.

**V. 14. ***{for many are called}* Chap. 20.16. Many that are called do not come so far as to profess religion, or any care for the life to come; and among them that do profess well, many are not saved for want of faith and holy conversation.

*{but few are chosen}* That is, (as Augustine says to Paulinus *epist. 59.*) few are, *secundum propositum vocati, in quibus Dei procul dubio praescientia falli non potest*, called according to his purpose (Rom. 8.28.) in whom doubtless the foreknowledge of God could not be deceived; neither is there any unrighteousness with God, who foreseeing the whole mass corrupted, in mercy chose some to be vessels of honor, to whom he does freely give believing, and obedient hearts, that they may be saved; and in justice leaves others to their own sinful wills; who disbelieving, and disobeying the Gospel, which calls them to faith and repentance, are the immediate causes of their own just condemnation, as *Clem. Alexandr. strom. lib. 1.* says, *οἱ* *δὲ* *μὴ* *θελήσαντες* *πείθεσθαι* *ἑαυτοὺς* *ἀπέστησαν* *δηλαδή*, but those who would not obey, doubtless withdrew themselves from hope of eternal life.

**V. 15. ***{then went the Pharisees}* When the Pharisees had attempted all means to destroy Christ, and could not prevail; at last they take this counsel. See Mark 12.13. Luke 20.20.

*{entangle him}* *παγιδεύσωσιν* *ἐν* *λόγῳ*, how they might ensnare him in his answers; this word is properly used for catching as hunters do, with gins, snares, or toils; their intent was to bring Christ into danger, by occasioning him to answer something prejudicious to the Roman emperor, and so they might deliver him to Pilate the deputy, to be put to death as a mover of sedition, Luke 20.20. or that which must offend the people, and then the Pharisees might destroy him without fear of the multitude, which now mainly awed them, chap. 26.5.

**V. 16. ***{they sent to him their disciples}* They would not go themselves to him; having been so lately foiled by him, chap. 21. but sent their disciples who should feign themselves just men, that they might avoid suspicion, and that Christ dealing more freely with them, might more easily be entrapped, Luke 20.20.

*{with the Herodians}* They were men who joined with Herod to patch up a religion, mixed of heathenism and Judaism: in likelihood they maintained the lawfulness of paying this tribute, and the Pharisees the unlawfulness of it; so that if he said it was unlawful; the Herodians were ready to accuse him to the governor: if he said it was lawful, the Pharisees were as ready to accuse him to the people: though these so much differed in their own opinions, yet both readily agree together against Christ: see the like chap. 16.1. Luke 23.12. See on chap. 16.6. The Syriac gives it, *דבית* *הרודס*, *debeth Hiraudis*, of the household servants, or courtiers of Herod. Jerome and Chrysostom understand Herod’s soldiers.

*{the way of God}* The way to heaven, revealed by God.

*{in truth}* Truly and sincerely: you speak neither for fear nor favor of Pilate or Herod. *Theophylact.* Thus are they self-condemned while they seek accusations against him: they confess they know that he is true, why then did they not believe in him, that they might have been saved?

*{neither carest thou for any man}* You are not so afraid of any man, as therefore to conceal the truth of God.

*{the person of men}* That is, the outward appearance, state, or condition of men.

**V. 17. ***{tribute}* *κῆνσον*, it is a Latin word made Greek, properly signifying a valuation of goods or estate, a taxing: according to the proportion whereof they paid tribute in the subdued provinces: and it is here taken for the tribute itself, chap. 17.24. It is called *δίδραχμα* the half shekel of the sanctuary; they ask if it were lawful to pay this to Caesar? The color of this *quaere* was, that God in the law had commanded it to be paid to him yearly, for the use of the temple, or tabernacle, Exod. 30.13. The Romans conquering Judea, did impropriate, and take it to their own use; for which they afterwards making land cesses by appointment of the Romans, called it tribute. So Augustus commanded all the provinces to be taxed, Luke 2.1. This was laid on every family (*Joseph. antiq. lib. 19. cap. 15.*) now the Jews murmured everywhere at this, as an impious, or at least unworthy thing; that profane men should thus invade God’s right, and dishonor them; hence was the insurrection of Theudas and Judas, Acts 5.36, 37. mentioned.

**V. 18. ***{Jesus perceived their wickedness}* Their malice and mischievous mind against him.

*{hypocrites why tempt ye me?}* Hypocrites; for you would seem to be holy, but are impious; and you come to me with pretense of your good opinion of my truth and sincerity; when yet I well know you come to tempt me, and to ensnare and betray me: he is God, and therefore cannot be deceived with words and fair pretenses, as Chrysostom notes: a hypocrite is he, *qui aliud est, aliud simulat*, *Jerom.* who is one thing, and counterfeits to be another.

**V. 19. ***{shew me the tribute money}* He calls for it, that he might take occasion from the sight thereof, to confute them the more effectually. Verse 21.

*{a penny}* In value seven pence halfpenny, the Syriac interpreter has, *denarium argenti capitationis*, a penny of silver, poll-money, or head-money.

**V. 20. ***{whose image or superscription}* Or, *inscription*; he so attempers to their captious question, as that, if they would acknowledge truth and reason, he had most appositely answered them: seeing Caesar had power to appoint coins among them, he had therewith power and right to impose tribute on them.

**V. 21. ***{They said unto him Caesar’s}* The emperors ever used to have their images and superscriptions stamped on their coin: probably containing some words, expressing their empire over the Jews and other nations to them subject; which might have satisfied any, but the impudent and malicious. See on Luke 20.19. for this whole story.

*{render therefore}* As a debt, Rom. 13.7. not as a benevolence, or arbitrary contribution, which in some cases is fit and necessary for subjects to relieve their princes withal, but that which being commanded by lawful authority ought to be paid. See Rom. 13.7.

*{unto Caesar}* You think your payments to maintain God’s service, free you from paying tribute to Caesar: but you must maintain, both religion and civil power.

*{which are Caesar’s}* His stamp upon them, shows you are his subjects, and therefore ought to pay tribute unto him: for coining was ever a sign of sovereign power. See on Luke 20.25.

*{unto God the things that are God’s}* Tithes, firstfruits, oblations, and sacrifices, *Jerom.*; above all, true sanctity; for as Augustine says well, *epist. 45. Armentario*, he thus said, that man might know *quod Deus exigeret ab homine, imaginem suam in homine ipso: sicut suam Caesar exigebat in nummis*; that God requires of man his own image in man himself; as Caesar would have his, in his coin.

**V. 22. ***{they marveled}* That he should perceive their close machinations, and so prudently disappoint their subtlety. See Psalm 9.15. Prov. 21.30.

**V. 23. ***{The same day}* Mark 12.18. Luke 20.27. So vigilant is malice, that they give Christ no rest.

*{the Sadducees}* Who are interpreted *justi*, says Jerome. Chrysostom says, the evangelist admires their impudency, who durst assail him, the same day that the Pharisees were confuted. See on chap. 3.7. and on Luke 20.27.

*{which say there is no resurrection}* Acts 23.8. As believing no spiritual substance severed from a body, and therefore that soul and body must die together.

**V. 24. ***{Moses said}* Deut. 25.5.

*{If a man die having no children}* That is, if he have never a son, for daughters raise no name to their father, but go into another family: and in Deut. 25. a son only is mentioned both of him that is dead, and of him that married his wife to raise up seed to him. See on Luke 20.28.

**V. 25. ***{left his wife unto his brother}* According to the law.

**V. 26. ***{Likewise the second}* This is added to make the thing seem more absurd, the more husbands the woman had, as they conceived.

**V. 27. ***{the woman died also}* Before her death there was no question, therefore her death is mentioned.

**V. 28. ***{whose wife shall she be, &c.}* As if they had said, in the resurrection, which you suppose shall once be; which of the seven shall be esteemed her husband? or shall all have her to wife? See on Luke 20.33.

*{for they all had her}* See verse 26. &c.

**V. 29. ***{not knowing the Scriptures}* Not acknowledging the books of the prophets, out of which the resurrection might be more clearly proved: nor yet, understanding the law of Moses; which you acknowledge to be God’s word, for so the Sadducees did, though they rejected the rest; therefore Christ here, having to deal with such, cited no other Scriptures of the Old Testament, but only Moses’ writing, which they allowed for canonical.

*{nor the power of God}* You consider not how well God is able to raise the dead; as he has promised. See Phil. 1.21.

**V. 30. ***{in the resurrection}* In the state of glory, which shall follow the resurrection.

*{given in marriage}* Neither do men take wives, nor parents give their daughters in marriage.

*{as the angels}* Not without bodies, but without marriage, or any bodily necessities, to which they are subject in this mortal life, but of a spiritual conversation, as Jerome speaks. See hereof *Augustin Consent. ep. 146.*

**V. 31. ***{Have ye not read}* See on chap. 12.5.

*{unto you}* You acknowledge it to be Scripture, and to bind you.

**V. 32. ***{I am}* Exod. 3.6.

*{The God of Abraham}* This was spoken when they were all three dead. He does not say, the Lord of Abraham, for he is Lord of liveless things also. Chap. 11.27. Psalm 24.1. But, the God of Abraham in covenant with him. Now seeing this covenant of God was with the bodies of these three patriarchs, as well as with their souls, as appears by their circumcision; it follows, that as their souls were then alive, so their bodies must needs be raised from the dead, and be made eternally happy.

*{but of the living}* *Θεῷ* *γὰρ* *ζῶσι* *πάντες* *οἱ* *κατὰ* *Θεὸν* *ζήσαντες*, all live with God, who lived to God, or according to his will, *Gr. Nazianzen orat. 21.* This place is very considerable for the confutation of the modern Sadducees, who were herein less knowing, than many prudent heathens, who acknowledged and professed the immortality of the soul, which these pretended Christians shamefully deny; but if they will not stand to the infallible authority of holy Scripture, we leave them with that: Rev. 22.11. *he that is unjust, let him be unjust still.* Unworthy are they to know God and be saved, who blaspheme his truth: if they do acknowledge the Scriptures, let them answer, how is God the God of the living in this instance of Abraham? Not in respect of the present state of the body, which lived not without the soul, and he in his time died, Gen. 25.8, 9. and was buried in the cave of Machpelah? If then Abraham’s soul lived not, how was God, the God of living Abraham? Or in what respect did Abraham live, if neither his body (as the Scripture says) nor his soul (as these new doctors affirm) did then live? Let these be ashamed of their atheism, and quickly repent, submitting to God’s truth, or they shall too late find, that their souls are immortal to torment, who would not believe the truth, that they might be saved.

**V. 33. ***{The multitude}* The people gave more respect to Christ’s doctrine than the Sadducees did.

**V. 34. ***{But when}* Mark 12.28.

*{he had put the Sadducees to silence}* They thought it would be a great honor to them, to silence him that had silenced the Sadducees.

*{they were gathered together}* They advised about the business; or, they came together about Christ: as verse 41. to dispute with Christ: for they thought themselves much more knowing and learned men, than the Sadducees. Though they abominated as impious and heretical the tenet of Sadducees, yet they envied Christ’s victory who had refuted and silenced them; therefore, though they also had often been overcome, yet presumed they once again to re-encounter him, in hope to blast the honor of his late victory before the people, of whose good opinion and applause they were extremely ambitious.

**V. 35. ***{a lawyer}* He is called a scribe. Mark 12.28. See chap. 2.4. & chap. 8.19. and on Luke 20.1.

*{tempting him}* Some think it was not out of malice, as verse 18. and elsewhere: but out of desire of knowledge, as the queen of Sheba proved Solomon, 1 Kings 10.1. But other conceive it was a captious question, to the end he might expose Christ to the hatred of the people: *non scire desiderans, sed tentans*, *Jerom.* Not desiring to know, but tempting. Theophylact says he came *ἐξ* *ἀμέτρου* *φθόνου*: out of unmeasurable envy; though being something better informed, and answering discreetly, he were not far from the kingdom of God. Mark 12.34.

**V. 36. ***{the great commandment}* The greatest: because some accounted those concerning sacrifices chiefest, as is intimated, Mark 12.23. Some, that of circumcision: some, that of the sabbath.

**V. 37. ***{Thou shalt love the Lord}* Deut. 6.5. Where we read, *with all thy heart, and with all thy soul, and with all thy might*, here left out, is added — Luke 10.27.

*{with all thy heart}* With all the faculties of your soul.

*{with all thy mind}* This is here added, so Deut. 6.5. and *with all thy strength* or *might*, left out here, is added, Mark 12.30. Luke 10.27.

**V. 38. ***{The first}* In order: for God must be loved before; and more than all men.

*{great commandment}* It was none of the ten commandments in particular, but contains all the commandments of the first table; and therefore is counted the greatest, for the whole is greater than any part thereof.

**V. 37. ***{is like unto it}* Not equal to it: for duty to God is above duty to man: but like it in greatness, because it contains all the duties of the second table, as the other did of the first.

*{Thou shalt love}* Levit. 19.18.

*{thy neighbour}* Another man. Luke 10.27, 29, 30. 1 John 3.23. Rom. 13.3. *nec enim Dominus vere sine proximo, nec proximus vere diligitur sine Deo*, *Greg. hom. 30.* For neither is God truly loved without our neighbor, nor our neighbor truly without God. See 1 John 4.20.

*{as thyself}* As truly, as yourself: sincerely, though not without some inequality of degrees: as, John 17.21. Acts 3.22.

**V. 40. ***{all the law and the prophets}* This is the contents and sum of them all, and by either law or prophets, proposed as the duty of man; whatever they command, caution, admonish, teach, or ordain, is hereto reducible, and all our duties to God and man, fall, like several lines into this one center, love, which is the fulfilling of the law.

**V. 41. ***{While the Pharisees}* Mark 12.35. Luke 10.41. The Pharisees who arrogated to themselves, the only knowledge of the law and prophets: but were extremely blind concerning the mystery of salvation by Christ: in the knowledge of whom only, is eternal life; John 17.3. 58Christ therefore very fitly questions them herein.

**V. 42. ***{What think ye}* You who boast of the key of knowledge, and interpretation of Scriptures: what do you believe concerning Christ?

*{of Christ}* Christ proves, that though he be truly David’s son, according to the flesh, yet that he is also David’s Lord, as being God eternal: which the Pharisees not understanding, were not able to answer his question, and not believing that, they could have no profit of the Messiah now exhibited, whom the patriarchs, prophets, all the faithful before them, so earnestly longed for, they taking Christ for mere man, and not the Savior of his Israel, the elect.

*{whose son is he?}* Of whose stock, or family? For the Hebrews called a man’s posterity, his sons.

*{of David}* See on chap. 1.1. They knew so much, that according to the promise of God, Christ was to come of the lineage of David: so much they commonly read and interpreted to the people: but they neither knew, nor expected in Christ, any more than the person of a man: therefore, thus much and no more could they answer Christ: they thought he should be *ἄνθρωπος* *ψιλὸς*, mere man, says Theophylact.

**V. 43. ***{How then doth David in spirit}* *Non errore incerto*, says Jerome: but by inspiration of God’s Spirit, he reproves them, not for confessing him to be the son of David; but whereas they taught the vulgar, to expect only a secular external kingdom under the Messiah: he hereby gives them occasion in the presence of the multitude, to consider the glory and divine majesty of his kingdom: as he is the Son of God, and David’s Lord. He says, *how doth David in spirit*; Mark adds *the Holy Spirit*: thus David spoke, Psalm 110.1. See 1 Pet. 2.19.

**V. 44. ***{The Lord said unto my Lord}* David professed that God the Father said so unto Christ, David’s Lord.

**V. 45. ***{call him Lord}* See on verse 42.

**V. 46. ***{Neither durst any man}* None of his adversaries durst ask him anything, after this, in the way of tempting him, for they saw plainly that they could not prevail against him; being ever convicted by evident truth, and divine wisdom: therefore they came with violence, to destroy him by power: the venom of envy may sometimes be easily overcome, but hardly ever rest.

## CHAPTER 23

**Verse 1. ***{To the multitude}* Gr. *Multitudes*: to many gathered together.

*{and to his disciples}* When he saw there was no more hope to do good on the Pharisees, he addresses himself to the people, and his disciples, to undeceive them, and forearm them against their corruptions.

**V. 2. ***{sit in Moses’ chair}* As masters and teachers, verse 7, 8, 10. and expounders of Moses’ law: for *καθέδρα*, imports the office of teaching the word of God, law and Gospel: belonging to the priests under the law, and the ministers under the Gospel: because while they taught, they were wont to sit: Suidas interprets *καθέδρα* by *ἐπίσχεσις*, a sitting or tarrying.

**V. 3. ***{all therefore}* Because God appointed teachers of the law, therefore Christ would have them to be heard, though their persons were wicked: while they who have a calling to preach, deliver the truth, they must be heard, though not imitated, where they practice not. See Mal. 2.7. Thus he speaks (says *Chrysostom. hom. 73.*) lest he should leave the hearers any occasion of disobedience to their greater condemnation: or contempt of the ministry, whom he should after send: in the ministry, the ordinance of God is to be considered; *sive Paulus, sive Judas baptizet, Christus peccatum lavat, absolvit, & [delet].* Whether Paul or Judas baptize, Christ washes away, forgives, and puts away sin. *de bapt. Christi* (*inter opera. Cyprian.*).

*{that observe and do}* If they teach according to Moses’ law, as they profess to do; and go not out of the chair by their traditions: for in such a case our Savior forbids men to obey them. Matt. 16.6, 12. True sanctity is in doing good, not in saying only.

*{But do not ye after their works}* He calls them their works, which he here condemns in all this speech following; as hypocrisy, ambition, avarice, pride, &c. for these things are no fruits of Moses’ chair, but of Satan’s; which shows how careful ministers ought to be, lest they cast down in works, what they would build in words: but withal, that auditors may not compose themselves to the examples of frail men, but to the truth of God’s holy word.

*{for they say and do not}* They speak well, but live ill: they are the sons (chap. 21.30.) who said he would go, and work in the vineyard, but did not: *qui autem docent tantum, nec faciunt, ipsi praeceptis suis detrahunt pondus*; says *Lactantius l. 3. c. 16.* They who only teach and do not (as they teach) themselves, take away the weight from their own precepts; for who will obey, when teachers teach men not to obey: teach by example you who require obedience of others: be you not like those stones set on the partings of highways, which stand only, (by their inscriptions) to show others which way to take, not to move themselves; it is a shame for teachers, not to do what they bid others do; but a greater shame to do that which they forbid others; *thou that teachest another, teachest thou not thyself?* Rom. 2.2. By you the name of God is blasphemed among the enemies; see 1 Cor. 9.27. Of all the world which perished by the deluge, least to be pitied were they, who built an ark for believing Noah, not themselves.

**V. 4. ***{For they bind heavy burdens}* Luke 11.46. They press others to the strict observance of God’s law; see Acts 15.10. This verse interprets the foregoing; *they say*, they bind heavy burdens to other men’s shoulders; that is, they press their consciences, telling them rigidly enough, what they must do, or be damned; but do not themselves; they will not move a finger to that duty themselves: it is a metaphor taken from some cruel master, laying intolerable burdens on his beasts and servants (under which they groan) but is moved with no compassion to ease or help them, not so much as with a finger.

*{with one of their fingers}* They will not touch them, much less lay them on their own shoulders; thus hypocrites, are commonly severe exactors of those things, which themselves chiefly neglect; as it was objected against the Athenians, they knew what was right, but would not do it; everyone can speak great things, but few do as they say; example is the most powerful rhetoric, for as *Gr.
