Nazianzen* says, *Theodor. epist. 81.* *οὐ* *γὰρ* *οὕτως* *ὁ* *λόγος* *πείθει* *τοὺς* *πολλοὺς*, *ὡς* *ἡ* *πρᾶξις* *σιωπῶσα* *παραίνεσις*, for truly, words do not so much persuade many men, as action, which is a kind of silent exhortation. *Non eloquimur magna, sed vivimus*, said Minucius Felix, after Cyprian *serm. 3. de bon. pat.* who after the like manner says, *qui virtutum conscientiam magis quam jactantiam novimus, &c.* We, who are rather acquainted with the conscience, than the ostentation of virtues, who do not speak great things, but live, as the servants and worshippers of God do.

**V. 5. ***{to be seen of men}* They do works indeed sometimes which seem morally good, but out of vainglory, and so wanting the right end, which is the glory of God, they are but (as Moses said Deut. 32.32.) *the vine of Sodom, and of the fields of Gomorrah, their grapes are grapes of gall, &c.* The fruits growing near the Dead Sea, where those cities stood, are fair without, but only dust and rottenness within.

*{They make broad}* Broader than other men’s. So ambitious are hypocrites in all semblances of good. (See Numb. 15.38. Deut. 12.12.) Specially in things external, for they are ambitious of honor with men, they have not learned of David, Psalm 16.8. *To set the Lord always before them*; but of Saul, 1 Sam. 15.30. *honor me now I pray thee, before the elders of my people.*

*{their phylacteries}* *φυλακτήρια*, *conservatories*. Chrysostom’s interpreter calls them, *quaedam custodientia*, means to keep in memory, to wit, the precepts of the law thereon written: for these were ribands of blue silk; or as some think of parchment, which they wore upon their garments and foreheads; not in obedience to God so much, as ostentation before men, of their singular zeal, and love to the law of God, as if they were very careful observers thereof. See Deut. 6.8.

*{and enlarge the borders of their garments}* Hypocrisy easily learns to abuse any ordinance of God; so here, God commanded that they should make them, *ציצת* *tsitsith*. Numb. 15.39. Seventy *κράσπεδα*, *fringes in the borders of their garments*, that they might look upon it, *and remember all the commandments of the Lord, and do them* — they enlarged them for show to men; as if all sanctity had consisted therein, that others might take the more notice of them, and think them to be the only religious men.

**V. 6. ***{and love the uppermost rooms}* Mark 12.38, 39. Luke 11.43. *πρωτοκλισίαν* the first place to lie down in feasts; for they lay on pallets to eat, and their tables were beds; they might have taken the uppermost rooms, when due, and offered unto them; but they loved them, and ambitiously contended for them *πρωτοκαθεδρίας* *the uppermost seats in the synagogues* likewise.

**V. 7. ***{and greetings in the markets}* To be first saluted, as if the chief honor and respect were due to them before all others; and that in public.

*{Rabbi, Rabbi}* *Ῥαββὶ*, *רבב*, *rabab*, signifies great, or excellent, &c. whence comes *רב*, a master, or one excellent in any art, and *רבי* *rabbi*, was taken for a title of dignity and eminency, importing those, who by imposition of hands were declared to be wise men: it signifies here, one preferred before other teachers, because of his abundant knowledge, which makes him to be instead of many: their fault was, that they ambitiously affected that title above others, and took it to themselves without giving due honor unto God their teacher: they loved to hear the title doubled, or often used.

**V. 8. ***{be ye not called so}* James 3.1. They could not bar men from calling them so, but they should not take pride and pleasure59 in it: he addresses this to his disciples, whom he meant to furnish with an extraordinary measure of knowledge and largeness of heart, that they might be the teachers of the world; lest thereby they should be vainly puffed up, and fall into Satan’s snares: for ambition begets love of eminency, and authority over others; and affectation of prelacy, ushered in Antichrist. Chrysostom notes here, *hom. 73.* the great mischiefs which came into the Church by the ambition of prelates: for which, he said, he could not abstain from tears, when he heard, or thought of the first places, &c. It is true, they that labor in the word and doctrine, are *worthy of double honor*, 1 Tim. 5.17. and much to blame are those who deny it them: the contempt of the ministry is a wide door open to heresy. Will the impious and ingrateful world, or no, they are the *ambassadors for Christ*, 2 Cor. 5.20. and stewards of the mysteries of God, 1 Cor. 4.1. But the dignity of the ministry is one thing, and the ambition of ministers another.

*{one is your master}* Your principal teacher: he alludes to Isaiah 54.13. Jer. 31.34. Arrogate not the glory of God to yourselves; for what have you excellent in you, which you have not received, and learned of Christ?

*{All ye are brethren}* All you apostles are not masters one of another, but all fellow disciples of Christ your Master. The main scope of this place is, to hold the disciples within the limits of humility and knowledge of their own imperfection; lest they should affect primacy and power to lord it over their brethren, 1 Pet. 5.1. (which he well knew would miserably afflict the Church) which they do who are ambitious of high titles.

**V. 9. ***{Call no man your father}* He speaks not this in a simple and literal sense: children may call their natural parent father, and the people may without offense, use this name in reverence to their spiritual fathers: so Isaac said to Abraham, Gen. 22.7. *אבי* *my father*. So Jacob to Isaac, Gen. 27.18. &c. 1 Sam. 20.2. 2 Kings 2.12. 2 Kings 13.14. But he taxes here the flattery of those who were the Pharisees’ sycophants, giving them too high titles; and the pride of those who ambitiously took the same, which is a greater title than Master, and so he attributes it to his heavenly Father, and the other to himself in the next verse. See more on verse 7, 8.

*{For one is}* Mal. 1.6. The universal parent, by right of creation, redemption, adoption in Christ, and preservation. God, *the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ; of whom the whole family in heaven and earth is named*, Eph. 3.15.

*{your Father which is in heaven}* See on chap. 6.9.

**V. 10. ***{Neither be ye called masters}* Governors: for teaching masters were mentioned, verse 8. guides of the way, verse 16. chap. 15.14. Rom. 2.19, 20. The scribes, although they were but blind guides, yet did they ambitiously affect such titles; but the apostles must not.

**V. 11. ***{He that is greatest}* Here it appears that different places and titles, are not prohibited; but ambitious seeking of them, and proud glorying in them.

*{shall be your servant}* That is, let him be so: as, chap. 20.27.

**V. 12. ***{Whosoever shall exalt himself}* Luke 14.11. and 18.14. This is contrary to the course of the world; for men esteem more of such, as set forth themselves, though with contempt of others: but God hates and resists the proud, and gives grace to the humble; the sweet watercourses find out the low valleys, which have the blessing of fruitfulness, while the high mountains are barren: the seeming excellency of the proud, like rising smoke, vanishes at its greatest height.

**V. 13. ***{But woe unto you scribes and Pharisees}* Luke 11.52. This word is used, either to denounce the judgment of God, imminent punishment, temporal or eternal: or it expresses the sympathy of those who deplore and pity. This was the last time he would warn the scribes and Pharisees of their condition; and therefore having admonished his disciples, and the people to beware of them, he now lays open to their consciences, their principal sins, and the miseries to which they were liable for the same.

*{hypocrites}* Christ using this term in his sharpest reproofs, shows us that nothing is more detestable than hypocrisy; a hypocrite is he who counterfeits, or acts another man’s person. See *Eustath.* *ὑποκρίνομαι*.

*{Ye shut up the kingdom of heaven}* You are so far from opening the door, that when it is opened by John Baptist, and me, you will neither enter into it, nor permit others to enter, who show some willingness thereto, John 7.48, 49. by threatenings, John 9.22. or you challenge the key of interpretation to yourselves, and would not suffer any truly to interpret those Scriptures that treat of the Messiah, and his kingdom; such fruit bears your abused authority in the Church.

*{against men}* *ἔμπροσθεν* *τῶν* *ἀνθρώπων*, *in the sight of men*, or *before the face of men*, when they are at the very door, and ready to enter: for many came to Christ’s teaching, ready to embrace the Gospel of the kingdom, whom the Pharisees disheartened, by their blaspheming of Christ, traducing his doctrine; or by threatenings, and decrees of excommunication, endeavoring so to do.

**V. 14. ***{Ye devour widows’ houses}* Mark 12.40. Luke 20.47. You oppress and injure widows, who have none to help them, till you leave them not so much as a house to live in, Hos. 4.8. It is said, they *eat up the sins of the people*: that is, the sacrifices offered for the expiation of the people’s sins; the meaning here is, that you persuade foolish widows to give you entertainment, or to bequeath their estates to your society, pretending that you will gain them salvation, by your continual prayers for them. See 2 Tim. 3.5, 6. He names widows here, to aggravate their impiety; but we may conceive, that they also abused many others in like manner.

*{for a pretense make long prayers}* *Vitia non decipiunt nisi sub specie umbraque virtutum.* *Jerom. Laeta. l. 2. ep. 17.* You make long prayers, that widows thinking you to be zealous and religious men, may trust you with their estates, which you defraud them of: you would be taken for godly men, because you make long prayers; so wrong you God and man.

*{therefore ye shall receive, the greater damnation}* Greater than other oppressors or impostors: and that first, because you are so cruel, as to beguile and impoverish helpless widows; God taking on him the protectorship of them and orphans. See Exod. 22.22. Secondly, because as all sin is out of measure sinful, (as being committed against an infinite justice) yet most damnable is that sin, *quod titulus bonitatis accusat*, *Salvian de Gub. D. l. 4.* which a title of goodness accuses: it more aggravates our cause before God, *si deteriores sumus, & meliores esse dicamur*, says the same, if so be we are evil, and are reported to be good; how much more, when we take up, and abuse the holy name, and worship of God, for a cloak of cruelty and wickedness? This is a high injury to the holy majesty of a just and all-seeing God: therefore (as *Chrysostom hom. 74. in Matth.* well says) whereas all that sin, are worthy of punishment, most grievously shall he be punished, who goes to wickedness, using such a pretense.

**V. 15. ***{compass sea and land}* You travel sea and land; you take great pains to convert Gentiles to your religion: as if you were very zealous for God’s glory.

*{to make one proselyte}* *προσήλυτον*, a stranger (Hebrew *גר* *gher*, of *גור*, which among other significations imports, *peregrinatus fuit, congregavit*) coming over from heathenish superstition, to Judaism.

*{a child of hell}* Much worse, and so more damnable. This word *υἱὸς* son, or sons, joined with a substantive of denominating sense, with the Hebrews, was very emphatical; as in these several instances may appear. *Children* (or sons) *of wrath*, Eph. 2.3. that is, *irae Dei devoti, & obnoxii*: *בן* *המות*, *ben hamaveth*, a son, or child of death: *qui capitalis criminis reus est*. So *son of perdition*, John 17.12. a hopeless, desperate castaway. So here.

**V. 16. ***{It is nothing}* It is a matter of nothing: he is not guilty of perjury who thus affirms an untruth: this is no oath, or he is not bound to perform his vow, by the temple.

*{he is a debtor}* He is bound to perform his oath or vow, who swears by the gold of the temple; or he is guilty (if he perform not: so the Syriac *חאב* *reus fuit*, or *debuit*; *peccavit*: *debitor reus*. *Mar. Trost. 234.* So Rom. 13.8. So they expressed a debtor, or guilty man, by one and the same word; whence it seems that phrase is used in the Lord’s prayer, chap. 6.12. *forgive us* *τὰ* *ὀφειλήματα* *ἡμῶν* our debts: that is, the sins whereof we are guilty: this seems to be out of the Syriac manner of speaking, which was the language of our Savior.

**V. 17. ***{that sanctifieth the gold}* That causes the gold to be counted holy, because it is sacred, as set apart from common use, unto the service of God, for the use of the temple.

**V. 18. ***{swear by the altar}* This Christ speaks not to allow any swearing by creatures though of the most holy use; but to tax the foolish vanity of their traditions.

**V. 19. ***{the gift or the altar}* The gift was not holy, before it was brought to the altar to be offered to God: but the altar was holy before, as being long before your gift consecrated to the service of God, in that use which he appointed it, and so denominates the gift thereon offered, holy or sacred to the Lord.

**V. 20. ***{and by all things therein}* He is as much bound to perform his vow, as if he did swear by all those things because they all pertained to it: he does not allow any swearing by creatures, though he condemn perjury, when men swear by them. See on chap. 5.33, 34.

**V. 21. ***{and by him}* That is, in God’s account he is tied to perform, as if he had sworn by God himself.

*{that dwelleth therein}* Not locally or circumscriptively, for he is infinite and comprehends all things. See Acts 17.24. Jer. 23.24. But because he chose and appointed that place for his public worship.

**V. 22. ***{by the throne of God}* See Acts 7.49. Heaven is called the throne of God; because as the greatest majesty of kings is seen when they sit on their thrones: so God manifests his glory most in heaven, and to the heavenly creatures.

**V. 23. ***{for ye pay}* Luke 11.42. That you may seem exact observers of the law: this was commanded by the law, Levit. 27. 6030. Numb. 18.23. See hereof Dr. Godwin, *Moses and Aaron*, lib. 6. cap. 3.

*{omitted the weightier matters}* Hypocrites are careful in small matters, but negligent in the greatest: *βαρύτερα*, is a comparative relating to tithing mints, &c. which they did by tradition, the law commanding tithes also of more important kinds: for it was as well justice to give the priests their tithes, as to render to any other that which was their own. See on Luke 11.42.

*{judgment, mercy, and faith}* Just dealing with all men, mercy in relieving the poor, and faithfulness in covenants, promises and contracts.

*{These ought ye to have done}* He blames not those who do the smallest things commanded; but such as think when they have done these things, they have done all that is required, and so securely neglect the greatest duties: *these, &c.* that is, *justice, mercy, and truth*; and not to have omitted your paying tithes: here is taxed the preposterous traditions of Pharisees. See on Luke 11.42. and Dr. Godwin, *Moses and Aaron* l. 6. c. 3. *Ecclesiam fraudare sacrilegium est*, *Jerom. Nepotian. ep. 14. l. 2.* It is sacrilege to defraud the Church.

**V. 24. ***{Ye blind guides}* It is a wretched blindness, not to see the absurdity and impiety of such courses; the worse because they were to lead others in religious practices. See Isaiah 42.19. Wickedness is ever pernicious: but then worse, when the wicked thinks he needs no amendment; but worst of all is it, when he thinks himself a sufficient guide, and reformer of others, who is himself incorrigible; *Chrys. in loc.* This they owed to their malice: the God of love is light without darkness, 1 John 1.5. but *Athanasius epist. ad solitar. vit. agent.* malice is truly blind.

*{Strain at a gnat}* It is a proverbial speech, the sense is: you make a scruple of small things, but nothing care for the main.

**V. 25. ***{For ye make clean the outside of, &c.}* Luke 11.39. Hypocrites make much ado about ceremonies, their own traditions, or things external and indifferent, but for the inward and most necessary parts of God’s worship, they are very careless. See Mark 7.4.

*{full of extortion}* Getting of other men’s goods away from them by oppression, ravage and violence, verse 14. Ezek. 18.16.

*{and excess}* In the intemperate use of the creatures: how admirable a folly was this in the Pharisees, to be over nice in having clean houses, and utensils, with souls so foul? Without controversy, external cleanness and decent compture is commendable, and necessary, but the soul’s cleanness is of concernment incomparably greater; for the purity thereof shall make us eternally blessed, chap. 5.

**V. 26. ***{Cleanse first that which is within}* He recalls them from hypocrisy, to a sincere and inward purity in which they shall have comfort, Tit. 1.15. This consists not in external ceremonies, &c.

**V. 27. ***{whited sepulchres}* See Acts 23.3. Like those sumptuous tombs which cover stenchy and corrupted flesh and bones of dead carcasses. See verse 6, 7, 14, 15, 23. So were the Pharisees only externally and to the eyes of other men, religious; but inwardly vicious and wicked; and so more abominably impious, than they who are apparently so. *Nescio enim quomodo turpiora sunt vitia, quae virtutum specie celantur*; says *Jerom. ep. 22. l. 2. Celantiae*, for I know not how, more filthy are those vices which are covered with show of virtues: visors of religion cannot deceive Christ our Judge: labor for cleanness of heart, which David prayed for, Psalm 51.10. If we have true purity there, all things shall be pure to us; it is only sin which defiles a man, whose root or fountain is within in the heart; if that be cleansed, then (as it was said of the unwholesome waters of Jericho healed, 2 Kings 2.21.) death and barrenness shall be no more in the words and actions.

**V. 28. ***{Even so ye also, &c.}* Thus our Savior applying his similitude, shows himself the searcher of hearts, and detects their secret hypocrisy, which men see not: as you see the fair outsides of a sumptuous tomb, you cannot see the rotten bones therewith covered: so you see the outward appearance only of the hypocrite, you see not the secret wickedness of his heart: but *the Lord seeth not as man seeth: for man looketh on the outward appearance, but the Lord looketh on the heart.* 1 Sam. 16.7.

**V. 29. ***{Ye build the tombs of the prophets}* You are like your fathers, though you disavow their cruelty against the former prophets: you are injurious in persecuting the living saints, and would seem religious in honoring the dead: you build and adorn the tombs and monuments of the prophets, and other just men, whom your fathers slew for preaching the truth, and reproving their sins: and you persecute Christ and his disciples for the same cause, and think you are yet religious. See on Luke 11.47.

**V. 30. ***{and say}* You pretend you would not have done so: thus, as if your fathers’ vices were your virtues, you can remember and censure them, but not your own sins.

**V. 31. ***{Ye be witnesses}* Your own words and actions testify.

*{unto yourselves}* Or, *against yourselves*; as, Mark 6.12. Luke 9.5.

*{That ye are the children of them which killed, &c.}* You imitate them in their impiety; though as yet you cannot compass your design; for even now you are plotting what they practiced: you seem to honor the silent dead, because they reprove you not; but the surviving teachers of the same doctrine, you hate to the death, as your fathers did the former prophets.

**V. 32. ***{fill ye up}* Since you will needs do so; that your judgment may come upon you. See Gen. 15.16. God permits not the malice or impiety of any person or nation, to increase above a certain measure by him limited and bounded, so that they shall no more exceed the same, than the raging sea her full; to which when her proud waves come, they shall soon find their ebb: so when iniquity is come to a certain height, then comes divine revenge to make the consummation, Jer. 30.11. Sin can have but a definite measure: seeing therefore you will not be persuaded to repent and leave your sins; take your course, go on.

*{The measure of your fathers}* They stored up to themselves great wrath, by their killing the prophets, but yet the measure is not full; there remains something which you will do to exceed their wickedness, and beyond which there can be nothing more impious: they slew the servants, but you will slay the Son of God; the Lord of all the prophets: *nihil est ultra Dei injuriam, gravius peccare quod possint.* *Ambros. in Luc. l. 7. c. 11.* There is nothing beyond the injuring of God himself, that men can, more grievously sin.

**V. 33. ***{ye serpents}* This he says to intimate their kindred in wickedness (as Chrysostom speaks) like their parents, *in veneno malitiae* — (as Euthymius expresses it) in the venom of malice.

*{generation of vipers}* See on chap. 3.7.

*{How can ye escape}* Seeing your malice is innate, as vipers’ poison, and your thirst of the saints’ blood hereditary; what hope can you conceive of escape, more than the vain hope of beguiled hypocrites? Though you may escape man’s judgment, you cannot God’s: or, whosoever escapes hell, certainly you cannot, who sin out of so destinate a malice.

*{of hell}* See on chap. 5.22. Eternal damnation of body and soul, in the most intense torments of hellfire: there is no way to escape that most wretched second death, but the living way Christ Jesus, whom you hate, and seek by all means to put from you, or destroy: the invincible hardness of your hearts will not suffer you to believe and repent, that you may obtain mercy: now however your fair semblances of, and pretenses to sanctity before men acquit you from their censures and judgment, yet no disguises can deceive God, who sees the very secret thoughts of all men, and whose judgment is inevitable, such as none shall escape, 2 Cor. 5.10.

**V. 34. ***{Wherefore}* Seeing you are so cruel, you shall have matter to exercise your cruelty: God’s work must be done, man’s impiety cannot overcome God’s mercy; nor the abusers thereof escape his justice.

*{I send unto you}* After my resurrection and ascension into heaven. Here appears the unspeakable goodness of God, and mercy of Christ, still sending his servants to invite the obstinate Jews to faith and repentance, that they might live: yea after they had put him to death with all the spiteful aggravations thereof, which the power of man could act, or Satan’s malice invent; notwithstanding all this, he that said of old to the rebellious Israel, *yet return unto me*, Jer. 3.1. after all these injuries foreseen, says — *behold, I send unto you prophets* — that is, as I have formerly sent you prophets, so will I send you wise men, *scribes*, that is, learned men, though I know beforehand, you will likewise kill, and persecute them.

*{Prophets and wise men, and scribes}* He calls the apostles and pastors under the Gospel, by that name of prophet, which was best known to the Jews; and mentioned verse 30. that they that honored the dead prophets, and their wise men and scribes; might know what account they ought also to have made of the apostles, that were more excellent than they. See on chap. 11.11.

*{ye shall kill}* You shall be permitted to exercise the like cruelty on them, which the prophets have suffered.

*{in your synagogues}* See chap. 10.17.

*{from city to city}* Chap. 10.23.

**V. 35. ***{All the righteous blood}* All the blood of righteous men: so terrible shall your judgment be, that you may seem to answer for all the murders of the godly from the beginning of the world, unto that time.

*{of the righteous Abel}* Gen. 4.8. Who was a righteous and just man, accepted of God.

*{of Zacharias}* 2 Chron. 24.22. These are named, because the blood of the one, was said to cry for revenge. Gen. 4.10. (as indeed all blood unjustly shed, does) as did the mouth of the other at his death: or because the one was the first righteous man, and the other Zechary son of Jehoiada the priest stoned, 2 Chron. 24.21. a prophet of the same name, or one of the last prophets, whose name is upon record in the Old Testament. See note on Luke 11.51.

*{whom ye slew}* Because the nation is still accounted the same: so Mark 10.3. John 6.32. and 7.22. Acts 7.38.

**V. 36. ***{All these things}* All these punishments: the just revenge on an impious nation, for the blood of the servants of God, shed for the testimony of his truth: however vengeance on them was slow, yet it was sure, to their utter extirpation and dispersion of their remnant unto this day.

*{upon this generation}* For Jerusalem was soon after destroyed.

**V. 37. ***{O Jerusalem, Jerusalem}* Luke 13.34. The name is doubled, to express great affection in the speaker: as 2 Sam. 18.33.

*{that killest the prophets}* Nehem. 9.26. Acts 7.32.

*{and stonest them which were sent}* 2 Chron. 24.21.

*{How often would I have gathered}* He speaks of his human and ministerial will: or, *de voluntate signi*: God often offered them mercy, protection, and sundry favors, which they willfully put by through their sins, from which they would not be reclaimed. See on Luke 13.34.

*{thy children}* The Jews, whose mother city was Jerusalem, to which they repaired for solemn and public worship, judicature, and succor, in war, or public calamities, Luke 10.14.

*{together}* A long time striving with them, to bring them to faith and repentance. See Psalm 17.8. & 91.4.

*{ye would not}* So Prov. 1.24. Isaiah 65.12. & 30.20. Jer. 7.13. Ezek. 24.13. *nos calamitatum nostrarum auctores sumus — nos, inquam, nos, etiam Deo nolente, cruciamus.* *Salvian. l. 8.* We are the authors of our own woes — we, I say, we ourselves, (against that which God would in mercy) torment ourselves — *nihil est in nos, crudelius nobis*: there is nothing more cruel against us, than ourselves; we will not be healed. See Ezek. 18.31.

**V. 38. ***{Your house is left unto you desolate}* Your city and temple shall be destroyed: God often and long offers mercy, but at last (as Cardan speaks out of *Valer. Max.*) *tarditatem supplicii — gravitate compensat*; he recompenses the slowness or delay of the punishment, with the grievousness thereof. See on Luke 13.35.

**V. 39. ***{Ye shall not see me}* In this bodily manner.

*{henceforth}* For this was in his last journey to Jerusalem; and the last time of his public teaching. See on Luke 13.35.

*{till ye shall say}* You would be glad to have me present, when Jerusalem shall be distressed; then would yourselves be glad to cry, save Lord, which you thought much to hear the children cry, chap. 21.16. Or after my resurrection, you shall see me no more, till good men among you rejoice at my coming to judgment, with such acclamations as Jerusalem heard, chap. 21.9.

## CHAPTER 24

**Verse 1. ***{Jesus went out}* Mark 13.1. Luke 21.5. To the mount of Olives.

*{departed from the temple}* Purposing to come there no more; having taught there, divers days together.

*{to shew him the building}* Chap. 22.38. He had foretold the destruction of the temple, they show him the building, as they went toward the mount of Olives, from whence was the temple’s fairest prospect: pitying that such a magnificent pile should be destroyed: as if they said, behold, what a goodly house you will lay waste. *Theophylact.*

**V. 2. ***{There shall not be left here}* Luke 19.44.

*{one stone upon another}* It shall be utterly destroyed, and demolished: it signifies an extreme and utter devastation, which came to pass by Titus and the Roman army, wasting all with fire and sword, and evening with the ground, that magnificent temple and city, which was the glory of the world. (See *Joseph. l. 7. c. 9, 10, 18. de bel. Jud.*) Though Titus by a strict edict at first storming the city, forbad the defacing of the temple; yet the soldiers burnt it and the city; this siege began at the Passover, and the temple was burnt (as some say) August 10. when it had stood 589 years: the city was burnt, Septemb. 8. in the year of our Lord, 71. *Abr. Bucholcer. ind. chron.* Such is the truth of Christ no power or counsel of men can defeat it: so miserable was the blindness of the Jews, who seeing these things come to pass, according to the prediction of Christ, could not then, cannot yet, repent and believe in him. See Jer. 7.4. So just is God in rejecting them: so long as the malady seems to promise any hope of recovery, the physician uses gentle means of recovery, but when he despairs of the cure, then as Athanasius says, *de prodit. Jud.* *ἀποτέμνεται* *μέλος* the disaffected part is cut off: so dealt God with them.

**V. 3. ***{upon the mount of Olives}* Mark adds, *over against the temple*, which words show the occasion of this.

*{The disciples came unto him}* That is, Peter, James, John, and Andrew, Mark 13.3.

*{privately}* Because it was a dangerous question to be asked in public, concerning the destruction of the temple, as may appear, chap. 26.61. Acts 6.14.

*{tell us}* Here are three distinct questions propounded; the first concerning the destruction of Jerusalem and the temple: the second, about Christ’s coming, not to judgment, but as they dreamed, to his temporal kingdom: the third, concerning the end of the world: the answer to the first, (as some conceive) reaches to verse 23. to the second, from thence to verse 36. to the third, from that verse to the end of the chapter: but indeed, to the first and third, he answers somewhat promiscuously, so that some things must be understood of the destruction of Jerusalem, and some things concerning the end of the world.

**V. 4. ***{That no man deceive you}* Satan will be busy to seduce you by many and variable impostors, which he will send into the Church to corrupt the doctrine, which I have delivered you, but be you vigilant, that you be not deceived.

**V. 5. ***{in my name}* Some pretending that they are authorized by Christ: as, Acts 19.13. Some that they had the same authority with Christ; or that they were Christ the Messiah: as here verse 23. John 5.43. Some promising them temporal deliverance, which they expected, Luke 24.21. See Acts 5.36, 37. and 21.38.

**V. 6. ***{of wars}* Slaughters, and bloody tumults, as among them under Caius: so also among other nations.

*{rumors of wars}* Reports, and expectation of other like troubles.

*{Be not troubled}* Be not affrighted at them; be not dejected, but consider God’s power and protection over you.

*{the end is not yet}* Understand this of the Jews’ final destruction, or of the end of the world.

**V. 7. ***{Nation shall rise against nation}* As, 2 Chron. 15.6. Such troubles fell out under Claudius and Nero, as also many since, in divers parts of the world.

*{There shall be famine}* Acts 11.28. *λιμοὶ* *καὶ* *λοιμοί*. Sword, famine, pestilence, deaths; three weapons; like Joab’s three darts, 2 Sam. 18.14.

*{pestilences}* Which usually follow wars, and famine, Revel. 6.5, 6, 8.

**V. 8. ***{Of sorrows}* They shall go before the destruction of Jerusalem, as the pains of a woman before her travail. See Psalm 48.6. Isaiah 13.8. Jer. 30.6, 7.

**V. 9. ***{Then shall they deliver}* Chap. 10.17. Luke 21.12. John 16.1. Christ here passes from his discourse of common calamities, to the peculiar crosses and sufferings of his: the Jews shall apprehend you, and convent you in their consistories: as Peter and John, Acts 4. and 5. chapters.

*{all nations}* Not the Jews only. But you shall be hated also of all other nations into which you shall go to preach the Gospel; where Satan’s malicious influence will ever be ready to instigate persecutors of the truth. All nations through which the devil travels, 1 Pet. 5.8. Revel. 2.10. who, ever works in the children of disobedience, Eph. 2.2.

*{for my name’s sake}* For the profession of Christ. See chap. 5.11.

**V. 10. ***{Many be offended}* Scandalized at the present calamities, befalling God’s people, shall fall from the Christian profession, chap. 13.21. See 2 Tim. 1.15.

*{shall betray one another}* The apostates shall betray those who will not with them deny the faith, and run to destruction of body and soul for company.

**V. 11. ***{False prophets}* Acts 20.30. 2 Cor. 11.13. 2 Tim. 2.18. Such as shall feign themselves to be the most holy teachers of truth: many such shall rise intruding themselves into the work of the ministry, unto which they are not called of God: neither shall they be easily discovered, but so subtly act, as closely to carry on their designs under fair pretenses of singular zeal for the cause of Christ; in so much as that they shall deceive many good and knowing men.

**V. 12. ***{Iniquity shall abound}* Because of their cruelty toward Christians, love to God and man, shall grow cold, not showing itself as before in offices of piety and charity. See 2 Tim. 4.16. Heb. 10.25. Malice of neighbors, and decay of charity, are certain symptoms of imminent judgments of God over a nation.

**V. 13. ***{But he that shall endure}* Rev. 2.10. Not a good beginning only, but perseverance shall crown our hopes.

*{shall be saved}* Either freed from the common temporal calamities, or however he shall obtain eternal life, and salvation in Christ Jesus; *virtus boni operis perseverantia est*, *Greg. hom. in Evang. 25.* The virtue, or excellency of a good work, is perseverance, the end makes happy: Paul began ill, Acts 8.1. & 9.1. but ended happily (see Rom. 8.38. Phil. 3.6, 7, 8. &c. 2 Tim. 4.6, 7, 8.) *Judae laudantur exordia, sed finis proditione damnatur.* *Jerom. ep. 21. l. 2. Furia* (see chap. 10.4. & 26.47, 49. & 27.4, 5. Acts 1.18.) Judas’ beginning is commended, but his end is condemned of treason: *let him that thinketh he standeth, take heed lest he fall*, 1 Cor. 10.12. See Ezek. 18.24.

*{gospel of the kingdom}* Joyful tidings concerning the kingdom of heaven.

**V. 14. ***{In all the world}* To all the then known parts of the world the Gospel was preached before the destruction of Jerusalem. See Mark 13.9, 10, 11. Rom. 1.8. Col. 1.23.

*{for a witness unto all nations}* Of the mercy of God offered them in Christ, and of the obstinacy of the Jews, that they might understand that God had justly rejected them, who were incorrigible.

*{Then shall the end come}* Then the utter ruin of them, and the ruin of Jerusalem, their temple, and service there, shall come, as verse 8. See Ezek. 7.2.

**V. 15. ***{When therefore ye shall see}* Mark 13.14.

*{the abomination of desolation}* These words are taken from Dan. 12.11. where we read, that from the time that the daily sacrifice should be taken away, *to give*, or *set up* *שקוץ* *שמם* *shikkuts shomem*, seventy as here, *τὸ* *βδέλυγμα* *ἐρημώσεως* the destructive abomination; or as the Hebrew is given by some, *abominationem stupendam*, or *obstupefacientem*; thus the prophet called the profanation of the temple, whether we understand it, with Theophylact, of Caesar’s image there set up; or of the setting up of the eagle (the sign in the Roman standard) in the thirteenth year of Nero, in the month of May, in Judea, according to the word of the Lord by Moses, Deut. 28.49. *The Lord shall bring a nation against thee from far*; as, *ידאה* *הנשר* *iide hannasher*, *the eagle flyeth*; *in similitudinem aquilae volantis &c. Hutter*: as a flying eagle: *a nation whose tongue thou shalt not understand*: or that which (after the Roman eagle was seen there in their flying colors) fell out in the time of Caesar Adrianus; who for the Jews’ rebellion under their pseudo-Messiah, Barcochebas, interdicted the Jews’ return to Jerusalem; and to that purpose, not only changed the name of Jerusalem to Elia, but also caused the marble statue of a hog (which was an abomination to the Jews) to be set up over that gate of the city which opened toward Bethlehem; it was a certain sign and token of their desolation and final destruction; their scepter departed; they subjected to the Roman empire, their temple demolished, their daily sacrifice ceased, and they scattered over the world; was the completion thereof, which Daniel foretold, about 670 years before it came to pass; and this was about a hundred years after the blasphemous Jews had cried out, *his blood be on us, and on our children*: God’s justice is ever sure, though sometimes slow. See on Luke 21.20. &c.

*{by Daniel}* See Daniel 9.27. & 12.11.

*{in the holy place}* In Judea, the holy land: before the holy city. See Isaiah 63.18. Or as Theophylact thinks, Caesar’s image set up *ἐν* *τοῖς* *ἀδύτοις* *τοῦ* *ναοῦ*, in the inward parts of the temple.

*{let him understand}* Let him take notice, that when they see the Roman colors flying in Judea, the temple and city shall be destroyed, and the nation of the Jews finally scattered, according to the prophet Daniel’s prophecies, Dan. 9.26.

**V. 16. ***{Then let them}* Being warned by these signs, let them flee to save their lives. See on Luke 21.21.

*{into the mountains}* For there would be no safety in any of their cities. So Gen. 19.17.

**V. 17. ***{Not come down}* If he can escape with life some other way; let him make all the haste he can to be gone, and not stay to save ought of his goods.

**V. 18. ***{His clothes}* His coat, or cloak, or other garment, such as men use not to work in, in the fields; lest tarrying too long in the city, the Roman siege shut him up to destruction, with the rest which shall perish there: *ἱμάτιον* signifies either house furniture, carpets, coverlids, &c. or a coat, *Budaeus, ex Athenae. l. 12.*

**V. 19. ***{That are with child}* Such could not well flee, at the enemies’ approach, because of their burden; nor nurses from their children. See Luke 23.29.

**V. 20. ***{Nor in the winter}* Because bad ways, and foul weather might hinder them.

*{Neither on the sabbath day}* They not understanding, that in case of necessity, it was lawful to flee or make defense on the sabbath day, might more be exposed to the fury of the enemy, neither could they that day get things necessary, by reason of their strict observation of the sabbath. Christ accommodates this speech unto the infirmity of the Jews, the sum whereof is, that they should pray unto the Lord, whose divine providence rules over, and disposes of all, that nothing might befall them which might make their afflictions more grievous and dangerous unto them, or hinder their flight from their enemies, and the fury of the war to come upon them; as incommodiousness of ways, shortness of days, cold, stormy, winter weather, &c. or importunity of the sabbath, wherein they might be troubled in conscience, if necessity should compel them to flee beyond the short measure of a sabbath day’s journey, which would not set them beyond the lines of a besieging enemy: finally he admonishes his disciples hereof, that they might acquaint the Christians among the Jews the survivors, that when they should see these things drawing nigh, they might by no means delay, but speedily be gone (as Elijah said to Ahab before the storm, 1 Kings 18.44.) *that the shower stop you not*: or as the angels said to Lot, Gen. 19.17. *escape for your lives, look not behind you neither stay in all the plain, escape to the mountain, lest you be consumed*, and the storm overtake you. *Remember Lot’s wife*, Luke 17.32.

**V. 21. ***{Such as were not, &c.}* To that nation, Luke 21.23. Exod. 9.24. Luke recites some kinds of those tribulations under the name of the *days of vengeance* upon the Jews; to wit, that they should fall by the edge of the sword, be led away captive into all nations, &c. which accordingly came upon them.

**V. 22. ***{No flesh be saved}* He speaks of the calamity of the Jews (as may appear in that our Savior said, Luke 21.23. *there shall be great distress in the land, and wrath upon this people*) and in his speech relates to the same; the meaning is, none of the Jews should be saved from that slaughter, except the Lord should shorten those days of affliction before mentioned: flesh is by a figure, taken for mankind, and here, as Jer. 12.12. and 45.5. for the Jews.

*{for the elect’s sake}* For the elect’s sake, *ἐξ* *αὐτῶν*. *Theophylact*, of themselves; or, which were among the Jews which should perish.

*{those days shall be shortened}* He means *τὰς* *θλίψεις* *καὶ* *τὸν* *πόλεμον*. *Theophylact*, those (days of) afflictions and wars, which were coming on the Jews: had they long continued, they must have all perished by the sword and famine: but they had a speedier end of their calamities; the obstinate impiety of reprobates provoked God’s justice, to have continued them long; but his mercy prevailed for the elect’s sake to shorten them, and quickly end that bloody war, by giving the Roman a speedy conquest: so that Jerusalem was taken in five months, or thereabout; which could have been, but that the seditious citizens burnt up their own store of corn. See *Joseph. de bel. Jud. l. 6. c. 1.* and the many fortified towers in Judea and Galilee, were taken in a year and half: which was then imputed to the hand of God, favoring and prospering the Roman; *Joseph. q. s. l. 3. c. 11. l. 7. c. 16.* All which God did for his truth and elect’s sake: for from that miserable slaughter, Jerusalem being rendered, there escaped above four hundred thousand persons. *Joseph. q. s. l. 7. c. 15.* which were dispersed among the nations.

**V. 23. ***{Then if any man, &c.}* Mark 13.21. Luke 17.23. Then when Satan shall be loosed, Rev. 20.3. and become a lying spirit, in the mouths of seducing impostors. See 1 Kings 22.22. 2 Cor. 11.13, 14, 15. 2 Thess. 2.9. &c. Rev. 12.9, 12. Then, whatsoever men seem in excellency, or authority, if they preach otherwise unto you, than that you have received, *let them be accursed*, Gal. 1.8. Hear them not.

**V. 24. ***{false Christs}* Either such as John calls Antichrists, 1 John 2.18. or such as shall feign and call themselves Christ.

*{false prophets}* Those who teach lies; falsely interpreting and applying the word of God to sinister and impious ends: as do sectaries, schismatics, and heretics: men pretending to much godliness and gifts, with power to exercise the same in the ministry, to which God calls them not: so their opposition of the truth of Christ denominates, and shows them false; and their usurping the ministry, prophets, but false ones; and therefore not to be heard of those that love the truth that they may be saved: see 2 Thess. 2.10, 11, 12. For otherwise they could be but false and erring laics, or people, they had not so much as a semblance of prophets. Some understand here the false prophet, mentioned, Rev. 16.13. and some, Mahomet.

*{shew great}* *δώσουσι* *σημεῖα*: *They shall give*, or openly show forth: so the Hebrews use their word *נתן* *nathan*.

*{signs}* That is, lying wonders; as, 2 Thess. 2.9. Revel. 13.13.

*{deceive the very elect}* Not among the Jews only, but among the Gentiles also, called to the faith of Christ, which they did dangerously attempt, by their specious pretenses, and shows of extraordinary zeal and godliness: for, as Minucius Felix says, *incaute creduli circumveniuntur ab his quos bonos putaverunt.* The incautiously credulous are circumvented of these, whom they thought to be good men.

**V. 25. ***{Behold I have told you before}* That yourselves by this forewarning, may be armed against their delusions, and may not be troubled, when you shall see so many falling away from the truth, and giving heed to the spirit of error. See John 16.14.

**V. 26. ***{He is in the desert}* That is, Christ, or his true Church where he is present, is in the separation, and among those who withdraw themselves from the public professors of Christianity: for those false Christs, and their instruments, used to draw men and women out into the deserts, where they might more securely, and without discovery or resistance, make head, and gather congregations.

*{in the secret chambers}* In the inward rooms, and private places, apt for conventicles: it is true, *honesta semper publico gaudent, scelera secreta sunt*: things honest ever rejoice in being public, impieties are secret: which though it be not always so, because the best are most persecuted and suppressed, and God is just in bringing wickedness to light; yet ever the good would appear, and the vicious would be concealed.

**V. 27. ***{As the lightning}* They need not seek Christ, his kingdom, or coming to judgment, in this or that peculiar place; he will appear like the lightning in a moment to the startled world.

**V. 28. ***{For wheresoever}* Luke 17.37. A proverb used concerning things everywhere alike: the meaning is, it will be found of those who look after it.

**V. 29. ***{Immediately after}* The like calamities shall befall the 63Roman empire, that had by them afflicted the Jews before. See Revel. 6.12, 13. These things may be spoken allegorically: as, Isaiah 13.10. & 24.23. Ezek. 32.7. Joel 2.10, 31. & 3.15. Job 30.28. Jerem. 15.9. Amos 8.9. Some understand it literally to be fulfilled a little before, or at the last day; but that uses not so to be described, but by fire, 2 Pet. 3.12. Others take these things literally for strange sights in the heaven, either before the destruction of Jerusalem, or before the day of judgment: of which time they who understand it conceive that he says, *immediately*, that men may ever watch and provide for his coming: as, Phil. 4.5. Heb. 10.37. 2 Pet. 3.8. Or *immediately* after the troubles of the Church under Antichrist, which some think intended in the former verses, as, 2 Thess. 2.8. Or after the great troubles of the Church, Rev. 20.7, 8, 9. whereof these Jewish or Gentile troubles might be a foregoing sign: some think the certainty of the thing only here intended, and not the nearness of time. See on verse 33.

*{shall the sun be darkened}* Mark 13.24. Luke 21.25. See on Acts 2.19. Certainly he foretells the signs which shall precede the last day: but how the sun shall be darkened, the moon obscured, or the stars fall, it is rather to be expected than defined by us.

*{the powers of the heavens}* The spheres and stars of heaven; except we take it allegorically, as some do.

**V. 30. ***{sign of the Son of man}* Such as shall testify that Christ is coming to judge: what that sign shall be, whether some extraordinary comet, as some conjecture, or the sign of the cross, or some other glorious light; the Scripture not determining, we should but vainly inquire.

*{all the tribes of the earth}* All families and kindreds: it is an allusion to the Jews distributed into tribes.

*{shall mourn}* They shall be in such sorrow, that they shall smite on their breasts, as with horror of heart, and confusion of face, by reason of those grievous and inevitable judgments then upon them, for their contempt and abuse of this Gospel. See on Luke 21.26.

*{They shall see the Son of man}* Rev. 1.7. At his last coming, sitting on the clouds, as on a throne of glory, chap. 25.31. Acts 1.9, 11. 1 Thess. 1.17.

*{with power}* With all the holy angels attending him, 1 Thess. 4.17. chap. 16.27. & 25.31.

*{and great glory}* With a visible brightness and divine majesty.

**V. 31. ***{And he shall send his angels}* See 1 Thess. 4.16. and chap. 13.41. That is, his holy angels, who are his ministering spirits, sent forth to *minister for them that shall be heirs of salvation*. Heb. 1.14. This he here speaks to comfort his, against the terror of that most dreadful day of the Lord, which shall come as a snare and destruction upon all the children of disobedience. See Luke 21.28. 1 Thess. 4.18.

*{with a great sound of a trumpet}* Or, *with a trumpet and a great voice*: as, Exod. 19.19. & 20.18. See Psalm 47.5. 1 Cor. 15.52. 1 Thess. 4.16.

*{shall gather together}* As the manner was to gather the people together with a trumpet, Numb. 10.2.

*{from the four winds}* From all quarters of the world. See Isa. 43.5, 6. Rev. 7.1. Zech. 2.6. See on chap. 8.11.

*{from one end of heaven}* In respect of our sight: otherwise, heaven is round, and has no end. See Psalm 19.6. Deut. 4.32. & 28.64. From east to west, from north to south.

**V. 32. ***{yet tender}* In the spring, when the sap rises, and shoots out into the branches. See on Mark 13.28.

*{putteth forth leaves}* Cant. 2.13.

**V. 33. ***{When ye shall see these things}* When you shall see these things befall the Jews, look for those things which shall shortly after befall the rest of the world also: for the one is the forerunner of the other, as the putting forth of the leaves of the fig tree, is of the summer. Or when you shall see those extraordinary things mentioned, verse 29. look for Christ’s coming to judgment speedily.

*{is near}* The coming of the Son of man, Christ Jesus, verse 30. or trouble is near coming on the Gentiles, when Jerusalem is destroyed: then it is near, as it were certainly at the door. See Gen. 4.7. James 5.9. Phil. 4.5. Rev. 1.3.

**V. 34. ***{this generation shall not pass}* Some alive shall see all fulfilled that I have spoken, concerning the destruction of Jerusalem, and the troubles accompanying it.

*{all these things}* That are shortly to come, opposed to that day, verse 36. where he speaks of the last day.

**V. 35. ***{heaven and earth shall pass away}* Mark 13.31. May sooner pass away, than any of my words prove false: as chap. 5.18. Luke 16.17. Isa. 54.9. 2 Pet. 3.10.

**V. 36. ***{of that day}* Of the day of judgment, and of the end of world of which, verse 30, 31. which was expected of all God’s people, 2 Tim. 1.18.

*{hour}* The time and season, 1 Thess. 5.1. Hour taken at large, as 1 John 2.18. Rev. 3.10. & 14.7, 15.

*{knoweth no man}* God has hidden that day and hour from us, that we might never be secure, but always prepared for it; *ut pendula expectationis incerto mens sollicita — adventus diem semper expectans, semper expectando speraret, &c.* *Hilar. de trin. l. 9.* That the mind solicitous with a pendulous uncertainty, always expecting the day of his coming, might in expecting always hope for it, and have a watchful care against that uncertain, certain hour. So says our Savior, *be ye also prepared, because ye know not what hour the Son of man will come*. See Luke 12.40.

*{not the angels of heaven}* Much less sinful men on earth, who are far below them in knowledge.

**V. 37. ***{for as in the days of Noah}* Gen. 7. See on Luke 17.26.

*{so shall also the coming, &c.}* That came suddenly when the secure world thought of nothing less; and so also shall Christ’s coming be, to judgment, when the secure world least expected him, 2 Pet. 3.10. 1 Thess. 5.2, 3.

**V. 38. ***{they were eating and drinking}* Men were then also given to brutish sensuality, and secure luxury, as here, verse 49. *τρώγοντες*, *eating by gnawing*, which is proper to beasts: he reproves luxury, not a sober use of the creatures. See on Luke 17.27.

*{giving in marriage}* See on chap. 22.30.

**V. 39. ***{and knew not}* Took no more notice of it, than if they had heard nothing at all of it, not believing what Noah had foretold.

*{took them all away}* All the secure unbelieving world; of which number Noah and his family were not.

*{so shall also}* So unprepared shall men be at the day of judgment, as if they had never heard thereof: and many of them who cannot but believe, or at least fear it will come; yet vainly procrastinate, and put off their repentance and preparation from day to day, until death and judgment suddenly come upon them, and find them unprovided.

**V. 40. ***{Then shall two}* See on Luke 17.36.

*{in the field}* At their harvest work, other labor in sowing, about their cattle, or other like employment.

*{the one}* One of the two: they shall not be taken by families, as Noah was: but one friend shall be taken by Christ into heaven, and another left for the devil to carry into hell. Some think it also relates to the destruction of Jerusalem.

*{shall be taken}* As led by the hand out of the common danger and destruction: an allusion to Gen. 19.16. The Syriac gives by a word, which among other significations, imports to lead out, or into, as John 16.13. Rom. 8.14. or to take to oneself, John 14.3. See on Luke 17.36.

**V. 41. ***{two women}* Bondwomen, or of the meanest condition, such as used to grind at the mill, Exod. 11.5.

**V. 42. ***{watch therefore}* Mark 13.35. Watching is enjoined all Christians here; and ministers after, verse 45. Luke 12.41. We must by faith expect our Savior perpetually, and be ready to receive him, verse 44. *qui enim poenitenti indulgentiam promisit, dissimulanti diem crastinam non spopondit*, *Prosper ep.* For he that promised mercy to the penitent, has not promised tomorrow to the dissembling. So also *Greg. hom. 12. in evang.* We must therefore always expect the last day, which we can never foresee.

**V. 43. ***{But know this}* Luke 12.39. 1 Thess. 5.2. This you must necessarily know, except you are willingly ignorant: as 2 Pet. 3.5. This is evident to common reason.

*{in what watch}* Matt. 14.25. That is, in what hour of the night; which they divided into certain watches.

*{broken up}* Digged through, as thieves use to do, Exodus 22.2.

**V. 44. ***{such an hour as ye think not}* Unless you watch; for the master of the house dreams not of the coming of the thief. The secure man thinks not of the sudden coming of Christ which will be more terrible.

**V. 45. ***{who then is}* Luke 22.42. See note thereon: as if he said, faithful prelates are but rare, but ever blessed, as verse 46. Such a one is, as Theophylact expresses him, *σπάνιος* *καὶ* *δυσεύρετος*, *rare and hard to be found*.

*{ruler over his}* As a pastor over the flock, or a steward of the mysteries of God, 1 Cor. 4.1. See on Luke 12.44.

*{household}* Over his servants, *ἐπὶ* *τῆς* *θεραπείας* *αὐτοῦ*, *over his service*, or over the rest of his servants, *famulitiam*, interpreted, Theophylact, *over the sons of his house*, or family: Syr. over his domestical servants.

*{to give them meat}* As God’s steward: that is to preach the doctrine and knowledge of Christ, which like those five loaves which Christ blessed, is not diminished, but increased by giving out to many, or like the widow’s oil, 2 Kings 4. increased, only by pouring out.

**V. 46. ***{blessed, &c.}* Happy shall they be, if they persevere in their faithful diligence, and care for the people of God: though perhaps they may suffer many unworthy things of a present impious world, as the prophets, apostles, martyrs, and most faithful ministers, ever did; yet happy shall they be when Christ, appearing to call them from hence, shall find them vigilant over the flock committed to their charge.

**V. 47. ***{ruler over all his goods}* All that he has: of a steward he will make him overseer of all his estate, Gen. 36.4, 6. He shall advance him to a greater command and dignity. See Revel. 2.26. and here verse 45. The meaning is, he shall reign with Christ, and be partaker of the riches of his grace, and blessedness.

**V. 48. ***{that evil servant}* But if the evil steward neglect the salvation of the people; if he tyrannize over them.

*{say in his heart}* If he think with himself, Psalm 14.1.

*{my lord delayeth his coming}* It will be long ere he comes: as 2 Pet. 3. promising himself a long life, and time to repent in, and to make his peace. So securely contemning and abusing the long-suffering and patience of God; presuming to do any violence and injury to God’s people, because he does not presently appear against them, and their impiety.

**V. 49. ***{to smite}* To exercise any kind of cruelty on his fellow servants (by virtue of his authority) for whose good and welfare he was put into that office, which therefore he ought to have had the more care of, 1 Pet. 4.10.

*{with the drunken}* If he give lewd example of luxury and intemperance, making him scandalous, his ministry less effectual, and the very doctrine he preaches despicable, so causing the name of God to be blasphemed.

**V. 50. ***{when he looketh not for him}* Of which (day) he thought not, says the Syriac interpreter: the judgments of God are not so much as in the thought of the wicked: they cry, *peace, peace*, till sudden destruction come upon them, then are they the most dejected men of the world. See 1 Samuel 28.20. and Daniel 5.1, 2, 6, 7.

**V. 51. ***{cut him asunder}* *διχοτομήσει* *αὐτὸν*, *cut him in two pieces*; either by rending his soul from his body; or body and soul cast out of God’s blessed presence, divided him from the elect to damnation. See on Luke 12.46.

*{his portion with hypocrites}* He shall have greater punishment than ordinary: as also hypocrites must expect; because counterfeit sanctity is double iniquity. See Job 20.29. Psalm 11.6.

*{weeping, &c.}* Matt. 8.12. & 13.42, 50. & 22.13.

## CHAPTER 25

**Verse 1. ***{Then}* The evangelist prosecutes the conclusion of Christ’s last sermon, concerning his glorious and sudden appearance to judgment, and our duty in watching, lest that day find us unprepared, or unfaithful in our duties: *then*, in the day of Christ’s coming, mentioned chap. 24.51.

*{the kingdom of heaven}* The Church in this world, *Greg. hom. 12. in Evang.* as Matt. 13.41. or the condition of men in the Church of God.

*{virgins}* The custom then was to make marriage feasts in the night: the bridegroom used to come with a choice company of young men, which were called the *children of the bridechamber*, chap. 9.15. Certain virgins of the acquaintance or kindred of the bride, used to go meet the bridegroom, attending him unto the wedding house: to which custom Psalm 45.14. alludes; *she shall be brought unto the king, in raiment of needlework; the virgins her companions that follow her shall be brought unto thee*: by virgins here are signified pure souls, as 2 Cor. 11.2, 3.

*{which took their lamps, and went forth}* They carried them forth (as the custom then was) lighted for the service of the bridegroom and his train, in which there was both solemnity, and accommodation of light for the company passing by the way.

**V. 2. ***{Five of them}* He says not this to intimate that there were as many wise or good men, as wicked which are truly foolish: the whole world (unregenerate) lies in wickedness, 1 John 5.19. But he simply notes the difference, in an equal condition, in which all Christians alike, profess their hope of salvation by Christ, and their expectation of him to judge the living and the dead: yet among these there is much difference, some truly believe, some are hypocrites; some profess in words only, but not in heart, having a form of godliness, but denying the power thereof: they confess they expect a judgment; but live as if they thought in their hearts, there were no God to call them to account. See Psalm 14.1. &c. None but the foolish can think, and do thus; or hope to impose on God’s all-seeing justice: but they are wise, who truly fear God, and endeavor to serve him faithfully (Psalm 111.10.) though there are but few such.

*{were wise}* So showed they themselves such, in providing timely for their present employment.

*{were foolish}* These showed themselves such, in neglecting the main, for which they were called, and came together: such are they, who professing faith in Christ, yet prepare not themselves against his coming. For as Augustine says, *de verb. Domini serm. 23.* these are not *quaecunque animae, sed tales, quae habent catholicam fidem, & habere videntur bona opera in Ecclesia Dei*, not every, or any soul, but such as have (or profess) the Catholic faith, and seem to have good works in the Church of God. In his 120th epistle to Honoratus, he interprets this something nearer, of men (*aliena laude laetantibus, & propter ipsam inanem laudem, non propter conscientiam suam; ubi illa testis est Deus, bona operantibus*) rejoicing in others praising them, and doing good, not for their conscience’ sake, where God is their witness, but for vain praise of men.

**V. 3. ***{took their lamps}* By which is represented, profession of faith and sanctity.

*{but no oil}* Besides that which was burning, and spent ere the bridegroom came, verse 8. That is, they had no good works of faith, (charity and sanctity) without which faith is dead, and inactive. See Gal. 5.6. James 2.26. So Jerome on this place says, they have no oil, who seem to confess the Lord with like faith: *sed virtutum opera negligunt*, but neglect good works.

**V. 4. ***{oil in their vessels}* In their lamps: the meaning is, they prepared to meet Christ, with faith, repentance, and charity; which is, (as Jerome says) *lumen indeficiens*, a light which shall never fail, or go out: see 1 Cor. 13.8. Faith shall end in enjoying, but charity shall be perfected, not ended in heaven: this oil then is that which the apostle speaks of, 2 Cor. 1.12. the testimony of their consciences: this is to carry oil with them, not to buy of those that sell, that is of flatterers; *Augustine ep. 120.* who do good, *ut laudetur Deus*, that God may be praised.

**V. 5. ***{while the bridegroom tarried}* It was not a little time, *inter priorem & secundum adventum Domini*, *Jerom.*, between the first and second coming of the Lord Christ: Christ’s coming must be expected with holy patience, Habak. 2.3. *Mora sponsi poenitentiae tempus est.* *Hilar. can. 27.* The bridegroom’s tarrying is the time of repentance.

*{all slumbered}* Some died (as Jerome interprets) others after long attendance, and expectation, began to be possessed with secular cares, distracting their thoughts from a present watch, and looking for Christ; so *Augustine ep. 120.* which slumber, elect and reprobates are in several degrees, subject to. Good men are at some times somewhat more negligent than they should be; but they always keep a good conscience, and a heart prepared, in some good measure to receive Christ, Acts 23.1. and 24.16. 2 Cor. 1.12. Cant. 5.2. Their security is but of infirmity, and their estate was made sure before; the carnal and worldly have, at best, but the lamps of profession, and want the oil and substance of religion, that is, the power thereof, 1 Tim. 1.5. 2 Tim. 3.5.

**V. 6. ***{at midnight}* *Subito quasi intempesta nocte & securis omnibus*, suddenly, as in the still of the night, Jerome, Euthymius, because he will come *ἀνέλπιστος*, unexpected, as Theophylact speaks: or as *Hilary can. 27. cunctis ignorantibus*, in a time which none knows; Christ will come when he is least looked for: as the bridegroom came at midnight, later than the usual manner was.

*{a cry made}* This is generally understood, by Chrysostom, Theophylact, Euthymius, Jerome, Hilary, &c. of the voice of the archangel, and the trumpet of God, to awaken all, even those who are in the graves, and depths of the seas, of which John 5.29. 1 Thess. 4.16. 1 Cor. 15.52. which is most certainly true: but this circumstance relates also to the custom, and accommodation of the parable: and in the substance of the matter, we may understand this noise, to be also the voice and importunity of them, who exhort, and admonish us to prepare, and meet Christ.

*{the bridegroom cometh}* Christ comes: so will he do by his divine providence and comforting Spirit to call us hence at our death and day of judgment.

**V. 7. ***{trimmed their lamps}* Which is to be understood of the wise virgins, the foolish carried only dead lamps, without that which Christ commanded, chap. 5.16. An allusion to Exod. 30.8. 2 Kings 9.30.

**V. 8. ***{gone out}* Or, *going out*, which in the temple should not be, Exod. 27.20. The moral whereof was, we ought to persevere in faith and sanctity, chapter 24.13. Revel. 2.10. Col. 1.23. Heb. 3.14.

**V. 9. ***{not so, lest there be not enough for us and you}* Not fearing the failing of their own store, as if they were uncertain of their own salvation, but because (as Jerome says) *neque possunt in die judicii aliorum virtutes, aliorum vitia sublevare*, in the day of judgment, the virtues of the one cannot relieve the vices of the other: see Ezek. 14.14. & 18.20. So says *Augustine ep. 120. Non desperanter, sed humiliter dictum est*, it is not (by them) spoken despairingly, but humbly; for who can so presume on his own conscience, that he can be certain, that it can suffice him in God’s judgment, except the merciful God judge in mercy, not the rigor of justice, in which none can stand? Psalm 143.2.

*{go ye rather}* Nor that the just shall send the wicked at the day of judgment to purchase grace: but it shows that they who neglect to get it here, have no means left to get any then: so Elijah mocks the priests of Baal. 1 Kings 18.27.

*{buy}* Isaiah 55.1. Revel. 3.18. This word seems here inserted, only for the accommodation of the parable.

*{for yourselves}* Everyone must be saved by his own faith, Rom. 1.17. Rom. 14.12, 22. Gal. 6.4.

**V. 10. ***{while they went to buy}* This is spoken to show us, that (as there was no manna to be found on the sabbath) so shall there be no place for repentance and good works in death: and that the faithful only, who in a careful watch, continually expect the coming of Christ, shall be saved. See Job 14.14. Luke 12.37.

*{the door was shut}* They who neglect God’s call, and are not ready when he comes, shall be shut out of the gate of mercy, Psalm 95.11. Here life is gotten or lost to eternity. They that were ready with their lamps burning, that is, with faith and good works, as Augustine fore-cited interprets, went into eternal joy with Christ, the rest were shut out.

**V. 11. ***{Lord, Lord}* Chap. 7.22. & 25.37.

*{open to us}* To wit the door of mercy: receive us also.

**V. 12. ***{I know you not}* Chap. 7.23. The Lord knows who are his, 2 Tim. 2.19. But he will not own those in judgment, who will not own and obey him now, when he offers mercy. Admirable is the power of faithful prayer, and timely repentance; but as Jerome says, *quid prodest, voce invocare, quem operibus negas?* What avails it to invocate him with your voice, whom you deny in your works? See Psalm 50.15, 16. Esau’s too late tears show us the danger of deferring repentance and conversion unto God. See Heb. 3.13, 15. 2 Cor. 6.2. Eccles. 12.1.

**V. 13. ***{watch therefore}* Chap. 24.42. Mark 13.33.

**V. 14. ***{as a man traveling into a far, &c.}* Luke 19.12. Hereby is intended Christ’s ascension into heaven, and abiding there till the day of judgment, chap. 21.33.

*{delivered unto them his goods}* Put them into several offices, and places for the good of his Church, Eph. 4.11, 12.

**V. 15. ***{five talents}* Of a talent. See 1 Chron. 22.14.

*{according to his several ability}* According to the skill and wisdom in dealing which was given them, 1 Cor. 12.7. According as he knew they had knowledge to manage that which was entrusted to them: this *δύναμιν*, the Syr. interpreter gives, *juxta facultatem suam*. Jerome, *secundum propriam virtutem*, that is, as his self interprets, *pro accipientium viribus*, according to the abilities of the receivers: who yet considers not in his servants, so much *lucri magnitudinem*, the greatness of the gain, as *studii voluntatem*, the willingness of their care and endeavor. Prosper notes well here, *de vocat. Gent. l. 2. c. 8.* he gave according to their proper abilities, *sed non secundum proprium meritum*, but not according to their proper merit. In the one, two, or five talents, we must understand the diversities of God’s gifts, which are not all conferred on any one man. See Eph. 4.11. 1 Cor. 12.7, 11, 29. Rom. 12.6. 1 Cor. 4.2.

**V. 16. ***{traded with the same}* Was laborious in his calling, according to his gifts.

*{made them other five talents}* Increased his gifts by using them to the benefit of others, and did twice so much good, as before his employment. See 2 Tim. 1.6. The power and fruit of God’s graces in us, is in their use to his glory, and the gaining of souls to Christ: he shall be judged a faithful servant, who so employs them. See Rom. 15.15, 16. Such were the prophets and apostles.

**V. 17. ***{likewise he that had received two}* He means those who are of meaner gifts and measures of grace; (as were, and are the succeeding ministers of Christ, not assisted, as the apostles with infallibility of Spirit) who have not that admirable evidence of Spirit, and largeness of heart; yet of a measure which God makes sufficient to gain souls; these are also accepted of God as faithful servants; if they do, as Peter said, *as every one hath received the gift, even so minister the same one to another, as good stewards of the manifold grace of God*, 1 Peter 4.10. If they who are of mean gifts, be faithful and diligent, God accepts their will and endeavor.

**V. 18. ***{digged in the earth}* Importing those ministers, who for shame, sloth, or fear, will not exercise the gifts they have: or any other, who receive God’s grace in vain: against which the apostle exhorts, 2 Cor. 6.1. which concerns both teachers, and hearers.

**V. 19. ***{a long time after}* In the end Christ will come and call men to account; meaning in the general, from Christ’s ascension into heaven, unto his coming again to judge: this time has already been more than 1614 years: in particular, the life of man, the remainder whereof is very uncertain.

*{cometh and reckoneth with them}* Christ will certainly come again to judge every man: therefore it behooves us to live, as every hour expecting him: he will call everyone to a strict account, how we have employed his gifts: how careful ought we to be, how we use our time; health, strength, understanding, offices, or authority? These all are our Master’s talents, and he will once bring us to an account of their improvement.

**V. 20. ***{other five talents}* See on verse 16. The Hebrew talent of the sanctuary, contained a hundred Hebrew pounds, whereof every one was sixty sicles or shekels: a shekel was about half an ounce in weight, and in value about four groats, or *denarii*, at which rate, it amounts to 400 pound, but the common Hebrew talent was about half so much. Some say the talent of silver was 375 pound sterling, but the talent of gold 3750 pound. See notes on 1 Kings 20.39. 2 Kings 23.33.

**V. 21. ***{over a few things}* In a small charge, Luke 16.18.

*{over many}* I will prefer you: as men do servants whom they have found faithful. See on chap. 24.47.

*{into the joy of thy lord}* Come and receive the fruit of my bounty; the reward of your fidelity. See verse 34.

**V. 24. ***{which had received one talent}* He is also called to account, as well as they that had received more talents, that no man may think to be excused from giving account by the meanness of his place or parts.

*{that thou art an hard man}* He would (as hypocrites, and evil servants use to do) transmit the fault from himself to his master: one of Adam’s sons, Gen. 3.12. But indeed, God is (as Augustine says well *Armentario, ep. 45.*) *benignus exactor, non egenus: & qui non crescit ex redditis, sed in se crescere facit redditores*: a bountiful exactor, not a needy: and who increases not by things rendered to him, but who increases the renderers in themselves.

**V. 25. ***{Lo thou hast that is thine}* Chap. 20.14.

**V. 26. ***{His lord answered}* By this answer he signifies that they have no excuse for their sloth, who suppress God’s gifts and spend their time unprofitably, or that murmur, and lay their defects in duty on God’s providence; whereas it is most true which Prosper says, *de vocat. Gent. lib. 2. cap. 1. nec damnati justa querimonia, nec justificati verax est arrogantia, si vel ille dicat non meruisse se poenam, vel iste asserat meruisse se gratiam*; neither is the complaint of the damned just, neither the arrogance of the justified veritable; if either he says, he has not deserved the punishment, or this affirm that he has deserved grace.

*{thou wicked and slothful servant}* By these sharp and yet well deserved reproofs, that appears, which Prosper says, *de vocat. Gent. lib. 2. cap. 8.* that not only, *de non servato, sed de non aucto munere damnabantur*; for that men have not kept, but because they have not increased the gift, they shall be condemned.

*{thou knewest}* Were it as you say, you should have been the more careful to please me: and are now the more unexcusable by your own confession, chap. 12.37. Luke 19.22.

**V. 27. ***{to the exchangers}* Such as kept banks, to take men’s moneys, and make profit thereof for them: the meaning is, you ought to have committed it to the hearers who bringing forth fruits, would have rendered me the interest of good works; so Jerome, Chrysostom, Hilary, Euthymius, and others, understand here by usury.

**V. 28. ***{take therefore the talent from him}* God justly takes away his gifts, from those that abuse, or make no good use of them: understand those gifts or graces which are common to elect and reprobates; as gifts, or parts of nature, or art, office, charge, &c. for the gifts and graces of regeneration are *ἀμεταμέλητα*, Rom. 11.29. such as God never repents of, or recalls. See John 16.22. God sometime takes away that which therein men seem to have, Luke 8.18. when by pulling off their visards, he discovers their hypocrisy; making it appear to the world, that their pretenses to faith and good works, were but semblances and false shadows of things which had no being.

*{and give it unto him which hath ten}* Not that the elect shall have or receive any good from the reprobate: but because their reward in heaven shall be so great; as if the happiness which seemed due to all others, in man’s judgment must accrue to them, and be put on their account.

**V. 29. ***{for unto every one}* Chap. 13.12. Mark 4.25. Luke 8.18.

**V. 30. ***{the unprofitable servant}* He must be condemned though he did not waste his master’s goods, because he did not increase them by his using them: see the reason hereof, 1 Cor. 12.7.

*{into outer darkness}* See on chap. 8.12.

*{weeping and, &c.}* See on chap. 24.51.

**V. 31. ***{when the Son of man}* Christ, God and man.

*{in his glory}* Not in the form of a servant, as before, but as a judge, most glorious and dreadful appearing with incomprehensible glory, see chap. 24.30. Dan. 7.9, 10. Mark 13.26. Col. 3.4.

*{and all the holy angels}* An allusion to Zech. 14.5. Now he has a few poor men to attend him; then all the glorious angels shall administer unto him, their Lord.

*{sit upon the throne}* As a man on some visible throne: or he shall judge all, as they that sit on thrones.

**V. 32. ***{shall be gathered}* Chap. 24.31. Before him, to be judged by him.

*{he shall separate them}* This separation of good from bad, is set out by a similitude of fanning, chap. 3.12. of the tares, chap. 13.30. of the draw net, chap. 13.48. Here by parting the sheep and goats, that signifies the elect and reprobates: the flock of Christ, his Church (Luke 12.32.) in this life, is like the flock at Padan Aram, Gen. 30.32. speckled and spotted (see Judg. 6612.23. 2 Pet. 2.13.) but in the life to come, they shall be *as a flock of sheep which go up from the washing* (Cant. 6.6.) *without spot or wrinkle — holy and without blemish*: Eph. 5.27. Here, tares and wheat grow up together, Matt. 13.30. but in the world’s harvest, the day of judgment, they shall be parted.

*{one from another}* Not one nation from another: but one man from another. See chap. 24.31, 40, 41.

*{sheep from the goats}* An allusion to Ezek. 34.17.

**V. 33. ***{sheep on his right hand}* Right hand and left imply good and bad, Eccles. 10.2. or blessing and cursing, as Gerizim bare southward, which is on the right hand, when you look to the sunrising, (that was the mountain of blessing, Deut. 27.12.) and Ebal on the north, (that was the mountain of cursing ib. verse 13.) which is on the left hand.

**V. 34. ***{the king}* Christ, Revel. 19.16. *King of kings and Lord of lords*, Rev. 17.14. 1 Tim. 6.15. *the king eternal, immortal, invisible, the only wise God*, 1 Tim. 1.17, 19.

*{come}* Draw near to me: be not afraid of my majesty. The word *δεῦτε*, *come*, is here a word of singular favor and love, graciously inviting the elect, to the full fruition of eternal blessedness, in their conjunction with Christ, and participation of his immense goodness, so chap. 11.28. *δεῦτε* *πρὸς* *με*, *come unto me*, such words Jehu once said to Jonadab, 2 Kings 10.15, 16. when he took him up to him into his charet, *is thy heart right, as my heart?* *δεῦρο* *μετ᾽* *ἐμοῦ* *come with me*.

*{ye blessed of my Father}* Whom my Father loves: to whom my Father means to give the kingdom of heaven and interminable blessedness therein.

*{inherit}* For heaven comes to no man by natural descent, but divine adoption: not by merit, but free gift, Rom. 6.23. It is an inheritance given to God’s children by adoption in Christ. See 1 Pet. 13.4. 1 Pet. 3.9. Eph. 2.8.

*{the kingdom}* Not an earthly; but such, as that in respect thereof all earthly kingdoms, are prisons and vassalages.

*{prepared}* By God the Father, chap. 20.23. 1 Cor. 2.9. Heb. 11.16.

*{for you}* You only, who are his elect. Luke 12.32.

*{from the foundation of the world}* Chap. 13.15. Luke 11.50. Rev. 13.8. Eph. 1.4. 1 Pet. 1.20. 2 Tim. 1.9.

**V. 35. ***{for I was an hungered}* Christ takes that which is done for his poor servants, as done unto himself: and this reward of mercy, is not for their own sakes, or merit of the giver, but as they are members of Christ: and because Christ will justify these by faith, which works by love and charity.

*{ye gave me meat}* We need none other arguments to incite us to give to the poor saints, if we understood and firmly believed this one: he that gives to a hungry soul (which is a servant of Christ) food; feeds Christ in that alms.

**V. 36. ***{naked, and ye clothed me}* *In se assumit bonas operationes nostras, ad hoc, ut praestet nobis retributionem bonorum suorum*, *Irenaeus l. 4. cap. 34.* He takes on himself our good works, that he may yield us a retribution of his goods: he accepts what we do for the poor, as done to himself, not that he wants, but *a nobis propter nos fieri vult* (says the same) he will have us to do it for our own sakes: neither may we look hereon, as meritorious works of men, but as being the certain fruits and effects of true faith, apprehending his merits, by which we shall be saved: which being his gift, Eph. 2.8. he does but crown his own gifts in us: so true is that of Bernard: *meritum meum miseratio Domini est*: my merit is God’s mercy: as likewise that which Prosper says, *de vocat. Gent. lib. 2. cap. 1. Neminem indebite damnans, neminem debite liberans; nostra plectens cum punit noxios, sua tribuens cum facit justos*, condemning no man unduly, freeing or acquitting no man duly, (or according to his deserts) punishing that which is ours, when he corrects, giving that which is his own when he justifies or makes men righteous. Consider you oppressors, if they shall be damned, who fed not, and clothed not Christ’s poor, what hope of pardon have you, who take their bread from their mouths, and their clothes, and all temporal comforts from them: consider you merciful to your comfort, that whatsoever you have done for Christ’s poor, you have done for Christ, who is not unfaithful to forget the labor of your love.

**V. 37. ***{the righteous}* Those whom he called blessed, verse 34. now he calls righteous, for God will make all them righteous here, whom he intends to make blessed hereafter, Luke 14.14. Revel. 20.6.

*{when saw we thee, &c.}* In your own person: these answer as those, whose left hand knew not what their right hand did, chap. 6.3. The holy ever think humbly of their own works: and such shall be that bounty of Christ’s rewarding, that the receivers shall admire it as incomparably more than due to them.
