**V. 40. ***{unto one of the least, &c.}* Gr. *unto one of these my brethren, which are the least*; that is, which are least set by in the eyes of the world. It is a greater token of love, to do good to his, for his sake, when he is absent, than to do it to him, if he were present: and that which is done to a mean Christian for his sake, shall be rewarded as well, as that which was done to an apostle, chap. 10.41, 42. For Christ here counts the meanest Christian, as well as the most excellent, his brethren.

**V. 41. ***{Depart}* Chap. 7.23. Psalm 6.8. To be deprived of God’s blessed presence, is a chief part of the misery of the damned. *Depart*, signifies not an absolute going from God’s presence, for he is omnipresent, none can go from his presence, no, not when they go to the damned in hell. See Psalm 139.7, 8, 9. Amos 9.2, 3, 4. Heb. 4.13. But this is spoken in opposition to that, verse 34. *come ye blessed*; and to command them away to the place of execution of God’s just judgment.

*{from me}* None unclean, and defiled with the guilt of uncleansed sin, shall enter into heaven, the presence of Christ. See 1 Cor. 6.9, 10. Rev. 21.27. The wicked shall not have any part of happiness with him, but shall be turned into hell, Psalm 9.17. to be punished in everlasting destruction with the devils, 2 Thess. 1.9.

*{into everlasting fire}* Incorruption, and immortality shall be even to the bodies of the wretched castaways, so that *nec ipsi aeternam poenam finiant, nec ipsos consumat immortalis poena, sed puniat*, *Prosper de vit. contemplat. c. 3.* Neither shall they end their eternal punishment, neither shall immortal pain consume, but still torment them. See on verse 46.

**V. 42. ***{ye gave me no meat}* They that feed not the poor shall go to hell; what torments shall be to them, who by fraud, violence, or any whatsoever pretense, take away from them, and undo, and beggar them and theirs?

**V. 43. ***{sick and in prison, and ye visited me not}* If they shall be damned (whatsoever an impious world think or say to the contrary) who neglected to comfort the sick, and the prisoners; how dreadful and heavy shall their judgment be, who afflict the servants of Christ, cast them into prison, or with heavy exactions take from them their means of comfort? There remains yet one way for them to escape the severe, and otherwise inevitable wrath of a just God; that is, by true and timely repentance; which is not only in saying, God be merciful to me; but in restitution, and present repairing those whom they have injured or oppressed, after the example of Zaccheus, Luke 19.8, 9. and the converted jailor, Acts 16.30, 31, 33, 34. who brought Paul and Silas out of the dungeon, and washing their stripes which he had given them, set meat before them, and wherein he could comforted them.

**V. 44. ***{When saw we thee, &c.}* See on verse 37. The scope hereof, is to show, that whatever the damned shall plead in judgment for their justification or excuse before God, nothing can hold: as here, they might possibly think and in man’s judicature, it might have much availed to clear them in point of not relieving Christ, it being manifest, that they never saw him, neither lived near that age of Christ on earth; but in God’s judgment it can nothing help them which have neglected to relieve the poor, for whom God offers himself a surety, Prov. 19.17. and concerning whose relief he gives many commands, Deut. 15.7, 8. &c. See Psalm 41.1. Isaiah 58.7.

**V. 45. ***{to one of the least}* Let all oppressors now take to heart these words, which, will they nill they, they shall hear in their judgment: the injuries which are done to the least, meanest, or poorest servant of Christ (however men despise, or judge them evil, it skills not) he takes as done to his own sacred person, see Acts 9.4. Because then, the wisest of the sons of men are not able to determine, who are Christ’s: Gamaliel’s grave counsel, is most necessary for them, Acts 5.38. *Refrain from these men and let them alone, lest happily ye be found even to fight against God.*

**V. 46. ***{These shall go away}* See Dan. 12.2. John 5.29. Rev. 14.11. & 20.11. No appeal, traverse, or writ of error can be admitted in this supreme court of God’s justice, they shall go away not as men acquitted or enlarged, but as chained prisoners compelled. See chap. 13.42, 50. They shall go away as Korah, Dathan and Abiram, and that which appertained to them, Numb. 16.31. &c. Hell opening her mouth and swallowing them up quick: in this dreadful spectacle, the elect shall see the severity of God toward reprobates, and his clemency to themselves, Rom. 11.22. saving them, *as a firebrand plucked out of the burning*, Amos 4.11.

*{into everlasting punishment}* Which, verse 41. he calls *everlasting fire*, in other places, hell, Gehenna, *the worm which dies not*, *the lake burning with fire and brimstone*, extreme darkness, *where shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth*: certainly the most intense torments of body and soul, hopeless, helpless: behold the end of sin, specially oppression and cruelty.

*{but the righteous into life eternal}* These shall go with Christ into heaven: in this life elect and reprobates are mixed together, like the tares and wheat, chap. 13. The separation shall be in the day of judgment; the reprobate shall live also, but to eternal punishment: to these (says *Augustine de spir. & anim. cap. 56. mors est sine morte, finis sine fine*) death is without death, and the end without end. (See on verse 41.) But the elect shall live in an endless, changeless, full, secure, and unspeakable happiness, eternally filled with joy and glory.

## CHAPTER 26

**Verse 1. ***{When Jesus had finished}* All the fore-mentioned speeches concerning the destruction of Jerusalem, and his glorious coming to judgment.

*{he said to his disciples}* They only were now with him; to whom he would reveal the instance of his passion; to inform them that he laid down his life for the elect; and foreknowing that which should befall him, he went willingly to Jerusalem to do his Father’s will, concerning which he here informs them, to prevent the scandal, which might otherwise have dangerously shaken their faith, if they should have conceived, his sufferings, fortuitous, compulsory, and involuntary. Which doctrine, though it was for the present, but like new-sowed seed under clods, no growth thereof yet appearing; it did, after the resurrection of Christ, spring up to maturity of faith and confidence in them, and those who since believe in that; there appears the voluntary sacrifice for the remission of sins; and his admirable victory over death, which he so willingly encountered.

**V. 2. ***{Ye know}* Mark 14.1. Luke 22.1. John 13.1. For Christ had often foretold them of his death. See on chap. 27.23. The time and day when the Passover was to be kept, all the Jews well knew, it being positively determined by the law of God: Exod. 12.3. &c. &c. and they might have known that his passion prefigured (as in other legal sacrifices, so specially in the paschal lamb. John 1.29. 1 Cor. 5.7. Heb. 13.11, 12.) was near.

*{Passover}* It was requisite that Christ whom the paschal lamb represented should be offered up at that feast. See on Luke 22.1. and undoubtedly, it was by the divine providence of God; that the sacrilegious priests and elders of the Jews, who had so often sought occasions of putting Jesus to death should not, at any other time find it, than at this, solemnity of the Passover: that he then suffering might actually show, that he was indeed that Lamb of God, *which taketh away the sins of world*, John 1.29. to the end, that now the legal shadows might cease; in the exhibition of him, and the work by him to be performed for the accomplishment of our redemption, which had been long before promised and prefigured: so were the legal ceremonies fulfilled in their change: and so it appears that the Passover was not only appointed to remember them of their deliverance from the Egyptian bondage; but to assure the faithful of a greater, and more happy one, from the spiritual Pharaoh, and his heavy-handed taskmasters, sin, and death eternal.

**V. 3. ***{Then assembled}* As John 11.47. shows the cause of this their convention; because the people more and more flocked to him daily; and Caiaphas counseled to destroy him, as if he could not otherwise be overcome than by death; in which was indeed the most glorious victory of Christ and the elect: the general advice was, not at the feast, lest the people should tumult; yet here it appears, that nothing was disposed by them, but by the divine hand of God; no, not so much as the time of his passion: they who proposed only to kill Jesus, judged another time more seasonable, but God, who appointed hereby both to redeem the elect, and to strengthen their faith concerning the same, appointed this very time for Christ’s passion.

*{Caiaphas}* See on Luke 3.2.

**V. 4. ***{by subtlety}* They durst not take him by force for fear of the people. *δόλῳ*, by some treachery, not openly and by force of arms, but secretly, the people not knowing, nor Jesus (as they conceived) suspecting any such thing, they did not now consult and advise whether they should apprehend and kill him, but might do it so, as not to be seen in it.

*{and kill him}* By the power and authority of the Roman president, which they thought their safest way, Acts 3.16.

**V. 5. ***{Not on the feast day}* That is, of the Passover: for that must be spent in holy duties: after that the Galileans to whom he is so much known, will be gone home again: this resolution they changed, when Judas presented them a sooner opportunity, by his offering to deliver Christ unto them.

**V. 6. ***{Now, when Jesus}* Mark 14.3. John 11.1. This story was before the assembly of the priests; for it was six days before the Passover, John 12.1. and the assembly was but two days before, verse 1, 3. but it is set after, because it was the occasion of Judas repairing unto the priests.

*{of Simon the leper}* Who had been a leper; if he had been so still, Christ would not have eaten with him, contrary to the law: it may be he had been healed by Christ, and now made him a feast, as John 12.2. and as Matthew did after his calling to be an apostle. Mark 2.14, 15. Luke 5.29. John names the woman who anointed our Savior’s feet, which Matthew and Mark omit, and these mention the name of the man, which John omits; John says *she anointed his feet*, Matthew and Mark say *the head*: both were anointed; for the ointment being so abundantly poured on his head, some ran down to his feet, when he stood up, or rose up from table; or she might successively anoint both his head and feet. See on John 12.3. See Psalm 133.2.

**V. 7. ***{a woman}* Mary, sister of Lazarus, John 12.3. Of divers who anointed Christ, see *Is. Causab. exerc. 14. n. 17. & ib. n. 26.*

*{an alabaster box}* These boxes kept ointment without corruption, as some say. See hereof *Plin. N. hist. lib. 36.8. and lib. 13.2.*

*{of very precious ointment}* *μύρου* *βαρυτίμου*, ointment of a heavy price, that is, *πολυτίμου*. So the Syriac *סגי* *דמיא* *saggi demio*, of great price. See verse 9.

*{on his head}* As they used to do to welcome their friends, and chief guests, Luke 7.47. Psalm 23.5. See on verse 5.

**V. 8. ***{his disciples}* One of them, to wit, Judas who bare the bag, John 12.4, 6. See like phrase, chap. 27.41. with Luke 23.39. Or it may be others might second Judas in it.

*{this waste}* Unnecessary spending of precious ointment.

**V. 9. ***{and given to the poor}* Under a pretense of charity to the poor, he cloaks his own covetous, and thievish affection, who would have been filching some part thereof, for his own private use, had such a sum come into his hands. See John 12.6. Though the other disciples, if they seconded him therein, might speak simply, and mean well to the poor.

**V. 10. ***{why trouble ye the woman}* We ought not rashly to condemn all that we think not orderly or reasonably done, chap. 7.1.

*{she hath wrought a good work}* Christ, the most absolute pattern of temperance, defends this expense, in respect of the circumstances, not to draw it into a common example of waste: doubtless Mary was moved to this extraordinary work, by a secret instinct of the Spirit of God, making her confident to perform this office: Christ’s testimony was a sufficient assurance to her, of God’s acceptance of her work, though the perverse condemned it: never any good wanted some adversary to calumniate it: let the impious world (which likes nothing, but its own) use their liberty of rash judgment; it shall be happy for us, if God appear for us. See Isaiah 50.7. 1 Cor. 4.2. Rom. 8.33.

**V. 11. ***{For ye have the poor}* Deut. 15.11. You shall always have occasion to do good to the poor: but this is the least office of love and piety, wherein she can show her gratitude to me; who shall very shortly be taken from you.

**V. 12. ***{for my burial}* For a preparation to my burial. Mark says, chap. 14.8. *she is come, aforehand, to anoint my body to the burying*: for this respect Christ accepted it, that in this sign, they might, as in the figure, learn, that his sepulcher should breathe out the sweet savor of life and salvation to the elect. See John 12.7. Also, anointing the dead (then in use, Mark 16.1. John 19.40.) was no idle ceremony, but a token of their hope and assurance of the resurrection from the dead.

**V. 13. ***{wheresoever this gospel}* The Syriac has, *this my gospel*: the Gospel in general, not only this part thereof: thus he confirmed what he had said before: Mary was so far from being blameworthy, that the memory of this act of piety, shall live to all ages: true and happy applause, is not from men, who many times mistake, but God who will set a just price on the saints’ works, however men undervalue them. See Rom. 2.29. 1 Cor. 4.5. 2 Cor. 8.18.

*{in the whole world}* This made much for the consolation, and confirmation of the disciples (that Christ here, not obscurely intimates their success in preaching the Gospel, not only in Judea, but all the known world) as also of us, whose calling and salvation Christ intended before, and in his passion.

*{there shall also this}* He gently taxes their rash censure of Mary’s fact; and for that they had not his future kingdom in a due esteem: as if he had said, you think it much which is thus bestowed on me; but my kingdom shall be glorious through the world (Isaiah 11.10.) you censure her love and beneficence; but the odor thereof shall not only fill this house of Bethany, but the whole world, in which her memory shall be honored.

*{for a memorial of her}* The memory of ought done for Christ, or any of his, God will not suffer to perish, Psalm 112.6. *the memory of the just is blessed*, Prov. 10.7.

**V. 14. ***{Then one of the twelve}* Mark 14.20. Luke 22.3. What society of men shall we find free from traitors, when the holy family of Jesus has a Judas?

*{Judas Iscariot}* See on chap. 10.4. and on Luke 22.3. and John 6.71.

*{went to the chief priests}* He undertook the betraying of his Master, not solicited by them; but offering himself to this employment. See Luke 22.4. He knew that they hated Christ, and would gladly destroy him, so that he should be surely welcome to them with this offer: so easily fall they into mutual compliance, who are led by one, and the same spirit: Satan was entered into Judas, John 13.27, 30. The Pharisees were filled with the devilish spirit of envy and malice, James 3.14, 15. Matt. 27.18. To them therefore he goes, confident of a market: of chief priests, see Mark 14.1. and chap. 27.1.

**V. 15. ***{What will ye give me, &c.?}* So mischievous a root of evil, is covetousness (1 Tim. 6.9, 10.) that it will easily set Christ himself to sale. This unhappy wretch will make up his thievish hope of gain, which he promised himself, or would have had for the precious ointment, out of the 68sale of his holy master: nor does he set any definite price; but asks an indefinite one; as one willing at any rate to part with his interest, puts the price to the buyers’ pleasure.

*{and I will deliver him}* This was Satan’s master-piece on Judas, whom he had as his liege-man: to make the traitor hang himself, was much to persuade a self-loving caitiff to, but the just judgment of God on the traitor: on Satan’s part principally evil, in that he did not sooner put him on to self-execution, which at last must be so; on Judas’ part, in all this action the only appearance of good (there is nothing good in sin, but the just punishment thereof) but to make himself a brand of hell-fire, the most unhappy, and most damnable traitor that ever breathed God’s air, by selling an holy Jesus (the inestimable, matchless price of our redemption) at so poor a price, sets off to admiration, the malice of Satan, and the impudent presumption of the covetous traitor. The sale of Joseph but the sellers’ conscience, with sad remorse at last. (See Gen. 42.21. and 44.16.) God will certainly find out all unrepented wickedness, specially such: they sold a brother, not the Lord’s anointed: Slaves are sold, not Lords. *Remember Lots wife*, says our Savior, Luke 17.32. remember Judas, you that think it lawful gain by any sale: take heed of covetousness, it will make you willing to betray Christ; and so not much more innocent, than if you did it.

*{thirty pieces of silver}* See chap. 17.9. So much as a slave was valued at; Exod. 21.32. See how mean account Judas makes of his Master.

**V. 16. ***{from that time}* From the time that he had contracted with the high-priests for the treason.

*{he sought opportunity}* Luke 22.6. Expresses it; that he was to deliver him *ἄτερ* *ὄχλου*, without the multitude, in their absence, or without stirring them, without tumult, when the multitude might not know of it, who were wont to follow him. Sometime when he was private, only with his disciples: which as Origen notes, he did by finding opportunity of night, and his being with his disciples only in the garden at Gethsemane, without the city; he greedily longed to perform his wicked undertaking, that he might have his money promised him, verse 15.

**V. 17. ***{the first day of the feast of unleavened bread}* That is, of the Paschal solemnity, during which no leavened bread might be had, Exod. 12.19, 20. to wit, on the fourteenth day of Nisan, on which, preparation was made; for the eating of the Paschal Lamb the night ensuing, Exod. 12.6, 18.

**V. 18. ***{to such a man}* They are taught how to find him, Mark 14.13, 14. Luke 22.10, 11.

*{my time is at hand}* The time of my death.

**V. 20. ***{When the even was come}* Mark 14.18. Luke 22.14. John 13.21. The Evangelist notes the time prescribed by the law to eat the Passover, Exod. 12.6. Levit. 23.5. Deut. 16.6. *thou shalt sacrifice the passover* *בערב* *bahareb*, 70. *ἑσπέρας*, *in the evening*, that is, between the two evenings, or before it was dark. *ערב* *barab*, properly signifies mixed; so here it may be spoken of the mixture of light and darkness; the end of the day, and beginning of night, in a doubtful twilight, to show us, how Christ our Passover fulfilled the law.

*{he sate down with the twelve}* *ἀνέκειτο*, it signified such a posture, as fitted that solemnity; so Aemilius Portus, the interpreter of Suidas gives *κεῖμαι*, *jaceo, or situs sum*; so Thucydides says, the city *κεῖσθαι* *καλῶς*, to be well situated; so is *ἀνακεῖσθαι* used also; some interpret this *ἀνέκειτο* here, *he supped or eat the Passover*, and the Syriac uses a word here *סמך* of the same value with the Hebrew *סמך*, *samach*, which in the first signification imports, *innixus fuit*, he leaned, (as here in the first institution on the staff which he was to have in his hand) and so also *incubuit*. But by the general consent of the learned in these rites, that posture mentioned Exod. 12.11. as the sprinkling of the lintels and door-posts with the blood of the Lamb, appertained only to that one night, in which Israel was to come out of Egypt in haste; and was not to be continued after their settling in Canaan; so that there appears not any necessity to conclude; that Christ administered the Passover standing, and afterwards sat down to the second course, or common supper, as if this *ἀνέκειτο* related only to that posture; some learned Divines are here of one opinion, and some of another.

*{with the twelve}* Here appears the admirable impudence of this blind traitor; who after he had sold his Lord, comes again to present his service; and to sit at table with him; possibly he hoped that Christ knew it not (though he had formerly seen so many experiments of Christ’s omniscience) or he might think, that for his admirable meekness, he would not reveal it; so presumptuous are wicked men; so merciful and long-suffering Christ, who well knowing Judas from the beginning, yet permitted him to communicate with him, and his holy Apostles: yea, says Augustine *ep. 163. Eleusio & Felici*, he suffered his betrayer, (who had already received his price) to remain amongst the innocent, *usque ad ultimum pacis osculum*; which (had there been any prejudice to the holy, by his society,) he would never have permitted.

**V. 21. ***{one of you shall betray me}* Seeing the impudence of Judas; who having conspired with his enemies, and contracted for the price of his blood, yet dared boldly present himself to this holy sacrament, he thought good to forewarn his disciples, by discovering the traitor; that by striking a terror into his conscience, he might lead him to repentance, or leave him inexcusable to the severity of God’s justice; *one of you* my disciples; this was to render, and declare the treason the more detestable; he could have named him at the first; but thus he revealed the treason, and concealed the traitor, until his incorrigible impudence enforced a detection of the person also.

**V. 22. ***{they were exceeding sorrowful}* That he must die now, that by treason, that by one of them, none but Judas knowing who the traitor was.

*{every one of them to say}* Or, *said each one of them*, verse 74.

*{is it I?}* Every one fearing, that himself might be within the compass of others’ suspicion: and desirous that Christ pronouncing for him, might before the rest of the disciples, acquit him of their jealousy.

**V. 23. ***{he that dippeth}* Or *has dipped*, Psalm 41.9. one of them that now eats with me, as Mark 14.20. Luke 22.21.

**V. 24. ***{The Son of man goeth}* Out of this world, or he shall be put to death.

*{as it is written of him}* His death with the remarkable circumstances thereof, and the means, by treason, was all foretold by the former Prophets, Luke 24.26, 44. Isaiah 53.4, 5, 7, 9, 12. Psal. 41.9.

*{but woe unto that man}* He cannot hurt me, but he will make himself eternally unhappy.

*{good for that man}* God will glorify his justice in punishing him, but it had been better for himself, that he had never had any being.

**V. 25. ***{which betrayed him}* Judas who awhile after betrayed him, and was now plotting it, verse 16.

*{thou hast said}* You have said the truth: compare verse 64. with Mark 14.62.

**V. 26. ***{as they were eating}* Before they removed from the table: for it is not likely, that Christ would mix the Lord’s Supper with the Passover: and it is said Luke 22.20. 1 Cor. 11.25. of the cup, that he took it after supper, that is, after the Paschal supper.

*{Jesus took bread}* 1 Cor. 11.23, 24.

*{blessed it}* Many Greek copies have *εὐχαριστήσας*, *giving thanks*. Christ minding forthwith to fulfill the promises of the Old Testament, institutes a new covenant; with new signs; and consecrates them with prayer to God, consisting, partly of praise and thanksgiving to God his Father, verse 27. and partly of petition for a blessing upon the elements, as Luke 9.16. and yet, the bread and wine are changed, not in nature, but in use: for they become undoubted tokens of Christ’s body and blood, by his own institution, that faith may find what to lay hold on, both in the word, and in the elements.

*{this is my body}* Either by a trope called *metonymia*, one name is put for another; the bread is called Christ’s body, because it is a sign thereof: or by a figurative enunciation, the bread is said to be his body, because it represents his body, truly and properly so termed; as the rock is said to have been Christ, because it represented Christ, 1 Cor. 10.4. both in effect come to one, the phrase being Sacramental, and of common use in speech of signs and resemblances, either sacred, or other: Gen. 17.13. and 40.18. and 41.26, 27. Exod. 12.11. Matth. 13.38. Revel. 17.9, 12, 15, 18. See on Luke 22.19.

**V. 27. ***{the cup}* To wit, with the wine in it, verse 29.

*{gave thanks}* As verse 26. the prayer which then he used, consisted, as before, of two parts; it is called sometimes by the name of the one part, a thanksgiving; as here; sometimes by the name of the other part, a blessing, as 1 Cor. 10.16.

*{drink ye all of it}* They then which take away the Cup from the people, do not according to Christ’s institution of the sacrament, to be received of all in both kinds, which they then strictly observed.

**V. 28. ***{this is my blood}* The wine in this cup is a seal of the covenant ratified in my blood, which shall be shed shortly, to procure new legacies for many. See Exod. 24.8. See here verse 26.

*{of the new Testament}* Whereby the inheritance of eternal life is given as a legacy to all true believers, or of the new covenant.

*{which is shed}* That shall shortly be shed, for his blood was not yet shed. See the like phrase, chap. 3.10. See on Luke 22.20.

*{for many}* Nor for you alone, but for many others. See John 17.20.

*{for the remission of sins}* To satisfy God’s justice for their sins, that in mercy, through the merits of Christ he may forgive them, who could make no satisfaction for themselves, Rom. 3.25, 26. Ephes. 1.7.

**V. 29. ***{I will not drink henceforth}* This is the last solemn 69meal, or feast, that I shall make with you on earth: but I shall converse with you after my resurrection for a time, and to all eternity in a new spiritual way, in heaven.

*{till I drink it new}* In a new kind, after my resurrection: the main thing here intended, is an expression of the heavenly joy.

**V. 30. ***{an hymn}* Or *Psalm*: when they had made an end of their other solemn singing; which, some think, was of these six Psalms, Psalms, Psal. 112. and 113. and 114. and 115. and 116. and 117. it may be, some other.

*{to the mount}* Towards it; for Gethsemane lay between the brook Kidron, and mount Olivet.

**V. 31. ***{Then}* About that time; for the words immediately following seem by Luke and John to be spoken in the house.

*{all ye}* Mark 14.27. John 16.32. my disciples who hitherto, without scandal adhered to me, professed and preached the Gospel, as on occasion I commanded, doing great works in my name, yet now you will be offended because of me, and those things which you shall see me suffering: you will forsake me, and fly for fear, and diffidence in that I have told you concerning my resurrection from the dead.

*{shall be offended}* See on chap. 11.6.

*{for it is written}* That shall not fail which is written of me: it was such a speech, as may be proverbially used: when the shepherd is smitten, the sheep are scattered.

*{I will smite the shepherd}* Zech. 13.7. Christ here (as John 10.) compares himself to a shepherd, in whose passion the disciples should be scattered for fear.

**V. 32. ***{I will go before you}* This he says to comfort them; his smiting should be but for a short time; but he should quickly go before them again, like a shepherd before his flock, Mark 14.28. and 16.7.

**V. 33. ***{yet will I never be offended}* Peter trusted overmuch in his own strength, which is a dangerous confidence.

**V. 34. ***{before the cock crow}* John 13.38. his morning crow to break of day, Mark 13.35. or *crow thrice*, as Mark 14.30. for Peter denied him once before the first cock-crowing, and thrice before the second, Mark 14.68, 72.

*{thou shalt deny me}* You that are now more forward in promising, than the rest, shall yet not only be offended at me, but also utterly deny me, that ever you knew me.

**V. 35. ***{Though I should die with thee}* Peter meant no less: but was not yet well acquainted with his own infirmity.

*{Likewise also said all the disciples}* Lest they should seem inferior to Peter in love and duty, in affection or fidelity: they equal him in words, though not altogether in their failing: they were all warned as well as Peter, in the general of their being scattered, yet were thus confident; therefore Christ leaves them to the experience of their own disability.

**V. 36. ***{then cometh Jesus}* Mark 14.32. Luke 22.39.

*{Gethsemane}* The valley of fatness, near mount Olivet; like that, by Lebanon, Isaiah 28.1, 4. or an oil-press, as some interpret the name; or the pasture of butter, as others; howsoever the name seems to import a rich and fat soil. See Genes. 27.28, 29. Christ is not taken in the city, lest the people should rescue him: not in the daytime; but in the night, and in a solitary place, to which he was retired.

*{sit ye here}* See the like, Gen. 22.5.

**V. 37. ***{Peter and the two sons of Zebedee}* who had been in private with him, to see his divine power and glory, (to arm them against any scandal to be taken at his sufferings) to wit, at the raising of Jairus’ daughter from the dead, chap. 9. and in his transfiguration on the mount, chap. 17.

*{very heavy}* This shows the truth of his human nature; in that he feared death, and felt so much sorrow at the approach thereof; but the reason chiefly was, because it brought with it a deep sense of his Father’s wrath, against sin, and sinners; for whom he was now to suffer, and become a ransom.

**V. 38. ***{My soul is exceeding sorrowful}* Thus he expresses the grievousness of his approaching passion for sinners, in whose place he stood a surety, that they might afterward resent his admirable love to the elect, and preach the same to the edifying of others: as also to stir them up to an holy sympathy.

*{and watch with me}* He says not, watch and pray for me, as if he had need of their prayers, or mediation; but to express a common danger (whereof he had warned them verse 31.) lest themselves should enter into temptation, which he saw now imminent over them.

**V. 39. ***{fell on his face}* Mark says, he prostrated himself, in effect it is the same; Luke says, he kneeled on the earth, it may be so first, and after prostrated; all is to express a vehement and ardent humility in prayer; for which the most humble posture is best, as to acknowledge God, the Lord of body and soul; so is it the more apt to stir up ourselves and others to fervency of spirit, which is the life of prayer: Look on Jesus in this posture, you unreverend professors of faith in him; how wretched an impudence do you express, while with unbended knees, and zealless hearts, you petition him, now sitting at the right hand and glory of God the Father, carelessly sitting? The Lord Jesus in the days of his flesh kneeled, and prostrated himself on the earth, and are you too good to bend the knee to him in prayer, who descended so low to save you?

*{let this cup pass from me}* A kind of expression which the Hebrews used, to signify God’s wrath, and man’s afflictions in the execution thereof, under the similitude of a cup. See chap. 20.22. See on Luke 22.42. he says, *if it be possible*; that is, as Augustine *lib. 3. de Consens. Evang. c. 4.* interprets; if it be your holy will: the same *epist. 121. Probae* notes; that God gives to some in anger, and denies others in mercy: he gave the Israelites quails, Numb. 11. he gave the devil that which he desired and begged, leave to enter into the swine (Luke 8.32.) These things are written, lest any should think highly of himself, that he is heard, in that which it had been good for him not to have obtained: and lest any should deject himself, or despair of God’s mercy, if he be not heard in something which he petitions for; *quo accipiendo affligatur atrocius vel à prosperitate corrumpatur, penitus evertatur*, ib. c. 14. in receiving whereof he should be more heavily afflicted, or (corrupted by prosperity) quite overthrown: we know not what to pray as we ought. Rom. 8.26. the safest way is to learn of our Savior, *not as I will, but as thou wilt*: not what I would in my human infirmity, but what you will, and know best in your divine will, and wisdom.

**V. 40. ***{findeth them asleep}* Here is an example of man’s infirmity, in duties most important; specially in sorrow, or any other vehement perturbation of mind. See on Luke 22.45.

*{and said unto Peter}* Who had promised and protested so much before: with him he reproves the rest: to show the most confident, how unable they are, of themselves, to resist temptation; and consequently, what need they have to watch and pray.

*{one hour}* If you cannot watch with me one hour, how much less would you be able (as you professed) to suffer death for, or with me?

**V. 41. ***{Watch and pray}* See chap. 24.42. This watchfulness is not so much from sleep, as carnal security; but now he bids them take heed of both these; neither is it enough to watch to discover the dangerous approaches of the enemy, but we must also implore the merciful assistance of the Lord, that we may be able to withstand.

*{that ye enter not into temptation}* That you be not tempted: because they were drowsy in his great conflict, he awakens them to a sense of their own hazard: as if he had said, except you watch and pray (however confident you are of your own strength) a fearful storm of temptations will endanger your faith: this is that he said to Peter, *Satan hath desired to winnow you*. Luke 22.31. therefore Peter afterwards, thus endeavored to strengthen his brethren: *be sober and watch*, &c. 1 Pet. 5.8. he says not you shall be overcome; but shows the means by them to be used, that they may not be overcome, which is not in their own strength, but God’s assistance.

*{the spirit indeed is willing}* You have through God’s grace, a good will; but the corruption of flesh and blood much hinders; by spirit, he means that affection which was guided by the holy Ghost, the regenerate part: by flesh, he means the remainder of corrupted nature: he remembers them of the one, lest they should despair; and of the other, lest they should presume on their own ability, and so be overcome in their security; these two Paul expresses. Rom. 7.18, 23.

**V. 42. ***{If this cup}* Since this cup may not be avoided. See on verse 39.

*{thy will be done}* This is the voice of one resting on God’s will. See chap. 6.10. Acts 21.14. Thus Christ taught us, both by precept and example, to submit our wills and desires, to God’s good pleasure: this he says, not that his will differed, or was contrary to the will of his Father, for as Gregor. Nazianzen says, *orat. 36.* *ὡς* *μία* *θεότης*, *οὕτω* *ἡ* *βούλησις*, as the Godhead is one, so also is the will of God; but for that the will of the assumed nature was subject to human infirmity, without sin; which therefore (even in that it would not have suffered, as man) yet submitted itself to the will of God, by that abundance of the spirit which dwelt in him bodily. Colos. 2.9. whereby he also obeyed to the death. Phil. 2.8. willingly: for, as Prosper notes (*sentent. ex Augustino*) *nemo invitus bene facit, etiam si bonum est quod facit*: no man unwillingly does well, though that be good which he does.

**V. 43. ***{and found them asleep again}* Being oppressed with sorrow, as Luke expresses it: this shows the necessity we have to watch and pray, seeing our very dangers make us dull and unmindful to call on God: so both in joy and sorrow, Satan lays his snares: so frail are we after many warnings, in the midst of greatest hazards we are apt to forget ourselves.

**V. 44. ***{prayed the third time}* As 2 Cor. 12.8. Leaving us an example of perseverance in prayer.

*{saying the same words}* Which yet, nothing detracted either from the substance, or efficacy of his prayer: this was no vain repetition (which he forbade, Chap. 6.7.) but an invincible, and unwearied fervency of spirit. See Luke 22.44.

**V. 45. ***{Sleep on now}* Infirm flesh and blood is still like itself, hardly amended by any admonitions: therefore he tells them, *you may sleep now*; or you will have little list to sleep anon, whether I awake you or no, your enemies will awake you: though my admonitions have not yet: this is God’s usual method, where words prevail not, afflictions must.

*{into the hands of sinners}* To the power of the Roman soldiers. See Acts 2.23. Gal. 2.15. So God still afflicts and corrects his children by the wicked; yea, and by Satan himself. Rev. 2.10.

**V. 46. ***{Rise, let us be gone}* Let us go meet them: Christ offered himself willingly to be taken, that in suffering willingly, he might make satisfaction for the willful fall of man: this shows the effect of his prayers, and a new supply of strength, and his victory over that fear and infirmity of flesh and blood, with which he had wrestled; he now willingly meeting with his enemies, and his passion, and offering up himself a voluntary oblation (as was prefigured. Exod. 35.5, 21, 29.) so says he, not let us fly, but let us go hence. John 14.31. that is, to meet with the armed band, which he knew were then coming to take him.

**V. 47. ***{While he yet spake}* Mark 14.43. Luke 22.47. John 18.3.

*{Judas one of the twelve came}* For he was their leader and captain, of those who came to take Christ, for he only of them all, knew the place which Jesus frequented, John 18.1, 2. Luke 22.47. he was awake to betray Christ, when the rest were asleep: it is the misery of secure flesh and blood; the righteous (too securely measuring others by their own shadows, and confident in the goodness of their cause) are not so vigilant for good, as the wicked are for evil: so good men sleep while thieves and murderers watch to pillage and destroy them: the willing spirit is infirm, while sleepless malice is patient of any labor or hardship to accomplish its design.

*{with him a great multitude}* He had the place of an Apostle, to lead men to the faith of Christ; but he leads them to destroy Christ; what is, or ever was worse, than the best things corrupted? you see whose disciples they are, who lead multitudes, against Christ and his truth.

*{with swords and staves}* To take him by force, as they used to apprehend malefactors, verse 55. and to keep him from rescue.

**V. 48. ***{gave them a sign}* Because many of them knew him not, and others might be mistaken in the night.

*{whomsoever I shall kiss}* In effect he says, whomsoever I shall familiarly salute: for in such salutes, they were wont to kiss. 1 Sam. 10.1. & 20.41. 2 Sam. 14.33. & 15.5. & 19.39. Exod. 18.7. Acts 20.37. 2 Sam. 20.9. Luke 7.45. Rom. 16. 1 Cor. 16.20. 1 Thess. 5.26. 1 Pet. 5.14. So that Judas is not said to have named him, but to give a sign of friendship, that he might under that semblance play the traitor; how vainly impudent are sinners, hoping to impose on God? Jesus foresaw all this, and discovered it.

**V. 49. ***{haile Master}* *χαῖρε* *ῥαββί*; it was an usual form of salutation which the Hebrews expressed by *שלום* *לך* *shalom lecha* or *שלום* *לך* *shalom lach*. Jud. 6.23. & 19.20. 1 Sam. 25.6. 1 Chron. 12.18. Exod. 18.7. *they asked each other* *לשלום* *to peace*, or *of peace*, and each other’s welfare; the 70. give it *καὶ* *ἠσπάσαντο* *ἀλλήλους*; they saluted each other: the Greek, here translated to the letter, signifies *rejoyce*: which the Latins interpret, *salve*, as we say, *God save you*; so this traitor, in words wishes Christ’s prosperity, joy, and safety, as in general, so possibly he would be understood concerning the present danger of his enemies; as if he had been none of their company: so impudent is he, that he blushes not to lie against so evident a truth; of him may be said that is written, Psal. 55.21. *The words of his mouth were smoother than butter, but war was in his heart*—and he calls him Master, whose Majesty he not only acknowledges not, but openly contemns, by leading up an armed company to apprehend him as a malefactor.

**V. 50. ***{Friend wherefore art thou come?}* He upbraids not his perfidiousness, but with all mildness invites him to repentance: thus styling him, he says not what he was, but what he should have been; or what he seemed, but was not: nor asks he the cause of his coming, as not knowing it, for he had before detected him; but as openly reproving him; as if he had said, you have confederated with my enemies to destroy me; you have forsaken my service, and the fellowship of these my Disciples; wherefore come you to us again? what have you to do here? by these like questions, he would recall the traitor to confer with his own conscience: I know you well, and your intention; consider, and be ashamed of your treason, by so much more detestable, by how much more veiled with semblance of love.

*{laid hands on Jesus}* God so permitting, that thereby the work of our redemption might be accomplished in his death and passion: he is taken, that we may be delivered.

**V. 51. ***{One of them}* Namely Peter; and the servant’s name was Malchus, whose right ear was cut off. Joh. 18.10.

*{drew his sword}* Misunderstanding Christ’s words, Luke 22.36, 38.

**V. 52. ***{Put up again thy sword}* You are no Magistrate to right me, nor am I to be defended by the sword, who could have obtained twelve legions, an army of Angels to fight for me and rescue me, verse 53. but he came into the world not to fight, but to die for sinners, if Christ would not have Peter, being a private man, strike with a sword in defense of his own sacred person wronged and so highly injured, what do they who use the sword in unjust quarrels, and without just calling?

*{for all they}* Gen. 9.6. Rev. 13.10. He means all they who kill, being not called thereto by the ordinance of God, either by a general warrant, as the Magistrate; or some special command from God, as 2 Kings 9.7. all shall perish who smite for private revenge: he speaks not of the public Magistrate, who bears not the sword in vain; nor does he so stint the punishment of murderers to the sword, as if none perished otherwise: the main is to show, that God, who has determined to maintain human societies, will commonly make them exemplary in this life, who are destructive to others: rarely does a murderer go unpunished here. See Psal. 55.23.

**V. 53. ***{Thinkest thou}* This is the second reason whereby Christ represses Peter’s endeavor so to defend him: he had no need of man’s defense, who was now to die for man’s redemption: cannot I destroy my enemies by my word which gave them life? have I not innumerable companies of Angels ready to defend me, if I would give them commission so to do? See Augustin. *Honorat. epist. 120. c. 11.*

*{twelve legions}* The Roman army consisted ordinarily of twelve legions: such an army of Angels could Christ have had: or it is a definite number for an indefinite; more than twelve, imports an infinite number. See Dan. 7.10. 2 Kings 19.35. Isa. 37.36. he here opposes Angels, to men; many to few; heavenly to earthly; the invincible to the infirm; where these charge, an host is not able to invest one of them. See on Luke 8.30.

**V. 54. ***{But how then}* The reason why I do not now pray to God for Angels to defend me, is because the Scripture has foretold my death for man’s salvation; not that I doubt of speeding, were it necessary, and according to my Father’s will.

*{that thus it must be}* Isaiah 53.10. As some say, *necessitate consequentiae, non consequentis*, in necessity of consequence (in reason; because God is true) not of the consequent (in fact because God is just, and his prescience according to which he revealed this in Scripture, *nihil ponit in esse*: his foreseeing of sin, is no cause thereof) for the Jews who thus apprehend, and crucify Christ, are not hereby excused; which they must have been, had that they did been of simple, inevitable, necessity, not voluntary: there is no necessity to sin, nor sin where there is an absolute necessity: for God cannot be the author of any sin: as there is no sin in him, so neither is there any of him. Fulgent. See Luke 24.26, 44.

**V. 55. ***{Are ye come out}* He expostulates with them, who came with so much warlike provision against him; what need you arm so many against one unarmed man? wickedness is ever cowardly and diffident.

*{as against a thief}* See on verse 47. as if so be you were to apprehend a thief, or some desperate malefactor, who knowing his offense to be capital and unpardonable, would rather fight desperately, than yield himself to the hand of justice.

**V. 56. ***{That the Scriptures}* Lam. 4.20. And all the rest which foretold of Christ’s sufferings.

*{Then all his disciples forsook him}* As he had foretold, verse 31.

**V. 57. ***{And they that}* See on John 18.2. Mark 14.54.

*{to Caiaphas}* After that he had been brought to Annas. See on John 18.13. and on Luke 22.60.

**V. 58. ***{But Peter followed him}* This is set down to make way to the story of Peter’s denying him. verse 69. &c.

*{afar off}* As one not belonging to him. Here was fear so mixed with love, that follow he would, but not so near as to come into evident danger, if they should apprehend him also as one of Jesus’ disciples.

*{unto the high-priests palace}* the high-priest’s house is also mentioned, as verse 3. to the evidence of the history. Palaces are houses of Princes; such were the high-priests for their authority and dignity among the people of Israel. How Peter was admitted into this place, we read John 18.15. at first as a stranger there, he stood at the door without, until the other disciple (not John the Evangelist, as some conjecture but some other believer and secret disciple of Christ, who not being known to be such, might more safely be there) who was known to the high-priest, spoke to the keeper of the door and brought in Peter.

*{sate with the servants}* who waited there to attend their masters coming out of the inward place, where they were busy in examining Jesus; they had dismissed the Roman soldiers 71when they had brought Jesus bound to the High Priest, and now it seems their own servants waited on them in the night; Peter puts himself into their company, that he might (as it is probable he hoped) without suspect hear and see the event, what they would do to Jesus; if he were put to death, that he might shift away; if he were only scourged and so dismissed, then he would be ready at hand to attend him: or however, if Jesus by his Divine power should deliver himself, Peter might suppose, that he would also deliver him from danger. Now it is probable, that Peter did not thus far engage himself out of any vain curiosity, but out of love and zeal to the person, and safety of his Master, yet was it incautiously done to put himself into the way of such dangerous temptation whereof Christ had before warned him and the rest, verse 31, 34. *all ye shall be offended, because of me this night*, &c. and to confident Peter he said, *this night before the Cock crow, thou shalt deny me thrice*.

**V. 59. ***{Sought false witness}* They were all resolved to destroy Christ, and therefore regard neither equity nor truth; but lay hold of the most impious means to compass their design: what greater injustice, than for Judges to suborn false witnesses? malice is ever diligent, but commonly unjust.

**V. 60. ***{Yet found they none}* That could accuse him of any evil; or whose witness agreed, Mark 14.56. and therefore could they not legally condemn him. See Deut. 17.6. Thus God did every way show forth his Son’s innocence; that it might appear, that he died not for himself, but us.

*{two false witnesses}* For one would not serve, Deut. 19.15. they, are called false witnesses, for depraving, and misreporting Christ’s words and meaning.

**V. 61. ***{This fellow}* *οὗτος*; *This man*. See on Chapter 12.24.

*{I am able to destroy}* Though that had been true if he had said it, it was false that he did say so; justly might he say, *every day they wrest my words; all their thoughts are against me for evil*, Psal. 56.5. he spoke concerning the Temple of his body, which when they should destroy, he would raise it up in three days John 2.16. they to render him odious to the people, (overmuch addicted to, and trusting in the dignity of their Temple) testify that he said, that he would raise up the material Temple in three days: we need not wonder that so many false, and injurious constructions are made of our words; thus they used Christ who could not err.

**V. 62. ***{Answerest thou nothing}* Seeing their depositions insufficient, he endeavors to draw something from Christ himself.

**V. 63. ***{But Jesus held his peace}* Seeing it was to no end to speak, the sentence being given, before any witness was examined.

*{I adjure thee}* So 1 Kings, 22.16. *ἐξορκίζω*; the meaning is, upon your oath, and by the authority of a Judge; in the Name of God, I command you: In the Primitive Church, they were called Exorcists, who having the gift of Miracles, cast out Devils, by calling on the Name of Christ. See on Acts 19.13.

*{that thou tell us whether thou be the Christ}* This he asks, not intending to believe the Truth, but to destroy Christ; thinking hereby to ensnare him, if he confessed it, as a blasphemer; if he denied it, as an impostor, and deceiver of the people.

**V. 64. ***{Thou hast said}* See on chap. 27.11. It is an ingenuous confession of Truth, which Mark more expressly histories; *he said I am*.

*{nevertheless}* Or *furthermore*.

*{I say unto you}* I tell you, though you will not believe what I now say; but hereafter shall, when you see it come to pass.

*{hereafter ye shall see}* Chapter 16.27. At my second coming; lest they should conclude, that Christ could not be the Son of God, whom they had convented before them, and bound as a malefactor; he who could have converted them by more evident arguments (the time of his birth; the Scepter departed from Judah, the place, Bethlehem; the lineage, or the seed of David, the manner, of a Virgin; by his Doctrine, which he preached to the poor; by his miracles, &c. would propose (for present) two more obscure Arguments: one from his sitting at God’s right hand, which they should once see: the other from his coming in the Clouds of heaven, to judgment: as if he said, you cannot deny him to be the Messiah and Son of the living God whom you shall see coming to judge you, whom you shall see sitting at God’s right hand: I tell you this now, to leave you unexcusable then, if you will not now believe.

*{at the right hand of power}* In the highest honor with God. Luke 22.69. *of the power of God*, that is, of the divine Majesty, Hebr. 1.3. & 8.1. or of God Almighty, Mark 16.19. Acts 7.55. Rom. 8.34. Ephes. 1.20. Coloss. 3.1.

*{in the clouds of heaven}* See on Chap. 24.30.

**V. 65. ***{The high priest rent his clothes}* As if his heart were ready to break, for zeal to God’s Honor: so the Jews used to do at blasphemy. 2 Kings 18.37. & 19.1. So the ancient heathens also did in their mourning.

*{what further need have we of witnesses?}* To prove that this man ought to die. He pretended that Christ blasphemed, in saying, that they should see him sitting on God’s right hand, and coming in the Clouds of Heaven; which because they all heard, he takes it for granted, that they needed no other witness to condemn him (as worthy by their Law to die) but his own public confession, which they judged blasphemy.

**V. 66. ***{What think ye?}* What punishment do you think him worthy of? he would have Jesus condemned by a common consent, and sentence of all: and asks that which he full well knew; for they had already condemned him before his trial, and resolved to destroy him, whatsoever he should or could allege in his own just defense. Sinners would have all share with them in their sin, hoping to find some patronage in multitudes with them consenting in wickedness.

**V. 67. ***{Then did they spit in his face}* A token of contempt and abhorring, Numbers 12.14. Isai. 50.6. See also Deut. 25.9. Job 30.10. Luke 18.32.

*{and buffeted him}* Isaia. 50.6.

*{palms of their hands}* See Chap. 5.39. Mark 14.65. John 18.22. and 19.3. The word in the Greek, is, *ἐῤῥάπισαν*, which signifies to beat with rods, (as also does *ῥαβδίζω*) with staff or club, Acts 16.22. we in a more general expression, give *ῥαβδίζειν*, to beat or smite; as also does the Syriac here *מחין* *הוו* *לה* *machin havu leh, caedebant eum*, they beat or struck him: which Mark 14.65. gives by *κολαφίζειν*, to buffet him: ’tis most likely they did both buffet and switch him, for Mark uses both these words.

**V. 68. ***{Prophesy unto us}* This they required after they had covered his face, Mark 14.65. as if they had said, by this we will try you; if you are so much as a Prophet, tell us now, who smote you?

**V. 69. ***{Now Peter}* Mark 14.66. Luke 22.55. John 18.25. After that the Evangelist had showed what hitherto became of Christ; he inserts the relation of that which befell Peter, and was done by him, of whose entrance into the High Priest’s house, he spoke before, verse 58. in all which we have an eminent example of the best men’s infirmity (a grave document of humility, vigilance and cautiousness to all, that they beware they fall not) as also of God’s infinite mercy to the penitent, to the comfort of wounded Consciences, in that he gives repentance, and forgives the penitent.

*{sate without in the Palace}* Remote from the Consistory of the High Priest; but some think in the same room, because he went out after, verse 71. but so might he do from the common Hall, where the servants and multitude were. This word *ἔξω*, without, must not here be understood of some place out of the House, but to import that he was not in the upper or inward part thereof, where Caiaphas and his assistants were examining Jesus, but in an inferior part of the House, he was, *ἐν* *τῇ* *αὐλῇ* *κάτω*, Mark 14.66. beneath in the Palace, Luke 22.55. we give the same word in the Hall; in the midst whereof, they had kindled a fire, at which Peter sat and warmed himself among the company.

*{Jesus of Galilee}* Either for distinction sake, because there were many of that name, Acts 13.6. Col. 4.10. or in way of contempt. See John 7.50.

**V. 70. ***{I know not what thou sayest}* I neither know him, nor well understand what you mean hereby. He answers not directly to the Damsel’s objection, I was not with him; unwilling to deny Christ, and not willing to come into danger, he dissembled: seldom is the Truth of most high concernment confessed before the wicked, without great danger, but never is Christ denied without the greatest.

**V. 71. ***{This fellow}* *οὗτος*, this Man. See on Chapter, 12.14.

**V. 72. ***{I doe not know the Man}* Now he more plainly denied Christ, than Verse 69. sins have speedy growths.

**V. 73. ***{Thy speech bewrayeth thee}* Coming nearer to the Syriac and Samaritan Dialect, than that of Jerusalem. See Judges 12.6.

**V. 74. ***{Then began he to curse}* Mark by what degrees sin springs up, if once admitted to root: First, he denies his Master: then forswears him: Thirdly, curses himself, if he knew him; or imprecates some fearful mischief to come upon himself, if he knew him: See here the wretched fruit of confidence in ourselves: it is a sad document hence to be learned; *let him that thinketh he stands, take heed lest he fall*.

**V. 75. ***{And Peter remembered}* Put in mind of Christ’s words, by the crowing of the Cock; and Christ’s looking back upon him, Luke 22.61.

*{before the cock crow}* See on verse 34.

*{he went out}* Either lest he should be endangered by discovery, or tempted again, or that he might weep privately. See Genesis 43.29. passionate grief loves solitude;

*{and wept bitterly}* The falls of God’s servants humble them, and cause in them a godly sorrow to repentance, never to be repented of, and self-loathing for their doing that which displeases God. See Ezekiel 6.9. See on Luke 22.62. So the sins of the regenerate, as those Canaanites, which should have been utterly destroyed by Israel; which not being done, they prove, as Joshua said, snares and traps unto us, scourges in our sides, and thorns in our eyes, Joshua 23.13. but though we cannot so overcome them in this frail state of mortality, yet by God’s mercy, humbling us by them, and giving us repentance for them, they are as those Gibeonites, Josh. 9.23. put under tribute, and made hewers of wood, and drawers of water for the House of God; that is, full of zeal and matter of repentance to salvation, 2 Cor. 7.11. so Peter wept bitterly, and was after more zealous, even to death.

## CHAPTER 27

**Verse 1. ***{When the morning was come}* As soon as it was day, Luke 22.66. they so much desired Christ’s death, that they could not sleep that night. Chapter 26.47. See Prov. 4.16. malice is vigilant as the Devil, and industrious to do mischief.

*{all the chief Priests}* Mark 15.1. Luke 22.66. John 18.28. By all the chief Priests, understand the High Priest, his surrogate, and those of the Sanhedrim: where the High Priest and his Sagan, or second High Priest sat chief. See 2 King. 23.4. for though the High Priest did not always sit in that Court in person, yet in matters relating to the Civil and Ecclesiastical interest (as they would have this cause of Christ to do) wherein both Consistories assembled, (as here, *all the chief Priests and Elders of the people tooke counsell against Jesus*) there it is most likely that the High Priest and his Counsel, would meet with the civil Consistory of seventy Judges: it is Satan’s mischievous policy to unite conspirators against Christ, so, as that none of them will be missing to an impious convention and enterprise, whereas the same finds his chief advantage against Truth by dividing the Professors thereof, or by making them cold, and secure on the goodness of their cause.

*{tooke counsell}* Assembled themselves to advise.

*{to put him to death}* They blasphemously accused him of blasphemy: (it is usual for the wicked to cast their own faults upon the guiltless) but fearing that Pilate would not put him to death for that, and themselves had no power (John 18.31.) they now resolve to endite him of treason and sedition, as appears in the charge, John 19.12.

**V. 2. ***{When they had bound him}* They put on him all appearances of a malefactor: so they lead the Holy Jesus bound to Pilate; the Lord of Life, to be tried as a Delinquent, for his life.

*{delivered him to Pilate}* That they might destroy him by the abused hand of justice: for to have done it privily, would have been infamous and dangerous to the actors as murder uses to be: and in a tumultuous way, would have been apparently dangerous to the liberties of the City, and privileges of State. See Luke 24.20. But God in his incomprehensible wisdom, and love to mankind, had appointed, that for the work of our redemption, he should die that death, which was then most infamous; that thereby he might descend as low by humility, and dishonor, shame and infamy for man’s repair, as man’s ambition had aimed high to his own ruin, and destruction: there man affected to be as God: here Christ God and Man, suffers the worst of men.

**V. 3. ***{When he saw that he was condemned}* By the Consistory of the Priests, whose Sentence he now thought would take effect; which it may be, fell out besides his expectation, who possibly might dream that his Master would now have escaped their hands by Miracle, as at other times he had done, Luke 4.29, 30. John 10.39. and 8.59.

*{repented himself}* Was sorry that he had done it; this was not any effect of Faith, or love to God, such as is a condition of pardon, Acts 2.38. Matth. 9.13. Mark 1.4. Luke 24.47. Acts 11.18. 2 Corinth. 7.9. 2 Pet. 3.9. but of desperate and impatient sorrow, proceeding of self-love, and fear of that which his conscience told him, he must suffer; not that he had offended God, or could hope and truly believe in his mercy: I believe (says Ambros. *l. 2. de paen. c. 5.* if that Book be his) that even Judas might not have been excluded from pardon, by so great mercy of God, *si paenitentiam non apud Judaeos, sed apud Christum egisset*: true repentance is in hope of pardon by Christ, to turn unto God, in newness of life.

*{and brought again the thirty pieces}* To restore that which is unjustly taken away, is such a necessary practice, as that there can be no true repentance without it: but this restitution to the Priests, nothing advantaged Judas, who neither believed, nor turned unto the Lord: the thirty pieces, were thirty Shekels, of which, See on Nehemiah, 5.15.

**V. 4. ***{I have sinned}* Here was contrition, confession to the Priest, and as much satisfaction as Judas could make them; yet no remission: he had sinned against Christ, and the Priests could not forgive him.

*{innocent blood}* Thus he that betrayed him, acknowledged his innocence; which serves to acquit Jesus, and to condemn Judas: he confessed, *quasi evitasse sit paenam, praevenisse sententiam*. Cypr. *lib. 2. Ep. 8.* as if to prevent the sentence, were to avoid the punishment; he says innocent blood, that is an innocent man: thus God would wound the consciences, both of Judas and the impious high Priests.

*{what is that to us?}* Such is their hardness of heart; they could not say he was guilty of any thing, wherewith they could charge him, (though they who gave money to betray him, would have the world believe, that they were guiltless) they lay all on Judas; yet they still proceed to kill the Lord of Life.

*{see thou to that}* Thus audaciously do they transmit their sin to others, and in their hardness of Heart and impenitence desist not from acting what they knew they had wickedly designed; but against their own Consciences, precipitately go on.

**V. 5. ***{He cast down the pieces}* To show his utter dislike of his own fact; perhaps to persuade them to desist, and not to present him to Pilate to be condemned.

*{in the Temple}* In the place where the Elders used to sit.

*{and departed}* Acts 1.18. Finding no comfort among these betrayers of souls.

*{and hanged himself}* The word here used, is *ἀπήγξατο*, from whence, a learned Author starts this useless Paradox, that Judas died of melancholy, or suffocation by some such humor, for which many things are alleged to very little purpose; and were it not to as little, to be expensive of time and paper in refutation, the invalidity of the Arguments, on which it seems grounded, might easily be showed. Suidas renders the word, *ἀπήγξατο* by *βρόχον* *ἧψε*; his Interpreter Aemilius Portus; thus, *laqueum nexuit quo se strangulavit, strangulatus est, laqueo vitam finivit*; the value of *ἄγχω*, *ἄγχομαι*, *ἀπάγχω*, *ἀπάγχομαι*, in the proper and common uses easily appears in every Lexicon; and sufficiently may in the ordinary acceptation of these words by several Authors: So that where Physicians use them to express suffocation by Melancholy, &c. they translate them from their proper sense, to a figurative: concerning the Syriac word *חנק* by him cited: the learned in that tongue give it, *suffocavit strangulavit suspendit*, in its proper sense, metaphorically it also signifies *molestia affecit*, v. Shind. *Pentagl.* citing this very place in the Syriac *חנק* *נפשה* translates it *strangulavit seipsum*. So also that 2 Sam. 17.23. & *praecipit super domum suam* *ויחנק* Targ. *ואתחנק* & *strangulavit se*. So translates the Arab. here, *& abiit & suspendit se*, and he went and hanged himself. So Beza on *Act. 1.* says, it properly signifies to strangle, which he interprets *constrictâ gulâ necare*: that Judas and Ahithophel hanged themselves the ancient Fathers generally affirm from this Scripture, &c. He that betrayed our Savior, now condemns himself to Hell, Acts 1.24. not enduring to see his Master suffer, in whom he could not believe unto salvation: it is a miserable sanctuary against sorrow, which the desperate self-murderers find in death.

**V. 6. ***{It is not lawful, &c.}* To put them into the Treasury of the Temple: the hire of a whore might not be brought into it. Whence some observe, that no proceed of sin against the Moral Law, or the Ceremonial; was to be brought into their Treasury, but rather to be held execrable, and an abomination to the Lord, who required his Sanctuary to be reverenced, Levit. 19.30. See Deut. 23.18.

*{the price of blood}* They were not afraid to give money to shed blood, but to put it into the treasury afterward: they feared to defile their treasury, but not their souls. Hypocrites strain at gnats, and swallow camels, chap. 23.24.

**V. 7. ***{the Potters field}* The best whereof was taken off for the Potters’ use: thus even the wicked, unwittingly, and oftentimes unwillingly fulfill that Truth of God, which the Prophets have foretold.

*{to bury strangers in}* Men of another Religion, with whom the Jews would not be joined, no not in the grave; or possibly to bury those of their own Religion and Tribes, who coming out of other Nations to Jerusalem, died there, the price of Christ’s blood purchased a place of rest for us Gentiles, who were strangers and aliens to the Commonwealth of Israel, Ephesians 2.12. See Augustine *To. 4. de consens. evang. l. 3. cap. 7. fine.* where he excellently opens the mystery concerning those that sleep in Christ.

**V. 8. ***{The field of blood}* Acts 1.19. See note ib.

*{unto this day}* It had a name from this occasion, so it kept it, till this Gospel was written: Hereby God would have the death of his Son, and their ungrateful wickedness remembered.

**V. 9. ***{Then was fulfilled}* When the Priest bought the Potters’ field, 73with the thirty pieces of silver, which Judas had received for betraying Christ, and restored unto them again.

*{by Jeremiah the Prophet}* Seeing this prophecy is read in Zachariah, it may be it was delivered at first by Jeremiah, and after written by Zachariah, who in many things imitates him, hence the Hebrews say, the spirit of Jeremiah was in Zachariah: or it might be partly out of Jeremiah, and partly out of Zachariah; for Jeremiah also bought a field. Chap. 32. or Zachariah might (as many other) have two names.

*{and they took}* Zach. 11.13. The Evangelist does not follow the very words of the Prophet, but the meaning, and shows how this prophecy was fulfilled.

*{the price of him that was valued}* He was sold, and that at a small price, but *ἐξαγοράζει* *κόσμον*, he redeemed the world, with the great price of his sacred blood. See Greg. Nazianz. *Orat. 35.*

*{whom they of the children of Israel did value}* *Whom they bought of the children of Israel*.

**V. 10. ***{As the Lord appointed me}* These words run, as if they were spoken by the Prophet, to show that in Zachariah, was a type of that in Matthew.

**V. 11. ***{Art thou the King of the Jews?}* Because the Jewish Rulers had, with that accusation, given him up to Pilate, as one that affected, or challenged, the Crown of Israel.

*{thou sayest it}* As the thing is, though not in your sense, chap. 26.64. or, *dost thou say it*, John 18.34.

**V. 12. ***{He answered nothing}* To this their second accusation: he answered to the former, verse 11. now he did not answer, either out of a deliberate purpose that he might die, or because he knew their resolution, and what should be. See Chapter 26.63. Isaiah 53.7.

**V. 13. ***{How many things?}* Many things indeed were objected, but either of no probability (as that of his affecting the Kingdom) or no proof for them, as forbidding payment of Tribute to Caesar; or far from the matter of their present charge; as his filling Jewry with his teaching: all which calumnies needed no refutation.

**V. 14. ***{to never a word}* Or, *not so much as to one thing*: As Chapter, 18.16. Or, *not so much as one word*, Isaiah, 53.7.

**V. 15. ***{Now at the feast}* Luke 23.17. The feast of Passover, John 18.39. which by way of eminency, is styled, *the feast* chap. 26.23. John 4.45.

*{to release a prisoner}* *One prisoner*: it may be they, at the beginning of that custom, desired it, as a memorial of their deliverance out of Egypt.

**V. 16. ***{A notable prisoner}* A robber: John 18.40. a raiser of sedition, and a murderer, Mark 15.7. Acts 3.14.

**V. 17. ***{When they were gathered together}* To wit, when before the multitude there was mention made of this custom. Mark 15.8.

*{Barabbas or Jesus}* It appears that Pilate was very willing to save Jesus, in that he set so vile a man against him: very likely it is, that he could have named some other prisoner, who had been committed for some lighter offense; but he names Barrabas, in hope the people would not have chosen him, but Christ.

*{called Christ}* By many so accounted.

**V. 18. ***{He knew that of envy}* As well as by what he had heard of him, so by their frivolous charges: and it is most likely that Pilate, who had been the Roman Deputy all the time of Jesus’ public Ministry, and doing Miracles, (the report whereof filled every mouth of that people) had some about him, who informed concerning Jesus, as well as all other eminent and considerable things acted in that imperial City and Province, whereof he had the charge; therefore no doubt but that he had likewise heard how the High Priests stood affected toward Jesus; how they envied the Honor which the people gave him; and that many Disciples followed him.

*{they had delivered}* That is, Jesus into the civil Magistrate’s hand: so true is that of Synesius, *de provident*; *Envie is not so much suppressed by Vertue, as inflamed; or not so much appeased, as enraged*.

**V. 19. ***{with that just man}* Guiltless, good and innocent. See v. 4, 24. Luke 23.47. Acts 3.14. and 23.14. & 7.52. Beware you condemn him not, though the Jews importune you so to do. She thus admonishes not without just ground and reason: first from the innocence of Christ, in that he was a just and holy man, for favor of men, and an impious vulgar, to destroy one good man, were an horrid impiety.

*{I have suffered many things, &c.}* I have been much troubled about him in my sleep this night: which might come to pass by special providence, the more to attest Christ’s innocence, and to render Pilate more unexcusable. Here is her second reason, why she would by no means have her Husband interpose, or meddle with that business, for the High Priests against Christ.

**V. 20. ***{But the chief Priests}* John 18.40. Acts 3.14. Here appears the devilish malice of the Priests, persuading the people (whom they ought to have led to Christ and salvation) to destroy Jesus with their outcries: *Duces esse principes erroris maxime prophanum*; Theodoret, *in Jerem.* it is most profane for Princes to be leaders into error: or, for the chief among the people to be ringleaders of error.

*{and destroy Jesus}* By not asking his life: or that they should not only ask pardon for Barabbas, but also clamor to have Jesus put to death: as they did, verse 22.

**V. 21. ***{Said unto them}* To the multitude of the Jews.

*{whether of the twain will ye that I release}* He had once before asked this question, verse 18. but it seems, the answer was retarded, by his wife’s sudden message to him; therefore (as Euthymius thinks) now he repeats the question.

*{they said Barabbas}* Being before instructed by their Priests and Elders, as Salome was by Herodias, to ask John Baptist’s head. See on John 18.40.

**V. 32. ***{let him be crucified}* Too much pity in sparing Barabbas, and as much impious ingratitude toward Jesus. Here is an example of an inconstant and precipitate vulgar; commonly ruled by passion and faction, seldom by right reason: multitudes are as fickle as the great waters, whose billows run furiously, one forcing on and moving another according to the several winds which blow upon them. So there but now they cried *Hosanna*, and presently, *Crucifie, Crucifie him* (such mischievous influence had the Priests on the people’s levity:) First, *save I pray*, (see Chapter 21.9.) then *Crucifie him*; so their High Priest blindly raved, John 11.50. *you doe not consider that it is expedient for us, that one man die for the people, and that the whole Nation perish not*, &c. So here the people cry *Hosanna sonne of David*; and *Crucifie him*, yet not understanding that by being crucified, he should save them.

**V. 23. ***{What evil hath he done?}* The Judge did reasonably enquire of a crime to be alleged, and proved by them, who were so audaciously forward to anticipate his sentence, and to prescribe so severe a punishment, and this very question summoned the people, to be witnesses of Christ’s innocence, and their own fury.

*{they cried out the more}* When they could do nothing legally, or justly against him; they fall to begging his Blood; that the evil which they could not achieve by Law, or Reason, *Vociferando perficiant*, Beda, they might do by outcries.

**V. 24. ***{A tumult was made}* He doubted, lest they would take him by force, and raise some sedition.

*{washed his hands}* Thereby intending to signify that he was free from the guilt of Christ’s death. See Deut. 21.6. Psalm 26.6. but the deep stains of blood cannot be washed off with seas of water.

*{of the blood}* Of the murder.

*{of this just man}* Christ being pronounced innocent now thrice; v. 4, 19. and here it appears, that he died only for our sins 1 Pet. 3.18.

*{see ye to it}* Take heed, lest you be plagued for importuning me to condemn him.

**V. 25. ***{His blood be on us, and on our children}* That is, the guilt and punishment thereof, See John 2.19. if there be any offense committed in slaying him, let us and our posterity smart for it, and bear the burden thereof, which they do to this day; and how heavily it fell on them, and their children, in the destruction of Jerusalem; the Ecclesiastical Histories abundantly show; the fruit of this rash and wicked imprecation, came speedily and heavily upon them, See on Chapter, 24.22. See 2 Samuel 3.28, 29.

**V. 26. ***{When he had scourged Jesus}* He satisfied their desire, in releasing Barabbas; and herein endeavored to pacify them, by scourging Christ: Luke, 23.16. John, 19.1, 4.

*{delivered him to be crucified}* When he saw nothing but Christ’s death would pacify them, he condemned him, to please the Jews.

**V. 27. ***{Then the soldiers}* John 19.1. When they had scourged him; for they were appointed here as Beadles, and as Executioners, to which there were four assigned. See John 19.23.

*{common hall}* *πραιτώριον*, the *pretory*, or *Governours house*, Acts 23.35. Phil. 1.13. See on Acts 23.35.

**V. 28. ***{A scarlet robe}* John and Mark make mention of a purple robe, which in effect is the same, the usual habit of Kings; they put it on him in scorn, and out of zeal to Caesar. See on John 19.2.

**V. 29. ***{A crown of thorns}* Not a golden one to honor him, but of thorns, to disgrace and vex him; this says Prosper, *de promis. & praedict. part. 3. c. 20.* that sheep prefigured, *qui in spinis cornibus detentus immolatus est pro Isaac*; which being held by the head, was sacrificed instead of Isaac.

*{a reed in his right hand}* Some cane, such as they usually carried, either for stay and support, or to smite with: See chap. 10. this was instead of a Scepter.

*{they bowed the knee}* As the manner was, before great ones, Gen. 41.43. chap. 17.14. Mark 1.40. and 10.17.

**V. 30. ***{They spit upon him}* In sign of defiance and reproach. See on chap. 26.67.

**V. 31. ***{They took the robe off}* They would hereby seem to degrade and unking him again.

**V. 32. ***{And as they came out}* Mark 15.21. Luke 23.26. As they went out of the city, See Numb. 15.35. 1 King. 21.13. Hebrews 13.12.

*{a man of Cyrene}* See on Luke 23.36.

*{Simon by name}* See on Luke 23.26.

*{him they compelled}* Christ had carried his Cross so far, all along the City, John 19.17. as the custom was for condemned men to do, (Matthew 16.24. John 19.17.) but because Christ had been so rudely handled by them, that he could carry it no longer, therefore they compelled Simon to carry it out of the City: some conceive that he only helped Christ to bear it, carrying up the hinder part thereof, and cite to that end; Luke 23.26. John 19.17. others think, that only the transom or upper end thereof was borne from the ground, the nether part might easily be drawn along the same.

**V. 33. ***{And when they were come}* John 19.17.

*{place of a skull}* Because many skulls of dead men, there executed, lay there scattered. See on John 19.17. and Luke 23.33.

**V. 34. ***{They gave him vinegar}* See verse 48. and on Luke 23.36. and on John 19.29.

*{he would not drink}* Which afterward he refused not, when he had suffered all but death, and was athirst. See verse 48. John 19.30.

**V. 35. ***{They crucified him}* Nailed him to the Cross. See Psal. 22.16. John 19.18.

*{parted his garments}* They divided the rest, but cast lots for the upper coat. John 19.23, 24. See note thereon.

**V. 36. ***{They watched him there}* The soldiers appointed for the guard attended, by the Cross, lest any should take him down before he was dead: herein, by God’s providence, his enemies also became witnesses of his death, lest unbelievers should have objected against his resurrection, that he was not dead. See John 19.34, 35, &c.

**V. 37. ***{The King of the Jews}* So was he indeed, though this were done in mockery. See John 1.49. and on Luke 23.38. John 19.19, 22.

**V. 38. ***{Two thieves crucified with him}* This was to dishonor him, and to make the world believe that he also was a malefactor; therefore they place him in the midst, as a principal ringleader of such men: See Isai. 53.12. he was crucified with thieves, *sed quos passio jungebat, causa separabat*. Augustin. *Bonifac. Ep. 50.* But those whom the like passion united, the cause separated. See Luke 23.41. This preceded in the head, Christ, which followed in many Martyrs.

**V. 39. ***{They that passed by}* Who use to pity such as suffer (though justly) See Lamentations 1.12. Yet now they deride Christ, who was innocent; we read not that they were so inhuman and pitiless to the thieves who were truly guilty, Luke 23.41. See on Luke 23.35.

*{wagging their heads}* A sign of derision and insultation, Psal. 22.7. 2 King. 19.21. Isai. 37.22. Lam. 2.15.

**V. 40. ***{Thou that destroyest the Temple}* See on Chapter 26.61. This they insultingly say, blasphemously intimating that he had deceived the people, boasting before them that he could destroy and re-edify the Temple of Jerusalem in three days, who, as they supposed, could not so much as save himself, from the torment and death of the Cross: this they added to render him pitiless, and odious to the people of the Jews, who heard nothing so unwillingly as concerning the destruction of the Temple, of which they chiefly gloried.

*{if thou be the Son of God}* See on Chapter 4.3. See on Luke 23.35.

*{come down from the Crosse}* But therefore he would not come down, until he had perfected the work of our Redemption, there to be performed; because he was the Son of God, and the Messiah; who therefore came into the world, that he might fulfill this his Father’s Will, by becoming obedient to the death of the Cross, Phil. 2.8.
