**V. 41. ***{The chief Priests}* Their malice to Christ was showed in life and in death: it might seem much below their dignity, to be present at an execution, and it was unworthy of their high calling to insult over a dying man.

**V. 42. ***{He saved others, himself he cannot save}* He would not save himself, that he might save others: this is a scoff like that (Luke 4.23.) which the Proverb expressed, by *Physitian cure thy selfe*.

*{let him now come downe from the crosse}* Foolish persecutors, he will rise from the dead, who will not yield to your insulting opprobries, by descending from the Cross; *plus fuit mortem, resurgendo, destruere, quam vitam, descendendo, servare*. Gregor. *hom. 21.* It was more, to destroy death, by rising again from the dead, than to preserve life by coming down from the Cross: could Pilate say, *what I have written, I have written*; and shall not Christ perfect our salvation which he has begun: but they say, let him come down and we will believe him: vain tempters, he will not so purchase credence with you, for, *si descenderit neminem salvum faciet*. Bernard. *de 7. sig.* if he should come down, he should save none.

*{we will believe him}* This they said in scorn, and confidence that he could not have come down, if he would: for when he did more than this, by raising himself from the dead, (for it is more to restore, than to preserve life) yet they believed not.

**V. 43. ***{He trusted in God}* Psalm 22.8.

*{if he will have him}* or, *if he delight in him*, as Psalm 22.8. whence this seems to be taken.

**V. 44. ***{The thieves also}* or, *even the thieves*; that is, one of the thieves. See Luke 23.39, 40. and the like manner of speaking, chap. 26.8.

**V. 45. ***{From the sixth hour}* About noon: for they began their day at Sun-rising, thence began their first hour of the day. See on Luke 23.44. So was that literally performed which was spoken of Amos 8.9, 10.

*{there was darkness}* To show God’s dislike of their cruelty, he would not have the Sun give light to so horrid an act, and as it were to see the Sun of Righteousness so unworthily handled; a natural Eclipse, it could not be: some think, it being so total, so general, and the Moon being now in the full (it being the midst of the lunar month, when the Passover was killed) and so of necessity, the body of the Moon (which uses to eclipse the Sun, by its interposition, and being between us and the Sun) must be opposite to, and distant from the Sun, the diametrical breadth of the Hemisphere; the full Moon ever rising at the Sun’s setting. Apollophanes, who is reported to have been at this time in Heliopolis a City of Egypt with Dionysius Areopagita; when he saw this admirable Eclipse, said, *Θείων* *ἀμοιβαὶ* *πραγμάτων*, changes of the Divine works or administrations: but Dionysius said, *ἢ* *τὸ* *θεῖον* *πάσχει*, *ἢ* *τῷ* *πάσχοντι* *συμπάσχει*, either the Deity suffers, or has sympathy with that which suffers.

*{over all the land}* Of Judea, and the Countries and Regions near; or over all the earth as some suppose.

**V. 46. ***{My God}* Psal. 22.1. This shows that albeit in this bitter Agony, he had an heavy and intense sorrow, a deep apprehension and sense of the wrath of his Father against sin: yet he despaired not, for he called upon God, and called him his God, which terms and notions are not compatible with despair, apprehending no interest in God, or hope of audience in distress, Psalm, 22.1.

*{why hast thou forsaken me}* In my present apprehension: suffering my human nature to feel so much misery in sense of your absence, and your anger against sinners, for whom I stand a surety: this is not the voice of despair, but of so sad a conflict with an heavy passion: why have you left me in the hands of my enemies, and comfort me not in these bitter sorrows?

**V. 47. ***{Calleth for Elias}* He called God his God, and they mock him, as if he called for Elias, because the words are something alike in the Hebrew, but more in the Chaldee. Psalm 22.1. Whence it seems he took these words, accommodate to his present condition. *אלי* *אלי* *eli eli* my God, my God, See Mark 15.34. *אלהי* *אלהי* *elohi, elohi* *למא* *שבקתני* *lema Shebacthani*. Targ. These last words which the Greek retains, being Chaldee or Syriac, the Hebrew being *למה* *עזבתני*, *lama hazabtani*, the Syr. *שבקתני* *shebacthani*: that which the Hebrew has *עזב* *deseruit reliquit*, the Syriac gives by *שבק* *shebac, reliquit*, &c.

**V. 48. ***{Straightway}* After he had said I thirst, John 19.28. and they, or some of them had said, *This man calleth for Elias*, presently after that, one of them ran, &c.

*{a spunge}* To suck it, for he could not otherwise drink of it, his hands being nailed to the Cross.

*{filled it}* See Psalm 69.21. that he might suck some drink out of a full sponge, which out of an half-filled one he could not have done, having thereto no assistance of his hands, being fastened with nails to the Cross.

*{with Vinegar}* Or *broken Wine*, such as the Roman Soldiers, had their allowance of; and did usually drink, mixed with water; to which purpose they might have a Vessel then at hand by them. See John 19.29. It may seem they mixed it with some other ingredients, Verse 34. See Mark, 15.23.

*{put it on a Reed}* Or *Cane*. See on John 19.29.

**V. 49. ***{The rest said}* Others besides them that mocked him, as before.

**V. 50. ***{When he had cried}* And uttered those forementioned words, John 19.30. Luke 23.46.

*{again}* For he had cried aloud once before.

*{yielded up}* *ἀφῆκε*, Let go. He willingly commended his Spirit, into his Father’s hands, Luke 23.46. that is, He willingly died.

**V. 51. ***{The veil of the temple was rent}* Some think, that which divided the holiest of all, from the holy place, as Exod. 26.31. 2 Chron. 3.14. whereby was figured that the ceremonial law was abolished, and the way to heaven more plainly laid open, Heb. 9.8. & 10.19, 20, 22. and the wall of separation broken down. Ephes. 2.14. Others say that there were two veils, and which of the two was now rent, the inward or the outward, it is very uncertain; Jerome and some other of the ancients understand it of the outward, Cyril and others of the inward. See *Is. Causab. exercit. 16. N. 127.* See on Luke 23.45.

*{The earth did quake}* These were signs of God’s anger against the Jews, and of Christ’s Divine Majesty, See Psal. 114.7.

**V. 52. ***{The graves were opened}* The grave-stones it seems were moved, though the dead bodies there interred came not out till after Christ’s resurrection, as appears in the next verse; hereby was showed then, that our Savior had overcome death: as in his own person, so for others.

*{many bodies of the Saints}* Probably they were such as were lately dead, who appearing, verse 53. were known to many in the City (See on chap. 17.3.) who well remembered them, and if others, then were they known, as Moses and Elias on the mount, were known of the three Apostles there present with Christ: this is to give us assurance of our resurrection from the dead.

*{which slept}* This word is often used in Scripture to express the state of the dead: and to intimate that as we have often awaked from sleep, so shall we once from death, sleep is but death’s elder brother. See John 11.11.

*{arose}* Either to appear as witnesses of Christ’s resurrection, or to attend Christ into heaven (who was the first fruits of them that slept, 1 Cor. 15.20.) or both these.

**V. 53. ***{into the holy City}* Into Jerusalem, See on Chap. 4.5. for the places of burial were in the suburbs, out of the city.

*{appeared unto many}* Of the faithful, not unto all the people: so also Christ appeared, but not to all, Acts 10.41. For they who would not believe Moses and the Prophets, neither would they, if one should come to them from the dead. Luke 16.

**V. 54. ***{watching Jesus}* See on verse 36. A guard of Soldiers used to watch those who were crucified, *Jo. Drus. praeteritor. l. 1.* commanded by the Roman Deputy to see execution done, so that no private person could, or might presume to take down a crucified body from the cross, without his leave and warrant; or, possibly though they were their kindred or friends, to bury them, how and where they would; therefore Joseph of Arimathea begged the body of Jesus, of Pilate, that he might decently inter it.

*{they feared greatly}* They were much afraid of God’s wrath, having been instruments of cruelty exercised on Christ in his suffering; and having seen such dreadful signs of God’s anger, as the extraordinary eclipse, the earthquake rending of the rocks, and possibly the graves opening.

*{this was the Son of God}* A man beloved of God; or not guiltless only, but more than a man; for they being heathen men esteemed their great and excellent men, Sons of their Gods: See Luke 23.47. It is not certain that they acknowledged the deity of Christ; See Dan. 3.25.

**V. 55. ***{Afar off}* Either in modesty they would not be among the throng of Soldiers: or, it may be, they permitted none to come near the cross; or they were afraid to be questioned as his followers, as Peter was. chap. 26.58.

*{which followed Jesus}* They had followed him out of Galilee, and would not leave him; so great was their zeal to the gospel, and love to him: yet were they not all Galileans. Luke 8.3. & 23.27, 28.

**V. 56. ***{Mary Magdalen}* So called of the place of her abode. See chap. 15.39.

*{the mother of James and Joses}* See Mark 6.3.

*{the mother of Zebedee’s children}* See chap. 20.20. Whose name may seem to have been Salome, Mark 15.40.

**V. 57. ***{When the even was come}* Mark 15.42. Luke 23.50. John 19.38. After the ninth hour, at three of the clock in the afternoon.

*{a rich man}* The more likely to obtain the body of Pilate, and the more in danger of the malice of the Priests for his care of Christ.

*{of Arimathaea}* Or *Rama, or Remathaim*. See on Luke 23.50. Jerome *de loc. Hebraic.* calls it Armatha Sophim, the city of Elkana and Samuel, near Diospolis.

*{named Joseph}* Christ was not buried obscurely, but by the Governor’s consent, by an honorable man, in a place not far distant, in a new sepulcher; So that there cannot reasonably be any doubt of his death, See Isaiah 53.9.

**V. 58. ***{Begged the body of Jesus}* For the bodies of condemned persons were in the power of the Magistrates.

*{Pilate commanded the body to be delivered}* See on verse 54.

**V. 59. ***{In a clean linen cloth}* To bury it decently.

**V. 60. ***{In his own new tomb}* which Joseph of Arimathea had prepared for himself, but none yet had laid in it; that none might suspect the rising of some other body: See on Luke 23.53.

*{rolled a great stone}* As the Jews used to do on the sepulchers of their friends. See John 11.38, 39. which he did to secure the body from abuse by his implacable enemies; God’s providence disposing all that was done about this, to remove suspicion of his disciples taking it away, as is objected, chapter 28.13. also to set off the power and glory of Christ’s resurrection, whom neither that weight nor the bands of death could hold from rising again: this was laid by man but removed by an Angel.

**V. 61. ***{The other Mary}* Mentioned verse 56. See Mark 15.47.

*{sitting over against the sepulchre}* To see where Christ was laid, that they might embalm his body, Luke 23.55, 56.

**V. 62. ***{The day of preparation}* So was any day called, that was next before any solemn feast. See Mark 15.47. Luke 23.54. John 19.14, 31, 42.

**V. 63. ***{that deceiver}* See John 7.12. Thus would their restless malice annul the power of his doctrine and miracles: thus they hoped to wash from themselves blood-guiltiness, by asserting the equity of his condemnation.

*{said}* Chap. 12.40. Mark 8.31. John 2.19. which, by some of the Jews, came to their ears: for (generally all) the Jews jealous of the honor and safety of their temple, might in some debate with the disciples occasioned by Christ’s words, John 2.19. understand that he there spoke of his resurrection from the dead on the third day: for so Christ had taught them who were his disciples, Matth. 16.2. & 17.23. Mark 9.31. & 10.34. or possibly the traitor Judas, then one of the twelve, had let fall something before Christ’s enemies, concerning his rising again the third day.

*{after three days}* Mark 8.31. for he was laid in the grave on Friday, lay there all Saturday, which was their Sabbath, and rose on the Lord’s day morning, which is ours, chap. 28.1.

**V. 64. ***{Until the third day}* According to the time set by Christ, for his resurrection; for so they might keep his body safe till that time were past, and then probably they meant to have showed it to the people, hoping thereby to convince him of untruth in this particular, which would weaken the credit of all the rest of his doctrine.

*{the last error shall be worse than the first}* A proverbial form of speech: the people will believe in him more than before, John 11.12, 32. which they counted error.

**V. 65. ***{Ye have a watch}* He means the garrison Soldiers, appointed for the guard of the temple, Acts 4.1. & 5.56. especially at the Passover, for fear of tumults, in that confluence of people; it may be they were the same who went with Judas to take Christ, John 18.3.

*{as ye can}* *ὡς* *οἴδατε*. As you know, as you can devise, or you know well enough how to do it.

**V. 66. ***{They went}* The custody of the sepulcher was committed to them that murdered Christ, that there might be no doubt of the truth of his resurrection.

*{sealing the stone}* With some public seal of office, which might prohibit any to remove, or meddle with it, See Daniel 6.17.

*{and setting a watch}* Wickedness can never be secure: all this but confirmed the truth of his resurrection, See on verse 60. all that the adversaries of the truth of Christ shall at last effect, is to render it more certain and undoubted: had not all this been done by the malicious Jews, the ground of our faith concerning the resurrection of Christ, had not been so evident: the enemies of Christ, against their wills, and intentions, are made competent witnesses of his power and truth.

## CHAPTER 28

**Verse 1. ***{In the end of the Sabbath}* Mark 16.1. After it was ended.

*{as it began to dawn}* See on Luke 24.1. John 20.1.

*{the first day of the week}* On the Lord’s day morning Revel. 1.10.

*{the other Mary}* See on chap. 27.61.

*{to see the sepulchre}* To see whether it were safe, and shut up as they saw it, chap. 27.61. out of their love and fear: so solicitous were they for the body of Jesus, that they might embalm him, Mark 16.1. Luke 24.1. Not knowing that he was risen from the dead.

**V. 2. ***{There was}* Or *there had been*.

*{a great earth-quake}* Which seems to have been when the women were in their way, there was one at his passion chap. 27.51. and another at his resurrection. By many signs did the Lord manifest this glorious work of Christ’s rising again a triumphant conqueror over death and hell; as to confirm our faith concerning the resurrection of the dead: so also to form the hearts of these witnesses thereof, to a reverend elevation of mind to the truth of Christ’s deity; by the power whereof, he not only raised up his own human body: but also manifested himself to be the Lord 76of life, who will also raise up ours from the dust of the earth, which now trembled at the surrendry of her Lord to life again.

*{for the angel of the Lord}* Or *an angel*, two are mentioned. Luke 24.4. John 20.12. Either one was nearest at hand to speak for both; or one appeared at first, and two when they came back: this for imports not a cause, but a consequence of the earthquake: Christ rising from the dead, gave this triumphant signal of his victory over death, hell and the devil, as to the watch at Jerusalem, so to the inhabitants of the earth, whose foundations he thus shook at his pleasure; for it is most likely that he rose in this earthquake.

*{rolled back the stone from the door}* Performing the office of a doorkeeper; not but that Christ could have done it without his ministry, but out of duty to Christ: also he removed it that the women might go into the sepulcher, and see that Christ was gone thence, and that the soldiers might, to their astonishment, see what he came for; not to raise up Christ, who could command the creatures to give way to him; but for their sakes who were to behold the same, and testify it to others. See Justin. Martyr. *quaest. 117.*

*{and sate upon it}* Expecting the coming of those women, to whom he was to declare and testify the resurrection of Christ: for they were but in the way when the earth shook as Christ rose from the dead: they were solicitous by the way as they came, whom they should get to roll away the stone, that they might go into the sepulcher to embalm Christ. Mark 16.3. Here God (whose aid is never wanting in due time and manner, to holy desires) provided so, as that they found it readily opened by the Angel.

**V. 3. ***{His countenance was like lightning}* As Dan. 10.6. See here, chap. 17.2. Mark 16.5. says, they saw a young man; that is, the Angel who had assumed that shape, which intimated him, of entire strength and agility: this Majesty in his countenance, showed that he was no man of the vulgar, but an heavenly messenger.

*{white as snow}* Acts 1.10. See this phrase, Psal. 51.7. Lam. 4.7. Dan. 7.9. Rev. 3.4, 5, 18. & 4.4. & 6.11. & 7.9, 13.

**V. 4. ***{The keepers did shake}* At the sight of his glory, and in conscience of their own guilt: seeing the earth tremble, the grave open, the heavy stone rolling away from the sepulcher, the dead body which they had in charge to watch, gone; a person of such a dreadful presence, and heavenly glory, as spoke him more than man; they could not but know, that they had not now to deal with infirm man, but God: therefore, all astonished, they tremble, and become like dead men: here were two main causes of fear. 1. Their own horror of conscience, who were actors in the death of Jesus. 2. Fear of their Governor, whose anger they might well expect, if he should not credit their reports, but construe all, as incredible excuses of their own default, and negligence in watching. Thus God showed them how vain it was to take up arms against him; and also made them witnesses against themselves and the malicious Priests.

**V. 5. ***{The angel answered}* To the women’s fear, verse 8. or he spoke, as chap. 22.1. it is possible that the watch coming to themselves again, fled into the city, as verse 11. as the women came some other way, so that they missed them; for the women, without any let by the watch, entered into the monument (which probably they would not have suffered, had they been present) they saw the angel, and were afraid; but not as these armed keepers: a good conscience is the best armor, and surest fortification.

*{fear not ye}* Though the soldiers be fled for fear of me, yet be you not dismayed: they watched Christ for evil, you seek him for good: the keepers had cause to fear God’s revenging hand, who helped to murder his beloved, only Son; but you have cause of hope and joy, not fear and sorrow: the resurrection of Christ is a due cause of terror to the enemy, and of joy to us who believe.

*{I know ye seek Jesus}* This is one cause, why he would not have them fear; for he is on their side, who came not with the impious watch, as enemies; but as faithful servants of their Lord, to do him honor; for he saw the provision which they brought to embalm Christ.

*{was crucified}* He means that which is a scandal to the world, as the Christians’ ground of rejoicing, Gal. 6.14.

**V. 6. ***{he is not here, he is risen}* This is another argument of consolation; he is not now in the sepulcher: that was not enough; for Magdalen thought they had conveyed him thence, John 20.13. therefore he adds, *he is risen*, to life again; the conqueror of death, and Lord of life.

*{where the Lord lay}* Believe your own eyes (See Mark 16.14.) let them help to conform you: see the place where the sacred body of Christ was laid in the sepulcher, he is not now there, but risen from the dead.

**V. 7. ***{Go quickly and tell his disciples}* To put them out of their heaviness: the Lord will quickly comfort the afflicted, except the cause of his delay be in ourselves.

*{he goeth before you into Galilee}* So had Christ foretold them, Mark 16.7. chap. 26.32. (See Micah 2.13.) therefore he said, Luke 24.6. *remember how he spake unto you*. See note thereon.

*{lo, I have told you}* Assure yourselves of it: fail not to deliver your message I have delivered mine.

**V. 8. ***{with fear and great joy}* Being affrighted at the sight and presence of the Angel, but comforted at the joyful tidings of Christ’s resurrection.

*{to bring his disciples word}* They are made the first witnesses of his resurrection, *quae ardentius dilexerunt*, Cyprian *de resur.* who more fervently loved, and more devoutly sought Christ. Death began from that sex, and to the same, God appointed the first witness of life restored, in the resurrection of Christ.

**V. 9. ***{Jesus met them}* Christ not only sent an angel to publish his resurrection, but appeared in his own person to the women, and sends them to tell his disciples: here’s both a remedy against fear, and reward of obedience.

*{all hail}* See chap. 26.49. *χαίρετε*, *rejoyce ye*; it is a form of salutation which the Syriac expresses by *שלם* *לכן* *shelom lechen*, *peace be unto you*: as the Hebrews said, *שלום* *לכם* *shalom lachem*, peace be unto you, fear not, Gen. 43.23. the 70. *ἵλεως* *ὑμῖν*, Chald. *שלם* *לכון* *shelam lechon*. So Moses is dismissed by Jethro, Exod. 4.18. *לך* *לשלום* *lech leshalom*, 70. *βάδιζε* *ὑγιαίνων*, *go in*, or *to peace*, or health: Christ here not only said this, but wrought the effect in their hearts, in a spiritual illumination and joy.

*{held him by the feet}* Worshipping him: Either they endeavored so to do, and were prohibited by Christ, as John 20.17. or Christ first appearing to Magdalen, forbade her only to touch him then; but permits these now: (See John 20.17.) for now they were comforted, and their wavering minds better assured than at first: most likely Christ hastened Magdalen to contribute her information to the rest; intimating that her holding him by the feet now, though holy, was unseasonable, he having assigned her a greater work, which ought first to be dispatched; that his disciples’ faith might run no longer an hazard, by her delay. See on John 20.17.

*{and worshipped him}* Though the humanity of Christ be a creature, and so, not of, and by itself to be adored with divine adoration, but the Deity, to whom, alone, all divine worship is due (Matthew 4.10.) yet because, from his conception, the Deity is personally and indivisibly united to his humanity, and so Christ is truly God, as truly man, from whom the Deity cannot be divided, neither in true faith, nor faithful invocation; therefore whole Christ is to be adored; that is, God the eternal Word become flesh, in, and with the humanity of Christ.

**V. 10. ***{Tel my brethren}* His disciples: this high title Christ gave them before, chap. 12.50. so now also after his resurrection, to show the continuance of his affection to them. See Psalm 22.22. John 20.17. Heb. 2.11. Rom. 8.29.

**V. 11. ***{some of the watch came into the city}* Now appear another sort of witnesses, above all exception, because of the adversaries’ party; which this Evangelist exactly relates, showing particularly when, who, to whom, and with what success these testified concerning those things they had seen. 1. the time was, before the women could come to the sepulcher, the watch themselves ran into the city, to inform. 2. the parties were some of the watch: the more understanding part thereof, the rest for fear scattering and dispersing themselves in the city. 3. The high Priests, who set them on work, are informed, not without a singular hand of divine providence, to convince and shame the malicious enemies of Christ, and authors of that counsel to guard the sepulcher. 4. The success was not, as in reason might have been expected, the high Priests’ repentance, and happy acknowledgment of their error to undeceive the people whom they had misled: but a Pharaoh-like hardness of heart: for they further endeavor to suppress the truth, by bribing the soldiers to lie, and the president to connive at their wickedness.

**V. 12. ***{large money}* *ἀργύρια* *ἱκανὰ*, *sufficient moneys*; *money not a little*, Syr.

**V. 13. ***{his disciples stole him away}* Pilate might the rather believe this, because the Priests had given him an hint hereof beforehand, chap. 26.64. more easily do wicked men believe improbable lies, than probable truth.

*{while we slept}* Very credible witnesses, that could certainly tell what was done, while they were asleep: if you were then asleep, how knew you that his disciples stole him away? Lactantius’s rule is commonly true, *haec enim est mendaciorum natura ut cohaerere non possint*, l. 5. c. 3. this is the nature of lies, they can seldom hold together. This lie wanted all color of truth: they would have made Christ’s virtue his disciples’ crime, who recovered their Master (not as was pretended, by the theft, but) by faith, alive, not dead.

**V. 14. ***{come to the governor’s ears}* Be heard by the Governor, as they might well fear it would, though they would do what they could to keep it from him: and might well fear the issue: for if they appeared to be the authors of a lie, they might expect his displeasure: if they said true, they might expect some severe punishment for sleeping in their appointed watch.

*{secure you}* We will make you *ἀμερίμνους*, without care: we will provide for your indemnity, by preventing the danger, that might come to you thereby, we will stand between harm and you, take you no care for that; for it was punishable to fail in the due performance of such like trusts. See Acts 12.10.

**V. 15. ***{this saying is commonly reported}* Though the president could not but know it was false, as contradicting itself: so probably he could not but question them, why they came off their watch, before the end of the third day: yet for fear of tumult (as it is too common nowadays) he had rather bear with the dishonor of God, than his own personal danger or detriment.

*{unto this day}* So easily do untruths find entertainment among the vulgar, too incredulous of truth: this was the just judgment of God, to send them strong delusions, that they should believe lies, who would not receive the love of the truth, that they might be saved, 2 Thess. 2.10. yet for all this, the elect everywhere believed, See 2 Cor. 4.3, 4. and so shall do still, notwithstanding all these pernicious mists of untruth, which ascending from the bottomless pit of hell, darken the truth of Christ so that the men of this world cannot discern the light thereof, 2 Cor. 4.3, 4.

**V. 16. ***{the eleven disciples}* This Evangelist is brief in repeating Christ’s apparitions to his disciples after his resurrection, which the other supply. The eleven are mentioned, chap. 10.1, 26, 44. Judas the twelfth was gone, ch. 27.5. the chief are mentioned; but it is likely that more, or all the rest went into Galilee to meet him; for they also were brethren. See ver. 10. the Apostle says he was seen of more than 500. brethren at once, 1 Cor. 15.6. Some make this *ἐπάνω*, to relate to the place; not to the number: as if the sense were, he was seen above; when he ascended: so this 500 seeing him at once, may be understood of their coming together to the mount of Olivet, Acts 1.12. *ἵνα* *αὐτοῖς* *καὶ* *τὴν* *ἀνάληψιν* *αὐτοῦ* *δείξῃ*, *Oecumenius*; that he might show them also his assumption.

*{into a mountain}* What mountain of Galilee this was, is not mentioned; probably he went thence to mount Olivet near Jerusalem; for thence he ascended, Acts 1.12. and this was near Jerusalem.

**V. 17. ***{when they saw him}* When the multitude of disciples saw him alive from the dead, in his own former figure, lineaments and appearances, not yet in the Majesty of his glorified body, lest they should have conceived that it had been some other body risen, not that Jesus which was crucified, dead and buried: this glory after his resurrection was veiled; rather for their sakes, than missing on his: in that they could not have known him, and it was necessary for his disciples so to attend him, *ut possit cognosci*, Augustin. *de C. D. lib. 22. cap. 19.* that he might be known of them.

*{they worshipped him}* Acknowledging his resurrection and divine power by adoring him. See on verse 9. being now more confident than ever that he was indeed their Messiah, the Son of the living God: for so he was declared by his resurrection from the dead, Rom. 1.4.

*{but some doubted}* Till he came near to them, showed them the prints of the nails (John 20.25.) and spoke to them: so the Syriac interpreter gives it, *dubitaverunt*, *had doubted*: one tense for another. See Mark 16.11, 13. Luke 24.11, 28. their unbelief makes for the confirmation of our faith: for their doubts being cleared, and the truth manifested, they left posterity nothing rationally to doubt of.

**V. 18. ***{All power}* Absolute power without restraint and limitation, all dominion and authority to rule and govern.

*{is given unto me}* God the Father has given it to me; and I have now received it as man, who as God had the same power with the Father from eternity, (Philip. 2.7.) and now unto me did he give the same in the fullness of time: now I have put off the form of a servant, wherein I was to suffer death for man’s redemption; therein I was obedient, but now God has highly exalted me, and given me a name above every name, &c. Phil. 2.8. &c.

*{in heaven}* Which comprehends power of sending the holy Ghost, Acts 2.33. power over Angels, Phil. 2.10. Heb. 1.4. Coloss. 1.16. power to give heaven to all his, chapter 25.3, 4.

*{in earth}* Power to gather a Church out of all nations. Psalm 2.8. Mark 16.15, 16. and to rule over all. See Acts 10.36, 42. Ephes. 1.20, 21, 22. Rev. 17.14. Dan. 7.14.

**V. 19. ***{go ye therefore}* Be not therefore afraid to execute your charge, which by a supreme authority I commit to you: fear not the face of man, doubt not the success: be you my ambassadors to this purpose, administering this power among them. See 2 Corinth. 5.20.

*{teach all nations}* The word *μαθητεύσατε*, here used, signifies most properly, *make disciples*, *discipulos reddite, aut facite*, as John 4.1. *Ἰησοῦς* *πλείονας* *μαθητὰς* *ποιεῖ* *καὶ* *βαπτίζει*. *Jesus made more disciples and baptized*; that is, made them disciples by baptizing them: and to this rather than teaching, the words immediately following relate, where it is said, verse 2. *though Jesus himself baptized none, but his disciples*; which provision against the misunderstanding of the means of making disciples needed not, except baptism had been the consummating act and seal thereof, whereby one became a disciple, though for present want of capacity (as it is in infants) he were not yet taught; for, as for that, it is undeniably and expressly manifest, that though our Savior did not himself baptize, yet he preached constantly and usually, and the word *μαθητεύειν* here used, is distinct in signification from *διδάσκειν* (which word is used in the next verse) for *μαθητεύειν* signifies to make them disciples, who were aliens from our magistry, as Paul did at Iconium, Acts 14.21. where we read, *καὶ* *μαθητεύσαντες* *ἱκανοὺς* *Beza, & discipulos multos adjunxissent*, that is, *had admitted, or gained many disciples*, by teaching them the gospel, and baptizing them; as expressly that text shows, where it is said, *εὐαγγελισάμενοί* *τε* *τὴν* *πόλιν* *ἐκείνην*, preaching (or teaching) the gospel to that city, *καὶ* *μαθητεύσαντες*, &c. & making many disciples—where teaching, and preaching were improperly rendered, specially where two actions as distinct as preaching and baptizing, are signified; as here, the meaning is, by preaching the Gospel (for they had there to deal with men of years and capacity to learn in a Church to be constituted) they persuaded many to give their names to, or to become disciples of Christ by baptism; as that secret disciple, Joseph of Arimathea did, of whom we read, Matth. 27.57. *ὃς* *καὶ* *αὐτὸς* *ἐμαθητεύθη* *τῷ* *Ἰησοῦ*, *who also himself was a disciple of Jesus*, in the former place, the Syriac distinctly reads *וכד* *מסברין* *vechad mesabberin*—*cum evangelizassent filiis civitatis illius*, *תלמידו* (the very word here used) *discipulos fecerunt multos*. Lastly, as Hebrew infants became disciples of Moses (John 9.28. 1 Corinth. 10.2.) by circumcision; so do infants of believing Christians, by baptism become disciples of Christ; whereof we have elsewhere more largely discoursed. Finally by all nations, he means, not only Jews, but Gentiles also, See Acts 10.34, 35, 47.

*{baptizing them}* Administering to them the Sacrament of baptism; to all nations, wherein were ever many children, not here excepted in the Apostles’ charge to baptize.

*{in the name}* *εἰς* *τὸ* *ὄνομα*, *into the name*, by that right initiating them, and receiving them into the profession of the service of one God in three persons, and of depending on Christ alone for salvation. See 1 Corinth. 1.13, 15. and 10.2. with Exod. 14.31. Rom. 6.3.

**V. 20. ***{teaching them}* Note here that teaching, in order, follows *discipling*, or making disciples (which all teaching in the world, can never do, except they be also sealed by baptism) so that there is no advantage to the adversaries of infant-baptism: for suppose that the former word *μαθητεύσαντες*, were to be translated teaching (which we grant not) if their argument from the order of the words were concluding—(*therefore they must be first taught, and afterward baptized*—) why may not we as well conclude from the second order of the words of Christ—*baptizing them*, &c. *teaching them to observe all*, &c. therefore children (of believing parents) are first to be baptized, and after, as soon as they shall be capable thereof, taught?

*{to observe}* Obedience is required of believers. See Rom. 2.13. faith without works is dead; it says, as once barren Rachel, Gen. 30.1. *give me children, or else I die*.

*{all things}* Not some only, See Psalm 116. he that makes choice of God’s commandments, which he will obey, and which not, is his own servant, not God’s: almost to be persuaded to be a Christian, is but as good as nothing: it is not enough to be baptized, but we must adorn the profession there made, by doing that which God commands: Christ’s speech comprehends (as Theophylact well notes) *θεωρίαν* *καὶ* *πρακτικὴν* *ἀρετὴν*, that is, contemplative and practical virtue; knowledge and obedience.

*{I have commanded}* Not what man’s traditions command: will worship is a stranger to his Sanctuary: none such may come near, Num. 16.40. it is as strange as fire which came not from Heaven, God will not be served therewith.

*{I am with you alway}* Though your work be hard, I will assist you, and your successors in the ministry at all times, to the world’s end: *Os Christi, evangelium est, in caelo sedet, sed in terra loqui non cessat; non simus surdi, nam ille clamat*, Aug. *de temp. serm. 205.* the gospel is Christ’s mouth; he sits in Heaven, but ceases not to speak on earth; let us not be deaf for he cries out. Lastly, here is comfort to all believers; for this shows, as Jerome says, *se nunquam à credentibus recessurum*: that he will never depart from believers: as more particularly for all the successors of the Apostles, the faithful Ministers: Christ thus speaks, lest our Ministry should seem to be executed, *humana tantum operâ*, only by the work of man, Prosper *de vocat. Gent. lib. 2. cap. 2.* To the Ministers may be applied that which Jehoshaphat said to his judges, 2 Chron. 19.6, 7. when he had told them the Lord would be with them: *wherefore now, let the fear of the Lord be upon you, take heed and do it; for there is no iniquity with the Lord our God, nor respect of persons*: it was God’s promise to him, who bare the figure and name of Jesus, Joshua 1.5. *I will be with thee, I will not fail thee nor forsake thee*: it is the promise of our Jesus, who leads us into our promised rest: Behold I am with you always 78unto the end of the world; be strong therefore, and of good courage, fear not the malice of persecutors; *ipse certamine agonis nostri, & coronat pariter & coronatur*, Cyprian *lib. 2. ep. 6.* Christ in our conflict both crowns, and is crowned. Fear not the success; it is Christ who gives the increase, 1 Cor. 3.7. God could have done this work without our Ministry, or by his Angels: but he will give man the honor of this Ministration, where his own holy Spirit will work to make it effectual to the edification, and salvation of his elect: so fitted and prepared here, as were the materials of Solomon’s temple, that no noise of the builder shall be heard in rearing up the same there, where prophecies shall cease, love be perfected, and faith end in enjoying the promises of God in Christ.

*{Amen}* This word is current in all languages, and is originally Hebrew: so Jeremiah the Prophet said, *אמן* *יהוה* *amen Jehovah*, Jerem. 11.5. The Hebrews used it after their prayers or blessings, therein wishing the success, truth, confirmation and consummation of those things they prayed for: it is as much as *faxit Deus, certe stat*. See Deut. 27.15. which the 70. give *γένοιτο*, *so be it*. It is sometimes doubled for the greater confirmation. See Numb. 5.22. so afterwards in the primitive Church the people used to answer, when their prayers ended Amen. Pity that too many now are silent, and not so much as say Amen, to signify their consent, and vigilance of spirit in prayer: it is much to be feared that they neither regard, nor think of that which is said in public prayer; who in their own and others’ so great interest, not so much as say, Amen. See 1 Cor. 14.16. 2 Cor. 1.20. Rev. 1.7. Jer. 2.8, 6. It is put here for confirmation of a certain and infallible truth. (See on John 3.11.) Faithful is he that has promised to be with us unto the end. Lord Jesus so be it. Amen.

## ADDITIONAL NOTES

*Reader,*

The haste of the Press has occasioned the omission of these Notes following.

## MATTHEW, CHAPTER 1

**Verse 1. **between the word, *Enumeration*, and Jer. 3.8. Add [though therein they used the word *מגלה* of *גלל* *volvit, complicavit*, where is *מגלה*, *volumen membrana, charta*; a book, a roll of parchment, for so were they wont to make their books, which they rolled up like a cylinder. So have the Jews the law now in their Synagogues, therefore it is said of Christ in the Synagogue at Nazareth, Luke 4.20. *καὶ* *πτύξας* *τὸ* *βιβλίον*, and shutting, or folding up the book, *he gave it to the minister*. See Jerem. 36.2. Ezra. 6.2. which the 70. express sometimes by the Greek word *κεφαλὶς*, *capitulum*, Ezek. 2.9. &c. sometimes by *τόμος*, a tome (which Suidas and Hesych. express by *χάρτης*, *eo quod exciditur è pellibus animalium*, a roll or volume of parchment or vellum, written: or a Section or part of a divided volume.)]

And in the same verse after Gen. 5.1. Add, [or begin or end other their treatises: so the Psalms are called *ספר* *תהילים* *a book of praises*.]

**V. 2. **between the word, *covenant*, and, *Luke*, Add [and did expect Christ their promised Messiah, *to come of the seed of Abraham*, according to the promise of God made unto him on that behalf, that all the nations of the earth should be blessed in him. See Gen. 12.3. Gen. 18.18. Acts 3.25. Gal. 3.8. that is, in Christ to come of his posterity; as was also promised unto David, and therefore they that believed and reverenced Christ, used this title—Son of David, Matth. 9.27. Matth. 15.22. &c. and it was therefore most necessary in the beginning of this sacred history of our Savior Jesus Christ (the eternal Son of God, John 1.1. &c. and *in the fulnesse of time*, becoming truly *man of a woman, under the law*, Gal. 4.4.) that the writer should first make it appear, by a clear and certain declaration of his pedigree derived from Abraham and David, that this Jesus borne of the Virgin Mary, a daughter of David, or of his posterity, was indeed the Lord’s Christ, and the very promised Messiah, in whom all must believe that will be saved.]

And after the word, *Abraham* in the same verse, Add [yet were they by God’s covenant of the Gospel, accounted for sons of Abraham by faith in Christ, in whom Abraham believed, and through whom he was justified, Gal. 3.7. *they which be of faith, the same are the children of Abraham*, and verse 29. *If ye be Christs, then are ye Abrahams seed, and heirs according to promise*. See Rom. 2.29.]

**V. 5. **after Luke 4.22. Add, [but his supposed Son, see Luke 3.23.]

**V. 20. **after the word, *man*, Add [only; for the deity and humanity of Christ united without confusion of natures (neither the deity becoming an humanity, nor the humanity a deity) became one person, Christ Jesus, God and man, our Savior; whereas the reasonable soul, and human body united, make the person of mere man, here to the constitution of the person of Christ our redeemer, concurred the deity and humanity of Christ Jesus, the Son of the Virgin Mary: therefore the Angel said, not *ὁ* *γεννηθεὶς* but—*τὸ* *γὰρ* *ἐν* *αὐτῇ* *γεννηθὲν*, &c. that which is conceived in her.

## MATTHEW, CHAPTER 2

**Verse 1. **between. Hebr. 11.23. and the word, *before*, Add [*Μωϋσῆς* *γεννηθεὶς* *ἐκρύβη*: *Moses when he was born was hid*—that is, not long after he was born; for he was hid but three months, before he was carried out, according to the tyrannical Pharaoh’s command, to the river, Exod. 1.2. Exod. 2.1. So here when Christ was born, that is, shortly after his birth.]

And after the word, *star*, in the same verse, Add [in Greek *μάγοι*, *quod genus sapientum & doctorum habebatur in Persis*, &c. *de divinat. l. 1.* which were esteemed a sort of wise and learned men among the Persians. So also the Greeks understood hereby wise men of the Chaldees and Persians: so the Indians accounted their Gymnosophists; the Gauls their (see Alexand. ab Alexand. *Genial. dier. l. 4. c. 13.* Strabo *l. 6.*) Druids; the Grecians and Latins their Philosophers: such as possibly Zamolxis, Pythagoras, Plato, &c. were at first esteemed very prudent and knowing men, insomuch as that the Athenians in all their public counsels would ever have some of them to advise with, whom they called Mantes (and their art *μαντικὴν*. Cic. q. s.) diviners, or conjecturers of things to come. Such as the Hebrew called *קסם*, Greek *μάντις*, so are the Philistines’ Soothsayers called. 1 Sam. 6.2. So Balaam, Joshua 13.22. Ezek. 13.7. *μαντείας* *ματαίας*—*divinationem mendacii*, So said Saul to the witch at Endor, 1 Sam. 28.8. *μάντευσαι*, &c. divine unto me by the familiar spirit, the study of such, at first, seemed to have been a curious search into the secrets of nature and conjectural knowledge of things future, by the courses and aspects of stars; whence they obtained the opinion of interpreters of things divine: but overcurious in the study of judicial Astrology, they thence slid into vanities, superstitions; and oft-soon devilish arts, enchantments, sorceries, malices, &c. See 2 Kings 17.17. So that the name of Magus, as also Mantis, though at first honorable, became an odious note of infamy, and men impious, Jerem. 17.7. & 29.8. Ezek. 12.24. &c. and 22.28. Mich. 3.7. Zech. 10.2. but these here mentioned Magi; or *wise men*, seem not to have been such, but only certain Philosophers, and possibly astronomers; which might be the cause why God taught them (as has been said) by a star: which very probably men nothing versed in that study, would have taken little or no notice of.]

**V. 2. **after the words, *King, &c.* Add [*ὁ* *τεχθεὶς*, *recens natus*, the new born king. See verse 1.]

And in the same verse after the word, *Revelation*, Add [therefore was it called his star, because it declared him and his birth to them, as God had appointed it, and because it was a new and extraordinary star made for his sake, to bring those first-fruits of the Gentiles to him.]

**V. 3. **After the word, *others*, Add [who are impious and tyrannical Princes: he must needs fear many, whom many fear.]

In the same verse after the word, *Jerusalem*, Add [that is, all the inhabitants, citizens, or others then being in Jerusalem.

Again in the same verse after the word, *government*: Add [which confusion of state never was, nor ever can be without much trouble and affliction of the people: which the more knowing, forecasting, or probably suspecting, could not but be troubled, considering79 that their principle was that the kingdom of their Messiah, was to be a temporal Monarchy.]

**Verse 4. **After, Luke 20.12. Add [Matth. 8.19.]

**Verse 7. **After the words, *First of all*, Add [he thus diligently enquired of the wise men concerning the rising of this new Star (whereby they understood the birth of Christ, the King of Israel) under a fair pretense, as if he meant no other thing than that which the wise men came from the East to Jerusalem to do, verse 2. to worship him; supposing as it is most probable that the Star appeared first in the day of Christ’s Nativity, or about that very time; they meaning nothing herein but good, concealed nothing thereof from the King; charity and holiness think no evil (1 Cor. 13.5.) they nothing suspected the inhuman cruelty which Herod had then in his heart to murder harmless Jesus, and for his sake, so many tender Infants.]

**Verse 9. **After, *verse 2.* Add [it was by God’s divine providence, then hidden, and disappearing, lest it should have begotten danger to the sacred person of Christ, if it had been without this intermission visible, until it had stood over the house at Bethlehem where Christ was then newly born.]

**Verse 11. **After the words, *it self*, Add [more accommodate and safe for the Infant and woman in childbed.]

In the same verse, after, Exod. 34.4. Add [which besides that it was the manner of the people of those Eastern parts, so to prostrate themselves before their Kings) they were doubtless moved to it, by a divine instinct, there being no semblance of Majesty or Royal presence, then visible, to man: a more excellent Star, the light of God’s Spirit guiding them to their duty, shined in their hearts.]

**Verse 22. **After the word, *themselves*, Add [as when God preserved us in our infancy, and when we sleep and cannot guard ourselves, the keeper of Israel, who sleeps not, protects, defends, and provides for us and our safety.]

## MATTHEW, CHAPTER 3

**Verse 1. **After, Luke 1.80. Add [Gr. *παραγίνεται*. Some think it notes a sudden appearance of some unexpected thing or person: See John 1.6. in the Heb. he was called, *יוחנן* *Jochanan*. Syr. *Juchanan*, signifying, *the Lord hath shewed or performed grace or mercy*: thus was Christ’s forerunner named of the argument of his preaching the grace and mercy of God in Christ, now (after the long expectation of the promised Messiah and Savior) exhibited. Concerning John’s parents, conception, imposition of name, and admirable things concerning the same, St. Luke writes expressly and fully, which our Evangelist omits. It was God’s holy providence so to direct them, that all exactly agreeing in the same truth for the main; that which one of them omits, the other records. Why the Anabaptists, as weary of, and declining the proper name of their Sect, would be called Baptists it may seem strange; seeing, in that name, and office they would seem to agree with us who indeed are Baptists (as John was appointed of God to baptize) because we baptize, but do not as they, re-baptize: and therefore they are Anabaptists; and in spite of their railing, the Ministers of Christ who administer and profess only one God, one Savior, one Baptism, are Baptists: their frivolous objections against paedobaptism, as if the baptism of Infants were no baptism, that thereby they might avoid the name of Anabaptists or re-baptizers, have been sufficiently refuted. John is here styled Baptist, to distinguish between him the son of Zechary, Luke 1. and John the Apostle, son of Zebedee, Matth. 10.2. as also because he first, by the appointment of God (Luke 3.3. Mark 1.2, 3, 4. Mal. 3.1. Joh. 1.33.) did administer the seal of the new Covenant, the Sacrament of Baptism, to repentance and remission of sins in Christ.]

**Verse 3. **After, Dan. 2.24. Add [of which the Prophets foretold, to which the living way, Christ only, gives entrance, by baptism, Joh. 3.3, 5. prefigured by that one door of the Ark, Gen. 6.16. 1 Pet. 3.21. and the promised rest, the land of Canaan, into which God’s Israel, delivered from the bondage of sin (Rom. 6.3, 17, 18.) passed through the Red Sea, prefiguring Christ’s blood. See 1 Cor. 10.2. &c. and so understand these words; as if he had said, hitherto you have been held in the bondage of sin, and under the severe curse of the law, Gal. 3.10, 13, 23, 25, &c. but now Christ your Redeemer is come, and his Kingdom is at hand; not an earthly, secular, transitory kingdom, such as many impious, and so unhappy Kings and subjects enjoy; but a heavenly, spiritual, and eternal kingdom, here to be begun in the state of grace, but to be perfected in the eternal glory and happiness to come. Now John Baptist, being the messenger appointed to prepare the way before Christ, Mal. 3.1. had this honor, openly and clearly to proclaim that which the Jews hitherto did never generally, by the Prophets understand, concerning the Kingdom of Christ, which they expected in external, secular glory, prosperity, deliverance, victory, &c. Thus therefore this silver Trumpet began to proclaim the spiritual Jubilee, calling to repentance, faith, and newness of life; sounding a note much dissonant from that on Sinai, Exod. 19.16. which was with threatening and terror to all Israel: but this was the gladsome tidings of grace, mercy, and salvation, by Christ: and this is called the Kingdom of God and Christ, because therein God through Christ reigns in us. Sometimes it is called absolutely the Kingdom, Matth. 4.23. Matth. 5.14. and 8.12. and 9.35. and 13.19. because this is indeed, a true and absolute Kingdom, in which only, is perfect freedom: other Kingdoms are but vain shows and semblances of that which is not; wherein the greatest Rulers and Commanders of others, are most enslaved to sin and Satan, and so can govern others, but not themselves. But why says he, the Kingdom of heaven is at hand, or draws near? was it not with them long before, in Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, and all the elect of every age, who looked *for redemption in Israel*, Luke 2.38? Yes; but first, in respect of the paucity of believers, compared with them under the Gospel. Secondly, in respect of the clearer revelation and better understanding of this Kingdom now then before, when all was under dark shadows of legal ceremonies. Lastly, though many believed and expected Christ the King of Israel then to come, yet not being then, but shortly to be exhibited unto Israel, and to the ends of the world; his Kingdom was properly said to draw near, wherein he was actually and eternally to reign: and in whose spiritual kingdom free remission of sins by his merits, was to be preached to all that truly repent and believe in him who freely justifies sinners.]

**Verse 5. **after the word, *Jerusalem*, Add [having had no Prophet among them from the repair of the second Temple unto this present, and now being persuaded by the extraordinary things, immediately foregoing or accompanying John’s nativity; as also the admirable and unusual austerity of his life, they expecting, (from the report of his preaching concerning the Kingdom of Christ at hand) some change of state, or some great and remarkable accident, they not only begin to listen, but to run in multitudes, out to see and hear him: hence was that confluence of people from Jerusalem, and the region adjacent.]

And in the same verse, next after the word, *river*, Add [Jordan which is the bound of Judea from the East: so called from the fountain, and steep descent of the waters thereof, as it were *יורד* *מן* *דן* *iored min Dan*, that is, descending or running down from Dan, as some say. It seems that Josephus *de bel. Jud. l. 1. c. 16* to whom Adricomius *Theatr. S. Mann. N. 76* joins Egesippus, will have it to be higher, at Panium an high mountain. Pliny *l. 5. c. 15.* says, *Jordanis amnis oritur è fonte Paneade qui cognomen dedit Caesareae*—which nothing hinders the truth of the forementioned etymology: for it runs from Dan, afterward named Caesarea Philippi, as they say, though the first rising of the springs and heads thereof, (long time unknown) are at Paneas: the Saracens (says Adricomius) call that spring, Medan, that is, the waters of Dan: for *מי* in their tongue, which is near the Hebrew *מים* signifies waters: now the people from those regions near Jordan flocking to John’s baptism must be from Judea, Galilee, Perea, Batanaea, and the country of the Trachonites.]

**Verse 6. **Between the word, *Jordan*, and the word, *as*, Add [or sprinkling with the water of Jordan,]

In the same verse, after, John 1.33. Add [and differing very much, and in many things, (as to the institution, end, effect, and form,) from those dippings, washings, or aspersions in the Jews’ manner of purifications, used.]

**Verse 12. **After, Mark 9.45. Add [unquenchable fire, that is, eternal; such as can never be put out, never make an end of the damned, that they might no more be sensible of the torment they are condemned to: it cannot be put out, it not being like this fire which we here use, that must be fed with some combustible matter, or it will go out, of its own accord; but *the breath of the Lord, like a stream of brimstone doth kindle it*, Isai. 30.33. that is, the justice and omnipotent power of God so perpetuates their torment by hell fire, as if indeficient streams and rivers of brimstone ran in upon it, so that it can never go out for want of supply; neither can it ever end the life and sense of the damned, who shall call to the mountains to fall upon them, and death to end them and their torments, but not obtain their desire: the eternal justice of God which in their lifetime they securely contemned, as it were so tempering the most active quality of the fire, that it shall ever torment, but never consume them whom it once seizes on: Nor need this seem strange to any: do we not (says Chrysostom) see the sun begetting, by it, light, and an admirable heat by beams perpendicular, yet in so many generations, that heat is not at all consumed? Do we not read of the bush on fire, not consumed? Exod. 3. By another expression he affirms the same thing, Isai. 66.24. *their worme shall not die, neither shall their fire be quenched*—See chap. 25.41, 46.]

## MATTHEW, CHAPTER 4

**Verse 1. **After the word, *parents*, Add [But why would God’s Spirit carry him to be tempted, who tempts no man? Jam. 1.13. and teaches us to pray, Matth. 6.13. *lead us not into temptation, but deliver us from evil*? We must for answer know, that,

1. There is considerable the difference between Christ invincibly holy, and sinful man, prone to all evil, and therefore easily overcome by temptations.

802. Christ came into the world to destroy death, and him that had the power thereof, the devil, Heb. 2.14. and to annul and destroy his works, 1 John 3.8. therefore presently after his inauguration by baptism, he, as it were, enters into the lists with the prince of darkness to a fierce monomachy and single combat, in his own sacred Person, to begin our victory over him and sin, which by and through Christ we shall obtain: as also to teach us not to be secure, but when we give our names to God, and resolve to leave our sins, then to look for the most furious charges of Satan; who then will pursue us, like a spiritual Pharaoh, with all his power, until the rod of God open us a passage to our promised rest, through the red sea of Christ’s blood.

3. He was led into the wilderness near Judea; not that Satan could not as easily and dangerously tempt in the populous City as in the eremite wilderness. See verse 5. but because solitude was most accommodate to this conflict, as most free from disturbance (of Satan’s machinations and trial of his uttermost power,) by any intervenient occasion or party of Christ against him, by counsel or consolation obstructing, or any ways hindering the worst that Satan could do against him.

4. Seeing we are subject to various temptations, all our life long, it behoves us to pray that we may not enter into temptation, that we may be preserved from it, or in it: we are hereto permitted, that we may be humbled in the knowledge of our own corruptions ever ready on all occasions to betray us to the enemy: that we may be stirred up to a faithful vigilance, having such restless adversaries to deal withal, 1 Pet. 5.8. to kindle in us an hearty thankfulness to God, and confidence in his mercy, of whose providence in such conflicts preserving us, we have perpetual experience. See Rom. 7.24, 25. 2 Cor. 12.9. To awaken us to frequent prayer, lest we enter into temptation: lastly, that through many combats exercised, we may grow more strong and confidently grounded in Christ: as trees in tender valleys, often shaken with winds, take firmer root: so grow the regenerate more strong, by temptations overcome. (See 2 Cor. 7.11.) Therefore Christ (being like us in all things, sin only excepted, to show us, that by him sin and temptations thereto, has in his own person, and in his body the Church, shall be overcome) would be subject to temptations.

5. That he might teach us not only by his precept, but also by his own example how to overcome temptations, by fasting, vigilance, prayer, and meditation, on the holy Scriptures.

6. That he might in his own sacred person have experience of our misery—*in that he himself hath suffered, being tempted, he is able to succour them that are tempted*—Heb. 2.17, 18.

7. That he might still and put the enemy and careless sinner to such silence, as can have no apology. Had not the regenerate been permitted to temptations, Satan would have said, as once of Job; *hast thou not made an hedge about him*, Job 1.10? had not Christ been tempted, the sinner would say, I cannot do that which you command me, because it is impossible: you bid me not be overcome of anger, lust, fear, sorrow, death, &c. but this is so contrary to nature (whose laws yourself have set) that all living creatures are subject to such affections; or if you think nature can be so easily resisted, *tu ipse qui praecipis, fac, ut sciam fieri posse*—do you that which you command me, that I may know ’tis feasible; but if you do it not why impose you laws on free men, which yourself will not obey? *prius disce qui doces*—*loqui facile est, praestare difficile*. Lactant. *l. 4. c. 23.* therefore Christ our perfect Teacher, ought both to teach by precept, and confirm the same by his practice. Now, how should he have done that which he commanded man to do, if he had not become man like unto him he taught? Had he been subject to no passion on which temptation could have taken hold, man might so have answered: truly, I would not sin, but am overpowered; I am clothed with frail flesh and blood—I am carried against my will, and sin, not because I would do so, but because I am compelled. I am sensible of my sinning, but the necessity of human frailty which I cannot resist, enforces me. How should he who shall judge the living and the dead, confute and reprove the sinner thus excusing his sins by the infirmity of flesh and nature, except himself had assumed the very same flesh and nature of man, without sin, that he might demonstrate, that even flesh and blood may be without sin? Men prone to sin, would fain seem, not only to be worthy of pardon, but to sin with good reason; and so would plead: Lord you sin not because you are free from this body: you covet not, for the immortal has no need or use of any thing; but I have need of many things to preserve my life: you fear not death because you are immortal: you contemn sorrow, because you can suffer no violence: but alas, I am mortal, and therefore fear those sorrows which flesh and blood cannot bear. Lest therefore the sinner might object, you command impossibilities, Christ subjected himself, to all human infirmities, necessities, yea and temptations, that to the pretense of impossibility he might have to answer: Lo, I am human, clothed with frail flesh, yet perform I that which I command you: behold, I go before you, *si praecipientem sequi non potes, sequere antecedentem*. Lactant. *q. s. c. 24.*]

**Verse 2. **After the word, *Gospel*, Add [Christ was pleased so to do, as to confirm the truth of the Gospel which he would henceforth preach; so to afford Satan place to tempt him with bodily necessities (See verse 3.) wherein he could not be overcome: also to teach us how to resist those temptations which are to be overcome, only with fasting and prayer. The first Adam was overcome in Paradise by tasting the forbidden fruit; the second Adam Christ overcame Satan in the fearful wilderness, by fasting.]

**Verse 4. **After, Deut. 8.3. Add [or shall not have his life sustained and prolonged by the creature, but the power of the Almighty Creator whose will is his word, and his word his work who in the creation set the laws of nature whereby this or that creature is nutritive, and not another: he yet works (Joh. 5.17.) with or without (sometimes against) the ordinary means, as he pleases.]

**Verse 6. **After, *as chap. 3.17.* Add [was affirmed.]

In the same verse, after the word, *armes*, Add [or save them from falling dangerously, by sudden catching hold on them.]

**Verse 7. **after the word, *doe*, Add [so Scripture must be interpreted, that one place may stand true with another: so here we must know that God has promised so to bear up his, that they shall not perish, but yet we must not neglect the ordinary means of our preservation which God has appointed; as here, by descending by steps, and safe degrees: for in neglect or contempt of God’s ordinance, we tempt God; which another Scripture, by Christ alleged, forbids.]

**Verse 10. **After the word, *world*, Add [if you could give it; which I know is none of yours to dispose of, but God’s.]

**Verse 11. **After the word, *able*, Add [1 Cor. 10.13.]

**Verse 16. **After, Psal. 97.11. Add [And the knowledge of God’s will concerning the same, which Christ taught and teaches in the Gospel preached.]

**Verse 17. **After, Mark 1.14. Add [That John being cast into prison by Herod, Jesus came into Galilee, and dwelt at Capernaum when John Baptist his forerunner had finished his course, witnessing concerning the true light Christ, Joh. 1.7, 9. and preparing the way for him, then it was necessary that he should appear, and manifest himself unto Israel.]

**Verse 25. **After the word, *therein*, Add [concerning which see on Mark, 7.3.]

## MATTHEW, CHAPTER 5

**Verse 6. **After the word, *satisfieth*, Add [comforts and strengthens the body, making it able and active to its several offices. Hence is the metaphor accommodate to the spirit, and duties spiritual: as if he had said, be not dejected you that are sensible of your spiritual wants; for if you do but desire grace, God will not be wanting to you: which our Savior teaches us by: argument from the less to the greater, Luke 11.13. if men in whom is but very little goodness, will yet give their children that which is good how much more will God infinitely good, wise powerful—*give the holy Spirit to them that ask him*? that is, a comfortable and sufficient measure of grace, as he said to Paul hungering and thirsting in his hot conflict with the messengers of Satan—*my grace is sufficient for thee*, 2 Cor. 12.9.]

**Verse 10. **Between the words, *are*, and, *two*, Add [of this secular and belonging to this life]

In the same verse, after the words, *adversity here*, Add [This is the most ample reward, of which St. James says, chap. 1. *Blessed is the man that endureth temptations: for when he is tryed he shall receive the Crown of life*. See also, Rev. 2.10. Seeing therefore the condition of the Saints as to this present life, is commonly afflicted and calamitous; Christ is pleased to comfort them with assured hope of blessedness in the life to come: and hence it may appear how happy a thing it is, to cast our cares and selves on God’s mercy and providence, who is a bountiful Rewarder of all those that trust in him. If you trust in riches, they cannot deliver you in the day of wrath: if in power of men &c. they will deceive your trust; in the end you shall find, that all confidence in creatures, will, like Pharaoh’s chariot-wheels, there fall off, when you are in greatest danger, and leave you to be overwhelmed with desperate miseries: but God is a most sure and bountiful Rewarder of his, giving them for the loss of an earthly possession, for some [unclear] and transitory estate, glory, riches, honor, here lost for his [unclear], the kingdom of heaven, eternal, unmeasurable, unspeakable, endless blessedness.]

**Verse 11. **Between the words: *Gr.* and *lying*, Add [*ψευδόμενοι*]

In the same verse, after the word, *Martyr*, Add [now even as truth, virtue, excellency; cannot, without apparent [influence] of Satan, be persecuted; therefore the father of lies furnishes [unclear] whom he instigates to injure the holy, with mischievous pretenses against them, suggesting that they are innovators, disturbers of the public peace, enemies to the state, seducers of the people, impious, &c. See Jer. 18.18, 23. Act. 6.11. Act. 24.5, see 23.2.]

**Verse 12. **After the words, *with them*, Add [and also the [executed] Prophets’ reward in heaven: because God is yesterday, [today], and the same forever, inviolably just and good. Now ’tis [unclear] which crowns all actions, and shows them good or bad, [happy] or unhappy: the false Prophet who loved the wages of unrighteousness, knew this; therefore, without exception to the cross desired to die the death of the righteous, and that his last end might be like his, Numb. 23.10. therefore fear no persecutors, their rage cannot be long, but the happiness of the persecuted shall be eternal.]

**Verse 17. **Between the word, *Gr.* and the words, *to dissolve*, Add [*καταλῦσαι*, which word among other things, signifies to overthrow; as here, by abrogating, repealing, or rendering it no longer binding: in which sense, Demosth. *Olynth. 3.* uses *λύσαι* *νόμον* to dissolve, or destroy the law: to bring to nothing, as Act. 5.38. *καταλυθήσεται*—it will *come to nought*.]

**Verse 23. **After the word, *seasoned*, Add [without which all is but the sacrifice of fools, and cannot be accepted.]

In the same verse, after the word *man*, Add [See 1 Cor. 11.28.

**Verse 42. **After, Deut. 15.8. Add [*δανείσασθαι* here used, commonly signifies, to take up on usury: and so the word *δανειστὴς* a usurer, coming of *δανείζω*, to put out to interest: yet here we must understand Christ speaking of a free lending. See Luke 6.35.]

**Verse 44. **After the word, *Saints*, Add [which they can have over their enemies in this world.]

**Verse 45. **After, John 3.10. Add [John 13.35.]

In the same verse, after, Eph. 5.1. Add [the most religious worship of God, is imitation of his holiness.]

## MATTHEW, CHAPTER 6

Before the beginning, of the Chapter Add this.

**Verse 1. ***Take heed*] This caution shows how prone we are to fail in our best duties: and the word *προσέχετε*, here used, imports a very solicitous care to be had, as Luke 12.1. as it were *προσέχειν* *τὸν* *νοῦν*, *adhibere animum*: attend and be cautious, lest the malicious enemy pervert your alms-deeds, and make a sin of your, otherwise, good works, depriving you of the fruits thereof.]

**Verse 7. **After 1 Kings 18.27. Add [Gr. *μὴ* *βαττολογήσητε* do not like that prating Battus, who was notorious for his absurd talkativeness and vain repetitions of that he had said, or might in few words have said. See Suid. *Βαττολογία*. Erasm. *Adag. chil. 2. Cent. 1. Ad. 91.*]

**Verse 13. **After the words, *seest best*, Add [the power] This is for divers good reasons added; as first to confirm our faith of obtaining, seeing that God who thus commands us to pray, has absolute power to do for us that which we want and pray for: nothing is impossible to him. Secondly, that we may not seem to attribute to him a prefect-like kingdom, or any less than an infinite and unlimited government over all creatures in heaven and earth. Again, that we may rest assured, that it is not of infirmity or want of ability, that he does not presently revenge their rebellion who acknowledge not, or obey not the laws of his kingdom; or that he does not instantly grant the requests of his servants, to deliver or supply them: however in his wisdom he delays it, he ever has that power which none can resist.

*and the glory*] Either yours is, or to you of right belongs all honor and glory; as Rom. 4.11. Rev. 5.12. 1 Chron. 16.28, 29. Rom. 11.36. Gal. 1.5. Isai. 6.3. Psal. 8.1. Psal. 24.7, 8. Psal. 29.3. and 113.4. and 148.13. &c. The sum is: We beg all these things of you; confident that we shall obtain, relying on your promise, holy will and power; for we know you have begun in us the kingdom of grace, to prepare us for the kingdom of glory, to the manifestation of this glory; and in this confidence with ardent voices we cry, Amen.]

In the same verse, after, John 3.11. Add [having acknowledged his kingdom, power and glory, to be eternal, as Psal. 145.10. in this one word, Amen, we express the fervency of our desire to obtain that which we prayed for, and our assurance of obtaining because he is faithful and true, and cannot deceive: for this is an Hebrew word, importing certainty and truth.]

**V. 26. **After the words, *fashion thereof*, Add [Gr. *ἀφανίζουσι*, they deform: *ἀφανίζω*, is, to obscure, deform, disfigure, &c. So that the natural color or complexion and favor may not appear: the word properly signifies, *è conspectu auferre*, to cause a thing not to appear, or be seen: So here they did away the nitor and comeliness of the visage, that only squalor appearing, they may seem in the neglect of their bodies, to fast.]

**Verse 24. **After, Luke 16.13. Add [he will not share with the World, Sin, Satan; he will be served with our body and soul: he made and redeemed them, and we are his, 1 Cor. 6.20.]

**Verse 34. **After the words, *but faith*, Add [every day of man’s life has care and evil enough; so that we need not anticipate our sorrows, and as it were, pull them upon us before the time: that evil comes too soon, that comes at last. Load not your minds with thoughts of tomorrows, wants, or afflictions. The gatherers of Manna in the wilderness were foolishly and unnecessarily solicitous for the morrow’s store, except only for the Sabbath Exod. 16.19. take all possible care for *the rest which remaineth for the people of God*, Heb. 4.9. for the things of this life, do a prudent and holy endeavor, and commit the rest to God; afflict not your soul; every day of transitory life will bring its sorrow with it.]

## MATTHEW, CHAPTER 7

**Verse 7. **After, James 1.6. Add [God often encourages us to earnest prayer, by promising us audience. See Psal. 50.15. Jer. 29.12, 13, 14. and also by the admirable effects of the same. See Psal. 81.7. Exod. 17.11. &c. Psal. 3.4. and 6.9. and 10.17. &c. Act. 12.5. &c. Jam. 5.15, 17, 18. Now the three words here used, are to teach us so many necessary rules concerning prayer. 1. That we be sensible of our wants; for we ask not that which we have, or think we want not; but that which we know we want, and would have. 2. That we seek, as using the means to attain: this intimates, care, and discretion in seeking, where, and when, we are likely to find. 3. That we constantly persevere; (though we find not the way to our desires presently open) and as it were knock at the door until it be opened to us.]

**Verse 10. **After the words, *hurt us*, Add [or deny us any thing to do us good. See Psal. 84.11. Rom. 8.32.]

**Verse 13. **After the words, *born such*, Add [and custom of sinning makes it more easy; men fear their first offenses but use emboldens them to shameless impudence: and therefore ’tis here compared to a spacious and easy way: so the word, *εὐρύχωρος* here used, signifies, spacious, broad: here’s nothing to hinder or trouble the sinner, but that he may go smoothly on for a time.]

In the same verse, after, Salvian. *de gub. dei l. 3.* Add [The greater the multitude of sinners, the greater is the mercy of the Deity.]

**Verse 21. **After, James 1.22. Add [though doubling of the word sometimes imports the vehemency of the speaker; yet here it seems to import a frequency and often having God in their mouths. This is not all: to seem religious or to talk much of Religion, like the bees humming at young Plato’s lips, may possibly be a presage of future eloquence: but if you will be saved, your Religion must be like that swarm which Samson found by the vineyards of Timnah, within your heart: he is an Israelite, who is one within, Rom. 2.29. God regards the heart.]

**Verse 22. **After the words, *damnation be*, Add [*δυνάμεις* here, as also elsewhere, signifies miracles. See Mark 9.39. Luke 10.13. and 19.37.]

**Verse 23. **After Psal. 6.8. Add [See on chap. 25.41. *ἀποχωρεῖτε*, get you far from me, and the joys of heaven, out of which can be nothing but irremiss sorrow and misery of body and soul to eternity; and that which he commands is and shall ever be inevitable.]

**Verse 24. **After, Luke 6.47. Add [This *Whosoever* declares, that God is no accepter of persons: all must hear him that will be saved. His word is written in the holy Scripture, and spoken by all faithful Ministers, concerning whom he says, *he that heareth you, heareth me*—Luke 10.16. Matth. 10.14. which all must hear; King, Priest, Prophet, People: if the Prophet, (speak he never so well to others) hear not himself, he must be cast away; because he that will not hear this word, despises not man, but God, whose truth, word and ordinance it is.]

**Verse 26. **After the word, *men* Add [Yet all superstructions must fall, if the foundation fail: so is it here.

## MATTHEW, CHAPTER 8

**Verse 2. **After Luke 5.12. Add [it is here a note of a memorable and unexpected accident, or thing which men present, could neither foreknow, nor in their natural abilities reasonably look for.]

In the same verse, after the word, *humility*, Add [and gesture, or posture of distressed suppliants, *accedere ad genua*: to fall upon their knees, or to the feet and knees, of those, of whom they begged mercy, favor, or relief.]

In the same verse again, after the words, *can heal it*, Add [and that he would show his power and mercy on him therein: had he known the power and mercy of Christ to others, never so much or confidently, except he had hoped, that it should be extended to, and showed on him also, he would not have come to entreat it: for who can pray for that, whereof he has no hope of attaining?]
